<meta>This page concludes the scholarly notes provided by Johannes Meursius on the *Lausiac History* of Palladius. It follows Meursius’s detailed philological and historical commentary on the preceding pages, focusing here on the correction of chronological records and consular lists before concluding the section.</meta>

### I. MEURSIUS’S NOTES ON PALLADIUS.

...tors, in the *Fasti* <note>Chronological lists or calendars of events, particularly those naming the annual consuls who served as the primary means of dating years in Rome.</note>, and the *Chronicle* of Cassiodorus. Under these consuls, Constantine the Great died, according to the *Fasti* and the Deacon <note>Likely Paul the Deacon, an 8th-century historian.</note>, in the place cited. Therefore, I believe Cassiodorus is in error, as he records this as happening two years later, under the consuls Constantius and Constans. But, since the opportunity arises, I will proceed to correct another passage in those *Capitoline Fasti* <note>The *Fasti Capitolini*, an important list of Roman magistrates found in the Roman Forum.</note> which has been corrupted by the similarity of names. Twenty-two years after the consuls Felicianus and Titianus, we read *Titus Fabius Titianus* and *Neratius Cerealis*. It is no different in the *Chronicle* of Cassiodorus: *Titianus and Cerealis*. But in both places, *Datianus* should be written instead of *Titianus*. For the *Sicilian Fasti* <note>Also known as the *Chronicon Paschale*, a 7th-century Byzantine universal chronicle.</note> correctly state: *Datianus and Cerealis* <note>original: "Δατιανὸς καὶ Κερεάλις" (Datianos kai Cerealis)</note>. Ammianus [Marcellinus] in Book 17 says: *Under the consuls Datianus and Cerealis, while all affairs throughout the Gauls were being arranged with more cautious care*, etc. And in both law codes <note>Referring to the Theodosian and Justinian Codes.</note>, imperial rescripts are found which explicitly name *Datianus* alongside Cerealis.

->THE END.<-

**In the Preface, page * 3, line 5, for "reddemur" read "redderemur."** <note>This corrects a printing error in the Latin text, changing "we shall be returned" to the more grammatically appropriate "we might be returned."</note>

<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s philological notes on the *Lausiac History* by Palladius. Having previously discussed the dating of Constantine’s death and the correction of consular names in the Roman *Fasti*, Meursius here focuses on specific vocabulary choices regarding female physicians and further chronological discrepancies in the historical record of the late 4th century.</meta>

### NOTES ON PALLADIUS.

### Concerning the double-meaning in Ancyra.
<note>The Greek heading refers to a passage in Palladius set in Ancyra, likely regarding a specific person or linguistic term.</note>

**He himself holding the place of a female physician** <note>original: "αὐτὸς ἰατρίνης τόπον ἐπέχων" (autos iatrines topon epechon)</note>] Eustathius <note>A 12th-century scholar and archbishop of Thessalonica known for his extensive commentaries on Homer.</note> discusses this word in his notes on the *Iliad*, book 11. He says that the word *iatrine* <note>A Greek term for a female physician or midwife.</note> is not Greek. However, it is found among the technical writers. He notes that Horos <note>An ancient grammarian.</note> says that *iatrine* is not a proper Greek word. Alexander of Cotiaeum <note>A 2nd-century grammarian and teacher of Marcus Aurelius.</note>, speaking with scholarly precision, says that it is simply not <term>Attic</term> <note>The prestige dialect of ancient Athens, often used by grammarians as the standard for "pure" Greek.</note>. It is used in place of a midwife in this passage, as well as in the <term>Pandects</term> <note>Also known as the *Digest*, a vast collection of Roman legal opinions compiled under Emperor Justinian.</note>. We take the reasoning for this from these words of Ulpian <note>A famous Roman jurist.</note> in Law 1 of the *Digest* concerning extraordinary investigations:
> *But they should also hear the midwife, who certainly seems to practice medicine.*

### Concerning the deceived virgin.

**Or to destroy myself** <note>original: "ἢ ἐμαυτὴν διοκεῦσαι" (e emauten diokeusai). This refers to a passage where a character considers suicide.</note>] This should be written as: *or truly myself*. <note>Meursius is suggesting a textual emendation to the Greek manuscript.</note>

### Concerning the brother who was with me.

**From the consulship of Tatianus until today** <note>original: "ἀπὸ τῆς ὑπατείας Τατιανοῦ μέχρι τῆς σήμερον" (apo tes hypateias Tatianou mechri tes semeron)</note>] This **Tatianus** was Consul along with Symmachus, as testified by the *Sicilian Fasti* and the *Capitoline Fasti* <note>Ancient chronological lists of magistrates used for dating years.</note>, in the 1143rd year from the founding of Rome, which was the year of Christ 391. There exists more than one imperial rescript <note>A formal written reply by a Roman emperor to a legal query.</note> in the Theodosian and Justinian Codes given under these consuls. However, the *Capitoline Fasti* must be corrected; in them, it is published as *Tiberius Fabius Titianus and Quintus Aurelius Avianus Symmachus*. It should be rewritten as *T. Fab. Tatianus*. The same error is found in the *Chronicle* of Cassiodorus. In the tenth book of the Deacon <note>Likely Paul the Deacon, the historian of the Lombards.</note>, we read of a Consul **Tatianus** with Felicianus: *In a public villa he was consumed by disease, and in his own testament [he left things] to all his sons, in the consulship of Felicianus and Tatianus.* Is this the same man? I do not think so, for there is an interval of 54 years. Rather, it should be written here as *Felicianus and Titianus*. It is written this way in several rescripts of the emper-

# JOHANNES MEURSIUS

...to whom the care of the Constantinian bedchamber had been entrusted; breathing another spirit, and a dire one, they sentenced him to a lethal penalty <note>Meursius is likely referring to Eusebius the *praepositus sacri cubiculi* (Grand Chamberlain) under Emperor Constantius II, who was eventually executed.</note>. See also Paul the Deacon <note>An 8th-century monk and historian of the Lombards.</note>, book 11; Zonaras <note>A 12th-century Byzantine chronicler.</note>, *Annals*, book 3; and Cedrenus <note>Georgios Kedrenos, an 11th-century historian.</note> in his section on Julian. There was also another Eusebius, likewise a chamberlain, under Honorius and Theodosius; concerning whom see Zosimus <note>A 5th-century pagan historian of Rome.</note>, book 5.

**Taking for himself the *sticharion*** <note>original: "λαϐὼν ἑαυτῷ τὸ στιχάριον" (labōn heautō to sticharion)</note>] Regarding the meaning of the word <term>sticharion</term> <note>A long-sleeved tunic, often worn as a liturgical vestment in the Eastern Church.</note>, see our Glossary.

**And the book** <note>original: "καὶ τὸ βιϐλίον" (kai to biblion)</note>] See again our Glossary concerning this word.

->Concerning Juliana<-

**Who said she had received it from Symmachus himself, the translator for the Jews** <note>original: "ἥτις ἔλεγε παρ᾽ αὐτοῦ Συμμάχου τοῦ ἑρμηνέως τῶν Ἰουδαίων αὐτὸ εἰληφέναι" (hētis elege par’ autou Symmachou tou hermēneōs tōn Ioudaiōn auto eilēphenai)</note>] He is referring to Symmachus, the translator of the Bible <note>Symmachus the Ebionite was one of the major translators of the Old Testament into Greek in the late 2nd century.</note>. Concerning what is said here, Eusebius also records it in his *Church History*, book 6, chapter 17:
> *And commentaries by Symmachus are still circulated today, in which he seems to support the aforementioned heresy by directing his efforts against the Gospel according to Matthew. Origen indicates that he obtained these and other translations of the Scriptures by Symmachus from a certain Juliana, who, he says, inherited the books from Symmachus himself.*

One should also consult Euthymius Zygabenus <note>A 12th-century monk and theologian.</note> in his *Preface to the Psalms*, and Nicephorus <note>Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopoulos, a 14th-century church historian.</note>, book 5, chapter 12.

->Concerning a certain *magistrianus*<-

**A certain young man, a *magistrianus*** <note>original: "νεανίσκος τις μαγισριανὸς" (neaniskos tis magistrianos)</note>] Regarding this word <term>magistrianus</term> <note>An official or agent under the command of the Master of Offices in the late Roman Empire, often serving as couriers or inspectors.</note>, see our Glossary.

**And with the shirts** <note>original: "τοῖς τε καμισίοις" (tois te kamisiois)</note>] See also our Glossary for this word <term>camisia</term> <note>A linen shirt or undergarment; the root of the modern word "chemise."</note>.

NOTES ON PALLADIUS.

### Concerning Pinianus. <note>original: "περὶ Ὀππιανοῦ" (peri Oppianou). Meursius argues this is a corruption of the name Pinianus.</note>

**Likewise also Oppianus** <note>original: "ὁμοίως καὶ Ὀππιανὸς" (homoiōs kai Oppianos)</note>] One should write: **But Pinianus** <note>original: "ὁ δὲ Πινιανός" (ho de Pinianos)</note>. In the Table of Contents for this chapter, it should also be written: **Concerning Pinianus**. In the manuscript used by Hervetus <note>Gentian Hervet (1499–1584), a French humanist and translator.</note>, it seems it was: **Concerning Opianus**. Likewise: **Likewise also Opianus**. Both are incorrect.

### Concerning Macarius and Constantine.

**From the vicariate** <note>original: "ἀπὸ βικαρίας" (apo bikarias)</note>] A *vicaria* is a **Vicariatus** <note>The office or district of a *vicar*, a high-ranking administrative official in the late Roman Empire.</note>. See our Glossary on this term.

### The best law of the state

**The indefinite law of the state** <note>original: "τὸν ἀόριστον τῆς πολιτείας θεσμὸν" (ton aoriston tēs politeias thesmon)</note>] One should write: **The best law of the state** <note>Meursius suggests a correction from *aoriston* (indefinite/unlimited) to *ariston* (best), a common scribal error in Greek.</note>.

### Concerning the virgin who received the blessed Athanasius.

**I know a virgin in Alexandria** <note>original: "παρθένον οἶδα τὴν ἐν Ἀλεξανδρείᾳ" (parthenon oida tēn en Alexandreia)</note>] Regarding this virgin, one should consult Simeon Metaphrastes <note>A 10th-century Byzantine hagiographer known for his collection of Lives of the Saints.</note>, and a certain anonymous author in the *Life of Athanasius*. See also Sozomenus <note>A 5th-century historian of the Christian Church.</note>, book 5, chapter 6, and Nicephorus <note>Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopulus, a 14th-century church historian.</note>, book 10, chapter 6.

**The whole clergy** <note>original: "ὁ κλῆρος ἅπας" (ho klēros hapas)</note>] See our Glossary regarding this term.

**By Eusebius, who was the provost at that time under King Constantius** <note>original: "δι’ Εὐσεβίου τοῦ κατὰ τὸν καιρὸν πραιποσίτου ἐπὶ Κωνσταντίου τοῦ βασιλέως" (di’ Eusebiou tou kata ton kairon praipositou epi Kōnstantiou tou basileōs)</note>] This is Eusebius the eunuch, whom Athanasius himself mentions in his *Letter to the Solitaries*. Ammianus <note>Ammianus Marcellinus, a 4th-century Roman soldier and historian.</note> names him in book 14: *Truly, diligent effort was devoted to this, and once it was reported, Eusebius, the Grand Chamberlain of the bedchamber, was sent to Chalon-sur-Saône. Later, he was put to death by Julian.* Nicephorus says in book 10, chapter 5: *He also expelled the eunuchs from the Imperial court. Furthermore, he even punished with death Eusebius, who had held the primary position there.* Sozomenus writes in book 5, chapter 5: **He punished with death Eusebius, the chief of the royal court.** <note>original: "Εὐσέβιον δὲ, τὸν μείζονα τῆς βασιλικῆς αὐλῆς, θανάτῳ ἐζημίωσε."</note> Ammianus writes in book 22: *Eusebius regarding these matters,*

<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s scholarly commentary on Palladius’s Lausiac History. Following the previous page’s discussion on corrections to the names of officials and the virgin in Alexandria who sheltered Athanasius, Meursius now turns to identifying the specific identity and writings of Clement of Alexandria and the family of Melania the Younger.</meta>

# THE WRITINGS OF JOHN MEURSIUS

"Clement the author of the *Stromateis* <note>The title *Stromateis* means "Miscellanies" or "Patchwork," referring to a collection of diverse topics.</note> was known in Alexandria." And Jerome, in his *Catalogue of Ecclesiastical Writers*, calls him not "the Alexandrian," but a priest of the Alexandrian church. Ado does the same in his *Chronicle*. Therefore, I believe the error is sufficiently clear. His first and second names were *Titus Flavius*. Eusebius is a certain witness for me in his *Ecclesiastical History*, Book VI, Chapter XIII: <note>original: "τῶν δὲ Κλήμεντος στρωματεῖς οἱ πάντες ὀκτὼ παρ’ ἡμῖν σώζονται, οὓς καὶ τοιαύτης ἠξίωσεν ἐπιγραφῆς. Τίτου Φλαβίου Κλήμεντος τῶν κατὰ τὴν ἀληθῆ Φιλοσοφίαν γνωστικῶν ὑπομνημάτων στρωματεῖς."</note> "Of the writings of Clement, all eight books of the *Stromateis* are preserved among us, which he also deemed worthy of such a title: *The Stromateis of Titus Flavius Clement, of the Gnostic Memoirs according to the True Philosophy*." Eusebius, Jerome, and Nicephorus review his works in the places cited; yet no one mentions the *Commentaries on Amos*, which our Palladius praises here. As far as the books of the *Stromateis* are concerned, he wrote them to Alexander, as the author John of Damascus states in *On the Adoration of Images*, Book III: <note>original: "τοῦ ἀρχαιοτάτου Κλήμεντος πρὸς Ἀλέξανδρον ἐκ τοῦ ἑβδόμου βιβλίου τῶν Στρωματέων."</note> "From the seventh book of the *Stromateis* of the most ancient Clement, written to Alexander."

->Concerning Melania the Younger.<-

**To her own husband Pinianus**] In the manuscript used by Hervetus <note>Gentian Hervet (1499–1584), a French translator of church fathers.</note>, it seems it was written **Pipianus**. But this is incorrect. The husband of Melania the Younger was Pinianus, which is also well-known from the *Letters* of Augustine.

**Half-garments** <note>original: "ἡμιφόρια" (hemiphoria). A technical term for a type of short cloak or veil.</note>] See our Glossary regarding this word.

**And her mother Albina**] She had married the son of Melania the Elder. Thus our Palladius says above: "And he also instructed Albina, her own daughter-in-law, the wife of her son."

<meta>catchword: and</meta>

<summary>In this passage, Meursius provides a scholarly correction of the name and titles of Clement of Alexandria, citing Eusebius and Jerome, and clarifies the family relations of Melania the Younger.</summary>
<keywords>Clement of Alexandria, Titus Flavius, Stromateis, Eusebius, Jerome, Melania the Younger, Pinianus, Albina, Palladius, ecclesiastical history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s scholarly notes on the Lausiac History by Palladius. ### NOTES ON PALLADIUS

...and a *cover* <note>original Latin: "operculum"</note>; which women use especially in the summer. See more in our Glossary.

**Concerning a certain virgin, and Collythus.**

"The holy martyr Collythus who was in that place"] Hervetus <note>Gentian Hervet (1499–1584) was a French scholar who translated many Greek patristic texts into Latin.</note> is mistaken, for he thinks that "the martyr" <note>original Greek: "τὸν μάρτυρα"</note> here refers to a *Prefect of the martyrdom* <note>original Latin: "Præpositum martyrio"</note>. It is, in truth, the martyr himself.

"In the monastery"] In Hervetus's manuscript, it seems the text was "in the shrine of the martyr" <note>original Greek: "ἐν τῷ μαρτυρίῳ"</note>. This is correct; for he says afterward: "Having gone out, she went away into the shrine of the martyr." Regarding what a *martyrium* <note>A "martyrium" is a church, chapel, or shrine built over the grave of a Christian martyr.</note> is, see our Glossary.

"The writing of Clement, the author of the *Stromateis*, on the prophet Amos"] Clement is nicknamed the "author of the *Stromateis*" <note>original Greek: "Στρωματεύς" (Stromateus). His most famous work, the *Stromata* or "Miscellanies," was a collection of diverse observations on Christianity and philosophy.</note> by more recent writers. Thus in Nicephorus, Book 4, Chapter 33: *In that same course of time, Clement the priest, who was nicknamed the author of the Stromateis, applied himself to the same study as Pantaenus at Alexandria.* Theodorus Metochites, in his *History*, Book 1: "And after this, Severus [reigned] for 17 years; under whom lived Clement the *Stromateus*, and Symmachus, and Origen." Some make this man an Alexandrian, others an Athenian. Epiphanius is a witness to this in *Against the Secundians*, Heresy 32: "Whether those who wrote well, and proved the truth of these things in their own writings, such as Clement—whom some say was an Alexandrian, others an Athenian—but also the holy Irenaeus." To give my own opinion: I judge him to be an Athenian; but that he began to be called an Alexandrian because, as a priest of the Alexandrian church, he began to become famous there for his writings. Eusebius, in his *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 5, Chapter 11: "During this time, Clement was known at Alexandria for his exercises in the divine scriptures." So also says Cedrenus.

<meta>catchword: or Cle-</meta>

<summary>Meursius corrects earlier translations regarding the martyr Collythus and analyzes the historical identity of Clement of Alexandria, arguing he was an Athenian by birth who achieved fame in Alexandria.</summary>
<keywords>Clement of Alexandria, Collythus, Stromata, Gentian Hervet, Athens, Alexandria, martyrium, Eusebius, Epiphanius, ecclesiastical history</keywords>

# JOHN MEURSIUS

...daughter; and who was married off to Arsaces, the king of Armenia, by Constantius. He had joined Olympias—the daughter of Ablabius, the former Praetorian Prefect—to him (Arsaces) as his wife, though she had previously been the betrothed of his brother, Constans.

"The bride who belonged to Nebridius for only a few days" <note>original Greek: "νύμφη ἣ πρὸς ὀλίγας ἡμέρας Νεβριδίῳ"</note>. The *Menologium* <note>A liturgical calendar of the Eastern Orthodox Church containing biographies of saints.</note> agrees with this. However, Ammianus <note>Ammianus Marcellinus (c. 330–397 AD), a Roman historian and soldier.</note> says otherwise, and we have already quoted his words. Mention of this Nebridius occurs more than once in Ammianus’s work.

### Concerning Candida.

"The blessed Candida" <note>original Greek: "ἡ μακαρία Κανδίδα"</note>. I believe this is the woman mentioned by Paulinus in his *Life of Ambrose*.

### Concerning Amatalis.

"By the name Amatalis" <note>original Greek: "ὀνόματι Ἀματάλιδι"</note>. This should be written as: "By the name Mother Talis" <note>original Greek: "ὀνόματι Ἀμμᾷ Ταλίδι"</note>. For *Amma* <note>A title of respect, the feminine equivalent of "Abba" (Father), used for spiritual mothers or superiors in early Christian monasticism.</note> was a title of dignity among monastic women, as Palladius himself records above in the *Life of Pitirum*. Nor was she called *Amma* as an honor without cause; she had sixty virgins under her who were subject to her discipline, as is evident from Palladius’s following words.

"Of this nun" <note>original Greek: "ταύτης τῆς καλογήρου" (kalogeros). While this literally means "good elder," it became a standard term for a monk or nun.</note>. Regarding this word, see what we have written in our *Glossary*.

### Concerning the virgin Taor.

"Or a shoulder-cape" <note>original Greek: "ἢ ὠμοφόριον" (omophorion)</note>. This reading cannot be correct; for the *omophorion* <note>A distinctive liturgical vestment worn by bishops, draped over the shoulders.</note> belonged to patriarchs and bishops, as we demonstrate in our *Glossary*. I write instead, "or a head-veil" <note>original Greek: "ἢ μαφόριον" (maphorion)</note>, as it seems to have appeared in the manuscript used by Hervetus <note>Gentian Hervet (1499–1584), a prolific French translator of Greek patristic texts.</note>. The *maphorion* was a woman’s garment for covering the head. Philo of Carpasia, in his commentary on the *Song of Songs*, says: *The theristrum <note>A lightweight summer veil or shawl.</note>, which they also call a maphorion, is a woman’s covering for the head and a sign of faith—*

<summary>In this section, Meursius provides historical and philological corrections to Palladius’s text, clarifying the identities of noblewomen like Olympias and refining monastic terminology such as the title "Amma" and the specific garments worn by religious women.</summary>
<keywords>Olympias, Arsaces of Armenia, Nebridius, Amma, monastic titles, maphorion, omophorion, Candida, Palladius, ecclesiastical dress</keywords>

# NOTES ON PALLADIUS

...and Diaconus <note>Paul the Deacon (c. 720–799), a Benedictine monk and historian.</note> in the *Miscellaneous History*, book 13, report the year as 1164 from the Founding of the City <note>Latin: "ab V.C." (Ab Urbe Condita), the traditional system for dating years from the founding of Rome in 753 BC.</note>. Cedrenus and Ado report it as 1160. The numbers in Godfrey of Viterbo’s *Chronicle*, Part 16, must be corrected: *This second capture of the city of Rome, following Alaric's, was carried out by the violence of the Vandals in the year 1164 from the founding of the City.* Today it is published as 1201. Cassiodorus <note>A Roman statesman and writer (c. 485–585) who served under Theodoric the Great.</note> says in his *Chronicle* that it occurred during the consulship of Flavius Vararus and Tertullus, whom the *Fasti* <note>The Roman chronological lists of years and their presiding consuls.</note> place in the year 1162.

->Concerning Salvia.<-

"The blessed Salvia" <note>original Greek: "τὴν μακαρίαν Σαλβίαν"</note>. The manuscript which is in the Library of the King of the French <note>The Bibliothèque nationale de France.</note> also calls her by this name. In the manuscript of Hervetus <note>Gentian Hervet (1499–1584), a French theologian and translator.</note>, the name seems to have been "Silvania" <note>original Greek: "Σιλβανίαν"</note>.

"Sister of Rufinus, the former Prefect" <note>original Greek: "ἀδελφὴν δὲ Ῥυφίνου τοῦ ἀπὸ ἐπάρχων"</note>. We noted this above in the *Life of Ammonius*.

"To rest upon a warm cushion" <note>original Greek: "θερμοτύλῳ ἐπαναπαύεσθαι"</note>. In the Royal Manuscript I mentioned, it is written as *mermotylo* <note>original Greek: "μερμοτύλῳ"</note>.

"Benefiting not at all from a litter" <note>original Greek: "οὐ λεκτικίῳ ὠφελοῦσε που"</note>. Regarding the word *lectikion* <note>A term for a sedan chair or portable litter used by the wealthy in late antiquity.</note>, see our *Glossary*.

->Concerning Olympias.<-

"The most venerable Olympias" <note>original Greek: "ἡ σεμνοπρεπεστάτη Ὀλυμπιάς"</note>. Concerning her, see Nicephorus, book 13, chapter 24, and Sozomen, book 8, chapters 9 and 24. Several letters of John Chrysostom <note>The Archbishop of Constantinople (c. 347–407), known for his eloquent preaching.</note> to her are still extant. Gregory Nazianzen also writes about her in his letter to Anysius.

"She was the daughter, according to the flesh, of Seleucus, the former Count" <note>original Greek: "θυγάτηρ γέγονε τὸ κατὰ σάρκα Σελεύκου τοῦ ἀπὸ κόμητος"</note>. The Greek *Menologium* <note>A liturgical book of the Orthodox Church containing lives of the saints.</note> for the eighth day before the Kalends of August <note>July 25th.</note> records her as the daughter of Anysius II, a Count.

"Granddaughter of Ablabius, the former Prefect" <note>original Greek: "ἐκγόνη δὲ Ἀβλαβίου τοῦ ἀπὸ ἐπάρχων"</note>. Ammianus <note>Ammianus Marcellinus, the 4th-century Roman historian.</note>, in book 20, mentions her not as the granddaughter of Ablabius, but...

<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s philological commentary on the "Lausiac History" by Palladius. Having previously discussed the identities of Salvia and Olympias, Meursius now moves into detailed textual emendations and historical identifications of several other ascetics, including Elpidius, Sabvatius, Abramius, and Melania.</meta>

# JOHANNES MEURSIUS

"...the paralytic was also left behind because of sin, as he said: 'See, you have become well; sin no more'" <note>original Greek: "ἐγκατελείφθη καὶ ὁ παραλυτικὸς διὰ ἁμαρτίας ὡς εἶπεν οἷς ἴδε καὶ ὑγιὴς γέγονας μηκέτι ἁμάρτανε"</note> ] This passage is highly corrupt. For what does that mean: "as he said to whom" <note>original: "ὡς εἶπεν οἷς"</note>? I **re-punctuate** and **restore** it as follows: "...to whom Jesus said: 'See, you have become well; sin no more'" <note>original Greek: "ᾧ εἶπεν ὁ Ἰησοῦς. ἴδε, ὑγιὴς γέγονας, μ. ἁ."</note>. The scribe had written "Jesus" using a compendium <note>A "compendium" was a shorthand abbreviation used by medieval scribes to save space; here "ὁ ῖς" (ho Iēs) was likely misread as "οἷς" (hois).</note>, and from this it became "to whom." No one will doubt that this is the true reading of this passage.

->About Elpidius.<-

"A certain Elpidius, a Cappadocian by birth" ] **Basil the Great** mentions him in **Epistle 8**: "For I have spent this fiftieth day in illness, upon which our beloved and most diligent brother and fellow-minister **Elpidius** visited us."

"Of the <term>chorepiscopus</term> Timothy" ] Concerning the <term>chorepiscopi</term> <note>A "chorepiscopus" (country bishop) was a rank in the early church for a clergyman who exercised episcopal functions in rural districts under the authority of a city bishop.</note>, see our **Glossary**.

->About Sabvatius.<-

"And of Philoneolus Thomas" <note>original: "καὶ τοῦ Φιλονεόλου θωμᾶ"</note> ] It should be written: "of Philoneolus Thoues."

->About Abramius.<-

"A certain Abramius became a monk" ] **John Moschus** mentions the abbot Abramius in the *Spiritual Meadow* <note>original title: "Limon."</note>, **Chapter 97**, as does **Nicephorus, Book 11, Chapter 42**.

->About Melania.<-

"And the right of two children" <note>original: "καὶ τέκνων δύο δικαίωμα"</note> ] It should be written: "the right of three children." The **Right of three children** <note>original Latin: "Ius trium liberorum." This was a legal privilege in the Roman Empire granted to citizens who had fathered three or more children, exempting them from certain duties and providing inheritance advantages.</note> is well-known in Roman affairs. The origin of the error lies in the confusion between the Greek numerals β' (2) and γ' (3).

"A certain barbarian storm" ] He understands this as the invasion of the Goths <note>Likely referring to the Sack of Rome in 410 AD by Alaric and the Visigoths.</note>.

"Which had been cherished in beauty for one thousand two hundred years" ] Those years were not entirely 1200. See **Orosius, Book 7, Chapter 40**.

<summary>In this page, Meursius corrects several scribal errors in the Greek text of Palladius, using historical references from Basil the Great and Roman law to restore the original meaning. He specifically identifies errors in the numbering of children for Melania and clarifies the dates surrounding the Gothic invasion of Rome.</summary>

<keywords>Johannes Meursius, Palladius, Lausiac History, Elpidius, Basil the Great, chorepiscopus, Abramius, John Moschus, Melania the Elder, Ius trium liberorum, Goths, Orosius, textual criticism</keywords>

->NOTES ON PALLADIUS.<-

### Concerning Rufinus.

**"The most noble and solitary Rufinus"** <note>original Greek: "ὁ εὐγενέστατος καὶ μονότροφος Ῥυφῖνος"</note> ] This is that **Rufinus** <note>Rufinus of Aquileia (c. 345–411), a monk and translator who became famous for his bitter theological dispute with St. Jerome over the works of Origen.</note> to whom the Blessed **Jerome** was so hostile. **Nicephorus** mentions him in book 10, chapter 28, as does **Gennadius** in his *Catalogue of Illustrious Men*.

**"And they lived in the schism concerning Paulinus"** <note>original Greek: "ἐζώωσαν δὲ ἐν τῷ σχίσματι τὸ κατὰ Παυλῖνον"</note> ] Regarding this schism, one should consult **Nicephorus**, book 12, chapter 3. **Cedrenus** also mentions it.

### Concerning Chronius.

**"A certain man named Chronius"** <note>original Greek: "Χρόνιος δὲ τις ὀνόματι"</note> ] I would write it as "A certain Cronius" <note>original Greek: "Κρόνιος τις"</note>. We have spoken of him above, in the chapter **Concerning those in Nitria** <note>Nitria was one of the earliest Christian monastic centers in Egypt.</note>.

### Concerning Paphnutius.

**"When Paphnutius had come together"** <note>original Greek: "συνδραμόντος δὲ Παφνουτίου"</note> ] Regarding him, one should see **Cassian** in his *Conferences*, and **Rufinus** in the *Lives of the Fathers*, chapters 16 and 25. **Sidonius** also mentions him in *Poem 16*, and **Nicephorus** in book 9, chapter 14.

**"And the matters concerning Stephen, who fell away"** <note>original Greek: "καὶ τὰ κατὰ Στέφανον τὸν ἐκπεσόντα"</note> ] He means **Stephen**, the Bishop of Antioch, who was removed from his bishopric on account of a scandalous crime. See **Theodoret**, *Ecclesiastical History*, book 2, chapters 9 and 10, and **Nicephorus**, book 9, chapter 23.

**"And Eucarpius"** <note>original Greek: "καὶ Εὐκάρπιον"</note> ] **Nicephorus** mentions him in book 11, chapter 8.

**"And the matters concerning Valens the Palestinian"** <note>original Greek: "καὶ τὰ κατὰ Οὐάλεντα τὸν Παλαιστῖνον"</note> ] This is that **Valens** who, along with **Ursacius**, was removed from his bishopric at the Council of Ariminum <note>The Council of Ariminum (359 AD) was a major church council where the Arian controversy was debated; Valens was a leader of the Arian faction.</note>. See **Sozomen**, book 4, chapter 16, and **Nicephorus**, book 9, chapter 11.

**"And the matters concerning Ptolemy who was in Scete"** <note>original Greek: "καὶ τὰ κατὰ Πτολεμαῖον τὸν ἐν Σκήτῃ"</note> ] **Palladius** relates more extensively about this man above.

# JOHANNES MEURSIUS

->Concerning Philoromus.<-

**"To the most God-beloved priest Philoromus"** <note>original Greek: "τῷ Θεοφιλεστάτῳ πρεσβυτέρῳ Φιλορώμῳ"</note> ] **Nicephorus** <note>Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopoulos, a 14th-century Byzantine church historian.</note> mentions the martyr Philoromus in book 7, chapter 9.

**"Having assisted himself"** <note>original Greek: "ἑαυτὸν ἐπαρκέσας"</note> ] One should write, "myself" <note>original Greek: "ἐμαυτόν"</note>.

->Concerning Melania.<-

**"The thrice-blessed Melania"** <note>original Greek: "ἡ τριςμακαρία Μελάνη"</note> ] Regarding **Melania** <note>Melania the Elder (c. 350–410 AD), a prominent Roman noblewoman and desert mother who founded a monastery on the Mount of Olives.</note> and her husband **Pinianus**, see **Augustine's** Letters 224, 225, and 227. **Jerome** also mentions her in his *Letter to Paula* regarding the death of Blesilla <note>The OCR reads "Bresillæ," a common transcription error for Blesilla, the daughter of St. Paula.</note>; and **Nicephorus** mentions her in book 14, chapter 52. One should also see the *Synaxarium* <note>A collection of hagiographies or lives of the saints.</note> regarding her in the Greek *Menologium* <note>A liturgical book of the Orthodox Church containing the lives of saints.</note> for the 31st day of December.

**"Marcellinus, of the former consuls"** <note>original Greek: "Μαρκελλίνος τοῦ ἀπὸ ὑπάτων"</note> ] Perhaps this is the **Marcellinus** whom the *Fasti* <note>The Fasti were chronological lists of Roman magistrates, particularly the consuls for whom years were named.</note> establish as consul alongside Probinus, during the reign of the Emperors Constantine, Constantius, and Constans, in the year of Christ 341.

**"I do not well remember the name"** <note>original Greek: "οὐ καλῶς τὸ ὄνομα ὑπόμνημαι"</note> ] One should write, "of whom I do not well remember the name" <note>original Greek: "οὗ οὐ καλῶς τὸ ὄνομα ὑπόμνημαι"</note>.

**"And Adelphius"** <note>original Greek: "καὶ Ἀδέλφιον"</note> ] Regarding Adelphius, see **John Moschus**, *The Spiritual Meadow* <note>original Latin title: "Limonarium"</note>, chapter 128, and **Nicephorus**, book 13, chapter 26.

**"They receive a caracallion"** <note>original Greek: "καρακάλλιον λαμβάνουσι"</note> ] Regarding what a <term>caracallion</term> <note>A hooded cloak or garment, famously associated with the Emperor Caracalla, but here likely referring to a monastic habit.</note> is, see our *Glossary*.

**"The consular of Palestine"** <note>original Greek: "ὁ ὑπατικὸς τῆς Παλαιστίνης"</note> ] There were five <term>consulars</term> <note>High-ranking provincial governors who had previously held the rank of consul.</note> throughout the East: those of Palestine, Phoenicia, Syria, Cilicia, and Cyprus. Regarding these, see generally what we have said in our *Glossary* under the word **Consularius**.

<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s scholarly commentary on the "Lausiac History" by Palladius. Having previously discussed Melania and Philoromus, Meursius now turns to the figures of Sabiniana, Asella, Julian, Adolius, and Innocent, providing historical context and correcting textual corruptions in the Greek manuscript.</meta>

# NOTES ON PALLADIUS

### Concerning Sabiniana.

**"the divine John, Bishop of Constantinople"** <note>original Greek: θεία Ἰωάννη τοῦ ἐπισκόπου Κωνσταντινουπόλεως</note>] He means **John Chrysostom** <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407 AD) was a renowned Early Church Father and Archbishop of Constantinople, famous for his powerful preaching. Sabiniana was a deaconess and one of his close supporters.</note>.

### Concerning Asella.

**"the beautiful Asella"** <note>original Greek: τὴν καλὴν Ἀσέλλαν</note>] This is that woman whose praises **Jerome** <note>Saint Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), the translator of the Vulgate Bible.</note> treats in his *Letter to Marcella*; and there exists a letter from the same Jerome to her, numbered Letter 99. Again, in the *Prologue to the Life of St. Hilarion*:
> *In your holy prayers remember me, Asella, the honor and dignity of the consecrated virgins.* <note>The original Latin uses the term "nonna," a title of respect for a monastic woman or elderly virgin.</note>

### Concerning Julian.

**"I have heard about a certain Julian"** <note>original Greek: ἀκήκοα περὶ Ἰουλιανοῦ τινὸς</note>] See regarding him **Sozomen** <note>A 5th-century Christian historian.</note>, book 3, chapter 14, and **Nicephorus** <note>Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopoulos, a 14th-century Byzantine church historian.</note>, book 2, chapter 15. **Ephraim the Syrian** also described his life, as those very authors I have cited testify. <note>This refers to Julian Saba, a famous 4th-century hermit of the Mesopotamian desert.</note>

### Concerning Adolius.

**"in which the second antiphon"** <note>original Greek: ἐν ᾗ δέυτερον ἀντίφωνον</note>] Regarding **antiphons** <note>An antiphon is a short sentence or verse sung or recited responsively, typically during the liturgy.</note>, see our *Glossary* under that entry.

### Concerning Innocent.

**"serving as a domestic"** <note>original Greek: δομέστικον στρατευόμενον</note>] Regarding this term, see what is in the *Glossary*. <note>A "domesticus" was often a member of the imperial guard or a high-ranking staff officer in the later Roman Empire.</note>

**"seized by the spirit and by paralysis"** <note>original Greek: ὑπὸ τοῦ πν͞ματος ληφθεὶς, καὶ παραίσεως</note>] Read: "seized by the spirit and by paralysis" <note>original Greek: ὑπὸ τοῦ πνεύματος ληφθεὶς, καὶ παραλύσεως. Meursius is correcting a scribal error where 'paraiseos' was written instead of 'paralyseos.'</note>.

**"then the monk wept"** <note>original Greek: δακρύσας οὖν ὁ καλόγηρος</note>] We explain this term in our *Glossary*. <note>The word "kalogeros" literally means "good old man" and became a standard Greek term for a monk.</note>

**"and having healed the paralysis"** <note>original Greek: ϗ τὴν πάρασιν ἰασάμενος</note>] One should write "and [having healed] the paralysis" <note>original Greek: καὶ τὴν παράλυσιν. Again, Meursius corrects the misspelled 'parasin' to 'paralysin.'</note>.

**"for such was the paralysis"** <note>original Greek: τοιαύτη γὰρ ἦν ἡ πάρασις</note>] Again, write "for such was the paralysis" <note>original Greek: τοιαύτη δέ ἦν ἡ παράλυσις</note>.

<sig>Bb</sig>
<meta>catchword: Concerning</meta>

<summary>Meursius provides philological notes and historical citations for various figures in Palladius's history, focusing on identifying individuals like John Chrysostom and Asella and correcting recurring spelling errors related to the word 'paralysis' in the Greek text.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s scholarly commentary on the "Lausiac History" by Palladius. Having previously discussed John Chrysostom and Julian Saba, Meursius now moves into notes regarding Eustochia, Theodora, and the monastic lineage of Hesychas, focusing on cross-referencing these figures with the works of Saint Jerome and various ecclesiastical historians.</meta>

# JOHN MEURSIUS

->Concerning Eustochia.<-

**"whose daughter she is, and now practices asceticism, named Eustochia"** <note>original Greek: ἧς θυγάτηρ ἐστὶ, καὶ νῦν ἀσκεῖται, Εὐστοχία ὀνόματι</note>] In the place cited by **Jerome**, she is called *Eustochium*, and is called by another name, *Julia*, because her father traced his lineage from Julius Caesar. His words are these:
*She was joined to a husband, Toxotius, who draws his very high blood from Aeneas and the Julians. Whence also his daughter, the Christian virgin, is called Eustochium Julia.* <note>original Latin: Iuncta est viro Toxotio, qui AEneæ & Iuliorum altissimum sanguinem trahit. Vnde etiam filia eius, Christia virgo, Eustochium Iulia nuncupatur.</note>

->Concerning Theodora.<-

**"To the blessed Theodora"** <note>original Greek: Ἐπὶ τὴν μακαρίαν Θεοδώραν</note>] **Nicephorus** <note>Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopoulos, a 14th-century Byzantine church historian.</note> tells of a certain Theodora in Book 17, Chapter 5. In the Greek **Menology** <note>A collection of lives of the saints arranged by their feast days in the Orthodox tradition.</note> for the day of September 11, a **synaxarion** <note>A brief account of a saint's life or a liturgical lesson.</note> is read of a certain Theodora who lived in the times of the Emperor Zeno.

->Concerning Hosia.<-

**"in the monastery of Hesychas"** <note>original Greek: ἐν τῷ μοναστηρίῳ τοῦ Ἡσυχᾶ</note>] This Hesychas was a disciple of **Hilarion**. **Sozomen** <note>A 5th-century historian of the Christian Church.</note> mentions him and the monastery itself in Book 3, Chapter 14, and Book 6, Chapter 31. Likewise, Nicephorus mentions him in Book 9, Chapter 15, and Book 11, Chapter 39. In the *Life of St. Hilarion* by **Jerome**, he is called *Hesychius*.

->Concerning Adolia.<-

**"they shall live in the zeal of God"** <note>original Greek: ζήσουσι ζήλῳ Θεοῦ</note>] Write/Read: *they burn with zeal for God* <note>original Greek: ζέουσι ζήλῳ Θεοῦ. Meursius is suggesting a textual correction, as the Greek words for 'live' (zesousi) and 'boil/burn' (zeousi) are very similar in manuscript form.</note>.

->Concerning Basinilla.<-

**"and Basinilla"** <note>original Greek: καὶ Βασινίλλαν</note>] Perhaps it should be written "and Basilissa." Or, "and Basilla." Mention of both is found in the **Martyrology** <note>An official list of martyrs and saints of the Church.</note>. But I would not wish to change anything rashly.

->Concerning Photine.<-

**"and Photine"** <note>original Greek: καὶ Φωτεινὴν</note>] Regarding Photine, there is an entry in the Martyrology.

<meta>catchword: Concerning</meta>

**Vocabulary:** Eustochium, Theodora, Hesychas, Nicephorus, Sozomen, Jerome, Martyrology, Menology.

# NOTES ON PALLADIUS

...healed incurable diseases by prayer alone. **Sozomen**, Book 6, Chapter 23: <note>original Greek: μωσῆς ἤ πραότητι κ᾿ ἀγάπη ὑπερφυῶς εὐδοκιμηκέναι παραδέδοται, κ᾿ ἰάσεσι παθῶν εὐχῇ κατορθωμέναις</note> "Moses <note>Referring to Moses the Ethiopian, also known as Moses the Black, a Desert Father.</note> is handed down to us as having excelled extraordinarily in gentleness and love, and in the healing of suffering accomplished through prayer."

->Concerning Ephraim.<-

**"When a great famine gripped the city of the Edessenes"** <note>original Greek: Λιμοῦ καταλαβόντ@ μεγάλ@ τὴν Αἰδεσινῶν πόλιν</note>] See **Sozomen**, Book 3, Chapter 15, and **Nicephorus**, Book 9, Chapter 16. <note>Ephraim the Syrian (c. 306–373 AD) was a prolific hymnodist and theologian; the famine mentioned refers to his humanitarian efforts in Edessa shortly before his death.</note>

**"he left behind [many] compositions"** <note>original Greek: καταλέλοιπεν ἤ κ᾿ συντάγματ@</note>] Concerning his writings, one should consult **Sozomen** and **Nicephorus** in the places cited; **Jerome** in his *Catalogue of Ecclesiastical Writers*; and the **Menology** <note>A liturgical book of the Orthodox Church containing lives of the saints.</note> for the 28th day of January.

->Concerning Paula.<-

**"among whom was also Paula the Roman"** <note>original Greek: ἐν αἷς κ᾿ Παύλῃ τῇ Ῥωμαίῃ</note>] He refers to that Paula <note>Saint Paula (347–404 AD), a wealthy Roman widow and close associate of St. Jerome.</note> about whom he spoke above in the *Life of Posidonius*.

**"to the mother"** <note>original Greek: τῇ μητρὶ</note>] He adds this to distinguish her from her daughter, who was not actually called Paula, but Paulina. See Jerome in his *Epitaph* for her.

**"to the wife of Toxotius"** <note>original Greek: Τοξοτίῳ γυναικὶ</note>] The husband of this Paula was Toxotius. Jerome also testifies to this in the place cited: *“Born therefore of such a lineage, she was joined to the man Toxotius, who draws the most noble blood from Aeneas and the Julians. And she had a son, the youngest of five children, sharing his father’s name; as Jerome reports in that same place.”* <note>original Latin: Tali igitur stirpe generata, juncta est viro Toxotio, qui AEnea & Iuliorum altissimum sanguinem trahit. Et filium habuit, quinque liberorum minimum, patri cognominem: ut ibidem tradit Hieronymus.</note> The most learned **Hervetus** <note>Gentian Hervet (1499–1584), a French theologian and translator known for his Latin versions of the Church Fathers.</note> erred here, interpreting it as if Paula were the mother of Toxotius and the wife of some unknown man.

**"to whom a certain Jerome from Dalmatia became an obstacle"** <note>original Greek: ἧς ἐμπόδιον γέγονεν Ἰερώνυμ@ τις ἀπὸ Δαλματίας</note>] Our author Palladius was clearly quite unfair to the Blessed Jerome, as is also apparent above in the *Life of Posidonius*. <note>Palladius was a partisan of John Chrysostom and a follower of Origenist teachings, which led to a historical friction with Saint Jerome, who fiercely opposed Origenism.</note>

<meta>catchword: Concerning</meta>

<summary>Johannes Meursius provides scholarly annotations on the biographies of Moses the Ethiopian, Ephraim the Syrian, and Saint Paula. He corrects genealogical errors regarding Paula’s family and notes the historical bias Palladius held against Saint Jerome.</summary>

<keywords>Moses the Ethiopian, Ephraim the Syrian, Saint Paula, Toxotius, Saint Jerome, Sozomen, Nicephorus Callistus, Gentian Hervet, Edessa, Roman genealogy, Origenist controversy</keywords>

# JOHANNES MEURSIUS

...bearers of the teraine <note>original: τεραινοφόροι</note> and bearers of the citron] <note>Meursius is likely correcting a corrupted manuscript reading here.</note> It should be written: and bearers of scirrhous tumors. <note>original Greek: σκιῤῥοφόροι; referring to medical conditions or perhaps those carrying offerings related to healings.</note>

->Concerning the virgin in Rome.<-

**"and the moisture gave him an answer"** <note>original Greek: δεδώκει δὲ αὐτῷ ἀπόκρισιν ἡ νότις</note>] It should be written: **"and the nun gave him an answer."** <note>original Greek: ἡ νυνίς; Meursius is correcting "notis" (moisture/south wind) to "nynis" (a rare term for an old woman or nun). See **our Glossary** under this word.</note>

**"and you, why do you delay?"** <note>original Greek: καὶ σύ τί μέλλεις</note>] It should be written: **"and you, why will you delay?"** <note>original Greek: καὶ σύ τί μέλλῃ</note> And soon after again: **"for a dead man you shall never delay."**

**"buried in the desert itself"** <note>original Greek: ἐν αὐτῇ τῇ ἐρήμῳ ταφείς</note>] It should be written: **"buried in Rome itself."** For these events occurred in Rome, as is clear from what precedes.

->Concerning Pior.<-

**"One Pior by name"** <note>original Greek: Πίωρ τίς ὀνόματι; Pior was a 4th-century Egyptian hermit and disciple of Anthony the Great known for his extreme asceticism.</note>] One should consult **Sozomen, Book 6, Chapter 28** and **Nicephorus, Book 11, Chapter 37.** **Socrates [Scholasticus], Book 4, Chapter 22** also mentions him, as does **John Geometra** in his *Epigrams on the Paradise*, Epigram 41, Book 66.

**"at Phliasa"** <note>original Greek: ἐπὶ τῆς Φλιάσας</note>] It should be written: **"at Phliasis."** <note>original Greek: Φλιάσεως</note>

**"To see the fire of the saint brings the miracle of his endurance"** <note>original Greek: Ἰδεῖν δὲ τοῦ ἁγίου πῦρ φέρει τὸ ὑπομονῆς τὸ θαῦμα</note>] **John Geometra** also praises his patience in *Epigrams on the Paradise*, Epigram 41:

> When the King had sent a speaker, if he gave the city
> See the things that approach to honor, and the water.
> "Let it be so for you too," said Pior, "you shall destroy the passions,
> Until you blame these things, you will draw out the difficult part."

->Concerning Moses the Libyan.<-

**"This man was deemed worthy of the gift of healings"** <note>original Greek: οὗτος κατηξιώθη χαρίσματος ἰαμάτων; Moses the Libyan is better known as Moses the Black, a former bandit who became a Desert Father.</note>] **Nicephorus, Book 11, Chapter 36:** "We have received word that Moses attained great glory through his gentleness and charity; he also [healed] very many sicknesses, and those [which were] incur- <note>original Latin: invic-; the text breaks off here at the catchword for 'invictas' or 'invincibiles' meaning incurable or unconquerable diseases.</note>

He cross-references these accounts with other Byzantine historians and poets to ensure accuracy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s philological commentary on Palladius’s Historia Lausiaca. ### NOTES ON PALLADIUS

of the City, there exists a Decree <note>original Latin: Rescriptum; a formal imperial response to a legal query.</note> of the Emperors Valentinian, Theodosius, and Arcadius, in the Code *Concerning the privileges of the associations of the City of Rome*.

**"established in his mind"** <note>original Greek: σταθεὶς τὴν διάνοιαν</note>] It should be written: **"established in his [understanding]."** <note>Meursius is suggesting a slight grammatical or shorthand correction to the Greek text here.</note>

**"and to the servant of Christ, Poemen"** <note>original Greek: καὶ τῇ δούλῃ τοῦ Χριστοῦ Ποιμένι; Poemen is usually a male name, but the Greek text uses a feminine article here.</note>] In the manuscript of Gentianus Hervetus <note>A 16th-century French scholar and translator of patristic texts.</note> it seems to have been **"Poemania"** instead of **"Poemen."**

**"near the city of Nikiou"** <note>original Greek: πλησίον Νικίως τῆς πόλεως</note>] This is a city of Egypt, Nikiou, the capital <note>metropolis</note> of the Prosopite administrative district <note>original: præfecturæ; referring to an Egyptian "nome" or province.</note>. Ptolemy the Geographer in Book 4 writes: "from the east toward the great river is the Prosopite district, and the capital Nikiou." Stephanus [of Byzantium] calls the village Nikie, a village of Egypt. Aristagoras, in the second book of his *Egyptian Affairs*, calls an inhabitant a "Nikiote." Today this passage [in the manuscript] suffers from poor punctuation.

->Concerning Poseidonius.<-

**"beyond the Poemenium"** <note>original Greek: ἐπέκεινα τοῦ Ποιμενίου</note>] He understands this to mean the monastery of Abbot Poemen, whom John Moschus mentions in the *Spiritual Meadow* <note>original: Limonarium</note>, Chapter 167.

**"For a certain Jerome, a priest"** <note>original Greek: Ἱερώνυμος γάρ τις πρεσβύτερος</note>] He is speaking of the Blessed Jerome <note>Saint Jerome, the translator of the Vulgate Bible.</note>.

**"the noblewoman Paula"** <note>original Greek: ἡ μὲν ἐλευθέρα Παῦλα</note>] This is that same woman whose funeral oration <note>Epitaph</note> Blessed Jerome writes in a letter to her daughter Eustochium.

**"and Oxyperentius"** <note>original Greek: καὶ Ὀξυπερέντιον</note>] It should be written: **"and Exuperantius"** <note>original Greek: καὶ Ἐξυπεράντιον</note>. For this is that very man to whom a letter of Blessed Jerome exists.

->Concerning Serapion.<-

**"One Serapion by name"** <note>original Greek: Σεραπίων τις ὀνόματι</note>] Regarding him, one should consult **Sozomen, Book 6, Chapter 27**; **Rufinus, *On the Lives of the Fathers*, Chapter 18**; and **Nicephorus, Book 11, Chapter 34**. Cassian also mentions him in his *Conferences* <note>original: Collat.</note>, Book 11, Chapter 10 and Book 5, Chapter 1.

<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s commentary on the lives of the Egyptian desert fathers. The previous page discussed the legal status of Rome and topographical corrections near Nikiou. # JOHANNES MEURSIUS

*Book IV, Chapter XXII. Let us first place Abbot John, who resided near Lycon, which is a town of the Thebaid <note>A region in Upper Egypt named after its capital, Thebes.</note>, and who, having been elevated to the grace of prophecy on account of the virtue of obedience, became so renowned throughout the whole world that he was deservedly made illustrious even to the kings of this earth. For while he was staying in the furthest reaches of the Thebaid, as we have said, the Emperor Theodosius did not presume to proceed to wars against powerful tyrants before he was encouraged by John's oracles and responses. Trusting in these as if they had been brought down to him from heaven, he carried back trophies from desperate wars and enemies.*

One should also see Nicephorus Callistus, Book XII, Chapter XXXIX. Cedrenus <note>An 11th-century Byzantine historian.</note> is the authority that he used his staff in place of a spear, and a monk's cowl instead of a helmet. <note>original Greek: ἐξιών γοῦν (ὁ Θεοδόσιος) κατὰ τοῦ τυράννου Εὐγενίου, οὐκ ἰδίᾳ ἰσχύϊ ἐθάρρησεν· Ἰωάννου δὲ πυνθανόμενος Αἰγυπτίου μοναχοῦ μεγάλου τὴν ῥάβδον ἀντὶ δόρατος, τὴν ἐπωμίδα δὲ ἀντὶ κράνους ὁπλισάμενος, κατὰ κράτος τὸν ἀλιτήριον χειροῦται ζῶντα, καὶ τοὺς σὺν αὐτῷ ἀνεῖλε.</note> "Therefore, when [Theodosius] went out against the tyrant Eugenius, he did not trust in his own strength; but having learned of John, the great Egyptian monk, and arming himself with the John's staff instead of a spear, and the monk's cloak <note>original: epomida; literally a shoulder-strap or cloak.</note> instead of a helmet, he forcefully subdued the villain while he was still alive and destroyed those with him." Theodore Metochites records the same things in *Roman History*, Book II, but he names him Senuphius, not John. <note>original Greek: ἔθνη ποτὲ ἄπειρα κατὰ τοῦ μεγάλου ἐγειρομένου Θεοδοσίου· τί τὸ ἐπὶ τούτοις γράφει τηνικαῦτα ὁ βασιλεὺς τῷ τῆς Ἀλεξανδρείας προέδρῳ, ἵνα τὸν κατ' Αἴγυπτον μέγαν Σενούφιον ποιήσῃ πρὸς συμμαχίαν τοῦ βασιλέως ἐλθεῖν. ὁ δὲ οὐ πείθεται. στέλλει δὲ τῷ βασιλεῖ ῥάβδον, καὶ μικρὸν αὐτοῦ τριβώνιον· οἷς δὴ καὶ χρησάμενος ὁ βασιλεὺς ἀντὶ κράνους καὶ δόρατος κατὰ τῶν πολεμίων ὁρμᾷ καὶ διὰ χειρῶν αὐτοὺς κατατροποῦται.</note> "Once, when countless nations rose up against the great Theodosius, the emperor then wrote to the presiding bishop of Alexandria so that he might make Senuphius the Great, who lived in Egypt, come as an ally to the emperor. But Senuphius was not persuaded to come. Instead, he sent the emperor a staff and his small, worn cloak <note>original: tribonion; a simple, inexpensive cloak worn by philosophers and monks.</note>; using these indeed instead of a helmet and spear, the emperor rushed against the enemies and overthrew them with his own hands." The governor of the region entered, named Alypius. This is that same Alypius, to whom, as the then Prefect

<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s scholarly commentary on the lives of the Egyptian desert fathers, specifically annotating the work of Palladius. ->NOTES ON PALLADIUS<-

...Egypt. "Porphyrites <note>Porphyrites refers to the 'Purple Mountain' (Mons Porphyrites) in the Eastern Desert of Egypt, famous in antiquity as the only source of imperial purple porphyry stone.</note>, a city of Arabia, near Egypt." According to Ptolemy in Book IV: "Porphyrites is a mountain of the Arabian-Egyptians."

"Bless me, father [abba]" <note>original Greek: εὐλόγησόν με ἀββᾶ</note>] One should write, "Bless me, mother [amma]" <note>original: εὐ. με ἀμμᾶ</note>. For **amma** <note>original: ἀμμᾶς</note> was used for a woman, just as **abba** <note>original: ἀββᾶς</note> was for a man. Palladius himself says a little later: "For she is both your mother and mine; for so they call spiritual mothers" <note>original: αὕτη γὰρ καὶ ὑμῶν καὶ ἐμοῦ ἀμμᾶς ἐστιν. οὕτω γὰρ καλοῦσι τὰς πνευματικὰς μητέρας.</note>.

### On John of Lycopolis

"There was a certain man named John in the city of Lyco" <note>original Greek: γέγονέν τις Ἰωάννης ὀνόματι ἐν Λυκῷ τῇ πόλει. Lyco, or Lycopolis, is the modern Egyptian city of Asyut.</note>] In addition to others, Eusebius also mentions him in the later book of his *Chronicles*, for the year 2410 <note>This date follows an ancient calendar system; the events concern the late 4th century AD.</note>. Also Sidonius in *Poem XVI*, and Cassian in *Conferences*, Book I, chapter 21. For more on him, one should consult Rufinus, *Lives of the Fathers*, chapter 1.

"He withdrew alone to the Mountain of the Wolves" <note>original Greek: ἀνεχώρησεν μόνος εἰς τὸ ὄρος τῶν Λυκῶν</note>] This mountain was six miles away from Lyco. John Moschus teaches this in *The Spiritual Meadow* <note>original Latin: Limonarium</note>, chapter 161: "Lyco is a city of the Thebaid <note>A region in Upper Egypt.</note>. A certain mountain is six miles distant from it, and monks live upon it, some in caves and others in cells."

"Among which he also sent various prophecies to the blessed Emperor Theodosius" <note>original Greek: ἐν οἷς καὶ τῷ μακαρίῳ Θεοδοσίῳ τῷ βασιλεῖ διαφόρους ἀπέστειλεν προῤῥήσεις</note>] This John predicted various things to the Emperor Theodosius. Theodoret in *Ecclesiastical History*, Book V, chapter 24: "At that time there was a certain John in Egypt, who embraced the ascetic life. Having partaken of spiritual grace, he predicted many future things to those who inquired. To him the Christ-loving Emperor sent, desiring to learn if he should go to war against the tyrants" <note>original Greek: κατ’ ἐκεῖνον δὲ τὸν χρόνον ἦν τις Ἰωάννης... εἰ πολεμητέον τοῖς τυράννοις μαθεῖν ἐφιέμενος.</note>. And what follows, Cassian...

<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s scholarly commentary on the lives of the Egyptian desert fathers, specifically annotating the work of Palladius. # THE WORKS OF JOHANNES MEURSIUS

...took it and led it away with him. And shortly after: "That camel-driver, who had stolen the donkey, came bringing wheat." <note>original Latin: "Camelarius ille, qui asinum abstulerat, veniebat ferens triticum."</note> Victor of Vita, in *On the Vandal Persecution*, Book 1: "Once all the slaves and children had been sold off, his wife was given in marriage in the presence of the camel-driver himself." <note>original Latin: "Distratis omnibus mancipijs, ac filijs, uxor ejus ipso præsente camelario in conjugium traderetur."</note> This also occurs in the *Instructions* of Dorotheus <note>Dorotheus of Gaza, a 6th-century monk and abbot</note>, Book 11.

"to the extremities of the sick" <note>original Greek: τοῖς δ' ἄκροις τοῖς νοσοῦσιν</note>] One should write: "the extremities for those who are sick" <note>original: τὰ δ' ἄκρη τοῖς ν.</note>.

"to others the so-called *malakia*" <note>original Greek: ἄλλοις τὰ λεγόμενα μαλάκια</note>] Hesychius <note>An Alexandrian grammarian and lexicographer</note> defines *malakion* as a woman’s ornament. Julius Pollux, in Book 5, chapter 16, includes "braided ornaments" <note>original Greek: πλόκια</note>, "jewelry" <note>σίμια</note>, and "finery" <note>μαλάκια</note> among the names for women’s finery.

"he was seeking a marsh" <note>original Greek: ἐζήτει ἔλον</note>] One should write: "he was seeking work" <note>original: ἐζήτει ἔργον</note>.

->Concerning Pityrum<-

"to the holy Pityrum" <note>original Greek: τῷ ἁγίῳ Πιτηρὺμ</note>] Socrates <note>Socrates Scholasticus, a 5th-century church historian</note> also mentions him in Book 4, chapter 23. And in the *Suda* <note>A massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia</note> it is read: "Piteroum, a proper name." In Rufinus’s *Lives of the Fathers*, chapter 13, he is called Pithyrion.

"in the Porphyrite" <note>original Greek: ἐν τῷ Πορφυρίτῃ; referring to the "Purple Mountain" in the Eastern Desert of Egypt</note>] John Moschus also mentions the Porphyrite in *The Spiritual Meadow*, chapter 124: *"Twenty-two years ago I ascended to the Porphyrite, wishing to stay there."* <note>original Latin: "Ante annos viginti duos ascendi in Porphyriten, volens illic morari."</note> The text of Cassian, Book 10, chapter 24, should be corrected: *"Paul, the most venerable of the fathers, when he was staying in that more desolate desert which is called Porphyrite, etc."* <note>original Latin: "Paulus probatissimus patrum, cum in eremo vastiore consistens, qua Porphyrite nuncupatur, &c."</note> Today it is published as: "which is called Porphyrio." It occurs again shortly hereafter in our Palladius, in the section on Posidonius. Eusebius provides the origin of the name in his *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 8, chapter 18: "He previously held what was called in the Thebaid—fittingly named after the place where the mine of porphyry stone grows—a very great multitude of those who confessed their godliness." <note>original Greek: εἶχε μὲν πρὸ τούτου τὸ καλούμενον ἐν Θηβαΐδι Φερωνύμως οὗ φύεται πορφυρίτου λίθου μέταλλον, πλείστην ὅσην πληθὺν τῶν τῆς θεοσεβείας ὁμολογητῶν.</note> Stephanus <note>Stephanus of Byzantium, a 6th-century grammarian</note> identifies it as a city of Arabia, near Egypt.

<summary>Johannes Meursius provides philological corrections to the Greek and Latin texts of Palladius and other early Christian writers, specifically clarifying terms for women's jewelry and identifying the location of the Porphyry Mountain in Egypt.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Meursius, Palladius, Porphyrites, Pityrum, Desert Fathers, philology, textual criticism, Egypt, monasticism, Victor of Vita, Eusebius</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s scholarly commentary on the Egyptian monks mentioned in the works of Palladius. It moves from the monk Pityrum to the famous community of the Tabennesians, providing philological corrections to their names, descriptions of their clothing, and monastic titles.</meta>

<page-num>189</page-num>
<header>NOTES ON PALLADIUS.</header>

->Concerning the Tabennesians.<-

"Tabennesis is an island in the Thebaid so called" <note>original Greek: Ταβεννησίς ἐστι νῆσος ἐν τῇ Θηβαΐδι οὕτω καλουμένη</note> ] One should write: "It is a certain Tabenna" <note>original Latin: Scrib. Ταβέννη τίς ἐστι</note>. For this place was called Tabenna, not Tabennesis, and the monks living there were known as Tabennesians. Sozomen <note>A 5th-century historian of the Christian Church</note>, Book 3, chapter 14: "He stayed on the island of Tabenna in the Thebaid, from which they are still called Tabennesians to this day." <note>original Greek: διέτριβε δὲ ἐν Ταβέννη νήσῳ τῆς Θηβαΐδος, ὅθεν Ταβεννησιῶται εἰσέτι νῦν ὀνομάζονται.</note> Nicephorus <note>Nicephorus Callistus, a 14th-century Byzantine historian</note>, Book 9, chapter 14: "In the Thebaid, however, Philosophy <note>In this context, "Philosophy" refers to the ascetic or monastic life</note> had its workshop on a certain island called Tabenna: from which they are called Tabennesians even to this point."

"in which Pachomius became a monk" <note>original Greek: ἐν ᾧ Παχώμιος γέγονεν μοναχὸς</note> ] See Sozomen and Nicephorus in the passages just cited; and the *Menologium* <note>A collection of lives of the saints arranged by month</note> for the 15th day of May. Jerome also mentions him in his *Epitaph for Marcella*.

"belted linen tunics" <note>original Greek: λεβιτῶνας λινὲς ἐζωσμένους; "levitons" were sleeveless linen tunics worn by Egyptian monks</note> ] We have spoken of this word in our *Glossary*.

"and let them enter only with hoods" <note>original Greek: καὶ μετὰ κουκουλλίων μόνα εἰσίτωσαν</note> ] On this garment of the monks, see Cassian’s *On the Institutes of Monks*, Book 1, chapter 4, and our *Glossary*.

"and twelve at the lamp-lighting" <note>original Greek: καὶ ἐν τῷ λυχνικῷ δώδεκα</note> ] For what the *lychnikon* <note>The service of the lighting of the lamps, roughly equivalent to Vespers</note> is, see our *Glossary*.

"now there is a second-in-command in that monastery" <note>original Greek: νυνὶ δευτεράριον ἐστὶν ἐν τῷ μοναστηρίῳ ἐκείνῳ</note> ] This "second-in-command" <note>Greek: δευτεράριος; a deputy abbot or administrator</note> is mentioned by Gregory in *On the Lives of the Saints*, Book 1: "In the times of King Totila, there was a second-in-command in the monastery of Fuodesinus." <note>original Latin: ἐν τοῖς χρόνοις Τωτίλα τοῦ ῥηγὸς ἦν δευτεράριος ἐν τῇ μονῇ τοῦ Φουωδεσίνου.</note>

"Twelve camel-drivers" <note>original Greek: Καμηλαρίους δώδεκα</note> ] In the Middle Ages, the Greeks called someone a *kamēlarios* whom the ancients called a *kamēlités*. John Moschus also uses it in the *Limonarion* <note>The "Spiritual Meadow," a collection of tales about desert monks</note>, chapter 107: "Behold, a camel-driver coming from Arabia [found a lost donkey and] took it—"

<sig>Aa</sig>
<meta>catchword: took</meta>

<summary>This page contains Johannes Meursius's philological notes on the Tabennesian monks of Egypt, correcting the name of their location and explaining terms for their clothing and monastic roles, such as the deputy abbot and camel-drivers.</summary>

It provides textual corrections and cross-references to other early ecclesiastical historians like Sozomen, Socrates Scholasticus, and Nicephorus Callistus.</meta>

<page-num>185</page-num>
<header>NOTES ON PALLADIUS.</header>
<meta>Catchword: Heron</meta>

Book 8, chapter 11. John Scholasticus <note>Also known as John Climacus, author of *The Ladder of Divine Ascent*</note> likewise mentions him in chapter 24.

"It is not my concern" <note>original Greek: οὐ μέλλεί μοι</note> ] Read: "It is not my concern" <note>original Greek: οὐ μέλει μοι; Meursius is correcting a spelling error in the manuscript</note>.

"Enter into a monastery of many brothers" <note>original Greek: εἴσελθε εἰς κοινόβιον πλειόνων ἀδελφῶν</note> ] See our *Glossary* under the entry for *Koinobion* <term>a communal monastery where monks lived and worked together</term>.

"Father, do you wish for us to eat?" <note>original Greek: Παπα θέλεις φάγωμεν</note> ] See the *Glossary* regarding this word <note>The term "Papa" was a common honorific for elder monks or priests in Egypt</note>.

"He places biscuits upon the table" <note>original Greek: ἐπιτίθησι τῇ τραπέζῃ παξαμάτας</note> ] Concerning this expression, our *Glossary* should be consulted <note>Paxamatas refers to twice-baked, hard bread or biscuits that were a staple of the ascetic diet</note>.

->Concerning Pachon.<-

"A certain Pachon by name" <note>original Greek: Παχών τις ὀνόματι</note> ] Regarding him, see Sozomen <note>A 5th-century historian of the Christian Church</note>, Book 6, chapter 28, and Nicephorus <note>Nicephorus Callistus, a 14th-century Byzantine historian</note>, Book 11, chapter 36.

"to my teacher Evagrius" <note>original Greek: τῷ διδασκάλῳ μου Εὐαγρίῳ</note> ] In the manuscript of Hervetus <note>Gentian Hervet, a French scholar and translator</note>, it seems to have been "Eulogius" instead of "Evagrius." However, the reading in the Palatine manuscript is correct: for Evagrius was the teacher of our Palladius. See Socrates <note>Socrates Scholasticus, a 5th-century historian</note>, Book 4, chapter 23, and our author again below in the section on Valens.

->Concerning Stephen.<-

"A certain Stephen, a Libyan by birth" <note>original Greek: Στέφανός τις Λίβυς τῷ γένει</note> ] See Sozomen, Book 6, chapter 28, and Nicephorus, Book 11, chapter 36. John Scholasticus (Climacus) also mentions a certain Stephen in chapter 7.

->Concerning Valens.<-

"he thinks highly of himself in his pride" <note>original Greek: τῷ τύφῳ μέγα φρονεῖ</note> ] Read: "he thinks highly of himself in his pride" <note>original Greek abbreviated: τ. τ. μέγα Φ.</note>.

->Concerning Heron.<-

"An Alexandrian by birth" <note>original Greek: Ἀλεξανδρεὺς τῷ γένει</note> ] Nicephorus, Book 11, chapter 29, tells of another Heron who originated from Egyptian Thebes. Cassian <note>John Cassian, an ascetic writer who translated Eastern monastic traditions for the West</note> also mentions him in *Conferences* 2, chapter 5.

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s philological commentary on the "Lausiac History" of Palladius. Having previously discussed monks like Pachon and Heron, Meursius is now providing scholarly citations from legal and historical sources to contextualize the practice of swearing oaths on the Gospels, before moving on to the life of Paul the Simple.</meta>

<page-num>184</page-num>
<header>JOHANNES MEURSIUS</header>

"...let them judge at the reading of the sentence with the holy Gospels set before them, while the advocates of each party swear upon the holy Gospels at the very beginning of the proceedings, etc." <note>original Greek: τῆς ἀποφάσεως τὴν ἀνάγνωσιν... This refers to the Byzantine legal custom of placing the Gospels in the center of a courtroom to ensure divine witness to the truth.</note> Again, in Book 29: "...so that they, with the holy Gospels set before them, may state in the records of the proceedings whether in truth her father has died." **Menander in the *Excerpts on Embassies*** <note>Menander Protector was a 6th-century Byzantine historian who wrote about diplomacy</note>: "Immediately, therefore, the one presiding over the high priesthood of the city of Singidunum <note>Modern-day Belgrade</note> handed over the divine books to him through the intermediaries who carried the messages; and he, having most craftily concealed his intent, rose from his seat, and with much feigned fear and reverence, as if receiving them and most eagerly doing obeisance, said: 'I swear by the words of God spoken in this parchment that nothing said by him shall be falsified.'" **Harmenopulus, Book 1, Title 7** <note>Konstantinos Harmenopoulos was a 14th-century jurist from Thessaloniki whose legal handbook, the *Hexabiblos*, was widely used</note>: "He who, by a judicial vote or at the request of an adversary, takes an oath in church by touching the immaculate Gospels, but is convicted of perjury in this matter, let his tongue be cut out." **John Kantakouzenos, *History*, Book 1, Chapter 15** <note>Kantakouzenos was a 14th-century Byzantine Emperor and historian</note>: ***"By the holy Gospels and the venerable images of the saints, you command me to swear faithfully."*** **Again, Chapter 23:** ***"With the holy Gospels brought forward, and with the signature of his hand in red ink, faith was established by the sacrament of the oath."*** <note>original Latin: *miniata manus suae subscriptione*; Byzantine Emperors traditionally signed documents in cinnabar/red ink.</note> **One may observe this in many other authors of the middle and later ages.**

->Concerning Paul the Simple.<-
<note>Paul the Simple was a contemporary of Anthony the Great who became a monk in his old age after discovering his wife's adultery.</note>

"The holy servant of Christ, Hierax" ] **Nicephorus mentions a certain Hierax in Book 14, Chapter 14.** <note>Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopoulos was a 14th-century church historian.</note>
"A certain Paul, a rustic farmer" ] **Regarding this man, see Sozomen's *History*, Book 1, Chapter 13, and Nicephorus...** <note>Sozomen was a 5th-century historian; "rustic farmer" (Greek: ἄγροικος Γεωργός) highlights Paul's humble, uneducated background before entering the monastery.</note>

<meta>catchword: lib.</meta>

<summary>This page provides a scholarly compilation of Byzantine legal and historical citations regarding the practice of swearing oaths on the Gospels. It concludes by transitioning into a commentary on the life of the monk Paul the Simple.</summary>
<keywords>Oaths, Gospels, Byzantine Law, Menander Protector, Harmenopulus, John Kantakouzenos, Paul the Simple, Hierax, Singidunum, red ink signature</keywords>

<meta>This page provides philological commentary on Palladius's "Lausiac History," specifically focusing on the companions of St. Anthony the Great and early Christian funerary and legal customs. It precedes the discussion of legal oaths found on page 184.</meta>

<page-num>183</page-num>

<header>->NOTES ON PALLADIUS.<-</header>

"*Macarius and Matoïus, who also buried the blessed Anthony when he fell asleep*" <note>original Greek: Μακάριος ϗ Ματώϊος, οἱ ϗ ἔθαψαν τὸν μακάριον Ἀντώνιον κοιμηθέντα</note> These are surely those two who, as Athanasius relates in his *Life of Anthony*, lived with him for fifteen years and served him on account of his old age. Indeed, the one called *Matoïus* by Palladius in this passage is called *Matoes* by John Geometra <note>A 10th-century Byzantine poet and monk</note> in *Epigram 99 of the Paradise*. In the manuscript of Hervetus <note>Gentian Hervet, a 16th-century translator</note>, it seems the name was *Amatas*.

"*A certain Eulogius, an Alexandrian*" <note>original Greek: Εὐλόγιός τις Ἀλεξανδρεὺς</note> Regarding him, see Sozomen <note>a 5th-century church historian</note>, Book 6, Chapter 27, and Nicephorus <note>Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopoulos, a 14th-century historian</note>, Book 11, Chapter 34.

"*carvers of bowls*" <note>original Greek: χάται γλύπων (chatai glypon)</note> This should be written as *skaphoglyphon* <note>original Greek: σκαπογλύπων; meaning those who carve bowls or perhaps ditch-diggers</note>. See our *Glossary* under the entry for *chates*.

"*clad in a leather tunic*" <note>original Greek: ἐμπεφιβλωμένον χλαμύδα δερματίνην</note> Regarding this word, see our *Glossary* under *Phiblen* <note>referring to a buckle or clasp</note>. Furthermore, he calls the *melote* <term>melote: a sheepskin or goatskin cloak worn by monks</term> a "leather tunic." This was the garment Anthony used to wear, which he had received from Athanasius; even though it was worn out by long use, he wished it to be returned to Athanasius as a legacy upon his death. Athanasius himself is a witness to this in his *Life of Anthony*.

"*and it happened that the fortieth-day rites for the blessed Eulogius were being performed*" <note>original Greek: ϗ συνέβη τοῦ μὲν μακαρίου Εὐλογίου τὰ τεσσαρακοστὰ ἐπιτελεῖσθαι</note> It was the custom for the fortieth day after death to be celebrated with memorial rites. See our *Glossary* under *Tessarakosta*.

"*the third-day rites*" <note>original Greek: τὰ τρίτα</note> The memory of the dead was also celebrated on the third day. See the *Glossary* under *Tritegona*.

"*and taking the Gospel as a pledge for those hearing, he placed it in the midst of the brothers and swore to them*" <note>original Greek: ϗ λαβὼν Εὐαγγέλιον εἰς πίστιν τ̃ ἀκουόντων τέθηκεν ἐν μέσῳ τ̃ ἀδελφῶν, ϗ διωμόσατο αὐτοῖς</note> We should observe the ancient custom of swearing oaths by placing the Gospels in front of the parties. This is found in the *Basilica* <note>a late 9th-century collection of Byzantine laws</note>, Book 7: "*and by the [Gospels] themselves...*"

<meta>catchword: the</meta>

<summary>This page contains scholarly notes on the burial of St. Anthony, the attire of monks, and the evolution of early Christian funerary and legal customs, such as memorial rites and swearing on the Gospels.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s philological commentary on the "Lausiac History" by Palladius. Following the discussion of St. Anthony’s burial and the monk Eulogius on page 183, this page provides specific textual corrections and citations for the chapters concerning Moses the Ethiopian, Paul of Pherme, and the geographic location of Anthony's mountain, Pispir.</meta>

<page-num>182</page-num>
<header>OF JOHANNES MEURSIUS</header>

->Concerning Moses the Ethiopian<-

"*A certain man so called, named Moses*" <note>original Greek: Μωϋσῆς οὕτω καλόμενός τις</note>] Nicephorus <note>Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopoulos, a 14th-century Byzantine church historian</note> relates this in Book 11, Chapter 36, and Sozomen <note>a 5th-century historian of the early church</note> in Book 6, Chapter 28. One should also consult his *Synaxarium* <note>a collection of hagiographies</note> in the *Menologium* <note>a liturgical book containing lives of the saints</note> for the 28th day of August.

"*the brotherhood*" <note>original Greek: τὸ κολλήγιον (to kollegion)</note>] We discuss this word in our *Glossary* <note>Meursius is referring to his own scholarly dictionary of late and medieval Greek</note>.

"*against his very companion*" <note>original Greek: ἐπ’ αὐτὸν τὸν συμπαικτὴν αὐτοῦ (ep' auton ton sympaiktēn autou)</note>] I am still investigating the exact meaning of the word *sympaiktēs* <term>sympaiktēs: literally a 'playmate' or 'companion in sport,' used here to denote a fellow monk or close associate</term>. Gentianus <note>Gentian Hervet, a 16th-century French humanist and translator</note> omitted this entire sentence. It should be written as *ton sympaiktēn autou*.

"*in the butcher's shop*" <note>original Greek: ἐν μακέλλῳ (en makellō)</note>] Regarding this word, as well as the following word *makelleion* <note>a meat market</note>, see our *Glossary*.

"*if he should come across pieces of meat*" <note>original Greek: κωλεὰς περιύχῃ (kōleas periuchē)</note>] It should be written as *k. peritychē* <note>Meursius is correcting a spelling error in the source manuscript: changing 'periuchē' to 'peritychē', meaning to happen upon or find by chance</note>.

->Concerning Paul of Pherme<-

"*There is a mountain in Egypt*" <note>original Greek: ὄρος ἐν τῇ Αἰγύπτῳ ἐστὶν</note>] One should see Sozomen, Book 6, Chapter 28, and Nicephorus, Book 11, Chapter 36.

"*which is called Pherme*" <note>original Greek: ὃ καλεῖται Φέρμη</note>] So say Sozomen and Nicephorus in the places cited.

"*among whom was a certain monk Paul*" <note>original Greek: ἐν οἷς Παῦλός τις μοναχὸς</note>] This is the same man whom Basil calls "the Egyptian" in *The Miracles of Saint Thecla*, Chapter 30.

->Concerning Eulogius<-

"*Cronius related to me*" <note>original Greek: Κρονίως μοι διηγήσατο</note>] This is the man who was called "Cronion" earlier in the text.

"*to the place called Pispir*" <note>original Greek: εἰς τὸ λεγόμενον Πίσπιρ</note>] This was the name of the mountain of Anthony <note>St. Anthony the Great, the father of Christian monasticism</note> about which he is treating. Gentianus omitted this word. Freculphus <note>a Carolingian-era bishop and chronicler</note> writes in his *Chronicle*, Volume 2, Book 4, Chapter 18: "*Poemen and Joseph in Pispiri, which is called the mountain of Anthony.*" Ruffinus <note>Tyrannius Rufinus, an early Christian monk and historian</note> uses the exact same words in his *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 2, Chapter 8.

<meta>catchword: Maka-</meta>

<summary>Meursius provides scholarly commentary and textual emendations for the lives of Moses the Ethiopian, Paul of Pherme, and Eulogius. He identifies the geographic location of St. Anthony's retreat at Pispir using citations from late antique and medieval historians.</summary>
<keywords>Moses the Ethiopian, Paul of Pherme, Pispir, St. Anthony the Great, Johannes Meursius, philology, Greek lexicon, Desert Fathers, hagiography</keywords>
