<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s philological commentary on the "Lausiac History" of Palladius. While the previous page (182) discussed Moses the Ethiopian, this page (181) provides geographic clarifications on the Egyptian monastic settlements and transitions into the life of Mark the Ascetic and the miracles of Saint Macarius.</meta>

<page-num>181</page-num>
<header>NOTES ON PALLADIUS</header>

...is distant eighty miles from Nitria <note>Nitria was one of the earliest Christian monastic centers in Egypt, located in the Nitrian Desert</note>. The place is described in the *Conferences*, Book 6, Chapter 1: *He was staying in the "Cells," a place situated between Nitria and Scetis; it is five miles distant from the monasteries of Nitria, and separated from the desert of Scetis by a wilderness of eighty miles.* <note>Meursius is quoting John Cassian, a major figure in early Christian monasticism; "the Cells" (Kellia) was a remote region for monks who desired more solitude than Nitria offered</note> John Moschus also mentions it in Chapter 177 of the *Spiritual Meadow* <note>original: Limonarium</note>: *A brother from a far-off region arrived at the monastery of the Cells.* See also Freculphus <note>a 9th-century bishop and historian</note> in his *Chronicle*, Volume 1, Book 4, Chapter 18: *Pampus in the Cells.* Refer also to Rufinus’s *Lives of the Fathers*, Chapter 22.

"having heard not from some" <note>original Greek: ἀκούσας οὐ παρά τινων</note>] This should be written as: "having heard *then* from some" <note>original Greek: ἀκούσας οὖν π. τ.</note>.

"having broken the biscuit" <note>original Greek: κλάσας τὸ βουκελλάτον</note>] Regarding this word, see our *Glossary*. <term>boukellaton: a twice-baked hard bread or rusk, standard in the Roman military and adopted by desert monks for its long shelf life</term>.

"for sleep is sleep" <note>original Greek: ὕπνος γάρ ἐστιν ὁ ὕπνος</note>] Read this as: "for sleep is..." <note>A technical grammatical correction regarding the Greek article</note>.

"I have desired to set things right" <note>original Greek: ἐῶ ἐπεθύμησα κατορθῶσαι</note>] This should be written as: "I permit, I have desired, to set things right." <note>A correction of punctuation and phrasing in the Greek text</note>.

"if you recognize the sin" <note>original Greek: εἰ γνωρίζεις ἐ τὴν ἁμαρτίαν</note>] Write it as: "if you recognize *then* the sin" <note>original Greek: εἰ γνωρίζεις οὖν τὴν ἁ.</note>.

->Concerning Mark.<-

"this wondrous thing" <note>original Greek: τοῦτο τὸ παράδοξον</note>] Nicephorus relates this in Book 11, Chapter 35, and Sozomen in Book 6, Chapter 28.

"he sifted" <note>original Greek: ἀπεσήθησεν</note>] See our *Glossary* regarding this word.

"a hyena, having taken her cub, which was blind" <note>original Greek: ὕαινα λαβοῦσα αὐτῆς τὸν σκύμνον τυφλὸν ὄντα</note>] Theodorus Metochites <note>a Byzantine scholar and statesman</note> also recounts this event in his *Roman History*, Book 2: *At that time, the great Macarius healed the blind cubs of a hyena. In return, their mother brought a sheepskin to the saint as a gift for the healing of her children.* <note>St. Macarius of Egypt was famous for his gentleness toward all of God's creatures</note>.

<meta>catchword: Concerning</meta>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s scholarly commentary on the "Lausiac History" of Palladius. Having previously discussed the desert father Moses the Ethiopian, Meursius now moves to philological corrections regarding the two famous monks named Macarius and the geography of their ascetic settlements.</meta>

<page-num>180</page-num>
<header>JOHANNES MEURSIUS</header>

Hervet <note>Gentian Hervet (1499–1584) was a French scholar known for translating the works of the Church Fathers from Greek into Latin</note> notes in his interpretation: For *taurea* <note>original Greek: ταυρέα</note> in this passage is nothing other than a whip made of bull’s hide; which are called *byndra* <note>original Greek: βύνδρα</note> by more recent Greek writers.
"the youth" <note>original Greek: τὸ μελλάκιον</note>] Regarding this word, see our *Glossary*.

### Concerning Macarius the Egyptian.

"Macarius the Egyptian, and Macarius of Alexandria"]
Regarding both Macariuses, one should consult Suidas <note>a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the ancient Mediterranean world</note>, Socrates <note>Socrates Scholasticus, a 5th-century historian of the early Church</note> Book 4, Chapter 22; Sozomen <note>Salamanes Hermias Sozomenus, another 5th-century historian</note> Book 3, Chapter 13; and Nicephorus <note>Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopulus, a 14th-century historian</note> Book 9, Chapter 14. Sidonius <note>Sidonius Apollinaris, a 5th-century Gallo-Roman poet and bishop</note> also mentions them in Poem 16:
> Now two Macariuses, now the hero Paphnutius.

"He was a citizen of Alexandria, selling *tragemata*"]
The most distinguished Petrus Scriverius <note>a Dutch philologist and contemporary of Meursius</note> thinks that *tragemata* <note>original Greek: τραγήματα; usually translated as sweetmeats, dried fruits, or nuts served as dessert</note> in this passage does not mean delicacies, but designates a certain species of palm fruit, called by that specific name. Pliny <note>the Roman naturalist and author</note> writes in Book 13, Chapter 4, where he treats the types of palms: *Among the remaining kinds, some appear common. The Syrians, and Juba, call them tragemata.*
"vigorous in age" <note>original Greek: τῇ ἡλικίᾳ σφριγῶν</note>] This should be written as: "vigorous in his [frame]" <note>original Greek: τῇ σφριγῶν</note>.

### Concerning Macarius of Alexandria.

"being a priest of the place called the Cells"]
This place was distant from Scetis <note>one of the three main monastic centers in the Egyptian desert</note> by almost 120 stadia <note>approximately 14 miles</note>. Sozomen writes in Book 6, Chapter 30: *And as one travels there into the inner desert, there is another place distant by nearly seventy stadia, named Cellia* <note>original Greek: Κελλία; literally "The Cells"</note>. *In this place are many scattered monastic dwellings, from which it obtained such a name.* Here you see the reasoning for the name. But that same author must be corrected in the same book, Chapter 28: *As it is asserted that the same Macarius was a priest of the Cells.* It is incorrectly published there as "of the Kellinoi" <note>original: τῶν κελλίνων</note>. Cassian <note>John Cassian, an influential monk and theologian who brought Egyptian monastic traditions to the West</note> records that it is distant from Scetis—

<meta>catchword: distant.</meta>

<summary>This page contains Meursius's philological notes on the two Saints Macarius, discussing their backgrounds, previous occupations, and the specific geography of the Egyptian monastic settlement known as "The Cells" (Kellia).</summary>
<keywords>Macarius the Egyptian, Macarius of Alexandria, Cellia, Scetis, tragemata, palm fruit, desert fathers, monasticism, Sozomen, Petrus Scriverius</keywords>

**XXVIII. Rufinus’s *Lives of the Fathers*, Chapter VII. & Nicephorus Book XI, Chapter XXXV.**

**pantry items]** <note>original Greek: κελλαριϰὰ</note> Regarding this word, see our *Glossary*.

**fine wheat bread]** <note>original Greek: σιλίγνια</note> See our *Glossary*.

->About Paësius and Isaiah.<-

**on the part of Paësius]** <note>original Greek: ἔπερθ’ τῆς Παησίου</note> Nicephorus <note>Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopulus, a 14th-century Byzantine church historian</note> mentions him in Book IX, Chapter XIV. Also Cassian <note>John Cassian (c. 360–435), a monk and theologian whose writings brought the wisdom of the Egyptian Desert Fathers to the Latin West</note> in Book V, Chapter XXVII. However, it should be read there as *To the elder Paësius*. It is published incorrectly as *A. f. Pasius*.

**and Isaiah]** <note>original Greek: καὶ Ἡσαΐας</note> Mention of this man is found in the work of John Geometres <note>a prominent 10th-century Byzantine poet and monk</note> in the *Paradise*, Epigram LXIV:

> Come now, behold these unendurable things, said Achillas,
> Isaiah, eating his morsel, dips it into salt.
> If you wish to season these with broth,
> come here to Egypt, to the dwelling-places of the self-controlled.
<note>original Greek: δεῦτ’ ἄγετ’, ἴδετε πρὸς τάδ’ ἄχετα, εἶπεν Ἀχιλλᾶς, / ψωμὸν ἔδων βάπτει εἰς ἅλας Ἡσαΐας. / εἰ σὺ ζωμία ταῦτα διαρτύειν ἐθέλοισθα, / δεῦρ’ ἴθ’ ἐν Αἰγύπτῳ ἕρρεα δ’ ἐγκρατέων.</note>

**upward-runner]** <note>original Greek: ἀπανοδρόμου</note> Gentianus <note>Gentian Hervet (1499–1584), the French translator</note> omitted this word as it was not understood. We explain it in our *Glossary*.

**beatitudes]** <note>original Greek: μακαρισμοὶ</note> See our *Glossary*.

->About Macarius.<-

**beside the lake called Maria]** <note>original Greek: παρὰ τὴν λίμνην τὴν λεγομένην Μαρίαν</note> We have spoken of Maria above.

**and they know this]** <note>original Greek: καὶ τοῦτο ἴασι</note> It should be written *isasi* <note>original Greek: ἴσασι; Meursius is correcting a spelling error in the manuscript from "they heal" to "they know"</note>.

**he committed involuntary murder]** <note>original Greek: ἀκούσιον εἰργάσατο φόνον</note> See Nicephorus, Book XI, Chapter XXXV.

**from the scriptures]** <note>original Greek: ἀπογραφῶν</note> It should be written as two words: *apo graphon* <note>original Greek: ἀπὸ γραφῶν; Meursius corrects a compound word meaning "censuses" to a phrase meaning "from the scriptures"</note>.

->About Nathanael.<-

**for he seemed to be listless]** <note>original Greek: ἔδοξεν γὰρ ἀκηδίαν</note> It should be written *akedian* <note>original Greek: ἀκηδιᾶν; Meursius is correcting the grammar of the Greek verb for "to suffer from listlessness," a common spiritual affliction for monks known as the "noonday devil"</note>.

**holding back the cheek]** <note>original Greek: παρῥέαν κατίσχων</note> He wanders far from the truth here. <note>Meursius is criticizing a previous editor's or translator's reading of this difficult Greek phrase.</note>

Jerome in the *Catalog of Illustrious Writers*. <note>original: Hieronymus in Catalogo Script. Illustr.</note>

**and Sephauk]** <note>original Greek: καὶ Σηφαύκ</note> Eusebius mentions him in *Ecclesiastical History, Book VII, Chapter XXXII*, as does Nicephorus in *Book VI, Chapter XXXVI*.

**To Rufinus, who was at that time Praetorian Prefect]** <note>original Greek: Ῥουφίνῳ τῷ κατ’ ἐκεῖνο καιροῦ ἐπάρχῳ τῶν πραιτωρίων</note> This is the famous Rufinus who, after the death of the elder Emperor Theodosius, paid the penalty for his ambition for the Empire <note>Rufinus was a powerful minister who was assassinated after attempting to seize more power</note>. See Sozomen *Book VIII, Chapter I*, and Nicephorus *Book XIII, Chapter I*. Moreover, Cedrenus <note>an 11th-century historian</note> records that Theodosius himself ordered this while on his deathbed. Mention of him appears in several Rescripts of both the Theodosian and Justinian Codes <note>foundational collections of Roman law</note>.

**into the depositories of the saints]** <note>original Greek: εἰς τὰ καταθέσια τῶν ἁγίων</note> To understand what <term>katathesia</term> <note>places where sacred relics were deposited or stored</note> are, see our *Glossary* under this entry.

**since he himself built martyria]** <note>original Greek: ἐπεὶ αὐτὸς ἔκτισεν μαρτύρια</note> Greek Christians used the term <term>martyria</term> <note>structures built over the graves of martyrs or to house their relics</note> to refer to temples sacred to the Martyrs. See our *Glossary*.

**in the martyrium called the Rufinianae]** <note>original Greek: ἐν τῷ μαρτυρίῳ τῷ λεγομένῳ Ῥουφινιαναῖς</note> The place takes its name from Rufinus, its high-ranking founder. The *Chronicle of Constantinople* states: *The same Rufinus received a pledge from Theodosius as Master of Offices, and he built the Rufinianae.* Previously it was called <term>Drys</term> <note>The Oak</note>. Cedrenus says: *Who, arriving at The Oak—now called Rufinianae—carried out the plot against John.* <note>This refers to the "Synod of the Oak," where John Chrysostom was unjustly deposed</note>.

->Concerning Benjamin.<-

**a certain wonderful man named Benjamin]** <note>original Greek: ἀνήρ τις θαυμαστὸς ὀνόματι Βενιαμεὶν</note> Regarding him, see Sozomen *Book VI, Chapter XXVIII*, and Nicephorus *Book XI, Chapter XXXV*.

->Concerning Apollonius.<-

**a certain Apollonius]** <note>original Greek: Ἀπολλώνιός τις</note> See Sozomen *Book VI, Chapter XXVIII*.

### NOTES ON PALLADIUS.

He is called Theodorus, not Origen. But the reading of our manuscript is correct. Behold, it appears again a little later: **standing by Origen the priest and steward, and Ammonius, men of great renown.** <note>original Greek: παρεστῶσιν Ὠριγένει τῷ πρεσβυτέρῳ καὶ οἰκονόμῳ, καὶ Ἀμμωνίῳ, ἀνδράσι περιβοήτοις</note>

**to have spoken the answer]** <note>original Greek: τὴν ἀπόκρισιν εἰρηκέναι</note> Read: **to have spoken the answer.** <note>The author is suggesting a minor grammatical or accent correction to the Greek text.</note>

->Concerning Pierius.<-

**the blessed Pierius]** <note>original Greek: ὁ μακάριος Πιέριος</note> Regarding him, one should consult Suidas <note>a 10th-century Greek encyclopedia</note>, Nicephorus Book 11, Chapter 37; Sozomen Book 6, Chapter 28; and Joannes Geometra, *Epigrams*, 41, line 66.

**having been accused or]** <note>original Greek: ἐγκληθεὶς ἢ</note> Read: **he was accused.** <note>Meursius is correcting a Greek conjunction to a verb form.</note>

->Concerning Ammonius.<-

**to those who have reached the summit of the Love of God]** <note>original Greek: οἷς ἄκρον τῆς Φιλοθεΐας ἐλάσαντας</note> This passage is mutilated, and it was more complete in the manuscript belonging to Gentianus <note>Gentianus Hervetus, a 16th-century translator and scholar</note>.

**to the blessed Bishop Timothy]** <note>original Greek: Τιμοθέῳ τῷ μακαρίῳ Ἐπισκόπῳ</note> Nicephorus (Book 11, Chapter 37) and Socrates <note>Socrates Scholasticus, the 5th-century church historian</note> (Book 4, Chapter 22) call him Theophilus, not Timothy.

**his own left ear]** <note>original Greek: τὸ ἀριστερὸν οὖς ἑαυτοῦ</note> Nicephorus (Book 11, Chapter 37), Socrates (Book 4, Chapter 22), and Sozomen (Book 6, Chapter 30) say it was the right ear. For this reason, this specific Ammonius was called <term>Parotes</term> <note>original Greek: παρώτης; a nickname meaning "the one-eared" or "mutilated ear." Ammonius reportedly cut off his own ear to make himself ineligible for the priesthood.</note> The authors I have cited recount this matter.

**both Origen and Didymus]** <note>original Greek: Ὠριγένης τε καὶ Διδύμῳ</note> The writings of Origen are very well known. Concerning Didymus <note>Didymus the Blind, a famous theologian in Alexandria</note>, we have already made a note above.

**and Pierius]** <note>original Greek: καὶ Πιερίῳ</note> Regarding Pierius, see Eusebius, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 7, Chapter 32, and Nicephorus, Book 6, Chapter 35. He lived under the Emperors Carus and Diocletian, and was so distinguished by the praise of his erudition and the vast number of his writings that he was called "the younger Origen." He should be seen in...

# JOHANNES MEURSIUS

Chapter 40. Socrates, Book 4, Chapter 22; and Jerome in the *Catalog of Ecclesiastical Writers*. The most illustrious David Hoeschelius, who has served the republic of letters well, recently published that book in Greek, which had never been edited until that time.

->Concerning Hor.<-

**a certain wonderful man named Hor]** <note>original Greek: ἀνήρ τις θαυμάσιος ὀνόματι Ὡρ.</note> Regarding him, see Nicephorus, Book 11, Chapter 34, and Sozomen, Book 6, Chapter 27. Jerome also mentions him in his *Letter to Ctesiphon*, which is written against Pelagius; as does Sidonius [Apollinaris] in *Poem* 16. See Rufinus, *On the Lives of the Fathers*, Chapter 2.

->Concerning Pambo.<-

**the blessed Pambo]** <note>original Greek: ὁ μακάριος Παμβὼ</note> Nicephorus, Book 11, Chapter 34, and Socrates, Book 4, Chapter 23. In the *Chronicle* of Freculphus (Volume 2, Book 4, Chapter 18), he is called *Pampus*.

**to Eusebius and Euthymius]** <note>original Greek: Εὐσεβίῳ καὶ Εὐθυμίῳ</note> The manuscript used by Hervetus <note>Gentianus Hervetus, a 16th-century scholar and translator</note> does not seem to have included these names. However, they are correctly added here. They were brothers, and because of their great height, they were nicknamed the "Tall Ones" <note>original Greek: μακροὶ</note>. See Sozomen, Book 6, Chapter 29, and Nicephorus, Book 13, Chapter 10.

**and Origen]** <note>original Greek: καὶ Ὠριγένης</note> In the manuscript of Hervetus, it seems the text read "and John" <note>original Greek: καὶ Ἰωάννης</note>. But Nicephorus and Sozomen recognize Origen here in the passages cited.

**to Dracontius]** <note>original Greek: Δρακοντίῳ</note> Whom Nicephorus mentions in Book 9, Chapters 45 and 46.

**to his steward Origen]** <note>original Greek: τῷ οἰκονόμῳ αὐτοῦ Ὠριγένῃ</note> In the manuscript belonging to Gentianus...

### Concerning Arsisius.

<margin>[The great Arsisius]</margin> We have noted concerning him a little earlier.

<margin>[Whose soul]</margin> Antony saw the soul of this man being taken up by angels. Athanasius also mentions this in his *Life [of Antony]*: "And they knew on that day and at that hour that Amun had fallen asleep, in which the old man saw his soul being carried up." <note>original Greek: καὶ ἔγνωσαν ἐν ἐκείνῃ τῇ ἡμέρᾳ καὶ τῇ ὥρᾳ κεκοιμῆσθαι τὸν Ἀμοῦν, ἐν ᾗ τὴν ψυχὴν εἶδεν ἀναφερομένην ὁ γέρων αὐτοῦ.</note> And a little before: "He saw someone being led up in the mountain, and there was great joy among those meeting him. Then, wondering and blessing such a choir, he prayed to learn what this might be. And immediately a voice came to him: 'This is the soul of Amun.'" <note>original Greek: εἶδεν ἐν τῷ ὄρει ἀναγομένον τινα, πολλήν τε τῶν ἀπαντώντων γενομένην τὴν χαράν. εἶτα θαυμάζων καὶ μακαρίζων τὸν τοιοῦτον χορόν, ηὔχετο μαθεῖν τί ἂν εἴη τοῦτο. καὶ εὐθὺς ἦλθεν αὐτῷ φωνή. ταύτην εἶναι τοῦ Ἀμοῦν τὴν ψυχήν.</note> Socrates [Scholasticus], Book 4, Chapter 22: "How Antony, who lived at the same time as he, beheld the soul of Amun being taken up by angels after death." <note>original Greek: πῶς τοῦ Ἀμοῦν τὴν ψυχὴν μετ' θάνατον ἀναλαμβανομένην ὑπὸ ἀγγέλων ὁ κατ' αὐτὸν βιώσας Ἀντώνιος ἐθεάσατο.</note> Suidas <note>the *Suda*, a 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia</note> also tells the same things under the entry for *Amus*.

### Concerning Amun.

<margin>[They said that Amun also lived in such a manner]</margin> Synesius also mentions him in *Dion*: "Since Amus the Egyptian did not stay at home, but judged the need for letters." <note>original Greek: ἐπεὶ Ἀμοῦς ὁ Αἰγύπτιος οὐκ ἐξέδρευ, ἀλλ' ἔκρινε χρείαν γραμμάτων.</note> And there exists a letter from Athanasius to the same man. He is called Ammon by Rufinus in the book *On the Lives of the Fathers*, chapter 8, as also by Sidonius [Apollinaris] in *Poem* 16.

<margin>[Being an orphan, he says]</margin> The same things are recounted by Suidas under *Amus*, and by Nicephorus [Callistus], Book 8, Chapter 41; likewise by Socrates, Book 4, Chapter 22.

<margin>[The blessed Athanasius]</margin> Athanasius wrote a specific book on the life of Antony, which Palladius praises here; likewise Nicephorus, Book 8.

<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s scholarly commentary on the "Lausiac History." Having discussed Amun on the previous page, Meursius now turns to correcting the names and historical citations of other desert fathers, specifically Arsesius (or Orsiesius) and several of his contemporaries, by cross-referencing various ecclesiastical historians and poets.</meta>

# JOHANNES MEURSIUS #

Socrates [Scholasticus] must be corrected in Book 4, Chapter 22: "Another Arsesius did not separate the young men who were struck, but those who made progress." <note>original Greek: Α’ρσήσιος ἄλλος τοὺς νέας παίσαντας οὐκ ἀφώριζεν, ἀλλὰ τοὺς προκόψαντας.</note> Today it is published as *Arsenius*. Sozomen also mentions him in Book 6, Chapter 29. There was another Arsenius besides this one, who was a tutor to Arcadius and Honorius; he soon retired to Scetis <note>a famous desert monastic settlement in Egypt</note> and embraced a solitary life there. Regarding him, see Cedrenus, Zonaras in *Annals* Book 3, Michael Glycas in Book 4, and Johannes Geometra in *Paradise*, Epigrams 9, 10, and 11, and John Scholasticus [John Climacus] in *The Ladder*, Chapter 27. Furthermore, Jerome must be corrected in his *Preface to the Rule of Pachomius*: *Those who were ignorant of the Egyptian and Greek languages, in which the precepts of Pachomius, Theodore, and Arsesius were written.* Today it is published as *and Orsiesius*.

**[and Petybasa]** <note>original Greek: καὶ Πετυϐασᾶ</note> He is also named this way by Nicephorus and Sozomen. In the codex of Hervetus <note>the 16th-century scholar Gentian Hervet</note> it seems it was *Petybasos*. <note>original Greek: Πετυϐάσου.</note>

**[and Agion]** <note>original Greek: καὶ Α’γίωνος</note> It should be written: *and Arsion*. This monk was called Arsion. See Nicephorus, Book 11, Chapter 38; Sozomen, Book 6, Chapter 29. Johannes Geometra calls him *Asion* in Epigram 78. <note>original Greek: Α’σίων.</note>

**[and Chronion]** <note>original Greek: καὶ Χρονίῳ</note> It should be written: *and Cronion*. As also below on page 124. Frequent mention of him is made below in the chapters *Concerning Eulogius* and *Concerning Paul the Simple*. He is called Cronion by Nicephorus and Sozomen in the places cited. Johannes Geometra must be corrected in Epigram 51:
> *Cronion came down to the guestly feast.* <note>original Greek: δαιτὶ παρὰ ξενίῃ κατέϐαινε Κρονίων.</note>
Today it is published as *Carion came down*, <note>original Greek: κατέϐαινε Καρίων.</note> with the letters slightly transposed and the 'o' changed to 'a'.

He highlights discrepancies between the original Greek manuscripts and the Latin translations of scholars like Jerome and Hervetus.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s scholarly commentary on the "Lausiac History" by Palladius. Following the previous page's focus on correcting the names of various desert fathers like Arsesius and Arsenius, Meursius now moves to Chapter 32 (implied) to discuss the geography and customs of the Tabennesiotes, the monastic community founded by St. Pachomius.</meta>

# NOTES ON PALLADIUS #

->Concerning the Tabennesiotes.<- <note>original Greek: περὶ τῶν Ταβεννησιωτῶν. The Tabennesiotes were a community of monks in Upper Egypt who followed the Rule of St. Pachomius.</note>

**Tabennesis is a place in the Thebaid so called** <note>original Greek: Ταβεννησίς ἐστι τόπος ἐν τῇ Θηβαΐδι οὕτω καλούμενος</note>] It should be written: *A certain Tabenne is.* For this place was called *Tabenne*, not *Tabennesis*, and from it the inhabitant monks were called *Tabennesiotae*. Sozomen <note>a 5th-century church historian</note> writes in Book 3, Chapter 13: *He lived on the island of Tabenne in the Thebaid, from which they are still called Tabennesiotae today.* <note>original Greek: διέτριβε δὲ ἐν Ταβέννῃ νήσῳ τῆς Θηβαΐδος, ὅθεν Ταβεννησιῶται εἰσέτι νῦν ὀνομάζονται.</note> Nicephorus <note>referring to Nicephorus Callistus, a 14th-century historian</note> writes in Book 9, Chapter 14: *In the Thebaid, Philosophy <note>a common early Christian term for the monastic life</note> had its workshop on a certain island called Tabenna, from which they are called Tabennesiotae to this day.*

**In which Pachomius became a monk** <note>original Greek: ἐν ᾧ Παχώμιος γέγονεν μοναχός. Pachomius (c. 292–348 AD) is the founder of cenobitic, or communal, Christian monasticism.</note>] See Sozomen and Nicephorus in the places cited just above; also see the *Menologium* <note>a church calendar of saints' lives</note> for the day of May 15. Jerome also mentions him in his *Epitaph of Marcella*.

**Girded in linen tunics** <note>original Greek: λεβιτῶνας λινοῦς ἐζωσμένοι. The "lebiton" was a sleeveless linen tunic characteristic of Egyptian monks.</note>] We have spoken regarding this word in our *Glossary*.

**And let them not enter with hoods alone** <note>original Greek: καὶ μὴ κουκουλλία μόνα εἰσίτωσαν</note>] Regarding this garment of the monks, one should see Cassian’s *On the Institutes of Monks*, Book 1, Chapter 4, and also our *Glossary*.

**And twelve at the lamp-lighting** <note>original Greek: καὶ ἐν τῷ λυχνικῷ δώδεκα</note>] For what the "lamp-lighting" <note>the *lychnikon*, or Vespers service</note> is, see our *Glossary*.

**Now he is the second-in-command in that monastery** <note>original Greek: νῦν δευτερέων ἐστὶν ἐν τῷ μοναστηρίῳ ἐκείνῳ</note>] Here the "second-in-command" <note>original: δευτερέων (*deutereon*)</note> is mentioned by Gregory [the Great] in *Life of the Saints*, Book 1: *In the times of King Totila there was a second-in-command <gloss>of the</gloss> king in the monastery <gloss>of</gloss> Phumdesinus.* <note>Meursius cites a Latin source in Greek script; "Phumdesinus" likely refers to the monastery at Fondi.</note>

**Camel-driver twelve** <note>original Greek: Καμηλαρίος δώδεκα</note>] *Kamelarios* was the name used by the Greeks in the Middle Ages for what the ancients called a *Kamelites* <note>a camel-driver</note>. John Moschus also uses it in the *Spiritual Meadow*, Chapter 107: *Behold, a camel-driver coming from Arabia took the donkey he had found.*

<sig>A a</sig>
<meta>catchword: cepit</meta>

<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s scholarly commentary on the "Lausiac History" by Palladius. Following the previous page's discussion of the Tabennesiote monks and their founder Pachomius, Meursius now moves to identify and cross-reference other individuals and locations mentioned in the text, such as the city of Athribis and the desert saints Ptolemy, Elias, and Piamoun.</meta>

# JOHANNES MEURSIUS #

<margin>118.</margin> *then the great one* <note>original Greek: ἔπειτα τὸν μέγαν</note>] He calls it the "Great Psalm." <note>This likely refers to Psalm 118 (119 in modern numbering), which is the longest psalm and was a cornerstone of monastic prayer.</note>

->About Ptolemy.<- <note>original Greek: περὶ Πτολεμαίου.</note>

*yet another man by the name of Ptolemy* <note>original Greek: ἄλλος τις πάλιν Πτολεμαῖος ὀνόματι</note>] He also mentions this man further down in the *Life of Paphnutius*.

*Cilician clay jars* <note>original Greek: κεράμια κιλικίσια</note>] He spoke similarly above regarding a *Cilician amphora* <note>original Greek: ἀμφορέα κιλικίσιον</note> in the section on Macarius of Egypt. <note>Macarius of Egypt (c. 300–391 AD) was a disciple of Anthony the Great and a foundational figure in desert monasticism.</note>

->About Elias.<- <note>original Greek: περὶ Ἠλία.</note>

*a certain Elias by name* <note>original Greek: Ἠλίας τις τῷ ὀνόματι</note>] See Rufinus, *On the Lives of the Fathers*, Chapter 12. Sidonius <note>referring to the 5th-century poet and bishop Sidonius Apollinaris</note> mentions him in *Poem* 16.

*in the city of Athribis* <note>original Greek: ἐν Ἀθριβεῖ τῇ πόλει</note>] Athribis, a city of Egypt. By others it is called *Aithribis*. <note>original Greek: Αἴθριβις</note> Joannes Zonaras <note>a 12th-century Byzantine historian</note> writes in the *Annals*, Book 3, in the *Life of Heraclius*: *this man, therefore, coming forth from Aithribos, approached the king, asking for land to settle in.* <note>original Greek: οὗτος οὖν ἐκ τῆς Αἰθρίβου προϊὼν, προσῆλθεν τῷ βασιλεῖ, χώραν αἰτῶν εἰς κατοίκησιν.</note> An anonymous author in the *Saracen Matters* says: *and from Aithribos the word of this error went forth.* <note>original Greek: ἀπὸ δὲ τῆς Αἰθρίβου προῆλθεν ὁ λόγος τῆς πλάνης ταύτης.</note> And it occurs again shortly thereafter. Constantine <note>referring to the Emperor Constantine VII Porphyrogenitus</note> in *On the Administration of the Empire*, Chapter 18: *his heresy took hold in the parts of Aithribos.* <note>original Greek: ἐκράτησεν ἡ αἵρεσις αὐτοῦ τὰ μέρη τῆς Αἰθρίβου.</note> And he mentions it several times again in Chapter 21. See also Paul the Deacon, *Miscellaneous History*, Book 18. Cedrenus <note>referring to the 11th-century chronicler George Kedrenos</note> calls it by the same name. By Stephanus <note>Stephanus of Byzantium, a 6th-century geographer</note> it is called *Athlibis* <note>original Greek: Ἄθλιβις</note>.

->About Dorotheus.<- <note>original Greek: περὶ Δωροθέου.</note>

*whom a certain Dorotheus succeeded* <note>original Greek: ὃν διεδέξατο Δωρόθεός τις</note>] Is this perhaps the same man whom Cassian <note>John Cassian, whose writings brought Egyptian monasticism to the West</note> calls the Abbot <term>Theodore</term> <note>a leader of a monastic community</note> in Book 5, Chapters 32 and 35?

->About Piamoun the virgin.<- <note>original Greek: περὶ Πιαμοῦν παρθένου.</note>

*Piamoun was the name, and she became a virgin* <note>original Greek: Πιαμοῦν τὸ ὄνομα γέγονεν παρθένος</note>] See Sozomen <note>a 5th-century church historian</note>, Book 6, Chapter 28, and Nicephorus <note>Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopoulos, a 14th-century historian</note>, Book 11, Chapter 35. But both of them make the person a <term>presbyter</term> <note>original Latin: presbyterum; an elder or priest</note>, not a virgin.

<meta>catchword: About</meta>

<summary>Meursius continues his philological and geographical commentary on Palladius's history, identifying the city of Athribis and debating the identity and status of figures like Ptolemy, Elias, Dorotheus, and Piamoun.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Meursius, Palladius, Lausiac History, Athribis, Ptolemy the monk, Elias the monk, Piamoun, ecclesiastical history, Byzantine scholars, Egyptian geography</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s scholarly commentary on the "Lausiac History" by Palladius. Following the previous page's discussion of the Tabennesiote monks and their founder Pachomius, Meursius now moves to identify and cross-reference other individuals and locations mentioned in the text, such as the city of Athribis and the desert saints Ptolemy, Elias, and Piamoun.</meta>

# NOTES ON PALLADIUS #

<meta>catchword: Heron</meta>

Book 8, Chapter 52. John Scholasticus Climacus also mentions this in Chapter 24. <note>John Scholasticus, known as John Climacus (c. 579–649 AD), was a monk at Mount Sinai and author of "The Ladder of Divine Ascent."</note>

*It is no concern of mine* <note>original Greek: οὐ μέλλει μοι</note>] Read: *It does not matter to me* <note>original Greek: οὐ μέλει μοι</note>.

*Enter into a monastery of many brothers* <note>original Greek: εἴσελθε εἰς κοινόβιον πλειόνων ἀδελφῶν</note>] See our Glossary under the word *Koinobion*. <note>A 'cenobium' or 'koinobion' refers to a communal monastery where monks lived together, in contrast to the solitary life of a hermit.</note>

*Father, let us eat with zeal* <note>original Greek: Παπα ζῆλες Φάγωμεν</note>] See the Glossary for this phrase.

*Place the twice-baked bread on the table* <note>original Greek: Ε’πιτιθητι τῇ τραπέζῃ παξαμάτας</note>] Consult our Glossary regarding this expression. <note>Paxamata was a type of hard, twice-baked biscuit or barley bread, a staple of the simple monastic diet.</note>

->About Pachon.<-

*A certain man named Pachon* <note>original Greek: Παχών τις ὀνόματι</note>] Regarding him, see Sozomen, Book 6, Chapter 28, and Nicephorus, Book 11, Chapter 36. <note>Sozomen and Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopoulos were prominent ecclesiastical historians who recorded the lives of early Christian saints.</note>

*To my teacher Evagrius* <note>original Greek: τῷ διδασκάλῳ μου Εὐαγρίῳ</note>] In the manuscript used by Hervetus <note>Gentian Hervet (1499–1584), a French humanist and translator</note>, it seems the name was *Eulogius* instead of *Evagrius*. However, the reading in the Palatine manuscript is correct: for Evagrius was the teacher of our author, Palladius. See Socrates Scholasticus, Book 4, Chapter 23, and our author again further below in the section on Valens. <note>Evagrius Ponticus (345–399 AD) was a highly influential desert father and theologian whose teachings on prayer and asceticism shaped Eastern monasticism.</note>

->About Stephen.<-

*A certain Stephen, a Libyan by birth* <note>original Greek: Στέφανός τις Λίβυς τῷ γένει.</note>] See Sozomen, Book 6, Chapter 28, and Nicephorus, Book 11, Chapter 36. John Scholasticus Climacus also mentions a certain Stephen in Chapter 7.

->About Valens.<-

*He thinks highly of himself in his arrogance* <note>original Greek: τῷ τύφῳ μέσος Φρονεῖ</note>] Read: *He thinks greatly of his arrogance* <note>original Greek: τ. τ. μέγα Φ</note>.

->About Heron.<-

*An Alexandrian by birth* <note>original Greek: Α’λεξανδρεὺς τῷ γένει</note>] Nicephorus (Book 11, Chapter 29) tells of another Heron who was a native of Egyptian Thebes. Cassian also mentions him in the *Conferences*, Book 2, Chapter 5. <note>John Cassian (c. 360–435 AD) was a monk and theologian whose "Conferences" recorded the wisdom of the Egyptian desert fathers for Western audiences.</note>

<summary>This page contains philological notes and cross-references for the biographies of several monks, including Pachon, Stephen, Valens, and Heron, while correcting manuscript errors regarding Palladius's teacher, Evagrius Ponticus.</summary>
<keywords>monasticism, Evagrius Ponticus, John Climacus, Sozomen, Nicephorus, philology, Greek manuscripts, Cenobium, Paxamata</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s scholarly commentary on the "Lausiac History" by Palladius. Following the previous page's discussion of the desert fathers and the monastic rule, Meursius here provides legal and historical context regarding the swearing of oaths on the Gospels, a practice frequently mentioned in ecclesiastical histories, before transitioning to the life of Paul the Simple.</meta>

# THE WORKS OF JOHANNES MEURSIUS #

...let them judge the reading of the sentence while the holy Gospels are set forth, with the advocates of each party swearing upon the holy Gospels at the very beginning of the proceedings, etc. <note>original Greek: τῆς ἀποφάσεως τὴν ἀνάγνωσιν προκειμένων τῶν ἁγίων Εὐαγγελίων δικαζέτωσαν... Meursius is quoting from the *Basilica*, a 9th-century collection of Byzantine laws, illustrating the legal requirement to have the Gospels present during court rulings.</note> Again in Book 29: so that those women, with the holy Gospels set before them, might testify in the records of the proceedings whether her father had truly died. <note>original Greek: ὥστε ἐκείνας τῶν ἁγίων Εὐαγγελίων προκειμένων... This highlights the role of the Gospels in validating witness testimony.</note> Menander, in his *Excerpts on Embassies*, says: Immediately, therefore, the one holding the high priesthood of the city of Singidunum <note>Modern-day Belgrade.</note> handed over the divine books to him through those conveying the messages in the middle; and he, having most deceitfully hidden his intention, rose from his seat, and pretending to receive them with much fear and reverence, and having kissed them most eagerly, said: "I swear by the words of God spoken in these parchments, that nothing said to this man will be falsified." <note>original Greek: παραχρῆμα οὖν ὁ τῆς Σιγγηδόνου πόλεως... Menander Protector was a 6th-century historian; this passage illustrates the solemnity (and occasional hypocrisy) of swearing on sacred texts.</note> Harmenopulus, in Book 1, Title 7, writes: He who, by judicial vote or by the request of the opponent, takes an oath in church while touching the pure Gospels, but is convicted of perjury regarding these, let his tongue be cut out. <note>original Greek: ὁ ἐκ δικαστικῆς ψήφου... Constantine Harmenopulus was a 14th-century Byzantine jurist. The penalty of tongue-cutting for perjury reflects the severity of religious oaths in late Byzantine law.</note> John Cantacuzenus, *Histories*, Book 1, Chapter 15: *You command us to swear faithfully by the holy Gospels and the venerable images of the saints.* <note>original Latin/Greek: Per sacra Euangelia... Cantacuzenus was a 14th-century Emperor and historian.</note> Again in Chapter 22: *Having brought forth the holy Gospels, and with his hand's signature in vermilion, the faith was reconciled by an oath.* <note>original Latin/Greek: Adhibitis sacris Euangelis... The "vermilion" or red ink was a specific privilege of the Byzantine Emperors.</note> One may observe this in many other authors of the middle and later ages.

->About Paul the Simple.<-

*The holy servant of Christ, Hierax* <note>original Greek: ὁ ἅγιος τοῦ Χριστοῦ δοῦλος Ἱέραξ</note>] Nicephorus mentions a certain Hierax in Book 14, Chapter 14. <note>Hierax was a famous Egyptian ascetic and scholar of the late 3rd century.</note>

*A certain rustic farmer, Paul* <note>original Greek: Παῦλος τίς ἄγροικος Γεωργός</note>] Regarding him, see Sozomen, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 1, Chapter 13, and Nicephorus, Book... <note>Paul the Simple was a disciple of St. Anthony the Great; he was called "simple" due to his humble and uneducated nature, yet he was credited with great spiritual power.</note>

<summary>This page provides extensive legal and historical citations regarding the practice of swearing oaths upon the Gospels in the Byzantine tradition, referencing authors like Menander and Cantacuzenus, before introducing notes on Paul the Simple.</summary>
<keywords>oaths, Holy Gospels, Singidunum, perjury, Harmenopulus, John Cantacuzenus, Paul the Simple, Hierax, Byzantine law, ecclesiastical history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s scholarly commentary on the "Lausiac History" by Palladius. Following the previous page's discussion of the legal practices surrounding the swearing of oaths on the Gospels, Meursius here continues to annotate specific passages from Palladius, focusing on the disciples of Saint Anthony the Great, monastic attire, and early Christian funerary customs.</meta>

### NOTES ON PALLADIUS ###

**"Macarius and Matoïos, who also buried the blessed Anthony when he fell asleep"** <note>original Greek: Μακάριος ϗ Ματώϊος, οἳ ϗ ἔθαψαν τὸν μακάριον Ἀντώνιον κοιμηθέντα. "Fell asleep" is a common early Christian euphemism for death.</note> These are surely those two disciples whom Athanasius, in his *Life of Anthony*, records as having lived with him for fifteen years and served him in his old age. However, the one whom Palladius calls **Matoïos** in this passage is called **Matoës** by John the Geometer in the 91st Epigram of his *Paradise*. In the manuscript used by Hervetus <note>Gentian Hervet was a 16th-century French humanist and translator.</note>, the name appears to have been **Amatas**.

**"A certain Eulogius of Alexandria"** <note>original Greek: Εὐλόγιός τις Ἀλεξανδρεύς</note> Regarding him, see the histories of Sozomen, Book 6, Chapter 27, and Nicephorus, Book 11, Chapter 34.

**"sculpting dross"** <note>original Greek: χάται γλύπων. Meursius argues this is a scribal error and should be written as σκατογλύπον (*skatoglyphon*), literally a "dung-carver," likely referring to a scarab beetle or used as a derogatory term for a lowly craftsman.</note> This should be written as **skatoglyphon**. See our *Glossary* under the entry for **chates**.

**"fastened with a leather cloak"** <note>original Greek: ἐμπεφιβλωμένον χλαμύδα δερματίνην</note> Regarding this term, see our *Glossary* under **Phiblan**. Furthermore, he uses the phrase **leather cloak** to refer to the **sheepskin** <note>original Greek: τὴν μηλωτὴν, a simple garment of sheep's wool common among desert hermits.</note> which Anthony used to wear. Anthony had received this cloak from Athanasius, and at his death, even though it was worn out by long use, he willed it to be returned to him as a legacy. Athanasius himself testifies to this in his *Life of Anthony*.

**"and it happened that the fortieth day of the blessed Eulogius was being celebrated"** <note>original Greek: ϗ συνέβη τοῦ μὲν μακαρίου Εὐλογίου τὰ τεσσαρακοστὰ ἐπιτελεῖσθαι</note> It was the custom for the fortieth day after a death to be observed with memorial rites. See our *Glossary* under the entry for **Quadragesima** <note>original Greek: Τεσσαρακοστά, meaning "the fortieth."</note>.

**"the third day rites"** <note>original Greek: τὰ τρίτα</note> The memory of the deceased was also celebrated on the third day after death. See our *Glossary* under the entry for **Tritinagia** <note>original Greek: Τριτινάγια</note>.

**"and taking the Gospel, to ensure the faith of the listeners, he placed it in the middle of the brothers and swore to them"** <note>original Greek: ϗ λαβὼν Εὐαγγέλιον εἰς πίστιν τ̃ ἀκουόντων ἔθηκεν ἐν μέσῳ τ̃ ἀδελφῶν, ϗ διωμόσατο αὐτοῖς</note> We should observe here the ancient custom of swearing an oath by the Gospels set forth before the parties. This practice occurs in the *Basilica* <note>A 9th-century collection of Byzantine laws.</note>, Book 7, and throughout...

<meta>catchword: the</meta>

<summary>In this page of commentary, Meursius identifies the disciples who buried Saint Anthony the Great and discusses the origins of monastic garments and early Christian funerary rites on the third and fortieth days. He also highlights the historical tradition of swearing legal oaths upon the physical Gospel books.</summary>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly annotations by Johannes Meursius on Palladius's "Lausiac History," moving from the disciples of Anthony the Great to specific accounts of influential Desert Fathers: Moses the Ethiopian, Paul of Pherme, and Eulogius. Meursius compares Palladius's text with other historical sources like Sozomen, Nicephorus, and the Latin chronicles of Ruffinus.</meta>

# JOHN MEURSIUS #

->About Moses the Ethiopian.<-

**"A certain Moses, so called"** <note>original Greek: Μωϋσῆς οὕτω καλώμενός τις</note> Nicephorus relates this in Book 11, Chapter 36, and Sozomen in Book 6, Chapter 28. One may also look for his entry in the **Synaxarium** <term>Synaxarium: a collection of brief hagiographies or lives of saints, arranged by feast day</term> within the **Menologium** <term>Menologium: a liturgical book of the Orthodox Church containing the lives of saints</term> for the 28th of August.

**"the collegium"** <note>original Greek: τὸ κολλήγιον, referring to a monastic community or a specific gathering of monks</note> I discuss this term in my *Glossary*.

**"in his very 'symprotes'"** <note>original Greek: ἐν αὐτὸν τὸν συμπρώτην αὐτοῦ</note> I am still investigating what a *symprotes* might be. Gentianus <note>Gentian Hervet, a 16th-century humanist and translator of Greek texts</note> omitted this entire phrase. It should likely be written as **sympaikten** <note>original Greek: συμπαίκτην, meaning a "playmate" or "companion"</note>.

**"whom with a cleaver"** <note>original Greek: ὃν μακέλλῳ</note> Regarding this word, as well as the following word **makelleion** <note>a meat market or slaughterhouse</note>, see my *Glossary*.

**"containing hams"** <note>original Greek: κωλεὰς περιύχον</note> This should be written as **periechon** <note>original Greek: περιέχον, the grammatically correct spelling for "containing" or "holding"</note>.

->About Paul of Pherme.<-

**"There is a mountain in Egypt"** <note>original Greek: ὄρος ἐν τῇ Αἰγύπτῳ ἐστὶν</note> One should see Sozomen, Book 6, Chapter 38, and Nicephorus, Book 11, Chapter 36.

**"which is called Pherme"** <note>original Greek: ὃ καλεῖται Φέρμη</note> So it is named by Sozomen and Nicephorus in the places cited above.

**"among whom was a certain monk named Paul"** <note>original Greek: ἐν οἷς Παῦλός τις μοναχὸς</note> This is the same man whom Basil calls "the Egyptian" in *The Miracles of Saint Thecla*, Chapter 30.

->About Eulogius.<-

**"Cronios related to me"** <note>original Greek: Κρόνιος μοι διηγήσατο</note> This is the same person who was called **Cronion** earlier in the text.

**"to the place called Pisiri"** <note>original Greek: εἰς τὸ λεγόμενον Πίσιρι</note> This was the name of the mountain of Saint Anthony, which the author is discussing. Gentianus omitted this word. Freculphus writes in his *Chronicle*, Volume 2, Book 4, Chapter 18: "*Poemen and Joseph in Pispiri, which is called the mountain of Anthony.*" Ruffinus uses the exact same words in his *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 2, Chapter 8.

<summary>In this page of commentary, Johannes Meursius provides philological and historical notes on three desert monastics: Moses the Ethiopian, Paul of Pherme, and Eulogius. He corrects scribal errors and identifies geographical locations, such as Saint Anthony's mountain (Pisiri), by cross-referencing late antique and medieval sources.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s scholarly annotations on the "Lausiac History" by Palladius. The text continues a discussion regarding the distances between famous monastic sites in the Egyptian desert—Nitria, the Cells (Kellia), and Scetis (Scithis)—before moving on to linguistic corrections and a specific account concerning Saint Marcus and Saint Macarius.</meta>

# NOTES ON PALLADIUS #

...distant eighty miles from Nitria. See the location in *Conferences*, Book 6, Chapter 1: *He stayed in the Cells, a place situated between Nitria and Scetis, which is five miles distant from the monasteries of Nitria, and separated from the desert of Scetis by a wilderness of eighty miles.* <note>original Latin: "Morabatur in Cellis... octoginta millium solitudine ab eremo Scithi." This quote is from John Cassian’s *Conferences*, a foundational text for Western monasticism.</note> John Moschus also mentions this in Chapter 177 of the *Spiritual Meadow*: *A brother from a distant region arrived at the Monastery of the Cells.* <note>John Moschus (c. 550–619) was a Byzantine monk and author of the *Limonarion* or *Spiritual Meadow*, a collection of stories about desert ascetics.</note> Freculphus mentions it in his *Chronicles*, Volume 2, Book 4, Chapter 18: *Pampus in the Cells.* <note>Freculphus was a 9th-century bishop and historian.</note> One may also look at Ruffinus’s *Lives of the Fathers*, Chapter 22. <note>Tyrannius Rufinus was a monk and historian who translated many Greek works into Latin.</note>

**"having heard not from some"** <note>original Greek: ἀκούσας οὐ παρά τινων</note> Write: **"having heard therefore from some"** <note>original Greek: ἀκούσας οὖν παρά τινων</note>.

**"the breaking of the biscuit"** <note>original Greek: κλάσις τὸ βουκελλάτον</note> Concerning this word, see my *Glossary*. <term>boukellaton: a hard, twice-baked bread or rusk used as a staple in the Roman military and by desert monks due to its long shelf life.</term>

**"for he is the hypygmos..."** <note>original Greek: ὕπυγμος γὰρ ἐστιν ὁ ὑπ.</note> Read: **"for he is the hypykmos..."** <note>original Greek: ὕπυκμος γ. ε. ὁ τ. Meursius is likely correcting a spelling to "hypykmos," referring to someone who is thick-haired or shaggy.</note>

**"so that I desired to set right"** <note>original Greek: ἵν' ἐπεθύμησα κατορθῶσαι</note> Write: **"so that I desired, I set upright"** <note>original Greek: ἵν' ἐπεθύμησα, κατ' ὄρθωσα</note>.

**"if you recognize the sin"** <note>original Greek: εἰ γνωρίζῃς τὸ τὴν ἁμαρτίαν</note> Write: **"if you recognize therefore the sin"** <note>original Greek: εἰ γνωρίζεις οὖν τὴν ἁμαρτίαν</note>.

### About Marcus ###

**"this strange marvel"** <note>original Greek: τοῦτο τὸ παράδοξον</note> Nicephorus relates this in Book 11, Chapter 35, and Sozomen in Book 6, Chapter 28. <note>Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopulus and Sozomen were prominent Byzantine and Late Antique church historians.</note>

**"he learned by heart"** <note>original Greek: ἀπεστήθισεν</note> See my *Glossary* regarding this word.

**"a hyena, having taken her cub who was blind"** <note>original Greek: ὕαινα λαβοῦσα αὐτῆς τὸν σκύμνον τυφλὸν ὄντα</note> Theodorus Metochites also records this matter in his *Roman History*, Book 2: *At that time the great Macarius healed the blind cubs of a hyena; their mother then brought a sheepskin to the saint as a gift for the healing of her children.* <note>original Greek: τηνικαῦτα ὁ μέγας Μακάριος... ὑπὲρ τῆς τῶν παίδων ἰάσεως. This story illustrates the theme of holy men living in harmony with wild nature.</note>

<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s scholarly annotations on the "Lausiac History" by Palladius. The previous page discussed the geography of the Egyptian monastic settlements, specifically the distances between Nitria, the Cells, and Scetis. This page transitions into specific biographical and linguistic notes regarding the two famous monks named Macarius (the Egyptian and the Alexandrian) and clarifies terminology related to their lives.</meta>

# JOHANNES MEURSIUS #

Hervetus <note>Gentian Hervet (1499–1584) was a French scholar and translator of patristic texts.</note> in his interpretation. For *taureia* <note>original Greek: ταυρεία</note> is nothing other than a whip made of bull sinew in this passage; which among more recent Greeks is called a *byndris* <note>original Greek: βύνδρις</note>.

**"the youth"** <note>original Greek: τὸ μειλάκιον</note>] Concerning this word, see our *Glossary*. <note>Meursius refers to his own *Glossarium Graeco-barbarum*, a dictionary of late and medieval Greek.</note>

->**CONCERNING MACARIUS THE EGYPTIAN**<-
<note>original Greek: περὶ Μακαρίω τοῦ Αἰγυπτίω</note>

**"Macarius the Egyptian, and Macarius the Alexandrian"**] Concerning both Macarii, see Suidas <note>The *Suda*, a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia</note>, Socrates, Book 4, Chapter 23 <note>Socrates Scholasticus, a 5th-century church historian</note>, Sozomenus, Book 3, Chapter 14 <note>Salamanes Hermeias Sozomenus, another 5th-century historian</note>, and Nicephorus, Book 9, Chapter 14. Sidonius also mentions them in Poem 16:

> Now the two Macarii, now also the hero Paphnutius.
<note>original Latin: "Nunc duo Macarij, nunc & Paphnutius heros." Sidonius Apollinaris was a 5th-century poet and bishop.</note>

**"He was a citizen of Alexandria, selling sweetmeats"** <note>original Greek: Ἀλεξανδρέων πολίτης γέγονεν, τραγήματα πιπράσκων</note>] The most distinguished man, Petrus Scriverius <note>Peter Schryver (1576–1660), a Dutch philologist and contemporary of Meursius</note>, thinks that *tragemata* <note>original Greek: τραγήματα</note> in this place does not designate confections <note>original Latin: bellaria; sweet treats or desserts</note>, but a certain species of palm tree, called by that specific name. Pliny [the Elder], Book 13, Chapter 4, where he discusses the types of palms: *From the remaining kind, they seem common. The Syrians and Juba call them "tragemata" [table-sweets].* <note>original Latin: "Ex reliquo genere plebeia videntur. Syri, & Iuba, tragemata appellant."</note>

**"vigorous in age"** <note>original Greek: τῇ ἡλικίᾳ σφριγῶν</note>] Write: **"being vigorous."** <note>original Greek: τῇ σφριγῶν. Meursius is suggesting a textual emendation to the manuscript's Greek.</note>

->**CONCERNING MACARIUS THE ALEXANDRIAN**<-
<note>original Greek: περὶ Μακαρίω τοῦ Ἀλεξανδρέως</note>

**"being a priest of the so-called Cells"** <note>original Greek: πρεσβυτέρῳ ὄντι τῶν λεγομένων Κελλίων</note>] This place was nearly 70 stades distant from Scetis. Sozomenus, Book 6, Chapter 30: *And here, as one comes toward the inner parts of the desert, there is another place separated by nearly seventy stades, named Cellia [The Cells]. In this place there are many scattered monastic dwellings, from which it also received such a name.* <note>original Greek: ἐνταῦθα δὲ ὡς ἐπὶ τὰ ἔνδον ἐρήμου ἡκόντων... ἐν τούτῳ δὲ σποράδην ἐστὶ μοναχικὰ οἰκήματα πολλά</note> Where you see also the reasoning for the name. But he himself should be corrected in the same book, Chapter 28: *as he asserts that the same Macarius was the priest of the Cellia.* It is incorrectly published as *Kellinon* <note>original Greek: κελλίνων</note>. Cassian records that it is distant from Scetis

<summary>Meursius provides philological and bibliographic notes on the lives of Macarius the Egyptian and Macarius the Alexandrian, clarifying the definition of "tragemata" and the geography of the "Cells" (Kellia).</summary>
<keywords>Macarius the Egyptian, Macarius the Alexandrian, Kellia, philology, textual criticism, Petrus Scriverius, Sozomenus, tragemata, palm trees, monasticism</keywords>

# NOTES ON PALLADIUS #

**28.** See Rufinus, *Lives of the Fathers*, Chapter 7, and Nicephorus, Book 11, Chapter 35.

**"cellar-goods"** <note>original Greek: κελλαρικά. This refers to items or provisions stored in a monastery's pantry or cellar.</note>] See our *Glossary* regarding this word. <note>Meursius refers to his *Glossarium Graeco-barbarum*, a dictionary of medieval and late Greek terms.</note>

**"fine wheaten breads"** <note>original Greek: σιλίγνια. Silignia were high-quality breads made from the finest flour, often considered a luxury in an ascetic context.</note>] See our *Glossary*.

->**CONCERNING PAËSIUS AND ISAIAH**<-
<note>original Greek: περὶ Παησίου καὶ Ἡσαΐου</note>

**"Long ago there was a certain Paësius"** <note>original Greek: ἔπερθέν τις Παήσιος</note>] Nicephorus mentions him in Book 9, Chapter 14. Also Cassian in Book 5, Chapter 27. But it should be read there as *To the elder Paësius*. It is incorrectly edited as *A. s. Pasius*. <note>Meursius is correcting a common misspelling in contemporary editions of John Cassian's works.</note>

**"and Isaiah"** <note>original Greek: καὶ Ἡσαΐας</note>] Mention of him is found in John Geometres <note>A 10th-century Byzantine poet known for his "Paradise," a collection of epigrams on holy men.</note> in the *Paradise*, Epigram 64:
> "Come then, look, the children are being led away," said Achillas,
> As Isaiah ate a morsel dipped in salt.
> If you wish to season these broths,
> Come here to the mountains of Egypt through the self-controlled men.
<note>original Greek: δεῦτ' ἄγετ', ἴδετε τὰ παιδί' ἄγεται, εἶπεν Ἀχιλλᾶς, / ψωμὸν ἔδων βάψας εἰς ἅλας Ἡσαΐας. / εἰ σὺ ζωμία ταῦτα καὶ ἀρτύειν ἐθέλοισθα, / δεῦρ' ἴθ' ἐν Αἰγύπτῳ ὄρεα δι' ἐγκρατέων. The poem emphasizes the extreme asceticism of the desert fathers, who ate only bread and salt.</note>

**"without manly courage"** <note>original Greek: ἀπανδρόμως. A rare term suggesting a lack of fortitude or a "un-manly" retreat from the ascetic life.</note>] Gentianus <note>Gentian Hervet, a previous translator.</note> omitted this word because he did not understand it. We explain it in our *Glossary*.

**"beatitudes"** <note>original Greek: μακαρισμοὶ</note>] See our *Glossary*.

->**CONCERNING MACARIUS**<-
<note>original Greek: περὶ Μακαρίου</note>

**"beside the lake called Maria"** <note>original Greek: παρὰ τὴν λίμνην τὴν λεγομένην Μαρίαν</note>] We have spoken of Maria above. <note>Refers to Lake Mareotis, a large brackish lake in northern Egypt near Alexandria.</note>

**"and they know this"** <note>original Greek: καὶ τοῦτο ἴασι</note>] It should be written: *and they know this* <note>original Greek: κ. τ. ἴσασι. Meursius is correcting a grammatical error in the manuscript where a rare form of the verb "to go" was confused with "to know."</note>.

**"he committed an involuntary murder"** <note>original Greek: ἀκούσιον εἰργάσατο φόνον</note>] See Nicephorus, Book 11, Chapter 35.

**"of the censuses"** <note>original Greek: ἀπογραφῶν</note>] It should be written: *from the scriptures* <note>original Greek: ἀπὸ γραφῶν. A correction changing "public records" to "holy texts."</note>.

->**CONCERNING NATHANAEL**<-
<note>original Greek: περὶ Ναθαναήλ</note>

**"for he seemed to have listlessness"** <note>original Greek: ἔδοξεν γὰρ ἀκηδίαν. *Acedia* or "listlessness" was a common spiritual ailment among desert monks, often called the "noonday demon."</note>] It should be written: *for he seemed to be listless* <note>original Greek: ἔ. γ. ἀκηδιᾶν. Meursius corrects the noun to an infinitive verb.</note>.

**"holding a fire-pan"** <note>original Greek: πυρείαν κατέχων</note>] He is vehemently mistaken <note>Meursius is likely criticizing a previous editor's interpretation of this passage before the text cuts off.</note>

<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s scholarly commentary on the "Lausiac History." Having previously discussed the monks Paësius and Isaiah, Meursius now moves into notes regarding the Praetorian Prefect Rufinus, the holy sites he established, and the accounts of the monks Benjamin and Apollonius, drawing heavily on early church historians like Eusebius, Sozomen, and Nicephorus.</meta>

# OF JOHANNES MEURSIUS #

Jerome in the *Catalogue of Illustrious Writers*.

**"and to Stephanus"** <note>original Greek: καὶ Στεφάνῳ</note>] Mention of him is made by Eusebius in *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 8, Chapter 32, and by Nicephorus, Book 6, Chapter 36.

**"To Rufinus, who was at that time the Praetorian Prefect"** <note>original Greek: Ῥουφίνῳ τῷ κατ’ ἐκεῖνο καιροῦ ὑπάρχῳ τ̃ πραιτωρίων</note>] This is that famous Rufinus who, after the death of the elder Emperor Theodosius, paid the penalty for his attempt to seize the Empire <note>Rufinus was a powerful Byzantine statesman who was assassinated in 395 AD after being accused of treason.</note>. See Sozomenus, Book 8, Chapter 1, and Nicephorus, Book 13, Chapter 1. Moreover, Cedrenus <note>an 11th-century Byzantine historian</note> records that Theodosius himself gave the order for this while he was dying. Mention of him exists in several Rescripts of both the *Theodosian* and *Justinian Codes*.

**"to the depositories of the saints"** <note>original Greek: εἰς τὰ καταθέσια τ̃ ἁγίων</note>] Regarding what "depositories" <note>term: katathesia; refers to shrines or places where relics were deposited</note> are, see our *Glossary* under this word.

**"which martyrium he himself built"** <note>original Greek: ὅπερ αὐτὸς ἔκτισεν μαρτύριον</note>] Greek Christians called temples sacred to martyrs *martyria*. See our *Glossary*.

**"in the martyrium called the Rufinianae"** <note>original Greek: ἐν τῷ μαρτυρίῳ τῷ λεγομένῳ Ῥουφινιαναῖς</note>] The place takes its name from its founder, the master Rufinus. The *Chronicle of Constantinople* states: *And the same Rufinus finally became Master under Theodosius, and he built the Rufinianae.* Previously it was called **The Oak**. <note>original Greek: Δρῦς. This refers to a suburb of Chalcedon, famous for the "Synod of the Oak" which condemned John Chrysostom.</note> Cedrenus says: *He who, being with him at The Oak—now called the Rufinianae—carried out the plot against John.*

### Concerning Benjamin. ###

**"A certain wonderful man named Benjamin"** <note>original Greek: ἀνήρ τις θαυμαστὸς ὀνόματι Βενιαμίν</note>] Regarding him, see Sozomenus, Book 6, Chapter 28, and Nicephorus, Book 11, Chapter 35.

### Concerning Apollonius. ###

**"A certain Apollonius"** <note>original Greek: Ἀπολλώνιός τις</note>] See Sozomenus, Book 6, Chapter 28.

<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s scholarly commentary on the "Lausiac History" by Palladius. On the previous page, Meursius discussed the Prefect Rufinus and his construction of various religious sites (martyria). # NOTES ON PALLADIUS #

**Theodore** is said [to be the name], not **Origen**. But the reading of our manuscript is correct. Behold, again a little later: **standing near Origen the elder and steward, and Ammonius, renowned men.** <note>Meursius is defending the manuscript's mention of an "Origen the elder," who served as a steward (oikonomos) of the church, distinguishing him from the more famous and controversial theologian Origen of Alexandria.</note>

**"to have spoken the answer"** <note>original Greek: τὴν ἀπόκρισιν εἰρηκέναι</note>] Read: **"the things spoken."** <note>original Greek: τὰ εἰρημένα</note>

->About Pior.<-

**"the blessed Pior"** <note>original Greek: ὁ μακάριος Πίωρ</note>] See concerning him Suidas <note>a 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedist</note>, Nicephorus Book 11, Chapter 37, Sozomenus Book 6, Chapter 28, and the Epigrams of John Geometres, 41 and 66. <note>Pior was a 4th-century Egyptian hermit and disciple of Anthony the Great, known for his extreme asceticism.</note>

**"if he were accused"** <note>original Greek: ἐγκληθεὶς ἦ</note>] Read: **"if he were accused."** <note>Meursius is correcting the Greek spelling from ἦ to ᾖ to fix the grammatical mood of the verb.</note>

->About Ammonius.<-

**"to those for whom the summit of the Love of God is less"** <note>original Greek: οἷς ἄκρον τῆς Φιλοθεΐας ἐλάσσειν</note>] The passage is mutilated, and it was fuller in the manuscript of Gentian <note>Gentian Hervet (1499–1584), a French scholar and translator of many Greek patristic texts</note>.

**"to the blessed bishop Timothy"** <note>original Greek: Τιμοθέῳ τῷ μακαρίῳ ἐπισκόπῳ</note>] Nicephorus (Book 11, Chapter 37) and Socrates (Book 4, Chapter 22) call him Theophilus, not Timothy. <note>Theophilus was the Bishop of Alexandria from 385 to 412 AD.</note>

**"his own left ear"** <note>original Greek: τὸ ἀριστερὸν οὖς ἑαυτοῦ</note>] Nicephorus (Book 11, Chapter 37), Socrates (Book 4, Chapter 22), and Sozomenus (Book 6, Chapter 30) say it was the right ear. And for this reason, this Ammonius was called <term>parotes</term> <note>original Greek: παρώτης; meaning "the one with a cut ear." Ammonius reportedly cut off his ear to make himself physically ineligible for the priesthood to avoid being made a bishop.</note>. The authors I have praised relate this matter.

**"both Origen and Didymus"** <note>original Greek: Ὠριγένης τε καὶ Δίδυμος</note>] The writings of Origen are very well known. Regarding Didymus <note>Didymus the Blind, a prolific 4th-century theologian in Alexandria</note>, we have made notes above.

**"and of Pierius"** <note>original Greek: καὶ Πιερίου</note>] Concerning Pierius, see Eusebius's *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 7, Chapter 32, and Nicephorus, Book 6, Chapter 35. He lived under Carus and Diocletian, and was so famous for the praise of his learning and the multitude of his writings that he was called "the younger Origen." Jerome should be consulted.

# JOHANNES MEURSIUS #

Chapter 40. Socrates, Book IV, Chapter XXII; and Jerome in his *Catalog of Ecclesiastical Writers*. The Illustrious David Hoeschelius, who has deserved well of the Republic of Letters, recently published that book in Greek, which had never been edited until that time.

**"this Lycus river"** <note>original Greek: τοῦτον τὸν Λύκον ποταμὸν</note>] See Athanasius in the *Life of Anthony*, and Nicephorus, Book VIII, Chapter XII. <note>The Lycus is a river in Egypt near which several monastic communities were established.</note>

-> About Or. <-

**"a certain wonderful man named Or"** <note>original Greek: ἀνήρ τις θαυμάσιος ὀνόματι Ωρ.</note>] Concerning him, see Nicephorus, Book XI, Chapter XXXIV; and Sozomen, Book VI, Chapter XXVII. Jerome also mentions him in his *Letter to Ctesiphon*, which is written against Pelagius; and Sidonius, Poem XVI. See Rufinus, *On the Lives of the Fathers*, Chapter II. <note>Or was a famous 4th-century ascetic and leader of a large community of monks in the Nitrian desert.</note>

-> About Pambo. <-

**"the blessed Pambo"** <note>original Greek: ὁ μακάριος Παμβὼ</note>] Nicephorus, Book XI, Chapter XXXIV; and Socrates, Book IV, Chapter XXIII. In Freculphus’s *Chronicle*, Volume II, Book IV, Chapter **XVIII**, he is called ***Pampus***. <note>Pambo was a disciple of Anthony the Great and a founder of the Nitrian desert community, famed for his wisdom and silence.</note>

**"to Eusebius and Euthymius"** <note>original Greek: Εὐσεβίῳ καὶ Εὐθυμίῳ</note>] The manuscript of Hervet <note>Gentian Hervet (1499–1584), a French scholar whose edition of Palladius was a standard reference</note> does not seem to have known these names. But they are correctly added. They were brothers, and because of their great height, they were nicknamed "the Tall Ones" <note>original Greek: μακροὶ; these were the "Tall Brothers," a group of four Egyptian monks who became involved in the Origenist controversy</note>. See Sozomen, Book VI, Chapter XXIX; and Nicephorus, Book XIII, Chapter X.

**"and of Origen"** <note>original Greek: καὶ Ωριγένους</note>] In the manuscript of Hervet, it seems there was "and John" <note>original Greek: καὶ Ἰωάννης</note>. But Nicephorus and Sozomen recognize Origen here in the cited passages.

**"to Dracontius"** <note>original Greek: Δρακοντίῳ</note>] Whom Nicephorus mentions in Book IX, Chapters XLV and XLVI.

**"to his steward Origen"** <note>original Greek: τῷ οἰκονόμῳ αὐτοῦ Ὠριγένῃ</note>] In the Codex of Gentian [Hervet]...

<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s scholarly commentary on the "Lausiac History" by Palladius. On the previous page, Meursius discussed Or and Pambo, two prominent figures in early Egyptian monasticism. He now turns his attention to Arsisius and Ammoun (also known as Ammoun the Nitrian), providing historical evidence for the miraculous accounts of their lives and deaths from various early Christian authors.</meta>

# NOTES ON PALLADIUS #

**"and receiving"** <note>original Greek: καὶ λαμβάνοντα</note>] Write "and receiving" [plural]. <note>original Greek: καὶ λαμβάνοντας. Meursius is suggesting a grammatical correction to the Greek text to make the participle plural.</note>

-> Concerning Arsisius. <-

**"the great Arsisius"** <note>original Greek: ὁ μέγας Ἀρσίσιος</note>] We have made a note concerning him a little earlier. <note>Arsisius was a contemporary of Anthony the Great and a respected figure in the ascetic communities of Egypt.</note>

**"whose soul"** <note>original Greek: οὗ τὴν ψυχὴν</note>] Anthony saw the soul of this man being taken up by angels. Athanasius also mentions this in his life [of Anthony]: <note>original Greek: καὶ ἔγνωσαν ἐν ἐκείνῃ τῇ ἡμέρᾳ καὶ τῇ ὥρᾳ κεκοιμῆσθαι τὸν Ἀμοῦν, ἐν ᾗ τὴν ψυχὴν εἶδεν ἀναφερομένην ὁ γέρων αὐτοῦ.</note> “And they knew that on that day and at that hour Ammoun had fallen asleep, at the very moment when the old man [Anthony] saw his soul being carried up.” And a little before: <note>original Greek: εἶδεν ἐν τῷ ὄρει ἀναγομένην τινα, πολλήν τε τῶν ἀπαντώντων γενομένην τὴν χαράν. εἶτα θαυμάζων καὶ μακαρίζων τὸν τοιοῦτον χορὸν, ηὔχετο μαθεῖν τί ἂν εἴη τοῦτο. καὶ εὐθὺς ἦλθεν αὐτῷ φωνή. ταύτην εἶναι τοῦ Ἀμοῦν τὴν ψυχὴν.</note> “He saw someone being led up on the mountain, and there was great joy among those meeting him. Then, wondering at and blessing such a choir, he prayed to learn what this might be. And immediately a voice came to him: ‘This is the soul of Ammoun.’” Socrates, Book IV, Chapter XXII: <note>original Greek: ταύτην τοῦ Ἀμοῦν τὴν ψυχὴν μετὰ θάνατον ἀναλαμβανομένην ὑπὸ ἀγγέλων ὁ κατ' αὐτὸν βιώσας Ἀντώνιος ἐθεάσατο.</note> “Anthony, who lived at the same time, beheld this soul of Ammoun being taken up by angels after death.” Suidas relates the same things under the entry for ***Amas***.

-> Concerning Ammoun. <-

**"he said that Ammoun also lived in such a way"** <note>original Greek: ἔλεγε καὶ τὸν Ἀμοῦν τοιούτῳ τρόπῳ βεβιωκέναι</note>] Synesius also mentions him in *Dion*: <note>original Greek: ἐπεὶ δὲ Ἀμᾶς ὁ Αἰγύπτιος οὐκ ἐξέδραν, ἀλλ' ἔκρινε χρείαν γραμμάτων.</note> “Since Amas the Egyptian did not choose a lecture-hall, but judged for the need of letters.” And there exists a letter from Athanasius to the same person. In Rufinus, he is called **Ammon** in the book *On the Lives of the Fathers*, Chapter VIII; as also in Sidonius, Poem XVI. <note>Ammoun (c. 290–337 AD) was the founder of one of the most celebrated monastic communities in Egypt, the Nitrian Desert. He is often recognized as the father of Nitrian monasticism.</note>

**"he said he was an orphan"** <note>original Greek: ὀρφανός φησιν ὑπάρχων</note>] The same things are reported by Suidas under *Amas*, and by Nicephorus, Book VIII, Chapter XLI; likewise by Socrates, Book IV, Chapter XXII.

**"the blessed Athanasius"** <note>original Greek: ὁ μακάριος Ἀθανάσιος</note>] Athanasius wrote a specific book on the life of Anthony, which Palladius praises here; likewise Nicephorus, Book VIII. <note>Athanasius of Alexandria was a 4th-century bishop and theologian; his "Life of Anthony" became the foundational text for Christian hagiography.</note>

<summary>This page provides scholarly citations and cross-references regarding the lives of the desert fathers Arsisius and Ammoun, specifically focusing on the miraculous vision Anthony the Great had of Ammoun’s soul ascending to heaven.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s philological commentary on the names of the Desert Fathers found in the "Lausiac History." He focuses on resolving discrepancies between various manuscripts and the accounts of other church historians like Socrates Scholasticus, Sozomen, and Nicephorus Callistus, specifically regarding the names Arsisius, Patubastes, and Cronion.</meta>

### JOHANNES MEURSIUS ###

Socrates [the historian] should be corrected in Book IV, Chapter 22: *Arsesios*. <note>original Greek: Ἀρσήσιος</note> "However, he did not set apart the young who stumbled, but those who made progress." <note>original Greek: ἀλλ' οὖν νέους πταίσαντας οὐκ ἀφώριζεν, ἀλλὰ τοὺς προκόψαντας.</note> Today it is published as *Arsiesios*. <note>original Greek: Ἀρσιήσιος</note> Sozomenus also mentions him in Book VI, Chapter 29. Arsenius was a different person from this man; he was the tutor to [the Emperors] Arcadius and Honorius, and soon after, he withdrew to Scetis to embrace a solitary life there. <note>Arsenius the Great (c. 350–445 AD) was a highly educated Roman tutor turned desert father, distinct from the Arsisius mentioned by Palladius.</note> Concerning him, see Cedrenus, Zonaras in *Annals* Book III, Michael Glycas in Book IV, and John Geometra in *The Paradise*, Epigrams 9, 10, and 11; also John Scholasticus in *The Ladder*, Chapter 27. Indeed, Jerome should be corrected in his Preface to the *Rule of Pachomius*: "Those who were ignorant of the Egyptian and Greek language, in which the precepts of Pachomius, Theodore, and Arsenius were written." <note>original Latin: Qui ignorarent Aegyptiacum Graecumque sermonem, quo Pachomii, & Theodori, & Arsenii praecepta conscripta sunt.</note> Today it is published as "and of Orsiesius." <note>Orsiesius was the successor to Pachomius; Meursius is noting a common scribal confusion between the names Arsenius and Orsiesius in the manuscripts of Jerome.</note>

**"and Patubastes"** <note>original Greek: καὶ Πατουβαστῆ</note>] He is named thus by Nicephorus and Sozomenus as well. In the manuscript of Hervetus, it seems to have been *Patyphatus*. <note>Gentian Hervet (1499–1584) was a French scholar who translated many Greek patristic works into Latin.</note>

**"and Agion"** <note>original Greek: καὶ Ἀγίωνος</note>] It should be written "and Arsion" <note>original Greek: καὶ Ἀρσίωνος</note>. This monk was called Arsion. See Nicephorus, Book 11, Chapter 38, and Sozomenus, Book 6, Chapter 29. In John Geometra, he is called *Asion* in Epigram 78.

**"and Chronion"** <note>original Greek: καὶ Χρονίῳ</note>] It should be written "and Cronion" <note>original Greek: καὶ Κρονίῳ</note>. As also below on page 124. Mention of him is frequent below in the chapters "On Eulogius" and "On Paul the Simple." He is called *Cronion* by Nicephorus and Sozomenus in the places cited. John Geometra should be corrected in Epigram 51:

> Invited to a stranger's feast, Cronion came down. <note>original Greek: δαιτὶ παρὰ ξενίῃ κληθεὶς κατέβαινε Κρονίων.</note>

Today it is published as "Carion came down" <note>original Greek: κατέβαινε Καρίων</note>, by slightly transposing the letters and changing the *o* to an *a*.

<meta>catchword: and</meta>

<summary>In this passage, Meursius performs a detailed textual analysis of the names of several monks, correcting errors in the works of Socrates Scholasticus, Jerome, and John Geometra by comparing multiple historical sources.</summary>
<keywords>Arsisius, Arsenius the Great, Orsiesius, Patubastes, Cronion, philology, textual criticism, Desert Fathers, Socrates Scholasticus, Sozomenus, Jerome</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s philological commentary on Palladius’s "Lausiac History." Having previously discussed the desert father Arsisius, Meursius here continues with specific textual corrections and geographical notes regarding Nitria and its inhabitants.</meta>

### NOTES ON PALLADIUS ###

...by heavenly wealth. <note>original Greek: τῷ οὐρανίῳ πλούτῳ</note>
**"it is not going to become"**] <note>original Greek: οὐ μέλλει γίνεσθαι</note> Write: **"it is not a concern."** <note>original Greek: οὐ μέλει γ.; Meursius is correcting a common scribal error where "to be about to" (mellei) was confused with "to be a care/concern" (melei).</note>
**"instead of 'unceasingly'"**] <note>original Greek: ἀντὶ τοῦ ἀπαύστως</note> Write: **"of the unceasing."** <note>original Greek: ἀ. τ. ἀπαύστου</note>

->**CONCERNING MACARIUS THE ELDER**<-

**"whom they call a *kathidlarios*"**] <note>original Greek: ὃν λέγουσι καθιδλάριον</note> See our Glossary. <note>The term *kathidlarios* likely refers to a "chair-bearer" or an official associated with a ceremonial seat, though its specific use here refers to a monk's title or role.</note>

->**CONCERNING THOSE IN NITRIA**<-

**"to men as if two thousand"**] <note>original Greek: ἀνδράσιν ὡς δισχιλίοις</note> There were 2,000 holy men there. <note>The source uses the archaic Roman notation "cIɔ. cIɔ." to represent 2,000.</note> See Nicephorus, Book XI, Chapter 35. <note>Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopoulos (c. 1256–1335) was a Byzantine church historian.</note>

**"the mountain of Nitria"**] <note>original Greek: τὸ ὄρος τῆς Νιτρίας</note> Nitria is named after nitre. <note>Nitre refers to naturally occurring sodium carbonate, or washing soda, which was harvested in the area.</note> Sozomenus, Book VI, Chapter 30: "They call this place Nitria, because there is a neighboring village in which they collect nitre." <note>original Greek: καλοῦσι δὲ τὸν χῶρον τοῦτον Νιτρίαν, καθότι κώμη τίς ἐστιν ὅμορος, ἐν ᾗ τὸ νίτρον συλλέγουσιν.</note> See Nicephorus, Book XI, Chapter 38. Freculphus's *Chronicle*, Volume II, Book IV, Chapter 18, should be corrected: *"Moses and Benjamin in Nitria."* Today it is erroneously published as *"in Nuria."* <note>Freculphus (died 853) was a Carolingian bishop and historian; Meursius is identifying a typographical error in his printed editions.</note>

**"a lake lies there called Maria"**] <note>original Greek: λίμνη κεῖται ἡ καλουμένη Μαρία</note> Nicephorus also mentions this lake in Book XI, Chapter 43: *"There is a certain island near Alexandria, towards the north, situated beyond the lake which is called Maria."* He also mentions it in Book VIII, Chapter 41. <note>This refers to Lake Mareotis in Egypt, near the monastic settlement of Nitria.</note>

**"and of the Mazichi"**] <note>original Greek: καὶ τῶν μαζίχων</note> Regarding these peoples and their ferocity, one should consult Cassian’s *Conferences*, Book II, Chapter 6. Evagrius also mentions them in Book I, Chapter 7, as does Ammianus in Book 29. <note>The Mazichi were a Berber tribe from North Africa known for raiding the Egyptian monastic settlements in the 4th and 5th centuries.</note>

**"of Arsisius the Great"**] <note>original Greek: Ἀρσισίου τοῦ μεγάλου</note> Arsisius is also mentioned in Nicephorus, Book XI, Chapter 37: *"Arsesius, however, would grant grace for the sins of the fallen youths, but he would excommunicate those who were more advanced in age and virtue."* Cor-

# JOHANNES MEURSIUS #

Sozomenus and Nicephorus, in the places cited immediately above. <note>These refer to Hermias Sozomenus and Nicephorus Callistus, significant historians of the early Church.</note>

**"A bundle of small vegetables"**] <note>original Greek: λεπτολαχάνων δέμα</note> In Hervetus’s manuscript, it seems to have been: **"a judgment of small vegetables."** <note>original Greek: λ. δίκα; Gentian Hervet (1499–1584) was a French scholar and translator of patristic texts. Meursius is noting a likely error in Hervet’s source text.</note> This is incorrect.

**"he wove another [mat] from palm shoots for the sake of food"**] <note>original Greek: ἔπλεκεν ἑτέραν τὴν ἐκ θαλλῶν Φοινίκων εἰς λόγον τροφῆς</note> To this belongs a very elegant passage found in Synesius's *Dion, or On the Life of the Egyptian Monks*, which is quite lengthy: where he says, among other things: **"Or what do those baskets of theirs mean, and those woven items they handle, unless, first of all, they were no longer men, making a return to things of this world by nature; for they certainly do not contemplate and exercise wisdom regarding weavings at the same time."** <note>original Greek: ἢ τί αὐτοῖς οἱ κάλαθοι βούλονται...; Synesius of Cyrene was a 4th-century philosopher-bishop. This passage reflects on the spiritual purpose of the monks' manual labor, suggesting it keeps them grounded in human reality while their minds seek the divine.</note>

### Concerning Potamiana. ###

**"A certain Potamiana, a most beautiful maiden"**] <note>original Greek: Ποταμιαίνά τις ὡραιοτάτη κόρη</note> Regarding Potamiana, see Nicephorus, Book V, Chapter 7, and Eusebius's *Ecclesiastical History*, Book VI, Chapter 5. <note>Saint Potamiana was a famous martyr of Alexandria known for her beauty and her refusal to compromise her chastity or faith.</note>

**"with a fire-fighter"**] <note>original Greek: ἐπυῤῥομάχῳ</note> Write: **"with a tower-fighter."** <note>original Greek: ἐπυργομάχῳ; Meursius is correcting a technical term, likely referring to a type of soldier or siege engine mentioned in the context of the martyr's story.</note>

**"in a most bright fire"**] <note>original Greek: λαμπροτάτῳ πυρὶ</note> The later Greeks used the word **"bright"** <note>original Greek: λαμπρὸν</note> simply to mean **"fire."** See our *Greek-Barbarian Glossary*. <note>Meursius refers to his own famous dictionary of Medieval and Byzantine Greek, the *Glossarium Graeco-barbarum* (1610).</note>

**"not to be undressed"**] <note>original Greek: μὴ ἐκδυθῆναι</note> Write: **"not to be dressed."** <note>original Greek: μὴ ἐνδυθῆναι; Meursius suggests a correction to the Greek verb based on his manuscript readings.</note>

**"of the land of the meek"**] <note>original Greek: τῆς γῆς τῶν πραέων</note> He alludes to the saying of our Savior in Matthew, Chapter 5: **"Blessed are the meek, for they shall inherit the earth."** <note>original Greek: μακάριοι οἱ πραεῖς, ὅτι αὐτοὶ κληρονομήσουσι τὴν γῆν.</note>

### Concerning Alexandra. ###

**"and in the tenth year"**] <note>original Greek: τῷ δὲ δεκάτῳ ἔτει</note> In Gentianus’s manuscript, it seems to have been: **"in the twelfth year."** <note>original Greek: τῷ δωδεκάτῳ ἔτει; Meursius frequently compares his texts against the edition or manuscript of Gentian Hervet.</note>
