# NOTES ON PALLADIUS. #

**"In the second consulship of Theodosius the Great"**] <note>original Greek: ἐν τῇ δευτέρᾳ ὑπατείᾳ Θεοδοσίου τοῦ μεγάλου</note> Theodosius the Great held his second consulship with Flavius Cynegius as his colleague; in the year 1141 of the City [Rome], but in the year 388 of Christ. See the Sicilian and Capitoline Fasti. <note>The "Fasti" were official chronological lists of Roman consuls used by historians to pin down exact dates.</note>

**"To Isidore, being a priest and the hospitaler of the church of the Alexandrians"**] <note>original Greek: Ἰσιδώρῳ, πρεσβυτέρῳ ὄντι, καὶ ξενοδόχῳ τῆς Ἀλεξανδρέων ἐκκλησίας</note> John Scholasticus Climacus mentions this Isidore in Chapter 4. <note>John Climacus was a 7th-century abbot of Mount Sinai.</note> Freculphus also mentions him in his Chronology, Volume 2, Book 4, Chapter 18: **"Isidore in Scythia, Pampus in the Cells."** But I judge it should be rewritten as: **"Isidore in Scetis."** <note>Meursius is correcting a common geographical error in manuscripts; Scetis was a famous monastic settlement in the Egyptian desert, whereas Scythia was a region near the Black Sea.</note>

**"Outside the Phakialion"**] <note>original Greek: ἐκτὸς Φακιαλίου</note> Is *Phakialion* the Latin word *facialis* [a face-cloth or napkin]? It is mentioned in the Latin-Greek Glossaries: **"Give the face-cloth"**; <note>original Greek: δὸς προσώπιδιον</note>. Likewise in the Scholia to the Homilies, it lists: a scraper, a face-cloth, a foot-cloth, a flask. **"Strigil, facialis, pedalis, ampulla."** The most learned Hervetus does not correctly interpret this as **"a Villa."** <note>Meursius is arguing that the word refers to a specific laundry-cloth or napkin, not a country estate as Gentian Hervet had translated it.</note>

**"With the holy Bishop Demetrius"**] <note>original Greek: σὺν τῷ ἁγίῳ Δημητρίῳ τῷ Ἐπισκόπῳ</note> Nicephorus praises two men named Demetrius; one the bishop of Alexandria (Book 5, Chapter 18), the other of Pessinus (Book 13, Chapter 15). Eusebius also mentions the Alexandrian in his *Ecclesiastical History* (Book 6, Chapter 26), as does Jerome in his *Catalog of Ecclesiastical Writers*. And I judge Palladius to be speaking of the Alexandrian in this passage.

->### Concerning Dorotheus. ###<-

**"In the so-called desert regions"**] <note>original Greek: εἰς τὰ λεγόμενα ἐρημικὰ</note> Sozomenus, Book 6, Chapter 28: **"among the so-called desert ascetics."** <note>original Greek: ἐν τοῖς καλουμένοις ἐρημητικοῖς ἀσκηταῖς</note> Nicephorus, Book 11, Chapter 34: **"In lonely places, as it is said."**

**"To a certain Dorotheus, a Theban ascetic"**] <note>original Greek: Δωροθέῳ τινὶ ἀσκητῇ Θηβαίῳ</note> Concerning him, one should see...

<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s philological commentary on the "Lausiac History" of Palladius. Meursius is comparing various Greek manuscripts, including the Palatine codex and the royal collection in Paris, to correct scribal errors and clarify the text's meaning.</meta>

HEADER: JOHANNES MEURSIUS

This man himself also wrote *On the Lives of the Saints*. Nicephorus <note>Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopoulos, a 14th-century Byzantine historian.</note> explicitly relates in Book VIII, Chapter XLII: *“Whoever wishes to examine such things will find many books in which very many who shone forth in that age and thereafter for their integrity of life are commemorated. Especially indeed, those things which Lausus the chamberlain <note>Lausus was a high-ranking official at the court of Theodosius II to whom Palladius dedicated his history.</note> composed about them.”*

### Preface

**"In this book"**] <note>original Greek: ἐν ταύτῃ τῇ βίβλῳ</note> All these things were missing in the Palatine manuscript, up to the word **"previously taken."** <note>original Greek: προειλημμένης</note> I have indeed supplied them from a manuscript in the library of the Most Christian King of the French <note>Referring to the Royal Library of King Louis XIII in Paris.</note> when I was staying in Paris. <note>Lutetia is the Roman name for Paris.</note>

**"A smooth entrance of the heavens"**] <note>original Greek: εἴσβασιν λείαν οὐρανῶν</note> Read: **"Into the kingdom of heaven."** <note>original Greek: εἰς βασιλείαν οὐρανῶν. Meursius is correcting a scribal error where 'into the kingdom' (eis basileian) was misread as 'smooth entrance' (eisbasin leian).</note>

**"And the bottom of Hades"**] <note>original Greek: κ' πυθμία ᾅδου</note> Write: **"And the depth of Hades."** <note>original Greek: καὶ πυθμένα ᾅδου</note>

### Narrative of the Life of the Holy Fathers

**"Upon the ruin"**] <note>original Greek: Ἐπὶ λύμῃ</note> Write: **"Upon the ruin."** <note>Meursius is likely correcting a minor orthographic or accentuation error in the manuscript tradition.</note>

**"For the Word was God"**] <note>original Greek: Θεὸς γὰρ ὁ λόγος</note> In the manuscript of Hervetus <note>Gentian Hervet (1499–1584), a French scholar and translator of the Church Fathers.</note>, it seems it was: **"For the word was divine."** <note>original Greek: Θεῖος γὰρ ὁ λ.</note>

**"Irrational ones"**] <note>original Greek: ἀμογίσκους</note> Write: **"Irrational ones."** <note>original Greek: ἀλογίσκους. The scribe likely misheard or miscopied 'alogiskous' (irrational) as the non-existent 'amogiskous'.</note>

**"Of voluntary philosophers"**] <note>original Greek: ἐθελοφιλοσόφων</note> In the manuscript of Hervetus, it seems it was: **"Of theological philosophers."** <note>original Greek: θεολογοφιλοσόφων</note> For he translates it as, *Philosophical Theologians*.

**"For the healing of them"**] <note>original Greek: εἰς ἴασιν τῶν</note> "The" <note>Greek: τῶν</note> is used in place of "them" <note>Greek: αὐτῶν</note>. Thus it is in Hesychius of Miletus <note>A 6th-century chronicler and biographer.</note>: "the" <note>Greek: τοῦ</note> in place of "his" <note>Greek: αὐτοῦ</note>. **"Of the disciples of him asking."** <note>original Greek: τῶν δὲ μαθητῶν τοῦ πυθαγομένων</note> Again in Xanthus <note>Xanthus the Lydian, a 5th-century BCE historian.</note>: **"In the second of the Lydian [books] of those being recorded."** <note>original Greek: ἐν τῇ δευτέρᾳ τῶν Λυδιακῶν τοῦ ἱστορουμένων</note> It is a "half-barbaric" <note>Meursius uses the term 'mixobarbaron' to describe the evolving, less classical Greek of the Byzantine period.</note> style: nor do the modern Greeks speak or write otherwise, as I also remember noting there recently.

### Concerning Isidore

**"Nor to the..."**] <note>original Greek: οὔτε τοῖς</note> Write: **"Nor of the..."** <note>original Greek: οὔτε τοῦ</note>

<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s philological commentary on the "Lausiac History" of Palladius. Meursius is discussing the reception of the text in the Greek Church and the identity of the person to whom it was dedicated, the high official Lausus.</meta>

# NOTES ON PALLADIUS

*Palladius, the disciple of Evagrius, who gathered their deeds into a single book, has set them forth excellently; those who wish may read him. For in it, he has piously described the lives not only of men, but also of women, who vied in holiness of character with those divine men whom we have mentioned.* <note>original Latin: "fuerint, Palladius, Euagrii discipulus..." Meursius is quoting a historical source, likely Nicephorus, to establish the book's authorship and scope.</note> Among the later Greeks, this work was called the *Lausaikon* <note>The "Lausiac History," named after its patron, Lausus.</note>; and it was held in such high esteem that it was read aloud in the church. **I observe** this, for instance, in the *Triodion* <note>The Triodion is a liturgical book used in the Eastern Orthodox Church, particularly during Great Lent.</note>: "Read from the *Lausaikon*." <note>original Greek: ἄπε ἀνάγνωσιν εἰς τὸ Λαυσαϊκὸν</note> And later: "After the third ode, the hymn of the month, and a reading from the *Lausaikon*." <note>original Greek: ἀπὸ ἤ τρίτης ὠδῆς, κάθισμα τ̄ μηναίε, καὶ ἀνάγνωσις εἰς τὸ Λαυσαϊκὸν. This indicates the text was used as spiritual instruction during formal worship services.</note>

**"To Lauson the Chamberlain"**] <note>original Greek: πρὸς Λαύσωνα τ̄ πραιπόσιτον</note> **It should be written**, "To Lausus the Chamberlain." <note>original Greek: πρὸς Λαύσον τὸν π.</note> For he is named in this way by John of Damascus, Cedrenus, Nicephorus, and many times in this very work. This man was called **Lausus**, not **Lauson**: he was the *praepositus sacri cubiculi* <note>Literally the "Superintendent of the Sacred Bedchamber," a high-ranking court official, effectively the Grand Chamberlain.</note> to the Emperor Theodosius the Younger <note>Theodosius II, who reigned in the 5th century.</note>. This is the meaning of that title, "the Chamberlain," <note>original Greek: τὸν πραιπόσιτον</note> which is a well-known indicator of high rank. Our author states this clearly below in the epilogue of the work, where he addresses him again: "And how I have found you again, even more excellent in your character, having been appointed as chamberlain of the most pious bedchamber." <note>original Greek: κ̄ οἷόν σε πάλιν εὗρον τοῖς τρόποις χρηστότερον, χειροτονηθέντα πραιπόσιτον τ̄ εὐσεβεσάτε κοιτῶν @.</note> In these words, when he says he was chosen as chamberlain of the "most pious bedchamber" because of his virtue and excellence of character, he refers to the sacred bedchamber of the Emperor I mentioned. Cedrenus <note>George Cedrenus, an 11th-century Byzantine historian.</note> also mentions him during the reign of Theodosius the Younger: *“After his father’s fall, he sought help from Isdigerdes, who had been sent by Antiochus as a guardian, to see if he might consider Eutropius as his master. After him, he sought Lausus and Kalapodios.”* <note>original Greek: τ̄ πατρὸς ἐκπεσὼν... μετ᾽ αὐτὸν Λαῦσον, κ̄ Καλαπόδιον.</note> Here you see the authority of this distinguished man, and how much influence he carried with the Emperor. **Indeed**

<meta>catchword: indeed</meta>

<summary>In this passage, Meursius discusses the liturgical use of Palladius’s "Lausiac History" in the Greek Church and clarifies the identity of the work's patron, Lausus, identifying him as the Grand Chamberlain to Emperor Theodosius II.</summary>
<keywords>Palladius, Lausiac History, Lausus, Theodosius II, Triodion, Chamberlain, Byzantine court titles, philology</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues Johannes Meursius’s discussion on the biography and historical reception of Palladius, the author of the Lausiac History. Meursius is establishing Palladius's ecclesiastical rank and his relationship with other prominent figures of the early church like John Chrysostom and Evagrius.</meta>

# JOHANNES MEURSIUS

He was a bishop; and first, indeed, of Helenopolis <note>A city in the province of Bithynia, near modern-day Hersek, Turkey.</note>, then later of Aspuna <note>An ancient city in Galatia, Turkey.</note>. Nicephorus Callistus <note>A 14th-century Byzantine historian.</note> writes in his *Ecclesiastical History*, book 24, chapter 35: *Palladius moved from Helenopolis of Bithynia to Aspuna.* Cedrenus <note>George Cedrenus, an 11th-century Byzantine chronicler.</note> mentions him as a bishop during the reign of the Emperor Honorius and records him as a disciple of Chrysostom. **"He** (Chrysostom) **had as disciples: the bishops Proclus, Palladius, Brison, and Theodoret; and as ascetics, Mark and Nilus."** <note>original Greek: εἶχε δὲ μαθητὰς, (ὁ Χρυσόστομος) ἐπισκόπους μὲν, Πρόκλον, καὶ Παλλάδιον, καὶ Βρίσωνα, καὶ Θεοδώρητον· ἀσκητὰς δὲ, Μάρκον, καὶ Νεῖλον.</note> Furthermore, Palladius himself, in his *Life of Valens the Monk*, names Evagrius as his own teacher; as does Socrates <note>Socrates Scholasticus, a 5th-century church historian.</note> in the place already cited. He wrote this work, in fact, in the 33rd year of his monastic life, the 20th year of his episcopate, and in the 53rd year of his age. He himself testifies to this at the beginning, in these words: **"The thirty-third year has passed for me in the community of the brothers and in the monastic life, the twentieth in the episcopacy, and the fifty-third of my life; I have considered it necessary..."** <note>original Greek: τριακοστὸν καὶ τρίτον ἔτος ἄγοντί μοι ἐν τῇ τῶν ἀδελφῶν πολιτείᾳ διῆλθεν καὶ τῷ μονήρει βίῳ, εἴκοσι δὲ τῆς ἐπισκοπῆς, πεντήκοντα δὲ καὶ τρία τῆς ζωῆς μου· ἀναγκαῖον ἡγησάμην...</note> John of Damascus <note>An 8th-century monk and influential theologian.</note> praises that work in his treatise *On Those Who Have Fallen Asleep in the Faith*, with these words: **"I speak of the historical book of Palladius to Lausus, in which the miracles regarding the great and wonder-working Macarius are truly recorded. For having inquired of a dry skull about those who have fallen asleep, he learned everything."** <note>original Greek: λέγω δὲ τὴν Παλλαδίου πρὸς Λαῦσον ἱστορικὴν βίβλον, ἐν ᾗ τὰ κατὰ τὸν μέγαν καὶ θαυματουργὸν Μακάριον παναληθῶς ἀναγέγραπται θαύματα. πρὸς τὸ ξηρὸν γὰρ κρανίον πυθόμενος περὶ τῶν κεκοιμημένων, μεμάθηκεν ἅπαντα.</note> But that story, which he alleges there, is not to be found in the whole of this book by Palladius; therefore, I believe that Damascene’s memory failed him; he thought he had read here what he had actually read elsewhere. Nicephorus Callistus also mentions it in his *Ecclesiastical History*, book 11, chapter 46: *But if anyone desires to know those things accurately—namely, what they did and said for the benefit of their listeners, and also how even wild beasts were subject to them...*

<meta>catchword: were-</meta>

<summary>Meursius establishes the biographical timeline of Palladius, noting his roles as Bishop of Helenopolis and Aspuna and his connection to John Chrysostom. He also corrects a citation error by John of Damascus regarding a story about Macarius and a "dry skull" that does not appear in the Lausiac History.</summary>
<keywords>Palladius, Helenopolis, Aspuna, John Chrysostom, Evagrius, John of Damascus, Macarius, monasticism, ecclesiastical history</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the biography of Palladius into Meursius’s formal philological and historical notes on the *Lausiac History*. It establishes Palladius's chronology and his relationship to his teacher, Evagrius Ponticus, using citations from the historian Socrates Scholasticus.</meta>

## JOHANNES MEURSIUS
->To<-
# PALLADIUS’S
### *Lausiac History*
### NOTES.

Palladius, Bishop of Helenopolis] <note>original: ΠAλλαδίου Ἐπισκόπου Ἐλενουπόλεως</note> This Palladius lived and flourished a little after the time of Valens <note>Roman Emperor from 364–378 CE.</note>, that is, under Honorius and Theodosius the younger, 1,200 years ago <note>Meursius was writing in the early 17th century; 1,200 years prior places Palladius in the early 5th century.</note>, having had Evagrius <note>Evagrius Ponticus, a highly influential monk and ascetic writer.</note> as a teacher.
He was first a monk, then a bishop. Socrates <note>Socrates Scholasticus, a 5th-century church historian.</note> praises him as a monk in his *Ecclesiastical History*, book 4, chapter 22:
**"If anyone should wish to learn about them—both what they did and how they acted, and what they spoke for the benefit of those listening, and how even the wild beasts obeyed them—this has been labored over by Palladius the monk in a special single volume, he who was a disciple of Evagrius; and he recounted everything accurately about them, in which he also made mention of women who took up a life rivaling the aforementioned men. Evagrius, then, and Palladius flourished a short time after the death of Valens."** <note>original Greek: εἰ δέ τις βούλοιτο τὰ περὶ αὐτῶν μανθάνειν, ὧν τε ἐποίησαν, ὧν τε ἔπραξαν, καὶ ὧν πρὸς ὠφέλειαν τῶν ἀκουόντων ἐφθέγξαντο, ὅπως τε αὐτοῖς τὰ θηρία ὑπήκουον, πεπόνηται Παλλαδίῳ τῷ μοναχῷ ἴδιον μονόβιβλον, ὃς Εὐαγρίου μὲν ἦν μαθητής· πάντα δὲ ἀκριβῶς περὶ αὐτῶν διεξῆλθεν, ἐν ᾧ καὶ γυναικῶν ἐφάμιλλον τοῖς προειρημένοις ἀνδράσιν ἐπανελομένων βίον μνήμην πεποίηται. Εὐάγριος μὲν οὖν καὶ Παλλάδιος μικρὸν ὕστερον μετὰ τὴν Οὐάλεντος τελευτὴν ἐξήνθησαν.</note> Afterwards, as I said, he was made

# PALLADIUS
<note>original: ΠΑΛΛΑΔΙΟΥ</note>

...indeed, should there be anything I need. For me, it is sufficient to have been deemed worthy by divine grace to remember all these things and to have been able to hand them down in writing. For I do not believe, in my opinion, that your good mind was moved to command my humble self <note>The Greek text reads "εὐθενείᾳ" (prosperity/abundance), but in this context of a modesty formula, it is likely a transcription or scribal variant for "ἀσθενείᾳ" (weakness/infirmity).</note> to compose this book and to commit the lives of the holy and blessed fathers to writing without the active working of God. 

But you, most faithful and honored servant of Christ, Lausus—a person most dear and genuine to me—by reading this book with pleasure, you will provide sufficient benefit to your immortal soul through the reading about these righteous men. Taking to heart the virtuous lives, the deeds, the labors, the great endurance, and the perseverance in hardship of these renowned athletes <note>Early Christian writers often used the term "athletes" to describe monks, who "trained" their bodies and spirits to struggle against temptation and worldly desires.</note>, press forward eagerly. Nourished by a good and pure hope, always seeing the days ahead as shorter than those behind. 

And pray for me, keeping yourself blameless and guarding yourself pure, just as I have known you and held you to be from the consulship of Tatianus <note>Flavius Eutolmius Tatianus, who served as consul in 391 CE, indicating Palladius had known Lausus for several decades.</note> until today. I have found you to be even more excellent in your character now that you have been appointed *praepositus* <term>the Grand Chamberlain, one of the highest-ranking officials in the Byzantine imperial court</term> of the most pious Emperor. For he whom such high dignity, such vast wealth, and such great power did not corrupt—thanks to the fear of God—is truly dedicated to Christ, who himself heard from the Devil: "All these things I will give to you, if you will fall down and worship me" <note>A reference to the temptation of Jesus in the wilderness, as described in the Gospel of Matthew 4:9.</note>.

->The End.<-

### 165. LAUSIAC HISTORY. 165.

"...only agree with me to sin just once, and as it may seem to you to speak even a word of darkness <note>original: "ἀχλύδα" (achlyda), meaning mist or gloom; here it implies a clouded or blasphemous thought</note>, and if it be near, I will lead you easily." And again, another time, the demon of blasphemy wrestled with him for fourteen days and nights, as he himself used to relate to me. And in the night, dragging him by the foot, the demon spoke to him in a manly voice, saying: "Do not worship Christ." But he replied to him: "For this very reason—because you find this loathsome—I will worship and glorify Him. And again, and many times over, I will pray even more, and as long as breath remains in me, I will entreat Him, because you oppose the worship of God."

Having walked through one hundred and six cities, and having spent a long time in most of them, he had no experience of a woman by the mercy of Christ—not even in a dream—except for the "war" <note>Early monastic literature frequently describes temptation as a "war" or "combat" (polemos) against specific demons.</note> brought by the demon of fornication.

I knew this man to be the third person who, when in need of food, received it from the hands of holy angels. One day, being found in the deepest wilderness and carrying no food at all, he found three warm loaves of bread in his sheepskin <note>original: "μηλωτῇ" (melote); the characteristic rough sheepskin cloak worn by desert ascetics in imitation of the prophet Elijah.</note>. At another time, he found dried biscuits; and for the third time, wine with bread.

I also knew him when he was lacking supplies, hearing from an angel that he should go to a certain man to whom the angel had sent him. He said to that man: "Are you so-and-so, the monk?" He answered and said: "Yes, I am the one you speak of." And the messenger said: "The Master of the House <note>original: "οἰκοδεσπότης" (oikodespotes); a common New Testament term for the head of a household, here referring to God.</note> has commanded that you be given thirty modii <term>modius: a Roman unit of dry measure, approximately two gallons or 8.75 liters.</term> of grain, and twelve sextarii <term>sextarius: a Roman unit of liquid measure, roughly equivalent to a pint or 0.5 liters.</term> of oil." 

Of such a man I will boast—such was he whom I knew. I know that he wept over men in need and those who were distressed in poverty. He provided whatever he had, except for his own flesh. I know that he also wept for those who had fallen into sins, and by his tears, he led the fallen back to repentance <note>original: "μετάνοια" (metanoia); literally a 'change of mind,' the standard Greek term for spiritual repentance.</note>. He once swore to me: "I prayed to God that no one should ever be stung [with guilt], especially of the wicked near the gift..."

...we have written [these things], so that no one, by practicing slander, may be caught in the nets of the enemy and fall into unbearable pains of the flesh—just as did the pseudo-widow <note>original: "ψευδοχήρα" (pseudochēra); likely referring to a woman who falsely claimed the status of a widow, which was a specific protected and respected office in the early Church.</note> who was described—and then, after departure from the body, be handed over to eternal and unending punishments. For the slanderer provokes God to anger; but the one who is slandered and bears it with reverence, entrusting the revelation [of the truth] to prayers and awaiting the just judgment of God, is like the crowned one of Christ <note>A term for a victorious ascetic or martyr.</note>. Such a person is greatly glorified even here and is deemed worthy of eternal crowns. 

Let us therefore learn accurately the unconquerable power of prayer, which draws God to us in faith, pities those in sin, aids those who are perfecting virtue, holds all creation together, entreats the Creator of all things, crowns those who achieve the best <term>way of life</term> <note>original: "πολιτεία" (politeia); in monastic literature, this refers to the total conduct and spiritual discipline of a monk.</note>, exposes slanderers, and grants the kingdom of heaven to those who persevere in it.

### Concerning the brother who was with me.

Having spoken a few things concerning the brother who has been with me from his youth until today, I shall hereafter bring my discourse to a rest. I have known this man accurately over many years to have achieved the best way of life and to have mastery over his passions. I know him accurately: he has not "eaten" passions <note>A metaphor for internalizing or indulging in sinful impulses.</note>, he has not fasted with a predisposition toward passion, and he has conquered—as I believe—the passion of <term>love of money</term> <note>original: "φιλαργυρία" (philargyria); often called the 'root of all evils' in ascetic texts.</note>. He has largely escaped <term>vainglory</term> <note>original: "κενοδοξία" (kenodoxia); the desire for human praise, considered a subtle and dangerous vice for monks.</note>, being always content with his present circumstances. He does not adorn himself with fine garments, and when he is looked down upon or disparaged by some, he gives thanks. He puts himself at risk for his true friends and has experienced the trials of demons a thousand times and more. Thus it was that on one of those days, a demon met him and said:

After all this, through the prayers of the young man, the relentless heart of the corrupt and lying woman broke under the unbearable pains, and a <term>confession</term> <note>original: "ἐξομολόγησις" (exomologēsis); in the early church, this was a formal, often public, admission of sin required for repentance.</note> came forth as she cried out in wailing tones: "Woe is me, miserable woman! I am in danger of perishing because I have fallen into two evils: <term>slander</term> <note>original: "συκοφαντίᾳ" (sykophantia); specifically the crime of making a false accusation to a person in authority.</note> and sexual immorality. I lost my virginity; I was handed over to discord. Having been corrupted by another man, I lied about the <term>reader</term>."

When the virgins of the monastery heard these things, they reported everything to her father. But her father—feeling ashamed before everyone and wishing not to be condemned for the slander—either believed what was said or simply kept quiet about the whole matter. 

Yet as Righteous Judgment <note>A personification of divine justice acting as a judge.</note> presided, judging all things, it brought forth successive birth-pains upon this wretched woman once again. Consequently, she was unable either to live or to die. Since she could not escape the labor, the eighth day dawned, and then the ninth, ready to overwhelm her in the deepest darkness of unceasing contractions. Since the community <note>original: "συνοδία" (synodia); the group of monastics living together.</note> could no longer bear her loud wailing, they ran to the bishop and reported: "Today is the ninth day; this woman is confessing, saying, 'I lied about this reader and I cannot give birth because I slandered him.'"

It belongs, therefore, to Your just and unswerving judgment to reveal my sentence as well."

When the hour came, the righteous judgment of God also arrived, bringing sharp and unbearable pain to the woman who had slandered him. An immeasurable multitude of groans, unspeakable birth pangs, and terrifying visions of the punishments of the underworld strongly besieged this wretched woman, and the swelling fetus in her womb did not emerge. ->### OF PALLADIUS ###<-

<page-num>160</page-num>

# Concerning the woman who falsely accused a certain reader.

The daughter of a certain priest, a virgin in Caesarea of Palestine, having fallen into sin with the man who corrupted her, was instructed by him to <term>falsely accuse</term> <note>original: "συκοφαντῆσαι" (sykophantēsai); to bring a malicious or slanderous charge</note> a certain <term>reader</term> <note>original: "ἀναγνώστην" (anagnōstēn); a minor clerical rank in the early church responsible for the public reading of scriptures</note> of the city, and to lay the blame for her ruin upon him. When she became pregnant and was questioned by her own father, she spoke against the reader. When the priest heard this, he was deeply troubled and reported the matter to the bishop. Having learned of this, the bishop convened the <term>clergy</term> <note>original: "ἱερατεῖον" (hierateion); the local body of ordained ministers</note>. After he had the reader summoned, they began to examine the case.

Now, when the reader was questioned by the bishop, he would not confess. For how could he be judged for something he had not done? The bishop, becoming indignant, rebuked him with a most heavy voice: "Do you not confess your fault and repent, you wretch, you miserable man, filled with impurity?" The reader replied: "I beg of you—I have told you what is not so, for I have no part in this matter; I am innocent, even of the very thought of her. But if you wish to hear me say what is not so, then I have done it." Once he had said this, the bishop deposed him from the rank of reader.

After this, the man came forward and entreated the bishop, saying: "Since it seemed right to you, Lord Bishop—because I spoke and have been struck down—that I should be excluded from the <term>sacred sanctuary</term> <note>original: "τιμίου βήματος" (timiou bēmatos); the raised platform or altar area where the clergy served</note> and that I am no longer worthy to be a cleric under your holiness, command that she be given to me from now on as a wife. For I am no longer a cleric, nor is she any longer a virgin." Hearing these things, the bishop and the priest (her father) gave her over to the reader, expecting that the young man was devoted to her and that they could not otherwise be separated.

<note>This passage uses technical terms for the ecclesiastical hierarchy of the 4th and 5th centuries:
Palestine: The Roman province where the events occur.
Caesarea: A major coastal city and center of Christian learning.
False accusation: A key theme in desert literature, testing the humility of the accused.
Reader: The specific office held by the protagonist.
Bishop: The presiding authority.
Cleric: A general term for those in holy orders.
The Clergy: The assembly of officials.</note>

<summary>A priest's daughter in Caesarea is seduced and, at her lover's urging, falsely accuses a local church reader of the crime. To resolve the scandal and protect the woman, the reader accepts the false charge and agrees to marry her, leading to his removal from the clergy.</summary>
<keywords>Caesarea, Palestine, false accusation, reader, clerical discipline, marriage, bishop, Palladius, Lausiac History</keywords>

->### THE LAUSIAC HISTORY ###<-

<page-num>159</page-num>
<header>CH. 16. LAUSIAC</header>

...that she reached such a great measure of <term>repentance</term> <note>original: "μετάνοια" (metanoia); a transformative change of heart and mind.</note>, that she sought to starve herself through <term>self-control</term> <note>original: "ἐγκράτεια" (enkrateia); the practice of ascetic discipline, particularly over food and bodily desires.</note>, wishing to kill herself by hunger. She prayed for this with tears, entreating God and saying: "O Great God, you who bear all our sins and the immeasurable wickedness of the whole world; you who do not desire the death of sinners or the destruction of the fallen, but show mercy to all creation—for it is your will that all be saved—if you truly wish for me, the lost one, to be saved, show me your goodness in this. Even now, let your wonders reach me, and command the **fruit of my iniquity** to be gathered <note>This is a difficult passage where the woman prays for the death of her infant, which she views as the physical manifestation of her sin and an obstacle to her restoration.</note>—that which was conceived in impiety and born in the sin of my ruin. For if this does not happen, I will either use a noose or take my own life."

Because of this entreaty, she was heard; for the child died not long after it was born. From that day forward, she never again met with the man who had taken her captive, but gave herself over to those to whom God had granted extreme **self-control**. For thirty years, she served those who were sick and maimed. In this way, she so moved God and made Him merciful toward her that it was revealed to one of the holy elders: "This woman has pleased me more in her **repentance** than she did in her **virginity**." <note>This statement is particularly striking in the context of the early Church, where virginity was considered the highest spiritual state; it suggests that profound humility and repentance can surpass even "unsullied" virtue.</note> I write these things so that we might not look down upon those who sin greatly and

<margin>•</margin> **repent from the heart**, just as this blessed woman crushed her own heart and subjected her life to **humility**, and was not rejected by the Lord of the humble.

<meta>This page concludes the account of a remarkably charitable monk (likely Serapion the Sindonite) and begins the "History of the Deceived Virgin." It follows a previous discussion of ascetic labor and serves as a transition to themes of sin, remorse, and restoration.</meta>

<page-num>158</page-num>
<header>PALLADIUS</header>

...When the situation came upon him, he went out and looked. Finding no one to assist her in that time of necessity, he himself took the place of a **physician**, not loathing the impurity that follows women in <term>childbirth</term> <note>original: "ἐκτόκαις" (ektokais); the physical discharge and blood associated with labor, which was often viewed with ritual or social avoidance.</note>. A profound sense of <term>mercy</term> <note>original: "ἐλεημοσύνη" (eleemosyne); the virtue of active compassion or almsgiving.</note> had granted him a certain numbness to the physical disgust of the task. At that time, the clothing he wore was not worth a single <term>obol</term> <note>A small coin of very low value.</note>. Even the wear and tear of his garments was a matter of debate. He could not endure to peer into a <term>book</term> <note>original: "πυκτίῳ" (pyktio); a small codex or collection of writings.</note>, for his love for humanity drove him away from his reading to serve others. If any of the brothers gave him a book, he would sell it immediately, saying: "Why should I keep this?" When asked why he sold his books, he replied: "How else can I convince the **Teacher** <note>Referring to Christ.</note> that I have learned His craft accurately, unless I use that very book for the perfection of the craft?" <note>This is a famous desert father "apophthegm" or saying: the monk sells the Gospel book in order to give the money to the poor, thereby "doing" what the book says rather than just reading it.</note> This immortal man, remaining in this practice until the end, left a name of ever-blooming memory to all that surrounding region. He himself now reaps eternal joy in the Kingdom of Heaven, receiving the worthy prizes of his blessed labors. Having fed the hungry here and granted clothing to the naked, he now enjoys every kind of nourishment from the **Rewarder of good works**.

---

->### Concerning the Virgin who was Deceived,###<-

A certain ascetic virgin, living with two other virgins, practiced her discipline for nine or ten years. ### Concerning the Devout Man in Ancyra.

Likewise, in this same city, we found a monk who had refused to accept ordination to the priesthood. He had previously served as a <term>military commander</term> <note>original: "ἡγεμών" (hegemon); a high-ranking official or governor, indicating he left a position of significant worldly power.</note> for a short time—about twenty years. Living his life in asceticism, he became a close friend to the bishop of the city, a most holy man. This monk is so philanthropic and merciful that he even enters into the private inner rooms of homes to show mercy to those in need. He neglects no one: neither those in prison, nor in the hospital, nor the destitute, nor the poor; rather, he assists everyone. 

To the rich, he offers rebukes for their lack of compassion, speaking of mercy to those who are pitiless and unmerciful. For those in want, he personally sees to the specific needs of each individual. He brings peace to those who are fighting, provides clothing for the naked, and offers <term>remedies</term> <note>original: "ἀλεξητήρια" (alexeteria); amulets or medicines used to ward off disease or misfortune.</note> to the sick for their healing. 

As is common in all great cities, it is the same in this one: in the <term>portico</term> <note>original: "στοᾷ" (stoa); a covered walkway or porch attached to the church where the homeless and disabled often sought shelter.</note> of the church, a great multitude of the maimed lie together, gathering their daily food; some are single, while others are married. It happened, then, on one of these days during the night, that the wife of one of these men began to give birth in the portico, and this occurred during the winter. While she was crying out, pierced by that unbearable pain, this blessed man heard her wailing while he was praying in the church. Leaving his usual...

## Concerning Magna.

Among these women, Magna excels in piety, a most venerable and highly esteemed woman. I do not know what I should call her—whether a virgin or a widow. For though she was joined to a husband by force by her mother, she nevertheless enticed him with various postponements, pretending to have physical illnesses. She remained untouched and uninitiated in the <term>defilement</term> <note>original: "μύσους" (mysous); in this context, Palladius uses a strong word for physical intimacy to emphasize Magna's commitment to chastity despite her legal marriage.</note>, as her household members say. After her husband died a very short time later, she became the sole heir of all his property. Having exchanged temporal things for the eternal, she thereafter offered her whole self to God, caring to the utmost for the life that remains forever. Presiding with dignity over her own servants, she lived a most <term>ascetic</term> <note>original: "ἀσκητικώτατον" (asketikotaton); the most disciplined or rigorous way of life.</note> life full of temperance, maintaining such precise conduct that even the most high-ranking bishops respected her silence out of an excess of reverence. She consumed her great and superfluous material wealth with the fire of <term>voluntary poverty</term> <note>original: "ἀκτημοσύνης" (aktemosynes); the practice of owning nothing, a core monastic virtue.</note>; and her surplus means she dedicated to the service of monasteries, the poor, <term>hostels</term> <note>original: "ξενοδοχείοις" (xenodocheiois); early Christian institutions established to care for travelers, the sick, and the poor.</note>, churches, the needy, those traveling through, bishops, and orphans,

### Concerning Severianus.

**I**n Ancyra of Galatia, in that very city, I met a certain Severianus, a most illustrious man of the rank of <term>Count</term> <note>original: "ἀπὸ κόμητος" (apo komētos); 'comes' or 'count' was a high-ranking title in the Roman imperial administration. The prefix 'apo' often indicates a former or retired official.</note>, along with his free-born wife, Bosporia. I had a brief acquaintance with them. They were so filled with good hopes for the future that they even planned for their children’s lives by looking toward things to come. For they spent the revenues of their estates on those in need, even though they had four sons and two daughters, to whom they gave not even a single <term>vine-branch</term> <note>original: "κλῆμα" (klēma); here used to illustrate that they did not grant their children control over the productive assets of their lands during their lifetime.</note> of their low-lying vineyards.

They said to the rest of their children: "After our passing, everything is yours. But while we still live, we ourselves shall collect the fruits of the estates, distributing them to churches, monasteries, guesthouses, and to all those in need." Among their deeds, this virtuous act also stood out: when a great famine occurred and all the country-folk were acting heartlessly, they then brought the <term>heresies</term> <note>The author refers to various Christian sects outside the "Orthodox" or mainstream imperial church, which were common in the region of Galatia.</note> in that region over to <term>orthodoxy</term>. For in many villages they opened their own granaries and provided food for the hungry. Because of their unspeakable <term>philanthropy</term> <note>original: "φιλανθρωπίας" (philanthrōpias); in this context, it refers specifically to the love of humanity shown through practical charity.</note>, the heretical groups came together into a single harmony of the right faith, glorifying God for the couple’s simple faith and immeasurable kindness.

Their other <term>discipline</term> <note>original: "ἄσκησις" (askēsis); the root of the word 'asceticism,' referring to the spiritual training of the soul and body.</note> was also marvelous: an exceedingly venerable appearance, simplicity of dress, and a great, inexpressible frugality in their lifestyle. They were very sparing in their spending, using only enough simple food as was necessary to stay alive, practicing a supernatural <term>self-control</term> before God. They spent most of their time living in the countryside, conversing most happily with <term>quietude</term> <note>original: "ἡσυχίᾳ" (hēsychia); a key term in early Christian spirituality meaning inner stillness, silence, or contemplation.</note>. They were always fleeing the cities and the vices that hang about them, lest they ever [be pulled away] by the [social] inter-

### Concerning a Certain Magistrate’s Agent.

**A** young man who was an <term>agent of the Master of Offices</term> <note>original: "μαγισριανὸς" (magisterianos); these were members of the *agentes in rebus*, an elite corps of imperial messengers and secret police who served under the *Magister Officiorum*.</note>, beautiful in appearance and pious in spirit, was filled by God with such a fiery spiritual zeal that he even looked down upon death. In the guise of debauchery, he went late in the evening to the man who maintained these women <note>The keeper of a brothel.</note> and gave him five <term>gold coins</term> <note>original: "νομίσματα" (nomismata); the standard gold coin of the later Roman Empire, also known as a *solidus*.</note>, saying to him: "Permit me to stay this night with this girl." 

Having entered the private room with her, he said to her: "Rise up, save yourself." He stripped her of her garments and dressed her in his own clothing—in the <term>tunics</term> <note>original: "καμισίοις" (kamisiois); linen shirts or under-garments.</note>, the <term>official cloak</term> <note>original: "χλαμύδι" (chlamydi); the chlamys was a distinctive military and administrative cloak that denoted his high social status.</note>, and all the men’s attire. He said to her: "Cover yourself with the edge of the cloak and go out." She did so, sealing herself entirely with the mystery of the cross; she went out from that place untouched and completely undefiled, saved by the grace of Christ and by the agency of the young man, who by his own blood delivered her from dishonorable corruption. 

The next day, the drama was discovered, and the agent was brought before a most beastly judge. After the cruel man had interrogated this noble athlete of Christ and learned everything, he ordered him to be thrown to the wild beasts. Thus, even in this, the <term>hater of good</term> <note>original: "μισόκαλος δαίμων" (misokalos daimōn); a common Greek Christian epithet for the Devil, literally "the demon who hates beauty" or "excellence."</note> was put to shame. For while the demon thought he would wrap this brave man in an unlawful punishment, he instead revealed him to be a double martyr of Christ: one who competed nobly for his own immortal soul, and one who endured the labors of an athlete for the sake of that celebrated and blessed woman. Therefore, he was deemed worthy of double honor from Christ and of celebrated and blessed crowns from the Divine Mercy of that time.

<summary>This passage tells the story of a "Magisterianus" (imperial agent) who sacrifices his life to save a woman from a brothel by switching clothes with her. He is subsequently martyred by being thrown to wild beasts, earning him the title of a "double martyr."</summary>
<keywords>Magisterianus, martyrdom, self-sacrifice, chastity, agentes in rebus, hagiography, Palladius, rescue</keywords>

...thoughts. When she was brought before the bloodthirsty executioner <note>original: "μιαιφόνῳ" (miaiphono); literally "blood-stained" or "murderous," referring here to the cruel official or judge.</note>, he went even further into madness through his debauchery. When this profane man, after having used every engine of persuasion against her, was still unable to convince the woman of God, he subjected this lady to various tortures. 

When he failed even in these attempts, being unable to wrench her away from her confession of Christ, the savage man, driven to a frenzy against her, then delivered her to such a punishment: stationing the virtuous woman in a brothel <note>original: "πορνεῖον" (porneion); a house of prostitution.</note>, he commanded its manager, saying: "Take this woman, and bring me three gold coins <note>original: "νομίσματα" (nomismata); the standard gold solidus of the late Roman Empire.</note> from her earnings every day." 

The man, who collected the gold from this shameful business, exposed her to any who wished in that workshop of pollution. When the "woman-hawks" <note>original: "γυναικοΐέρακες" (gunaikoierakes); a vivid Greek term for predatory or profligate men who "prey" on women like hawks.</note> who haunt such places learned of this, they frequented that lawless workshop, paying the fee for her dishonorable labor and wishing to consort with her for her ruin. 

But this most venerable woman, turning their words into a ruse to deceive them, begged and entreated them, saying: "I have a certain sore <note>original: "ἕλκος" (helkos); an ulcer or wound.</note> in a hidden place, which smells most foully; and I fear that you might come to loathe me because of the repulsiveness of the ulcer. Grant me, therefore, a few days, and then you shall have power over me, and you may even have me for free." By these words, the blessed woman persuaded those profligates and sent them away for the time being. 

Having entreated God with most intense prayers, she moved Him to compassion with her heartfelt supplications. For God, who possesses foreknowledge and knows the thoughts of the heart, seeing how truly the virtuous woman cared for her purity <note>original: "ἁγνείας" (hagneias); referring to her chastity or holy state.</note>, the Guardian of the salvation of all mankind provided a remedy in those days. ### About Juliana.

There was a certain virgin named Juliana in Caesarea <note>original: "Καισαρείᾳ" (Kaisareia); Caesarea in Cappadocia (modern-day Turkey), a major center of early Christian learning.</note> of Cappadocia, who was said to be the most learned of women. She received Origen <note>original: "Ωριγένην" (Oregenen); Origen of Alexandria (c. 184–253 AD), one of the most influential and prolific early Christian theologians and scholars.</note> the author when he was fleeing the uprising of the Greeks <note>original: "Ἑλλήνων" (Hellenon); here referring to pagans or practitioners of traditional Roman religion during a period of anti-Christian violence.</note>. She hid him for two years, supporting him at her own expense and refreshing the man through her own personal service. I found these things written in a very old book of verses which had been written by the hand of Origen himself. This book had been kept hidden by the virgin Juliana in Caesarea; she said that she had received it from Symmachus <note>original: "Συμμάχου" (Symmachou); Symmachus the Ebionite, an important 2nd-century translator of the Old Testament into Greek.</note>, the translator of the Jews. I have not included these virtues of these renowned women without purpose; rather, it is so we may learn that there are many ways to gain spiritual profit if we are willing to learn from various persons.

### Narrative of Hippolytus, the acquaintance of the apostles.

In another small book inscribed with the name of Hippolytus <note>original: "Ἱππολύτου" (Hippolytou); likely Hippolytus of Rome, an early 3rd-century Christian writer and martyr.</note>, the acquaintance of the apostles, I found a story such as this. There was a most noble and beautiful virgin in the city of Corinth who was training herself in the virtuous life. At that time, during the period of the persecutors, someone slandered her before the judge of that day, who was a Greek <note>A pagan Roman official.</note>, claiming that she blasphemed the times, the emperors, and the idols. Having first described her beauty, these "hucksters of such matters" <note>original: "κάπηλοι" (kapeloi); literally retailers or tavern-keepers, used here derisively for those who trade in gossip or scandal to pique the interest of officials.</note> reported it to that godless judge, who was mad for women. He eagerly received the slander with "horse-like ears" <note>original: "ἱππικοῖς ὠτίοις" (hippikois otiois); a metaphor for ears that are pricked up or intensely attentive, often with a connotation of animalistic or unrestrained passion.</note> and with lustful thoughts.

# LAUSIAC HISTORY 151

...or a cleric, nor to any other of his acquaintances. But when the imperial officials <note>original: "ἐπαρχικῶν" (eparchikōn); refers to the agents or soldiers of the Roman Prefect.</note> suddenly entered the Bishop's residence and were searching for him, he took for himself his tunic <term>sticharion (a long, straight liturgical robe)</term> and his cloak <term>birrhion (a short, hooded cape)</term>, and in the dead of night, he fled to this virgin. She, being astonished by the event, was at a loss. The Bishop then said to her:

"Since I am being sought by the Arians <note>Arianism: a theological movement that denied the full divinity of Jesus Christ, which Athanasius famously spent his life opposing.</note> who are bringing unlawful slanders against me; so that I might not bear an irrational reputation and lead those who wish to punish me into sin, I have for this reason decided to flee. God revealed to me this night that I can be saved by no other than by you." 

Filled with great joy, she cast aside all worldly reasoning and devoted herself entirely to the Lord. She eagerly hid the most holy Bishop for six years, until the end of the life of King Constantius <note>Constantius II (reigned 337–361 AD), the son of Constantine the Great, who actively supported Arianism and frequently exiled Athanasius.</note>. She herself washed his feet, carried away his waste, and managed all his other needs. She also borrowed and provided books for him; and not a single person in all of Alexandria knew where the blessed Bishop Athanasius was staying. 

When the death of King Constantius was announced and reached his ears, he dressed himself with dignity and order, and was found in the church again during the night. When everyone saw him, they were astonished, having received him back as if he were living from among the dead. At the same time, his friends blamed the heresy <note>Arianism</note> for his disappearance and for their own ignorance of his whereabouts. 

The blessed man then explained to his true friends and acquaintances: "I did not flee to you for this reason: so that you might be able to keep your oath <note>An oath that they did not know where he was, thus protecting them from legal repercussions for harboring a fugitive.</note>. Furthermore, it was because of the search. I fled to her, toward whom no one could have any suspicion because of her beauty—myself a young man going to a young woman. Seeking to secure two things—"

# PALLADIUS
### Page 150

### On Pammachius.

A kinsman of these people, named Pammachius <note>Pammachius (c. 340–410 AD) was a Roman senator and a friend of Saint Jerome. He is famous for converting his house on the Caelian Hill into a church (the Titulus Pammachii, now Santi Giovanni e Paolo).</note>, who was a former proconsul <term>anthypatos (the Roman governor of a province)</term>, likewise renounced the world and lived the restricted life <note>original: "ὁριστὸν βίον" (horiston bion); refers to the disciplined, structured life of a monk or ascetic.</note>. While he was living, he distributed all his wealth, and at his death, he left whatever remained to the poor, and so departed to the Lord.

### On Macarius and Constantine.

Similarly, there was a man named Macarius, a former vicar <term>vicarius (a high-ranking imperial administrative official)</term>, and Constantine, who served as an assessor to the prefects of Italy. Both were distinguished and most learned men who reached the heights of love for God. I believe they are still living in the flesh even now, practicing the immaterial rule of the way of life <note>The "immaterial rule" refers to living a spiritual, ascetic existence that prioritizes the soul over bodily needs.</note>, awaiting the blessed and incorruptible life.

### On the virgin who received the blessed Athanasius.

I know of a certain virgin in Alexandria, whom I encountered when she was about seventy years old. The entire clergy bore witness to her, saying that when she was young—about twenty years of age—and exceedingly beautiful, she was avoided by virtuous men because of that beauty, so that she might not, even by suspicion, give cause for any blemish on her reputation.

Now, it happened that the blessed Athanasius, the Bishop of Alexandria, was being plotted against by the Arians <note>Arianism was a major theological dispute in the early Church; Arians believed that Jesus was a created being, whereas Athanasius championed the view that he was "of the same substance" as God the Father.</note> through Eusebius, the Chamberlain <term>praepositus (a high-ranking palace official, often the Grand Chamberlain)</term> at that time under Emperor Constantius. They were bringing unlawful accusations and slandering him.

Therefore, the blessed man, fleeing from being judged by a corrupt tribunal, did not trust himself to anyone—neither to a relative, nor to a friend, nor to a cler—

...saving her from the mouth of the lions <note>original: "στόματος τῶν λεόντων"; a biblical allusion to Daniel, here referring to the dangers of the world or the Gothic invasion.</note>. Regarding Alaric <note>Alaric I, King of the Visigoths, who sacked Rome in 410 AD. Melania famously escaped the city just before this event.</note>, she remained safe through her own faith. They also freed those slaves who desired it, numbering eight thousand. The rest did not wish to be freed, but rather refused, preferring to serve her brother, to whom she had yielded them. Except for [a nominal price of] three gold coins <term>nomismata (the standard gold coin of the later Roman Empire)</term>, she sold all her estates in the Spains, Aquitaine, Tarraconensis, and the Gauls. Such was the wisdom of this most God-beloved woman, Melania the Younger, who possessed a divinely-inspired and mature mind regarding the material burden of wealth.

Her ascetic discipline <term>askesis (spiritual training through self-denial)</term> is as follows: she eats only every other day. At the beginning of her journey, she would even go five days without food, setting herself to work alongside her former slave-girls, whom she had made into fellow-ascetics. She also has her mother, Albina, living with her, who practices the same discipline and is likewise distributing her own wealth privately. They now live in the countryside, sometimes in Sicily and sometimes in Campania, accompanied by fifteen eunuchs, as well as virgins and female servants.

### On Pinianus <note>The Greek text here reads "Oppianos," but the historical figure and husband of Melania the Younger was Pinianus.</note>.

Likewise, Pinianus, who was then her husband but is now her like-minded partner in virtue, practices asceticism with thirty monks. He spends his time reading the holy scriptures and occupying himself in the garden and in holy conversation. They showed no small honor to us (being many in number) when we went to Rome on account of the blessed Bishop John <note>Saint John Chrysostom. Palladius, the author, traveled to Rome in 405 AD to appeal to the Pope on Chrysostom's behalf.</note>; they gave us rest in a guest-house and provided very generous supplies for our journey. With great joy, they reap the fruit of eternal life through these God-given works of the most excellent way of living.

### Page 148

...distaste for marriage <note>original: "σις τοῦ γάμου"; likely part of "ἀποστάσις" (distancing) or "κόρεσις" (satiety/distaste)</note> drove her, as it were, to say to her own husband Pinianus—the son of Severus, a former prefect—the following: "If you choose to dwell with me according to the principle of chastity <term>sophrosyne (the virtue of moderation and, in this context, sexual renunciation within marriage)</term>, I shall acknowledge you as both the master and lord of my life. But if this seems too heavy a burden for you as a young man, take all my possessions and set only my body free, so that I may satisfy my desire for God. In this way, I may become the heir to the divine virtue of my grandmother, whose name I also bear <note>Melania the Younger was named after her grandmother, Melania the Elder (c. 325–410 AD), a famous desert mother and foundress.</note>. For if God had wished us to live in this world and enjoy the fruits of it, He would not have taken my children from me by an untimely death."

For a long time they struggled against one another <note>original: "ζυγομαχησάντων"; literally "battling under the yoke," a metaphor for the struggles of a married couple.</note>, but later, God took pity on the young man and instilled in him a zeal for godliness, leading him to renounce all the material things of the world. Thus was the saying of the Apostle fulfilled: *For how do you know, wife, whether you will save your husband? Or how do you know, husband, whether you will save your wife?* <note>A quotation from 1 Corinthians 7:16.</note> Having been married at thirteen years of age and having lived with her husband for seven years, she renounced the world in her twentieth year. 

First, she gave all her silk half-garment coverings to the altars <note>Silk was an immense luxury in the 4th century; donating it to churches for altar cloths was a profound act of divestment.</note>. The most venerable Olympias <note>Olympias the Deaconess (c. 361–408 AD), a wealthy widow and patron of John Chrysostom who likewise gave away a massive fortune.</note> had also done this. As for her remaining silk garments, she cut them up and made them into various church furnishings. Entrusting her silver and gold to a certain Paul, a presbyter, and the monk Dalmatius, she sent them by sea to the East: ten thousand gold coins <term>nomismata (the standard gold coin of the late Roman Empire)</term> to Egypt and the Thebaid; fifteen thousand coins to Palestine; and ten thousand coins to the churches in the islands and those in exile. To the churches in the West, the monasteries, the hostels <term>xenodocheia (charitable guesthouses for pilgrims, the poor, and the sick)</term>, and to all those in need, she provided for them personally, contributing four times as much as those previous amounts, as if before God... <note>The text breaks off here at the start of a word.</note>

### Concerning Melania the Younger.

Since I previously promised to recount the story of Melania's descendant <note>Melania the Younger (383–439 AD), the granddaughter of Melania the Elder. ->Concerning the virgin Tawor.<-

## Concerning the vainglorious anchorite.

Near these people, we saw another anchorite <term>anchorite (a person who has retired into a solitary life for religious reasons)</term>, who likewise lived in caves. This man, stung by the gadfly of vainglory <note>original: "κενοδοξία" (*kenodoxia*); literally "empty glory" or vanity, considered a grave spiritual danger in monastic thought as it replaces devotion to God with a desire for human praise.</note>, was mocked by dreams and would boast to those who came to him and were deceived. He had become a fool, as it is written, taking flight through his dreams, "shepherding the winds and chasing shadows." <note>A reference to Proverbs 9:12 in the Septuagint version of the Bible, used here to describe the futility of seeking spiritual meaning in vanity.</note> While he possessed chastity in his body—both due to his advanced age and the long passage of time, and perhaps even because of his vainglory—he had lost his mind with respect to virtue, having been corrupted by that impure vanity and having run aground while following the religious life.

## Concerning Amata.

In this city of Antinoë, there are twelve monasteries for women who lead the most excellent life. Among them, I met a servant of God named Amata <note>original: "Ἀματαλίδι" (*Amatalidi*). In many translations, she is known as Amma Amata or Talis.</note>, an old woman who had spent eighty years in ascetic practice <term>askesis (the practice of severe self-discipline and abstention from all forms of indulgence)</term>, as both she and her neighbors testified. Living with her were sixty young virgins, who purely completed the race of asceticism under the teaching of this venerable woman <note>original: "καλογήρου" (*kalogerou*); literally "good old woman," a term of respect for a nun or female elder.</note>. They all loved her so deeply that no lock was ever placed on the doors of that monastery, unlike in others. Instead, they were all held together by this immeasurable love and by her divine teaching, being transformed into a state of incorruption. She advanced to such a degree of passionlessness <term>apatheia (in early Christian thought, a state of mind in which one is no longer dominated by worldly desires or "passions," leading to spiritual peace)</term>...

### Concerning Diocles.

This Diocles was first led by the study of grammar <note>original: "γραμματικῆς" (*grammatikēs*); referring to the classical education in literature and language common for the era.</note>, but later gave himself over to philosophical pursuits; in time, grace drew him toward the heavenly Philosophy <note>original: "οὐράνιον Φιλοσοφίαν" (*ouranion Philosophian*); in early monastic literature, the "heavenly philosophy" refers to the ascetic life and the pursuit of divine wisdom rather than secular academic study.</note>. Thus, in the twenty-eighth year of his age, he renounced his worldly education and joined himself to the heavenly teachings of Christ. He himself spent thirty-five years living in the caves. 

He used to say to us: "The mind that departs from the contemplation <term>contemplation (Greek: *theoria*; a deep, prayerful focus on the divine presence)</term> of God in its thoughts becomes either a demon or a beast." When we inquired after the meaning of what he said, he spoke thus: "When the human mind withdraws from the contemplation of God, it necessarily falls either into the demon of desire, which leads to licentiousness, or it falls into the evil spirit of anger, from which irrational impulses are born." 

He further claimed that licentious desire was "beastly," while anger was a "demonic movement." When I argued back, asking how it is possible for the human mind to be with God without ceasing, he replied: "In whatever thought or matter the soul happens to be, it is with God only when it is occupied by a pious and divine reasoning."

He completed fifty years in the caves...

### Concerning Candida.

Following after her [the previous subject] and in a similar manner, the blessed Candida lived worthily of the Lord. She was the daughter of Trajan the general <note>original: "στρατηλάτου" (*stratēlatou*); a high-ranking military commander or general in the Late Roman administrative system.</note>. She reached the highest peak of modesty, adorned churches with sound judgment, and showed such reverence to bishops as was worthy of the mysteries of Christ <note>Refers to the sacraments, particularly the Eucharist.</note>. She honored all the clergy of Christ in a fitting manner. Having catechized her own daughter, she sent her forward as a gift to Christ into the state of virginity—the fruit of her own womb. Later, she herself followed her daughter in temperance, purity, and the distribution of her wealth to the poor.

I myself know this woman, for she would labor through the entire night, grinding grain with her own hands to make the bread for the offering <note>original: "ἄρτον τῆς προσκομιδῆς" (*arton tēs proskomidēs*); the bread prepared specifically for the celebration of the Eucharist.</note>. She did this to break down the physical power of her body, explaining: "Since fasting alone is not sufficient, I give it the ally of painful sleeplessness, so that I may destroy the arrogance of Esau" <note>A biblical reference to Esau (Genesis 25), who chose to satisfy his hunger rather than keep his birthright; in early monastic tradition, "Esau" symbolizes the impulsive appetites of the flesh and pride.</note>. She strictly abstained from the meat of animals and anything with life, though she would eat fish, oil, and vegetables—and even these only on feast days. For all other times, she spent her life content with a drink of water mixed with vinegar <note>original: "ὀξυκραμάτῳ" (*oxykramatō*); a humble drink similar to the "posca" given to Roman soldiers, used by monks to quench thirst without the pleasure of wine.</note> and dry bread. Having now fallen asleep after such a life of austerity <term>austerity (Greek: *sklēragōgia*; literally "hard-leading," referring to a disciplined and rigorous lifestyle intended to strengthen the soul over the body)</term>, this illustrious woman now enjoys the eternal blessings prepared for those who have loved the virtuous life.

...she surpassed even the innocence of children. For no fault was ever found in this **Christ-bearer** <note>original: "Χριστοφόρῳ" (*Christophorō*); a title given to a person perceived to be "carrying" or indwelt by Christ due to their holiness.</note>, nor among her neighbors. Instead, her entire life—lived as though she were already departed from this world—was spent in heartfelt compunction and a frequent flow of tears. One would sooner see a spring cease its flow during the summer heat than see her measureless eyes, which were always fixed upon Christ, ever cease their weeping.

And why do I delay? For however much I permit my mind the leisure to recount the struggles and virtues of this steadfast <note>original: "πεπετρωμένης" (*pepetromenēs*); literally "made of stone," here meaning a soul made firm and unshakeable against temptation.</note> soul, my words will be found to fall far behind her actual deeds. Let no one think I am using flowery or affected speech to describe this most dispassionate woman, who was a precious vessel of the Holy Spirit: **Olympias**. I have been an eyewitness to her, beholding the life and angelic conduct of this blessed woman.

Being a genuine spiritual friend and closer than any relative... she no longer concerned herself with the things of the flesh. She remained subject to rulers and yielded to authorities. She bowed before bishops, spoke with reverence of the **presbytery** <note>original: "πρεσβυτέριον" (*presbyterion*); the body of elders or priests within the church hierarchy.</note>, and honored the entire clergy. She was also deemed worthy of the title of **Confessor** <note>original: "ὁμολογίας" (*homologias*); in the early church, a Confessor was someone who suffered for their faith—through imprisonment, exile, or legal accusations—but was not actually executed as a martyr.</note> for the sake of the truth, having endured a storm of untimely and unjust accusations. Her life is ranked among the female confessors by all the pious people who inhabit the **City of Constantine** <note>Constantinople.</note>. She risked death itself in her struggles for God.

Having ended her life in these trials, she attained blessed glory. In the infinite age, she now dances wearing her crown, dwelling forever in the pure mansions alongside divine souls like her own, and with **boldness** <note>original: "παῤῥησίας" (*parrēsias*); a term for the "freedom of speech" or confidence a holy person has when speaking to God.</note> requesting the rewards for her good works from the Master God.

This blessed woman leaped to the furthest boundary of **humility** <note>original: "ταπεινοφροσύνης" (*tapeinophrosynēs*); a central monastic virtue involving the rejection of pride and social status.</note>, to a point where it was impossible to find anything further. Hers was a life free from vainglory. A character without pretense. An unadorned appearance. A body celebrated in song. A mind without vanity. A humble opinion of herself. An untroubled heart. She endured many trials through the activity of the one who loves evil and is shameless <note>original: "ἐθελοκάκου καὶ ἀπροκάλου" (*ethelokakou kai aprokalou*); a reference to the Devil as the source of malice and brazen temptation.</note>. With all reverence, she bowed before the holy bishops, revered the **presbytery** <note>The body of elders or priests within the church.</note>, honored the clergy, respected the ascetic life, and welcomed virginity. 

She supported the widows, raised the orphans, championed the elderly, visited the sick, mourned with sinners, guided the lost, and showed mercy to all, pitying the poor without reserve. Having instructed many unbelieving women in the faith and aided them toward eternal life, she adorned her entire life with a name for goodness that is remembered forever. 

By calling back a swarm of ten thousand servants from slavery and granting them freedom, she declared them equal in status to her own noble birth. Or rather, if the truth must be told, they appeared even more noble in their outward form than this holy woman. For nothing cheaper than her own garments could be found; the coverings of this courageous woman <note>original: "ἀνδρείας" (*andreias*); while meaning "manliness," this was a standard technical term in Greek hagiography for a woman who exhibited spiritual fortitude and "masculine" strength of character.</note> were less worthy even than those worn by the most ragged beggars. <header>SIXTEENTH CHAPTER OF THE LAUSIAC HISTORY</header>

...and of Stephen, and of Basil, and of certain other most diligent men—reading twenty-five myriads <note>original: "μυριάδας εἴκοσι πέντε" (*myriadas eikosi pente*). A "myriad" is ten thousand; here it likely refers to the number of lines or verses read across various theological works.</note>. She did not pass through these writings simply or by chance, but with great labor, having gone through each book seven or eight times. Therefore, she was able to be liberated from "knowledge falsely so-called" <note>original: "ψευδωνύμου γνώσεως" (*pseudonymou gnōseōs*). This is a direct quote from 1 Timothy 6:20, used by early Christians to distinguish between true faith and the "false knowledge" of Gnostic groups.</note> and take flight toward the grace of God. Indeed, by the power of the divine words of the Spirit, she transformed herself through good hopes into a spiritual bird, and having passed through the gloom of this life, she flew up to Christ to receive from Him the immortal rewards.

### About Olympias.

Following in the footsteps of this woman, and according to every virtue of the god-inspired spiritual way of life, was the most venerable Olympias. She was most zealous for the road leading to heaven, following the wisdom of the divine scriptures in all things. She was the daughter, according to the flesh, of Seleucus the former count <note>original: "Σελεύκου τοῦ ἀπὸ κόμητος" (*Seleukou tou apo komētos*). A *Comes* or Count was a high-ranking official in the late Roman imperial court.</note>, but a true child of God according to the spirit. She was the granddaughter of Ablabius the former prefect, and the bride for a few days of Nebridius, the former prefect of the city of Constantine <note>Constantinople.</note>. But in truth, she was the wife of no man. For she is said to have fallen asleep as an unblemished virgin, having lived as the companion of the Divine Word, a companion of all true humility, and a partner and servant to all those in need.

Having scattered all that infinite and immeasurable wealth of hers, she assisted everyone simply and without distinction. For no city, no region, no desert, no island, and no remote corner remained without a share of the gifts of this celebrated woman. Instead, she provided for churches for their sacred offerings, for monasteries, for communal houses, for almshouses, for prisons, and for those in exile—simply scattering her charity <note>original: "ἐλεημοσύνης" (*eleēmosynēs*); the root of our English word "alms," referring to acts of mercy and financial relief.</note> across the whole world...

<summary>This page concludes the description of Melania the Elder's extensive theological studies and introduces Saint Olympias, detailing her noble lineage and her transition from a brief marriage into a life of perpetual virginity and universal philanthropy.</summary>
<keywords>Olympias, Melania the Elder, Seleucus, Ablabius, Nebridius, Constantinople, asceticism, virginity, charity, Christian history</keywords>

### Concerning Salvia.

At that time it happened that we were traveling together from Aelia <note>original: "Αἰλίας" (*Ailias*). This is Aelia Capitolina, the Roman name for Jerusalem.</note> to Egypt, escorting the blessed Salvia, the virgin lady, and sister of Rufinus the former Prefect <note>original: "Ῥεφίνου τοῦ ἀπὸ ὑπάρχων" (*Rephinou tou apo hyparchōn*). This refers to Flavius Rufinus, a high-ranking official and Praetorian Prefect under the Emperor Theodosius I.</note>. Among those with us was Jubinus, who was then a Deacon, but has now become Bishop of the church of Ascalon; he is a devout man and a lover of learning <note>original: "Φιλόλογος" (*Philologos*). In this context, it refers to someone devoted to study and literature.</note>. When a very intense heat overtook us and we arrived at Pelusium <note>A major Egyptian city and port on the eastern edge of the Nile Delta.</note>, it happened that Jubinus, taking a washbasin, washed his hands and feet with very cold water. After washing, he lay down to rest upon a pallet <note>original: "θερμοτύλῳ" (*thermotylō*), a type of cushion or mattress.</note> thrown upon the floor.

The blessed Salvia stood over him and, like a wise mother caring for her true son, she mocked him for the softness of his body, saying to him: "How do you dare treat your body this way at your age? While your blood is still boiling, do you so beautify your little flesh <note>original: "σαρκίον" (*sarkion*), a diminutive term for 'flesh' used by ascetics to show disdain for the physical body.</note> that you do not perceive the harms born from it? Take heart," she said, "my child; for I am now in my sixtieth year, and except for the tips of my fingers—and that only for the sake of communion <note>original: "κοινωνίαν" (*koinōnian*). This refers to the physical contact required to receive the Holy Eucharist.</note>—neither my face, nor my feet, nor any of my limbs have touched water. Although I have been seized by various illnesses and forced by doctors to use a bath, I could not bear to give in to the habit of the flesh. I have never slept upon a bed, nor have I ever traveled in a litter <note>A portable couch or chair carried by servants.</note>."

This woman was most learned; because she loved the divine word, she turned her nights into days, lighting them abundantly with oil lamps while she went through every writing of the ancient commentaries. Among these, she read three million lines <note>original: "μυριάδας τριακοσίας" (*myriadas triakosias*). Literally 'three hundred myriads.' A myriad is ten thousand; this refers to the 'stichoi' or standard units of measurement for ancient Greek texts.</note> of Origen, and the works of Gregory and Pierius...

...of her relatives; but the servant of Christ <note>This refers to Melania the Elder, a prominent noblewoman who became a desert mother.</note> said to them: "Children, it was written four hundred years ago that it is the last hour <note>original: "ἐσχάτη ὥρα" (*eschatē hōra*). A reference to the First Epistle of John (2:18), used here to argue that the end of the world is near.</note>. Why then do you cling to the vanity of life? For fear that the days of the Antichrist might arrive, and you may not be able to enjoy your wealth and your ancestral property." And by these words, having set them all free, she led them to the monastic life.

Having also instructed the younger Publicola <note>The son of Melania the Elder.</note>, she brought him to Sicily, after selling off all her remaining belongings. And having received the proceeds, she returned again to Jerusalem. After distributing the funds within forty days, she fell asleep <note>A traditional Christian way of saying she died.</note> in a good old age and honorable memory, adorned with the deepest gentleness by her almsgiving, and refined by the fruit of her good works. She also left behind a monastery in Jerusalem and the funds for the monastery's maintenance.

When all those who had been instructed by the blessed Melania had departed from Rome, a certain Barbarian storm <note>This refers to the Sack of Rome by Alaric the Goth in 410 AD.</note>, which had been foretold by the prophets of old, fell upon Rome. It did not even spare the bronze statues in the forum, but having plundered everything with Barbarian madness, it delivered the city to destruction. Thus Rome, which had been beautified over twelve hundred years <note>The Roman tradition dated the city's founding to 753 BC; it had stood for nearly 12 centuries before this sack.</note>, became a ruin.

Then those who had been instructed by her rejoiced that they had not fallen alongside the incurable evils of the pillaging, glorifying God for this most excellent change of circumstances and for their miraculous <note>original: "παραδόξῳ" (*paradoxō*). Meaning "beyond expectation" or marvelous; the translator emphasizes that their escape seemed like a miracle.</note> salvation. This very outcome convinced the unbelievers that while all others were taken captive, these houses alone were saved—having become a whole burnt offering to the Lord through the renowned and immortal Melania, who, through the salvation offered by Him, had transferred all their possessions away from the present time <note>i.e., by giving their wealth to the church/poor, they "stored up treasures in heaven" before the earthly gold could be stolen by invaders.</note>.
