...the funds of her son and his guardians [were provided to her] every year, as if they were supplying oil to a bright lamp. Lighting the flame with the bright light of almsgiving, she enlightened everyone with her cheerful giving. Having persevered for so long in her life abroad <note>original: "ξενιτείᾳ" (*xeniteia*). A technical term in early Christian monasticism for living as a stranger or pilgrim in a foreign land for the sake of God.</note>, she did not acquire even a span of earth. For she was not drawn away from her longing for the desert by her affection for her son; the love for her only son did not divide her from her love for Christ. But through the prayers of this holy woman, her son <note>Publicola, the son of Melania the Elder.</note> also reached the peak of education and nobility of character. He achieved marriage and high standing in this world, becoming quite renowned. He also attained worldly honors and possessed the legal right to two children.

Therefore, after many years, when this most venerable woman heard about the situation of her granddaughter <note>Melania the Younger.</note>—that she had married and intended to renounce the world—she feared they might be hedged in by false teaching, or heresy, or an evil way of life. Although she was a sixty-year-old elder, she cast herself onto a ship. Sailing from Caesarea, she arrived in Rome within twenty days. Meeting those who were there, she instructed the most blessed and noteworthy, the most illustrious Apronianus—who was a pagan <note>original: "Ἕλληνα" (*Hellēna*). In the context of the 4th and 5th centuries, the term "Greek" was often used to denote a practitioner of traditional Greco-Roman religion (a pagan) rather than an ethnic identity.</note>—and made him a Christian. She also persuaded him to practice continence <note>original: "ἐγκρατεύεσθαι" (*enkrateuesthai*). This refers to the ascetic practice where a married couple remains together but abstains from sexual relations.</note> with his own wife, who was the niece of this blessed woman and was named Albina.

She also strengthened her own granddaughter, Melania the Younger, along with her husband, Pinianus. She also instructed Albina, her daughter-in-law and the wife of her son <note>Historical sources suggest Albina was the daughter of Ceionius Rufius Albinus and mother to Melania the Younger; here Palladius focuses on the spiritual kinship created by Melania the Elder's return.</note>. Having prepared all of them to sell off their possessions, she led them out of Rome and guided them toward the venerable and calm harbor of life <note>A common metaphor for the stability and peace of the monastic or ascetic life.</note>. In this way, she fought a spiritual battle against all the senators in Rome and against their noble wives, for the rest of them tried to hinder her in her renunciation...

### On Melania.

Concerning the wonderful and holy Melania, I have briefly recounted things about her above. Nevertheless, I shall now weave into the "variegated robe" <note>A metaphor for the author's collection of diverse stories about different saints.</note> of my discourse the rest—or rather, as much as I can recall—of her virtues, describing the unspeakable kindnesses of this woman most beloved of God. Through these, she wove for herself the blessed garment of incorruption by her own labors, and beautifully forged the unfading crown of glory for her precious head with her own resources. Clothed anew and with great boldness, she departed to God. If I wish to tell all that I know of this blessed woman’s achievements, even time itself will fail me. 

She consumed holy material in her divine passion <note>original: "θεομανίας" (*theomanias*). A term meaning "divine madness" or "frenzy for God," describing her intense spiritual zeal.</note>, seized by a heavenly fervor. I do not think a flame of fire falling upon such an abundance of wood could burn so brightly. It is not for me alone to tell of this fire, but for those who inhabit Persia, Britain, and all the inhabited islands. For regarding the benevolence and gifts of this immortal woman, neither the West, nor the East, nor the North, nor the South remained silent. For having lived as a stranger <note>original: "ξενιτεύσασα" (*xeniteusasa*). To live abroad or as a pilgrim for religious devotion, a central practice in early monasticism.</note> for thirty-seven years, she provided for churches, monasteries, strangers, and those in prison at her own expense. ## On Sabattius.

There was a man named Sabattius, a layman who lived in Jericho in his old age. Although he had a wife, he became such a "lover of monks" <note>original: *Philomonazôn*</note> through his fear of God that he would make the rounds through the cells and the entire desert during the nights. At every monastery, he would secretly leave one peck <term>modios; a Roman dry measure of about 9 liters</term> of dates and a sufficient supply of vegetables, because the ascetics living along the Jordan did not eat bread. One day, while this benefactor of the monks and "provider for those without tables" <note>original: *atrapézos*. This refers to his service providing food to hermits who did not use formal dining tables.</note> was bringing the necessities of the ascetic life to the holy ones, a lion encountered him through the malice of the Evil One. The enemy of the monks <note>A common reference to the Devil.</note> had stirred up the savage beast against him and his ministry, striving to deprive the monks of their relief and Sabattius of his holy purpose. The beast overtook him at a certain landmark <note>original: *seméiou*, likely a monk's cell or a known trail marker.</note>, and with the force of its charge, it shoved him and knocked him down. But He who taught the lions to fast for Daniel’s sake also restrained this lion, though it was violently hungry, from the generous Sabattius. Instead, the lion took the old man’s donkey and departed. For He who granted Sabattius his life also satisfied the hunger of the beast.

---

## On Abrahamius.

There was a certain Abrahamius, a strict monk, Egyptian by birth, who lived a very harsh and holy life in the desert. This man was struck in the mind by extreme conceit <note>original: *oiêsis*. In ascetic literature, this refers to a form of spiritual delusion where a monk becomes "puffed up" by his own progress.</note>. He went into the church and disputed with the elders, saying: "I too was ordained a priest <term>presbyteros; an elder or priest</term> this night by Christ, and you ought to receive me as a priest who is about to perform the liturgy." The holy <insert>fathers</insert> <note>The catchword "πατέρες" (fathers) at the bottom of the page indicates the subject of the next sentence.</note>...

### On Elias.

There was another monk named Elias, a man of very high reputation in those same regions, who dwelt in a cave. Possessing a most venerable and law-abiding life, he devoted himself to self-control and prayer, kindly hosting all those who arrived <note>original: *philophronos pántas xenodochōn*. Elias is depicted practicing *philoxenia* (love of strangers), a core virtue for desert monastics who often lived near pilgrimage routes.</note>. One day, then, when a great many brothers had come to him—for his home was on a passage-way—the supply of bread ran out. Swearing an oath to us, he said: "I almost fainted at the prospect of lacking food for my guests." Having entered into his cell together in great sorrow, wondering how I might fulfill "the measure of love" <note>original: *to tēs agapēs métron*; this refers to the spiritual obligation to provide hospitality and charity to others.</note> for those who had come to me, I found three fresh loaves of bread lying there. Taking them with joy, I set them out. And though twenty men ate until they were satisfied, one loaf still remained. I took that remaining loaf and used it for my own food for twenty-five days. This grace was granted to Elias alongside his hospitality; the rewards for his labors are kept with the Philanthropic Master <note>original: *Philanthrópō despótē*. In a Greek Christian context, "Philanthropos" refers to God’s essential nature as a "Lover of Mankind."</note>.

->Concerning Sisinnios.<-

Sisinnios by name became a disciple of this immortal Elpidius; he was born into the status of a servant <note>original: *oiketikēs... tychēs*; this indicates he was likely born into slavery or a domestic serving class.</note>, but was free according to his faith, and a Cappadocian by birth. For it is necessary to indicate their origins to the glory of Christ, who ennobles us and leads us into the blessed and true nobility of the kingdom of heaven. Having spent a long time with the blessed Elpidius—for Sisinnios was a fervent lover of the virtues of the ascetic life—for six or perhaps seven years, and having thoroughly learned the man's virtues and his endurance in the labors of <term>ascesis: rigorous self-discipline and avoidance of all forms of indulgence, typically for religious reasons</term>, he later shut himself in a tomb for three years. Standing there in the tomb, he continued in prayers, neither by night nor by day sitting down, nor lying down, nor walking outside. Thus he was deemed worthy of a gift against demons. 

Now, having returned to his own country, he was honored with the priesthood, having gathered a brotherhood of both men and women. He gave witness to <term>apatheia: a state of holy detachment where one is no longer ruled by emotional or physical passions</term> through his dignified way of life, having driven the male element of desire from himself and bridled the female element of the women through self-control, so that what is written was fulfilled: "In Christ Jesus there is neither male nor female" <note>A reference to Galatians 3:28.</note>. He is also exceedingly hospitable, even though he possesses nothing, serving as a rebuke to those wealthy people who refuse to share.

This is a metaphor for Elpidius as the spiritual leader of a monastic "hive."</note> who was to come, in the midst of the brotherhood. Then, on one of those days, while a certain brother held a cutting of a vine, the blessed man took it as he was sitting by the edge of the mountain and buried it as if planting. Even though he placed it out of season, it grew so much and became a vine that it shaded the entire church. And Eusebius, his brother of equal honor, who eagerly completed the contests of the life according to Christ. Blessed Elpidius reached such a state of <term>apatheia: a state of holy detachment where one is no longer ruled by passions, emotions, or even physical pain</term>, by wearing down his body, that the sun would shine through the very framework of his bones <note>This indicates extreme fasting and emaciation, suggesting his skin was so thin it was translucent.</note>. This too is related among the accounts of his virtue by his diligent disciples: that for twenty-five whole years he never turned toward the west. Even though the door of the cave was situated on the height of the mountain, he never saw the sun as it turned toward the west, nor even under the open sky when the sun stood overhead, nor did he ever behold the stars rising in the west during those twenty-five years. # PALLADIUS

### 130

...knowledge, God gave them over to a depraved mind to do those things which are not proper. <note>original: *eis adokimon noun poiein ta mē kathēkonta*. This is a direct quotation of Romans 1:28, which Palladius uses here to explain the spiritual downfall of certain monks.</note> Regarding others who seem to have the knowledge of God but possess a corrupted judgment and a vain puffing up of pride, the Scripture says: "Because, although they knew Christ, they did not glorify Him as God, or give thanks, but they became futile in their thoughts, and their foolish heart was darkened. Claiming to be wise, they became fools." <note>original: *Phaskontes einai sophoi emōranthēsan*. A citation of Romans 1:21-22. Interestingly, Palladius substitutes "Christ" where the biblical text usually says "God."</note> These are the ones who have turned toward the demon of conceit and arrogance. Therefore, God gave them over to shameful passions, to the dishonoring of their own bodies. <note>A citation of Romans 1:26.</note> Consequently, we must understand from this that it is impossible for anyone to fall into licentiousness unless they have first been abandoned by the providence of God—a thing that happens to those who are forsaken because of their own negligence and laziness. 

This was the rule <note>original: *horos*; can mean a definition, a boundary, or a formal rule of life.</note> of Paphnutius, the most virtuous of the "Gnostics," <note><term>Gnostics (gnōstikōn)</term>: In the context of early monasticism, this does not refer to the heretical sects of the 2nd century, but to monks who had achieved a high level of "gnosis" or spiritual knowledge through prayer and asceticism.</note> and the most proven of the monks. He spoke this while the holy and blessed fathers were gathered with him, deliberating on the subject of those who fall away from the path. Among them were Chronius and Jacob, men who shone brightly in the excellent way of the ascetic life in the caves near Jericho. These caves were originally made by the Amorites when they fled at the time Joshua, the son of Nun, <note>original: *Iēsous ho tou Nauē*. In Greek, the name Joshua is the same as Jesus. The "son of Nun" distinguishes the biblical leader who conquered Canaan.</note> went out against the foreigners at the mountain of Luca.

This Elpidius came and took up residence in one of the caves. He displayed such a great degree of self-restraint in his ascetic practice that he eclipsed everyone else. For twenty-five years, he partook of food and communion only on Saturdays and Sundays. He spent all his nights

It establishes a scriptural framework using figures like Job, Paul, and Judas to explain why God allows suffering or spiritual falls.</meta>

# PALLADIUS

### 129
### LAUSIAC HISTORY

...testimony of good works. There are, therefore, various reasons for these "abandonments." One kind occurs for the sake of hidden virtue, so that it may be revealed; as was the case with the great athlete of patience, Job, when God spoke to him and said: "Do not reject my judgment; nor should you think I have dealt with you for any other reason than that you might appear righteous." <note>original: *mē apopoiē me to krima*. A reference to Job 40:8. In the early church, Job was the primary model for the "athlete" of faith.</note> For to me, who sees what is hidden and looks into the depths of human thoughts, you were known from the beginning. But since such great achievements were unknown to other men—the idle and wicked among them suspecting that you served me only because of your wealth—I brought upon you such a great trial. I mowed down your wealth so that I might show them your grateful philosophy. <note>original: *euchariston Philosophian*. In this context, "philosophy" refers to the monastic or ascetic way of life and the wisdom gained through spiritual discipline.</note>

There is also another way one is abandoned: for the sake of averting pride and avoiding conceit or arrogance. This can be seen in the case of the blessed Apostle Paul. For he himself was abandoned, left in the midst of trials, buffeted by blows, and struck by various deficiencies. Therefore he said: "A thorn in the flesh was given to me, a messenger of Satan, to buffet me, so that I would not be too highly exalted." <note>A direct reference to 2 Corinthians 12:7.</note> This was done so that through his many miracles, the comfort, the cheerfulness, and the honor he received would not cast him into the pride of the Devil, should he become softened by a lack of hardship. 

The paralytic was also abandoned because of sin, as it was said to him: "See, you have become well; sin no more, so that something worse does not happen to you." <note>A citation of John 5:14.</note> Furthermore, Judas was abandoned because he preferred silver to the Word of Life, and for this reason, he hanged himself. Esau was also abandoned, which is why he fell into licentiousness, preferring the dung of the intestines <note>original: *kopron enterōn*. This graphic phrase refers to the "mess of pottage" or lentil stew for which Esau sold his birthright (Genesis 25), emphasizing the monk's view of worldly desires as waste.</note> over his fatherly blessing. 

The blessed Apostle Paul, perceiving all these things through the Holy Spirit, spoke concerning those abandoned in such a manner: "Therefore, just as they did not see fit to keep God in their

->knowledge,<-

<summary>Palladius explains the theological concept of "abandonment" (God withdrawing protection), citing scriptural examples like Job and Paul to show it can be a test of virtue rather than a punishment for sin.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discourse on divine abandonment (egkataleipsis). The previous page discussed how God allows trials to reveal hidden virtue (like Job) or to prevent pride (like Paul). This page explores how an ascetic can recover from such a state by embracing humility and ensuring that their outward speech matches their inner life.</meta>

# PALLADIUS

### 128

->PALLADIUS<-

...so that, having set aside the cause of his abandonment, he may put away his **pride** <note>original: *typhon*. This refers to a state of being "puffed up" or clouded by vanity, a common concern in monastic psychology.</note> and take up **humility**, recognizing his own limits. By not exalting himself over anyone and by giving thanks to God, a witness-bearing knowledge returns to him once more. For spiritual words that do not possess a holy and temperate life are silenced; they are like ears of grain blighted by the wind, having the shape of grain but robbed of the nourishment of **Demeter**. <note>The author uses a classical reference to Demeter, the Greek goddess of the harvest, to refer to the life-giving substance or "flour" of spiritual truth. In this context, it means the words are hollow husks without substance.</note> 

Every fall, therefore—whether through the tongue, or through sight, hearing, smell, or action, or through the whole body—occurs in proportion to one’s **pride** or **conceit**. This happens through the "abandonment" of a God who is actually sparing those who are being abandoned. <note>original: *kata egkataleipsin*. This is a central concept in Palladius: God "abandons" the soul to its own weakness as a pedagogical tool to cure pride before it becomes fatal.</note> For if the Lord were to bear witness to their natural talent by providing them with powerful words while they were still living in lack of restraint, He would turn them into demons, puffed up by their pride and impurity. 

And these things these holy men and the greatest of the fathers used to say to us: "Whenever you see someone who is difficult in his silence but persuasive in his words, remember the demon who conversed with the Lord in the Gospel, and the testimony of the Scriptures which says: *Now the serpent was the wisest of all the beasts on the earth.*" <note>A reference to Genesis 3:1. The "wisdom" of the serpent is used here as a warning against intellectual or rhetorical skill that is detached from moral goodness.</note> For the serpent, this prudence became a source of harm, because no other virtue accompanied it. 

For it is necessary for the faithful and good man to think those things which God gives, to speak those things which he thinks, and to do those things which he speaks. For if the truth of one’s words does not run alongside the virtue of one’s life, then the rest of his seemingly good habits is like **bread without salt**, according to the blessed **Job**. <note>A reference to Job 6:6: "Can that which is tasteless be eaten without salt?"</note> That which is bitter will never be eaten; and even if it were eaten, it throws the eater into a state of great ill-health. <note>original: *kachexian*. A medical term for a wasting away or general ill-health, used here metaphorically for spiritual sickness.</note> For he says: "Shall bread be eaten without salt?" And if there is any taste in empty words—that is, words not filled with spiritual benefit—[it is likewise useless].

<summary>Palladius argues that spiritual eloquence is worthless and even dangerous if not paired with a humble, virtuous life, using the imagery of blighted grain and saltless bread to describe hollow teachings. He explains that God allows monks to "fall" or feel abandoned as a necessary cure for the spiritual poison of pride.</summary>

<keywords>humility, pride, divine abandonment, virtue, hypocrisy, Job, serpent, Demeter, spiritual health, rhetoric</keywords>

...shame. Therefore, being left behind <note>original: *kataleiphthentes*, referring to "divine abandonment," a state where God temporarily withdraws His visible support.</note> through the humility and dishonor that comes upon them, they gradually and gently rub away the **pride** <note>original: *typhon*; a term for being "puffed up" or clouded by vanity.</note> they felt for their supposed virtue. They no longer trust in themselves, but acknowledge the grace of God who grants all things. 

For when one is puffed up and exalted by a natural talent for words, they no longer credit God for the gift of that talent or the provision of knowledge, but rather their own ascetic practice or nature. Then, God withdraws the **angel of providence** <note>original: *angelon tēs pronoias*</note> assigned to protect that specific grace. Once he departs, the exalted one is overpowered by the Adversary and falls into **licentiousness** <note>original: *akolasia*</note> through his natural talent, in order to drive out the demonic puffing-up, which is **pride** <note>original: *hyperphania*</note>. This happens so that, once the witness of their self-control is removed, what is said by them no longer carries credibility. The devout flee from the teaching of such a mouth as if from a spring containing leeches, so that what is written may be fulfilled: "But to the sinner God said:

> 'Why do you recount my decrees, and take up my covenant through your mouth?'" <note>A quotation from Psalm 50:16 (LXX). It suggests that God rejects the preaching of those who are internally corrupt.</note>

Truly, the souls of those driven by passions are like various springs. Those of the gluttons and wine-lovers are like **muddy springs**. Those of the greedy and covetous are like springs containing **frogs**. But those of the envious and proud, who possess a certain outward aptitude for knowledge, are like springs that nourish **snakes**. In these, the word always stagnates like foul-smelling water in a pond, because no one wishes to draw from it with pleasure, due to the bitterness of their character and the stench of their impure deeds. 

Therefore, **David** prayed, asking to be taught three gifts from God: **goodness**, **discipline**, and **knowledge**. <note>Psalm 119:66. In the monastic tradition, knowledge is considered dangerous and "stinking" unless it is accompanied by moral goodness and self-discipline.</note> For without goodness, knowledge is useless. And if such a person corrects...

<summary>This page describes the psychological and spiritual consequences of pride in monastic life, explaining how God withdraws protection to allow a fall into lesser sins to cure the soul of arrogance. It uses the metaphor of "impure springs" to describe how the teachings of a proud or immoral person become repulsive to the faithful.</summary>

<meta>The previous page established that God sometimes permits an ascetic to experience a "divine abandonment." This is not an act of cruelty, but a pedagogical tool to strip away the soul's reliance on its own strength and to cure it of hidden pride. # PALLADIUS

### 126

->THE LAUSIAC HISTORY<-

...and even piratical <note>original: *peiratika*; likely referring to sudden, violent spiritual attacks or temptations that "hijack" the soul.</note> attacks, these things happen by the permission of God. This permission arises from the irrationality or the lack of faith of those who are being abandoned. For it is impossible for the person who thinks rightly and lives piously to be abandoned by God, or to fall into a shameful sin or the delusions of demons.

Those, therefore, who seem to pursue virtue with a corrupt purpose—whether through the disease of "people-pleasing" <note>original: *anthrōpareskeia*; the act of doing good deeds specifically to be seen and praised by others.</note> or the arrogance of their own reasonings—eventually fall into error. God abandons them in accordance with their own perverted purpose, so that through this abandonment they might feel the change resulting from their shift in state. The goal is that they might correct either their **intention** or their **action**.

For sometimes the intention is at fault, and sometimes the action; this occurs whenever a deed is done corruptly or not in the way it ought to be. For example, it often happens that a licentious man gives alms with a corrupt purpose to a younger woman, seeking a shameful end. While the act of giving assistance to one who is an orphan, alone, and unprotected is an "irrational" action <note>In this context, *alogon* or "irrational" implies an action that lacks the proper spiritual logic or foundation because the motive is predatory.</note> when done with such intent. 

Conversely, it also happens that one might give alms with a right purpose—helping the sick, the elderly, or those who have lost their wealth—but does so **stingily** or with **grumbling**. In such a case, the purpose is right, but the action is contrary to that purpose. For the merciful person must show mercy with cheerfulness and without reservation. 

The saints also used to say this: that there are natural advantages <note>original: *proterēmata*; natural gifts or "head-starts" one is born with.</note> found in many souls. In some, it is a natural brilliance of mind; in all those who practice the ascetic life, it is an aptitude for discipline. But when an action is not performed for the sake of the **Good** itself, nor according to a divine purpose—and when neither the action, nor the brilliance of mind, nor the fame gained through eloquent words are attributed to God—then the person does not credit this grace to the Giver of all good things. Instead, they credit their own choice, their fine education, or their own capability. Consequently, these people are abandoned by Providence and fall into shameful deeds or shameful passions...

<meta>The catchword at the bottom of the page is "and," indicating the sentence continues on the following page.</meta>

<summary>Palladius explains that spiritual failures result from God's "permission" to allow the soul to face its own weaknesses, often caused by a mismatch between right intentions and right actions. He warns that failing to credit God for one's natural talents leads to a withdrawal of divine protection and subsequent moral falls.</summary>

<keywords>divine abandonment, providence, people-pleasing, almsgiving, intention vs action, spiritual discipline, pride, natural talents</keywords>

->16. LAUSIAC HISTORY<-

### Concerning Paphnutius.

On one of those days, we were joined by Paphnutius, the one nicknamed **Cephalas** <note>original: *Kephalas*; meaning "The Head," likely referring to his great intellect or his position of leadership.</note>. This man possessed a gift of knowledge regarding the Divine Scriptures, both the Old and New Testaments; he could interpret them entirely, despite never having read the scriptures <note>original: *mē anegnōkōs graphas*; this suggests he was either illiterate or had never formally studied the texts, receiving his understanding through direct spiritual inspiration.</note>. He was so remarkably gentle that his prophetic virtue was often hidden by his modesty. A tribute to his virtue is still told: that for eighty years, he never owned two tunics at once.

The blessed deacon **Albinius** and I happened to meet with him, and we sought to learn the reasons why some brothers fall away or are cast out, or why they stumble in the conduct of their required life. For it happened in those days that **Chaeremon** the ascetic died while sitting down, and he was found dead in his chair with his handiwork still in his hands. It also happened that another brother was buried alive while digging a well, and another, coming from **Scetis** <note>A famous desert wilderness in Egypt that served as a major center for early Christian monks.</note>, died of thirst.

We also brought up the cases of **Stephen**, who fell into shameful debauchery; **Eucarpius**; the case of **Heron** of Alexandria; the case of **Valens** the Palestinian; and the case of **Ptolemy** the Egyptian in Scetis. We asked him together: what is the cause of this—that men living such virtuous lives in such great solitude are sometimes deceived and suffer damage to their minds, or are seized by gluttony, or are ensnared by licentiousness?

In response to our questions, the holy **Paphnutius**, most knowledgeable in spiritual matters, answered us: "All things that happen are divided into two categories: they happen either by God’s **good pleasure** <term>Eudokia: God's direct, positive will for what is good.</term> or by His **permission** <term>Synchoresis: God's "allowing" of an event, even a difficult or tragic one, often for a hidden pedagogical purpose.</term>. Therefore, whatever happens according to virtue and for the glory of God happens by the good pleasure of God. On the other hand, things that are harmful and dangerous..."

...welcoming all who encountered them. They even settled the schism involving Paulinus <note>original: *to schisma to kata Paulinon*; likely referring to the Meletian schism or the internal divisions in the church of Antioch involving Bishop Paulinus.</note>, which included about four hundred monks. Furthermore, they persuaded every heretical "Spirit-fighter" <note>original: *pneumatomachon*; the Pneumatomachians were a 4th-century sect that denied the full divinity of the Holy Spirit.</note> and brought them back into the Church, honoring that place and their inheritance with gifts and virtuous conduct. By acting in this way, they lived out their lives in piety, causing offense to no one and benefiting almost the entire known world.

### Concerning Chronios.

There was a certain man named **Chronios** who came from the village called **Phoenice** <note>A village in Egypt near the desert, not to be confused with the coastal region of Phoenicia.</note>. After measuring out fifteen thousand paces from his own village—which was near the desert—counting each step with his right foot, he prayed in that spot and dug a well. He found excellent water at a depth of seven fathoms <note>original: *orgyas hepta*; an "orguia" was the span of a man's outstretched arms, roughly six feet.</note>. There, he built himself a small guest-dwelling. From the day he established himself in that monastery, he prayed to God that he might never again return to inhabited lands. After a few years had passed, he was deemed worthy of the priesthood, and a brotherhood of about two hundred men gathered around him. This is the hallmark of his ascetic virtue: that for sixty years, while serving as a priest at the altar, he never once left the desert, and he never ate bread that was not the product of the work of his own hands.

### Concerning Jacob.

A certain **Jacob**, nicknamed **the Lame**, lived with him as a neighbor; he was a man of extreme spiritual knowledge <note>original: *gnōstikōtatos*; meaning he was highly advanced in the "gnosis" or contemplative insight of the desert tradition.</note>. Both of these men were well known to the blessed **Antony** <note>Saint Antony the Great (c. 251–356), traditionally considered the "Father of Monks."</note>.

“For thirty-two years I did not touch any fruit.” He said that once, when a spirit of cowardice <note>original: *deilias*; in monastic literature, this often refers to a demonic "spirit of fear" or anxiety that attacks a monk in solitude</note> fought him to the extreme—to the point that even the daylight became terrifying to him—he shut himself in a tomb for six years to confront the surroundings. He overcame this by waging a hand-to-hand war against the spirit that brought this cowardice upon him. The blessed Bishop Basil <note>Basil the Great, Bishop of Caesarea (330–379 CE), one of the most influential figures in early Christian monasticism</note> cared deeply for this highly renowned man, taking great pleasure in his austerity, his intensity, and his devotion to his work. Even now, he has not been freed from the reed-pen and the writing-stone <note>original: *kalamos* and *petra*; this refers to his work as a scribe or copyist, showing that he maintained his professional discipline even into old age</note>, proving that his struggle continues despite his years.

Furthermore, the blessed man said: “From the time I was initiated into the mysteries and reborn from water and spirit <note>A reference to Christian baptism</note> until this very day, I have never eaten the bread of another for free; rather, I have lived by my own labors,” a fact of which he convinced us in the presence of God. He spent two hundred and fifty coins <note>original: *nomismata*; the standard gold coin of the later Roman Empire</note> on a journey as far as Alexandria, intending to pray at the shrine of the honorable athlete Mark <note>St. Mark the Evangelist, whose tomb was a major pilgrimage site in Alexandria. "Athlete" is a common term for a martyr or spiritual hero</note>. He also deemed it worthy, he said, for the sake of prayer, to travel a second time to Jerusalem on his own feet to visit the Holy Places, covering his own expenses. He told us these things for our own benefit, adding that he did not remember ever departing in his mind from his God. Such were the struggles of the blessed Philoromus, and in these lay his invincible victory; to him has been granted the end of his blessed labors: the unfading crown of glory.

## Concerning Philoromus.

We encountered in Galatia <note>An ancient region in central Anatolia, modern-day Turkey</note> and spent a long time with the most God-beloved priest Philoromus, a man of extreme asceticism and endurance. He was born of a mother who was a domestic servant and a father who was a freeman. He displayed such virtuous purity in his Christian way of life <note>original: *politeia*; a term referring to one's conduct or "citizenship" in the spiritual life</note> that his angelic life and the active virtue of his ascetic practice were respected even by the unconquered spirit within his soul. This man renounced the world in the days of Julian the ill-named King <note>Julian the Apostate (reigned 361–363 CE), the last pagan Roman Emperor, who was widely vilified by Christian historians</note>, and Philoromus, that noble athlete of Christ, spoke with great boldness to this impious ruler. Julian ordered him to be shaved and to be struck repeatedly by young boys. Philoromus endured the treatment and even expressed his gratitude to the emperor, as he himself related to us. 

In the beginning, as he used to say, the war of lust <note>original: *porneia*; referring here to the internal struggle against sexual temptation</note> and gluttony attacked this noble man. Having subdued these passions, he drove them out like an immense wildfire quenched with rushing water; he achieved this through the rule of extreme self-control, by wearing iron <note>original: *sidērophoreia*; the ascetic practice of wearing heavy iron chains or collars to mortify the flesh</note>, by confinement, and by abstaining from meat, wheaten bread, and—quite simply—anything cooked by fire. He persevered in tombs for eighteen years. Having overcome these disorderly passions, he sang the victory hymn to Christ: 
> I will exalt you, O Lord, for you have lifted me up, and have not let my enemies rejoice over me. <note>Psalm 30:1 (Septuagint 29:2)</note> 
Though he was attacked in various ways by the spirits of wickedness, he persevered in a monastery for forty years. He used to recount that—

Such was the nature of this paralysis that the boy’s body was so twisted he would spit pus onto his own back. He was so horribly contorted; yet, through the prayer of the righteous man, he was healed instantly. 

In another instance, an old woman who was grazing sheep in the areas around the Lazarion <note>A place likely named after a church or district dedicated to Saint Lazarus</note> lost one of them, as some younger men in that place had stolen it. Weeping, the old woman approached this God-inspired man. He said to her, "Show me the place where you lost it." As she went off toward the Lazarion, the great man followed her. Arriving at the spot, he stood and prayed. 

Now, the thieves had already slaughtered the sheep and hidden the meat in the vineyard. While the holy man was praying—even though the guilty parties would not confess—grace began to work. A crow came from somewhere and, hovering over the theft, took a piece of the offal <note>original: *kopron*; referring here to the waste or entrails of the slaughtered animal</note> from it and flew away again. ->Concerning Innocent.<-

You have heard the story of the blessed Innocent—the priest of the Mount of Olives—from many great men. Nevertheless, you might also learn about him from us humble folk who lived with him in his final years. For the virtues of this man, which often escaped the notice of others, have been made manifest to us. It is not easy to find such virtues in one man, nor in a second, nor even in a tenth. 

This man, then, the best of ascetics <note>term: *askētēs*; a person who practices severe self-discipline and abstention from all forms of indulgence for religious reasons</note>, was exceedingly simple. Having been one of the illustrious men in the palace during the early years of Emperor Constantius <note>Constantius II, reigned 337–361 AD, son of Constantine the Great</note>, he renounced the world, leaving his marriage behind. 

He had one son, named Paul, who served as a *domesticus* <note>term: *domestikos*; a high-ranking member of the imperial guard or a household official</note> in the army. When this son committed a sin with the daughter of a priest, the holy Innocent interceded regarding his own son, praying to God and saying: "Lord, give him this spirit <note>original: *pneuma*; here referring to a distressing spirit or demon</note>, so that he may no longer find opportunity to sin in the flesh." He said, "It is better for him to wrestle with a demon than to be a slave to licentiousness." And so it happened. Even now, he remains on the Mount of Olives, wearing iron chains <note>original: *siderophoron*; a form of extreme ascetic penance where the person wears heavy iron weights or chains</note> and being disciplined by the demon. 

This renowned Innocent—how merciful he was, and how simple! I shall seem to be telling trifles while recounting the truth about him. He would often steal from the brothers—if anything happened to fall into his hands—and give it to those in need. Because he had become exceedingly innocent and simple in his ways, he was deemed worthy of a gift against demons. Among these instances, once a youth was brought to him who was seeing...

...having come to be of such a practice <note>original: *as kēseōs*; referring to spiritual discipline</note>, they left behind their lives in good memory.

### Concerning Julian.

I have heard concerning a certain Julian in the regions of the Edessenes <note>The people of Edessa, an ancient city in Upper Mesopotamia, modern-day Urfa, Turkey</note>, a man most devoted to asceticism. Through excessive discipline, he so withered his flesh that he carried about only skin and bones. Toward the very end of his life, he was deemed worthy of honor for the sake of the gift of healing.

### Concerning Adolius.

I also knew a certain man in Jerusalem named Adolius, a Tarsian by birth. Having arrived in Jerusalem, he traveled the unswerving path of virtue to an extraordinary degree. It was not the path that many of us travel, but rather he carved out for himself a strange and new way of life <note>original: *politeia*; often used to describe the regulated lifestyle or "citizenship" of a monk</note> beyond human nature, in which he practiced his discipline. Even the evil demons themselves shuddered at the severity of his excellent way of life and did not dare to approach him. 

Indeed, through the intensity of his self-control and his vigil <note>term: *agrypnia*; the practice of staying awake for prayer through the night</note>, he was even suspected of being a phantom. During the entire period of Lent <note>original: *tessarakostē*; the forty-day fast preceding Easter</note>, he would eat only bread, and even that was measured; for the rest of the year, he would eat only once every five days. 

This was the great and marvelous thing about his virtue: he would maintain himself from evening until the morning hour when the brotherhood gathered again in the houses of prayer. This spiritual athlete would stand upon the Mount of Olives, on the hill of the Ascension—from where Jesus was taken up—and there he would remain standing, chanting and praying continually. He did this in every season; whether it snowed, or rained, or was frosty, he remained unshaken. When he had fulfilled the accustomed rule <note>term: *kanōn*; the prescribed set of prayers or psalms for a specific time</note> of prayers, he would then take the awakening-mallet <note>original: *sphyrio*; likely referring to a small *semantron*, a wooden board struck with a mallet to call monks to prayer</note> and knock on the doors of everyone’s cells, gathering them into the houses of prayer...

### Concerning Basinilla.

I also knew Basinilla, the wife of Candidianus the general <term>stratēlatēs; a high-ranking Byzantine military commander, often equivalent to a Magister Militum</term>, who practiced virtue with great zeal and piety, and who even until now continues to persevere intensely in her spiritual struggles.

### Concerning Photine.

I also knew Photine, a most venerable virgin to the highest degree, who was the daughter of Theoctistus, the presbyter <note>a priest or elder</note> of the church in Laodicea.

### Concerning Sabiniana.

I also encountered in Antioch a most venerable and exceedingly pious woman who communed with God, the deaconess <note>an official female office in the early church involved in liturgy and charity</note> Sabiniana. She was the aunt <note>original: *theia*</note> of John, the Bishop of Constantinople <note>This refers to the famous preacher John Chrysostom (c. 347–407 AD)</note>.

### Concerning Asella.

In Rome, I also saw the beautiful Asella, the virgin of Christ, who had grown old gracefully within her monastery <note>original: *monastēriō*; likely a domestic or communal ascetic house in the city</note>. She was a woman of exceeding gentleness who allowed others to live with her; among them, I observed both men and women who were newly instructed in the faith <note>original: *neokatēchētous*; recent converts or "neophytes" undergoing religious education</note>.

### Concerning Avita.

I also beheld the blessed Avita, who was truly worthy of God, along with her husband Pernianus and their daughter Eunomia. They were pleasing to God in all things, having successfully transitioned with ease from a relaxed and luxurious lifestyle to a life of virtue and self-control <note>original: *egkratē politeian*; a disciplined or ascetic way of life</note>. They were even deemed worthy through these efforts to fall asleep in Christ <note>a common Christian euphemism for death</note>, having been perfectly liberated from all sin, and possessing within themselves the knowl-

->On Paula<-

I have also considered it necessary to mention courageous and noble women in this book—those to whom God granted prizes equal to the men who lived in virtue. He has bestowed the crown of those who pleased Him upon them as well, so that women of a more negligent disposition might not grow soft or hold themselves back from the struggles for virtue and the achievement of a holy life as if they were too weak. I have seen many of the pious, and I have encountered very many refined women—both virgins and widows—devoted to virtue. ### About Ephrem

You have certainly heard the account of Ephrem, the deacon of the church of Edessa. <note>original: "Ἐφραῒμ τὸν διάκονον τῆς Αἰδεσινῶν ἐκκλησίας" — Ephrem the Syrian (c. 306–373 AD) was a prolific Syriac composer of hymns and a Christian theologian. Edessa was a major center of early Christianity in Upper Mesopotamia.</note> For he was a man truly worthy of being remembered by the holy servants of Christ. Having traveled the path of the Holy Spirit in a worthy manner, never turning aside from the straight road, he was granted the gift of "Natural Knowledge," which is followed by "Theology" and, ultimately, supreme blessedness. <note>In the tradition of the Desert Fathers, "Natural Knowledge" (physikē) refers to the ability to see the hand of God within the created world, which leads to "Theology" (theologia), the direct contemplation of God.</note> For many years he practiced a life of quiet discipline to the highest degree, always building up those who happened upon him. However, he later emerged from his cell for the following reason.

A great famine had gripped the city of Edessa. This godly man, taking pity on the entire countryside as it was being destroyed, went to the wealthy men of the city—those "thick with material goods"—and said to them: "Why is it that you do not pity human nature as it perishes, but instead let your wealth rot to the condemnation of your souls?" <note>original: "τοῖς ἀδροῖς ἐν ὕλῃ" — literally "those thick in matter," a play on words suggesting that their spiritual lives are weighed down by their physical possessions.</note>

They considered this and offered what seemed like a reasonable excuse, saying to the saint: "The problem is that we have no one we can trust to distribute bread to those who are starving; for everyone treats such business like a marketplace for profit." <note>original: "καπηλεύουσι" — referring to hucksters or merchants who trade for dishonest gain.</note>

This man of virtue said to them: "What is your opinion of me? How do I appear to you?"

"We know you to be a man of God," they replied. "And the truth is dear to us." Indeed, he held a great reputation among everyone—not falsely, but in truth.

The servant of Christ said to them: "If I seem such a man to you, then entrust the care of the starving to me."

"If only you would consider us worthy!" the noblemen replied.

Ephrem, the chosen of God, answered them: "Behold, from this day forward, for your sake, I ordain myself the 'host' of those in need." <note>original: "χειροτονῶ ἐμαυτὸν ξενοδόχον" — Ephrem uses the word for ecclesiastical ordination (cheirotonia) to describe his self-appointment to this charitable service, highlighting the sacred nature of hospitality.</note> And taking money from them, he partitioned off the public porches and set up about three00 beds. <note>original: "ἐμβόλους" — these were the covered walkways or porticos of the city, which Ephrem converted into a makeshift infirmary and distribution center.</note>

<summary>This page introduces Ephrem the Syrian, a renowned ascetic who breaks his solitary life to address a devastating famine in Edessa. He uses his impeccable reputation to persuade the wealthy to fund a massive relief effort, which he personally oversees by setting up three hundred beds in the city's public porches.</summary>

<keywords>Ephrem the Syrian, Edessa, famine relief, Christian charity, deacon, asceticism, Natural Knowledge, hospitality, historical biography</keywords>

### About Moses the Libyan.

**M**oses the Libyan, a man of exceeding gentleness and most full of love, was thus deemed worthy of the gift of healing. <note>original: "Μωϋσῆς ὁ Λίβυς" — Moses the Libyan was a desert ascetic. While less famous than Moses the Black, he is recorded here as a primary witness to the miracles of the early monastic movement.</note> He himself related to me that when he was a vigorous young man in our monastery, they were digging a massive well, twenty feet wide. Eighty men had been excavating this for three days, and though they had passed through the usual damp vein for about a cubit, we found no water. <note>original: "πήχυν" — A cubit, roughly the length of a forearm (18 inches). The "damp vein" refers to the initial moist soil layer that usually precedes a spring.</note> We were quite discouraged and were considering abandoning the work. And while we were deliberating this, the blessed Pior <note>Pior was an Egyptian hermit and a disciple of Anthony the Great, known for his extreme humility and for never looking at his own shadow or his family after entering the desert.</note> came upon us from the deep desert in the heat of the sixth hour <note>The "sixth hour" is midday, when the desert sun is at its most punishing intensity.</note>, an old man clothed in a sheepskin. After greeting us, he said: “Why have you become faint-hearted, you of little faith? For I have seen you discouraged since yesterday.” 

Having said this, he went down the ladder into the pit of the well and offered a prayer with them. Taking up the spade and striking the ground three times, he said: “O God of the holy patriarchs, do not let the labor of your servants be in vain, but send them the water they need.” And immediately, water gushed out so that we were all sprayed. After praying again, he departed. Though they urged him to eat there, he would not allow it, saying: “That for which I was sent has been accomplished; for the other, I was not sent.” <note>original: "δι᾿ ὃ ἀπεστάλην ἐτύχθη· δι᾿ ἐκεῖνο δὲ οὐκ ἀπεστάλην" — This reflects the ascetic's singular focus on divine service over physical needs.</note> These are the extraordinary wonders of Pior’s training in endurance, and this was the fulfillment of his virtue—that in place of a bitter spring, he now enjoys an eternal stream of sweetness through great spiritual grace on account of these things. <note>original: "γυμνασίου τῆς ὑπομονῆς" — The "gymnasium of endurance" refers to the ascetic life as a form of spiritual athletics or rigorous training.</note>

<summary>Moses the Libyan recounts a miracle performed by the ascetic Pior, who discovered water in a failed well-digging project through prayer and a single strike of a spade.</summary>
<keywords>Moses the Libyan, Pior, well-digging, miracles, desert asceticism, faith, prayer, Egypt</keywords>

<header>16. LAUSIAC HISTORY</header>

...due to the excess of <term>spiritual</term> <note>original: "πνευματικοῦ" — Referring to a love directed toward God and the divine life rather than worldly or familial affections.</note> love, he gave his word to God that he would no longer see any of his own kin. After fifty years had passed, his sister, now grown old, learned from someone that her brother was still alive; she was driven into an ecstasy of longing to see him. Being unable to travel into the deep desert herself, she petitioned the <term>bishop</term> <note>original: "ἐπίσκοπον" — An overseer of the local church; here, she uses ecclesiastical authority to influence the desert hermits.</note> of that region to write to the holy fathers in the desert, asking them to send him so that she might see him. Since great pressure was placed upon him, he obeyed these spiritual authorities and decided to depart, taking one other brother along with him. He sent word to his sister’s house, saying, "Pior your brother has arrived and stands outside." Sensing from the noise of the door that his sister was coming out to meet him, Pior shut his eyes tight and cried out to her:

> Sister so-and-so, I am Pior your brother. I am he. Look, see me as much as you wish.

Having been thus satisfied, she glorified God. Though she did many things to try to persuade him to enter the house, he would not. Instead, having offered a prayer at the threshold, he departed again for the desert, his true homeland. There he successfully practiced the virtue of <term>asceticism</term> <note>original: "ἀσκήσεως" — "Askesis" originally referred to athletic training but was adopted by Christians to describe the rigorous self-discipline and "training" of the soul through fasting and prayer.</note>. Even while he was still living, the miracle of this holy man's endurance was widely reported. In the place where he dwelt, he had dug a well and found the water to be exceedingly bitter; yet until the day he died, he remained there, persisting in the use of that bitter water alone, so that the endurance of this noble spiritual athlete might be made manifest. Indeed, many of the most proven <term>monks</term> <note>original: "μοναχῶν"</note>, after the death of this immortal man, vied with one another to live in that same cell, yet they were unable to complete even a single year there. For the water is bitter beyond measure, and the place is terribly fearsome and entirely without comfort.

<header># PALLADIUS</header>

...affairs. And some are able to say that she is out of her mind and possessed by a demon." The blessed Serapion answered and said to her: "And what does it matter to you if they say this? For to them, as you say, you have died. A dead man never cares if anyone insults or mocks him, for he is insensible to all things." Then that virgin said to him: "I beg you; if you wish anything else in the discipline of <term>asceticism</term> <note>original: "ἀσκήσει" — "Askesis" refers to the rigorous training and self-discipline practiced by monks.</note>, command me, and I will do it. For I have not yet reached this level, though I pray to reach it."

Then the servant of God, Serapion the <term>Dispassionate</term> <note>original: "ἀπαθής" — Referring to "apatheia," the Stoic and Christian desert ideal of being free from the disturbances of worldly passions and ego.</note> said to her: "Take care then, sister, that you no longer think highly of yourself as if you were holier than everyone, boasting that you have died to the world as if in a grave. For you have learned that you are still alive, and that you still seek to please men. For I can be 'more dead' than you, being able to show by deed that I have died to the world, holding myself dispassionately toward it. For I can do that which I commanded you without shame and without cause for scandal." After he had instructed her in <term>humility</term> <note>original: "ταπεινοφροσύνῃ"</note> with these words, he then left her perfected. Having broken the swelling of her <term>vanity</term> <note>original: "τύφου" — Literally "smoke" or "vapor," used to describe the delusion of pride.</note>, the blessed man thus departed from her.

This is the life of Serapion the Dispassionate, a man of the most extreme <term>poverty</term> <note>original: "ἀκτήμονος" — Literally "possessing nothing"; one who lives without any personal property.</note>. Many other great and wonderful things were done by this virtuous philosopher of Christ, which contribute toward dispassion; we have written a few out of many to show the purity of this life. This immortal man died at the age of sixty and was buried in that same desert.

...firmly in his vigor, he later met her. And seeing her, he says: "Why do you sit here?" She answers him, saying: "I am not sitting, but traveling." He says to her: "Where are you traveling?" The virgin says to him: "To my God." The servant of Christ says to her: "Are you living, or have you died?" She says to him: "I believe in my God that I have died to the world; for one living in the flesh <note>original: "ζῶν γὰρ τῇ σαρκὶ" — A common Pauline theological concept where "the flesh" represents the old, worldly nature.</note> shall not travel to God." Hearing these things, the blessed Serapion says to her: "Therefore, so that you may convince me <note>original: "πληροφορήσῃς" — literally "to give full measure," meaning to provide certain proof or assurance.</note> that you have died to the world, do what I do." She says to him: "I can; only command it, and I will do it." He answered her: "All things are possible for one who is dead as you are, except for committing an act of impiety." Then he says to her: "Come down and go out." She answered him: "I have been here for twenty-five years without ever going out, and for what reason should I go out today?" He says to her: "Behold, did you not say, 'I have died to this world'? It is absolutely clear then that for you, the world does not exist. If that is so, a dead person perceives nothing. Therefore, it is the same to you whether you go out or do not go out." Having heard these things, the virgin went out.

After she had gone out and arrived at a certain church, the blessed man came and found her in the church and says to her: "If you truly wish to fully convince me that you have died, and that you no longer live to please men, do what I am able to do, and then you will convince me that you have truly died to this world." And the virgin says: "What then do you want me to do?" He says to her: "Strip off all your clothes, as I do, place them on your shoulders, and walk through the middle of the city, for I am able to go before you without shame in this <term>state</term> <note>original: "σχήματι" — "schema" usually refers to the monastic habit or outward form; Serapion uses it here to refer to his intended state of nakedness to prove his lack of worldly shame.</note>." The patient man said to them: "I have no business with this matter. Take me away, and leave me where you found me." But the sailors said to him: "Are you out of your mind? Even if you had offered us a hundred gold coins, we would have done this journey gladly, seeing as we have such fair winds. Let us therefore keep to our purpose and finish the task before us." So he remained on the ship without a care, and the sailors provided for his food until they reached Rome. 

Having entered Rome, this athlete of Christ <note>original: "ἀθλητὴς τοῦ Χριστοῦ" — A common early Christian metaphor for an ascetic who "competes" against worldly desires and spiritual forces through rigorous discipline.</note> searched through the city to discover who might be the most excellent among the great male and female ascetics living there.

### Concerning Domninus

Among those he heard of was a certain Domninus, a man well-exercised in asceticism and a disciple of Origen <note>original: "Ὠριγένους" — Origen of Alexandria (c. 184–253 AD) was a massively influential, though later controversial, theologian and scholar whose teachings shaped early monastic thought.</note>. Domninus had practiced great discipline and was said to have performed many miracles; it was reported that even after his death, his bed could heal diseases. Having met him, Serapion was spiritually built up by him, for Domninus was a man refined <note>original: "τετορνευμένος" — literally "turned on a lathe," implying a character that has been carefully shaped, polished, and perfected through education and discipline.</note> in both character and knowledge. When Serapion asked to learn if there were any other ascetics or holy women in the city, he learned from him about a certain virgin who lived in stillness <note>original: "ἡσυχαζούσης" — a practitioner of *hesychia*, or inner silence and contemplative quiet.</note>. She was in her twenty-fifth year of being enclosed in a cell, never meeting with anyone.

But the servant gave him an answer saying that for many years the virgin had met with no one. He said to her again: "Tell her that I must meet her, for God has sent me to her." ...to these monks, he once again sold himself, repeating his first performance <note>original: "δράμα" — While this literally means a play or drama, in this context it refers to Serapion’s characteristic "act" of selling himself into slavery as a missionary strategy to convert his "masters."</note>. Within two years, he had led the man away from his heresy, and also brought the mistress of the house—who was a free woman—together with her entire household into the Church of God. These people came to love him no longer as a servant, but as more than a true brother and a father, holding him in great honor and glorifying God along with him. 

After a short time, the "spiritual diamond" <note>original: "πνευματικὸς ἀδάμας" (spiritual adamant) — A term used to describe an ascetic whose soul is as unyielding as a diamond against worldly temptations.</note> Serapion departed from them and boarded a ship, having returned the purchase price to these masters. He carried absolutely nothing at all with him, intending to sail for Rome. When the sailors saw him, they assumed he had his travel funds and supplies with him, or that he possessed a great deal of gold for his expenses. For this reason, they accepted him on board without questioning, each one assuming that someone else among them had taken charge of his baggage. 

Once they had set sail and were about five hundred stadia <note>A stadium was a Greek unit of distance; five hundred stadia is approximately 60 miles or 92 kilometers from the port of Alexandria.</note> away from Alexandria, the passengers and sailors began to eat their meal at sunset. Observing that he did not eat on the first day, they supposed that he was suffering from a bit of seasickness <note>original: "ἀηδίαν ἐκ τοῦ πλοὸς" — literally "unpleasantness from the voyage."</note>. It was the same on the second day, and again on the third, and even the fourth. 

On the fifth day, while everyone else was eating, they saw him sitting there quietly and said to him: "Why aren't you eating, man?" He replied to them: "I have nothing." Hearing this, the sailors began to question one another, wondering which of them had perhaps taken his supplies on the ship. When they found that no one had—because, in truth, he had nothing—they began to argue with him, saying: "How did you get on board here without any provisions? How do you expect to be looked after on such a long voyage? And another thing: how do you intend to pay us the fare?"

### PALLADIUS

...a monk by his way of life. <note>original: "πολιτείᾳ" (politeia) — This refers to a monk's regulated way of living or spiritual citizenship.</note> "Since I departed from my physical homeland, I fell into the hands of three creditors. Two of them have been satisfied and dismissed, having been paid their debt, and they no longer have anything to charge against me. But one does not leave me alone, and I have no means to satisfy him." 

Being curious <note>original: "Φιλοπραγμονοῦντες" — meaning meddlesome or inquisitive, here referring to the local philosophers.</note> and wanting to satisfy those creditors, they questioned him, saying: "Where are they, and who are these people troubling you? Show them to us, so that we may help you."

Then he said to them: "From my youth, Love of Money <note>original: "Φιλαργυρία"</note>, Sexual Immorality <note>original: "πορνεία"</note>, and Gluttony <note>original: "γαστριμαργία"</note> have troubled me. From two of them I have been freed—Love of Money and Sexual Immorality—for I possess no gold, nor do I own anything else. Nor do I enjoy luxury, which is the fuel of that disease; therefore, they no longer trouble me. But from Gluttony I cannot be freed. For today is the fourth day I have not eaten; and my belly, like a harsh creditor, chokes me with hunger and demands its usual debt, which if I do not pay, it will not permit me to live." 

Then some of the Philosophers thought the matter was a theatrical performance <note>original: "σκηνὴν" (skēnēn) — This suggests they thought he was acting out a parable or a play rather than speaking literally.</note>; nevertheless, they gave him one coin <note>original: "νόμισμα ἕν" — likely a gold solidus.</note>. He took it and placed it in a bakery, and taking one loaf of bread, he immediately left the city and never returned to it again. Then the Philosophers realized that this man truly possessed an extraordinary life; and having paid the price of the bread themselves, they took back the coin.

## XVI. THE LAUSIAC HISTORY

...of us, now let me tell you the whole secret of this drama. <note>original: "μυστήριον τοῦ δράματος" (mystērion tou dramatos) — Serapion had sold himself as a slave to a family of actors to convert them; he refers to this holy deception as a "drama."</note> I, having taken pity on your soul which was living in much error—being a free ascetic, Egyptian by race—for the sake of your being saved, I sold myself so that you might be set free from great sins. Since God has accomplished this through my humility, I rejoice. Take back your gold, therefore, so that I may go and help others." 

But they implored him, begging and affirming: "From now on we hold you as our father and the master of our souls. Only stay with us." 

When they were unable to persuade him, they said to him: "You give the gold to the poor, since it has become for us a pledge <note>original: "ἀρραβὼν" (arrhabon) — a down-payment or earnest money, used here metaphorically for the beginning of their salvation.</note> of salvation." 

He said to them: "You give it yourselves, for it is yours. For I do not give away the money of others to the poor." At this, they implored him: "At least visit us once a year." 

This holy servant of Christ, through his constant travels, once arrived even in Greece; and having spent three days in Athens, he was not offered bread by anyone, nor was he carrying any coin. For this saint had neither a pouch, nor a sheepskin, nor a staff, nor anything of the sort at all, but only the linen cloth <note>original: "σινδόνιον" (sindonion) — a simple wrap or shroud, which gave Serapion his nickname, the "Sindonite."</note> which he wore. 

When the fourth day arrived, he was extremely hungry, having tasted nothing during these days. For involuntary fasting is a terrible thing, especially when it has lack of faith <note>original: "ἀπιστίαν" (apistian) — implying that hunger without a spiritual choice can lead to a loss of trust in God’s providence.</note> as its ally. 

And standing upon a certain hill of the city, where the local officials used to gather, he began to cry out and lament violently, while clapping his hands, saying: "Men of Athens, help!" 

Running together all as one, both philosophers and officials <note>original: "κηροφόροι" (kerophoroi) — literally "wax-bearers," referring to the attendants or minor officials of the city.</note>, they said to him: "What is the matter with you, man? Or where are you from? Or what are you suffering?" 

He said to them: "I am Egyptian by race..."

<header>OF PALLADIUS</header>

### Concerning Serapion.

A certain man named Serapion existed, Egyptian by race, who was surnamed the Sindonite; <note>original: "Σινδόνιος" (Sindonios) — This nickname comes from the Greek word "sindon," meaning a linen cloth or shroud, which was his only garment.</note> for except for a linen cloth, he never put on anything else. He practiced a life of great poverty <note>original: "ἀκτημοσύνην" (aktēmosynēn) — Literally "the state of possessing nothing," a core monastic virtue involving the rejection of all private property.</note>, for which reason he was also called "the Dispassionate." <note>original: "ἀπαθὴς" (apathēs) — In this context, it refers to "apatheia," a state of being unmoved by worldly desires or suffering, rather than a lack of feeling.</note> Although he was illiterate, he had memorized all the Holy Scriptures. Due to his extreme poverty and his constant meditation on the divine oracles of the Spirit, he could not even remain still in a monastic cell, lest he be distracted by material things. Instead, wandering throughout the inhabited world, he achieved the virtue of the highest poverty, so that he possessed this state of dispassion as his only belonging. For he had truly entered into this disposition of soul; for there are differences of spiritual dispositions, though not of human essences.

The fathers used to relate that, taking along a fellow ascetic as a partner, he once sold himself in a certain city to some actors <note>original: "μίμοις" (mimois) — Mimes or actors. In the early Christian era, the theater was often viewed as a place of moral corruption and pagan influence.</note> who were Greeks—that is, pagans—for twenty gold coins. <note>original: "νομισμάτων" (nomismatōn) — Refers to the "nomisma" or "solidus," the standard gold coin of the Byzantine Empire.</note> Having sealed these coins, he kept them safely with him. He remained in service to them until he could make them Christians and cause them to withdraw from the theater. During this time, he consumed nothing except bread and water, and his mouth never ceased from the meditation of the Holy Scriptures. 

As the blessed man stayed with them for a long time, he first moved the lead actor to remorse, then the lead actress, and finally their entire household. It was said that as long as they were unaware of his true identity, he even performed the humble task of washing the feet of them both. Therefore, after both were baptized, they withdrew from performing in the theater from then on. Having advanced toward a dignified and God-loving life, they held the man in great reverence and said to him: "Come now, brother, let us set you free, since through you, you yourself have freed us from a shameful slavery." Then the great Serapion said to them: "Since God worked with me, and you also worked with me, your soul has been saved through me."

...of the woman, and her husband likewise, and he invited the holy **Poseidonios** to come. When we had entered together to pray, he stood and, after a second genuflection <note>original: "γονυκλισίαν" (gonuklisian) — A formal act of kneeling in prayer, common in monastic discipline.</note>, he cast out the spirit. The husband asked us for a sign to be given, so that we might be fully convinced that the demon had truly departed. And immediately, as the demon was coming out, it threw down the entire wall of the courtyard from its very foundations. This poor woman had been unable to speak for six years; but after the demon departed, she gave birth and recovered her speech.

I also learned a prophecy from this man, the "immortal" **Poseidonios**. <note>original: "Ποσειδονίου τοῦ ἀθανάτου" — The epithet "immortal" likely refers to his enduring spiritual reputation or his ascetic mastery over the flesh.</note> For a certain priest named **Jerome** <note>This refers to St. Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), the translator of the Latin Vulgate, who lived as a monk in Bethlehem.</note> lived in those parts, a man adorned with great skill in Roman words <note>original: "λόγων Ῥωμαϊκῶν" — Meaning the Latin language and its literature.</note> and possessed of considerable natural talent. However, he possessed such a spirit of envy <note>original: "βασκανίαν" (baskanian) — This can mean malice, envy, or even the "evil eye." Jerome was famously known for his difficult and polemical temperament.</note> that the excellence of his words was overshadowed by it. 

The holy **Poseidonios**, having spent several days with him, whispered in my ear: "The free-woman **Paula**, who takes care of him, will pass away before him, being delivered (as I believe) from his envious nature. Because of this man, no other holy man will dwell in these parts, for his spite will reach even his own brother." And this indeed came to pass. For he even drove the blessed **Oxyperentios** the Italian away from there, as well as **Peter**, another Egyptian, and **Simeon**—all of them wonderful men whom I have noted.

This **Poseidonios**, a most disciplined practitioner of virtue, related to me that he had not tasted bread for forty years, nor had he held a grudge against anyone even for half a day. These were the feats and signs of the noble athlete of Christ <note>original: "ἀθλητοῦ" (athlētou) — A common early Christian metaphor for monks, who "competed" in spiritual struggles like athletes in a stadium.</note>, **Poseidonios**, which he performed through the providence of that Power which is greater than all. And in these works was the celebrated end of the blessed man’s life.

## On Poseidonios.

**Chapter 27**. Regarding **Poseidonios** the Theban <note>original: "Θηβαῖον" (Thēbaion) — Referring to Thebes in Egypt, a major center for early Christian monasticism.</note>, a man most esteemed and of great endurance. There are many things, beyond description, that could be said of him—how gentle he was, how he practiced the most rigorous asceticism, and how much innocence he possessed within himself; I do not know if I have ever met anyone quite like him. Indeed, I lived with this man for one year in **Bethlehem**, where he stayed at a place beyond the Sheepfold <note>original: "Ποιμνίου" (Poimniou) — A site near Bethlehem traditionally associated with the shepherds of the Nativity.</note>.

And I observed many of his virtues. Among these, on one of the days we were together, he himself recounted a story to me: "While living on **Mount Porphyrites** <note>original: "Πορφυρίτου ὄρος" — The 'Porphyry Mountain' in the eastern desert of Egypt, famous for its red stone quarries and its remote hermitages.</note> for forty years, I did not meet a single person in an entire year; I heard no human conversation, and I touched no bread. Instead, I survived on a few small dates, and whatever wild herbs I might find. 

Once, when my food supplies had entirely failed me, I went out of my cave to return to the inhabited world. After walking through the whole day, I was barely two miles away from my cave. Looking around, I saw a horseman who had the appearance of a soldier, wearing a crested helmet <note>original: "καροφόρον κράνος" — A helmet with a plume or crest, typical of Roman military gear.</note> upon his head. Expecting him to be a soldier, I rushed back toward my cave to avoid the encounter. But when I entered, I found a basket of grapes and freshly cut figs. Taking these, and being filled with joy, I remained in the cave for another two months, using those foods as my sustenance."

The wonderful **Poseidonios** also performed this miracle in **Bethlehem**. A certain pregnant woman was possessed by an unclean spirit. At the very moment she was about to give birth, she suffered a difficult labor <note>original: "ἐδυστόκει" (edustokei) — A medical term for a difficult or obstructed birth.</note>, as the spirit was crushing her. While the woman was thus being tormented by the demon...

### [Page 101]

...the doctors, on account of the climate, [advised] me to reach **Palestine**. For it has a milder air better suited to our constitution <note>original: "κρᾶσιν" (krasin) — In ancient medicine, this refers to the balance of 'humors' or bodily fluids that determined a person's health and temperament.</note>. From Palestine, I then reached **Bithynia**. And there—I do not know how to say it, whether from human effort or by the favor of the Better One [God], God alone knows—I was deemed worthy of an ordination <note>original: "χειροτονίας" (cheirotonias) — Literally 'the laying on of hands,' this refers to Palladius being consecrated as a bishop.</note> that was beyond my merit, having shared in the trial concerning the blessed **John** <note>original: "Ἰωάννην" — This refers to John Chrysostom, the embattled Patriarch of Constantinople. Palladius was a staunch supporter and suffered exile for his loyalty.</note>. 

And for eleven months, remaining hidden in a dark cell, I remembered that blessed and wonderful man <note>referring to John the Prophet of Lycopolis, mentioned in previous chapters</note>, because he had foretold to me exactly these things I was now suffering. That great athlete of Christ <note>original: "ἀθλητὴς τοῦ Χριστοῦ" — A common early Christian metaphor comparing the monk’s spiritual struggles to the physical training and combat of an athlete.</note> also told me another story, which was meant to lead me, through its telling, toward the endurance of the desert so that I might never be separated from it. He said to me: "I have spent forty-eight years in this cell. I have never seen the face of a woman, nor have I seen a coin. No one has seen me chewing, nor has anyone ever seen me eating or drinking."

There is also the story of the servant of Christ, **Poimene**. Although she visited him for the sake of her own inquiry, she did not meet him face-to-face; nevertheless, he revealed certain secret matters to her. He warned her as she was traveling down from the **Thebaid** <note>The region around Thebes in Upper Egypt, a heartland of monasticism.</note> not to turn aside toward **Alexandria**, for she was destined to fall into trials. But she—either having disregarded the prophecy of that great man, or having forgotten it—turned aside toward Alexandria for the sake of inquiry, perhaps wanting to see the city. 

On the way, near the city of **Nikiou** <note>A city in the Nile Delta.</note>, her boats stopped for the purpose of rest. When her servants went ashore, they became involved in some disorder and entered into a fight with the local desperate men. These locals cut off the thumb of one of the servants and killed another. And **Dionysius**, the holy bishop, they even threw into the river...
