# PALLADIUS

...[you have the] peak of life remaining. But only seven years. Endure, therefore, in the desert, and do not wish to go back to your homeland for the sake of these things <note>The "things" refer to the physical ailments Palladius was suffering, which John suggests are being used as excuses to leave the ascetic life.</note>. For it is written: *No one who puts his hand to the plow and looks back is fit for the kingdom of heaven.* <note>original: "οὐδεὶς ἐπιβαλὼν τὴν χεῖρα ἐπ’ ἄροτρον..." — A quotation from the Gospel of Luke 9:62.</note> Benefiting then from the words of this divinely inspired man, and being sufficiently corrected, I gave thanks to God, having learned the excuses for my faint-heartedness through the foreknowledge of the holy man.

Then, he said to me again, joking: "Do you want to become a bishop?" But I said to him: "I already am one." He said to me: "Of what city?" I told him: "The kitchens, the taverns, the tables, and the jars—I oversee <note>original: "ἐπισκοπῶ" (episkopo) — Palladius is making a pun. The Greek word for bishop, "episkopos," literally means "overseer." He is saying he "oversees" the food and drink.</note> them; and if the wine is sour, I excommunicate <note>original: "ἀφορίζω" (aphorizo) — Another ecclesiastical pun. In a church context, this means to excommunicate or set apart, but Palladius uses it to mean setting aside bad wine.</note> it. But the good wine I drink. Likewise, I oversee the cooking pot; and if salt is missing, or any of the seasonings, I throw them in and season it, and so I eat it. This is my 'episcopacy.' For gluttony <note>original: "γαστριμαργία" (gastrimargia) — A term for one of the primary vices in monastic thought.</note> ordained <note>original: "ἐχειροτόνησεν" (echeirotonēsen) — The formal term for being consecrated or "hand-chosen" for the priesthood.</note> me."

The blessed man said to me with a smile: "Stop these jokes. You are destined to be ordained a bishop, and to labor much, and to suffer affliction. If, therefore, you flee from affliction, do not leave the desert; for in the desert, no one will ordain you a bishop." 

Departing then from him, I went into the desert to my usual place, relating these very things about the divine and spirit-bearing man to the holy fathers. Two months later, they sailed across and met with this blessed man. But I, wretched as I am, forgot his words; and after three years I fell ill with a sickness of the spleen and stomach, and from there I was sent to **Alexandria** by the brothers, for the ailment was eventually turning into dropsy <note>original: "ὕδρωπα" (hydropa) — A condition of swelling due to fluid retention, often associated in antiquity with organ failure.</note>. From the city of the Alexandrians, they again advised me...

I realized then with certainty that he was a spiritual <term>pneumatikos</term> <note>A "spiritual person" in this context refers to someone filled with the Holy Spirit and possessing divine insight.</note> man, and that he foreknew all things. Paying closer attention to this, I waited even more patiently. 

When the governor had finally left, he called for me and said: "Why were you offended <note>original: "ἐβλάβης" (eblabes) — literally 'harmed' or 'damaged,' but used here to mean taking offense or being spiritually stumbled.</note> by me? What did you find worthy of offense, that you thought those things which are neither true of me, nor was it fitting for you to think? Or do you not know what is written: *Those who are healthy have no need of a physician, but those who are sick?* <note>original: "οὐ χρείαν ἔχουσιν οἱ ἰσχύοντες ἰατροῦ, ἀλλ' οἱ κακῶς ἔχοντες" — A quotation from the Gospel of Matthew 9:12.</note> I can find you whenever I wish, and you can find me. And even if I do not comfort you, other brothers and other fathers will comfort you. But this man is handed over and given to the Devil through worldly affairs. And if he finds even a brief hour to breathe, he is like a servant who has run away from a harsh master. Just so, having found a brief hour, he came to us to receive some benefit. It would have been out of place for us to leave him to spend time with you, while you are already constantly devoted to your salvation."

When I had then asked him to pray for me, I was fully convinced that he was truly a spiritual man. Then, playing with me, he gently tapped my left cheek with his right hand and said to me: "Many tribulations await you, and you have often thought of leaving the desert; you have grown fearful, yet you have persevered. But the demon, bringing you seemingly pious and reasonable excuses, looks forward to your departure. For he suggested to you the desire of your father, and the instruction of your brother and your sister in the monastic life. Behold, I bring you good news: both of them have been saved. For they have renounced the world; and your father [will live]...

While we were in the Nitrian desert—myself, Evagrius, Albinus, and Ammonius—we were seeking to learn the exact truth of this man’s virtue. Evagrius said: "I would gladly learn from one who knows how to test the mind. If I learn what sort of man he is, I will visit him. But if I do not learn of his way of life <term>politeia</term> <note>original: "πολιτείαν" (politeian) — In a monastic context, this refers to a monk's disciplined way of living, his conduct, or his spiritual regimen.</note>, I will not travel all the way to the mountain."

Hearing this, and having said nothing to anyone, I kept quiet for a day. On the next day, I locked my cell, entrusted myself to God, and set out for the Thebaid <note>A region in Upper Egypt, named after the city of Thebes, which was a major center for early Christian monasticism.</note>. I arrived after eighteen days, part of the time traveling on foot and part by sailing on the river. It was the season of the rising waters <note>This refers to the annual inundation of the Nile, which typically occurred between June and September.</note>, a time when many people fall ill, and I indeed suffered from sickness.

Upon arriving, I found his oratory <term>proseuktikon</term> <note>original: "προσευκτικόν" (proseuktikon) — A specific place or cell designated for prayer.</note> closed. Later, the brothers built a very large oratory there, which could hold about a hundred men, but they would keep it locked with a key, opening it only on Saturday and Sunday.

Having learned the reason why it was closed, I waited quietly until Saturday. I arrived at the second hour <note>Approximately 8:00 AM, as the day began at dawn.</note> for an interview with the righteous man, and I found him sitting by the window. It was through this window that he appeared to give counsel to those who visited him. After greeting me, he spoke through an interpreter: "Where are you from, and why have you come? While we were speaking, the governor of the region, named Alypius, arrived. When he approached, the great man ceased his conversation with me. ### Concerning John of Lycopolis.

There was a certain man named John in the city of Lyco <note>Lycopolis, modern-day Asyut in Egypt. The name means "City of the Wolf."</note>, who from his childhood had learned the trade of carpentry; his brother was a dyer. Later, when he reached the age of twenty-five, he renounced the world <term>apetaxato</term> <note>original: "ἀπετάξατο" — A technical monastic term for the formal renunciation of property and secular life.</note>. Having spent five years in various monasteries, he withdrew alone to the mountain of Lyco, to the very summit, where he made for himself three vaulted chambers <term>tholous</term> <note>original: "θόλους" — Small, dome-shaped or vaulted rooms, often carved into the rock or built of brick.</note>. 

After entering them, he walled himself in. One chamber was where he prayed; the second was where he worked and ate; the third was for his physical necessities <note>A polite reference to a latrine or a space for bodily hygiene.</note>. Having completed thirty years shut away in this manner, receiving what was necessary for his needs through a window from a servant, he was granted the gift of prophecy <term>prorrhēseōs</term> <note>original: "προρρήσεως" — The ability to foretell the future or reveal hidden things; John was famous for being the "Oracle of Egypt."</note>. 

Among his deeds, he sent various prophecies to the blessed Emperor Theodosius <note>Theodosius I (the Great), who ruled 379–395 AD.</note> regarding the usurper Maximus <note>Magnus Maximus, who had seized power in the West; Theodosius defeated him in 388 AD.</note>, foretelling that he would defeat him and return from Gaul. Likewise, he brought good tidings to the Emperor concerning the usurper Eugenius <note>Eugenius was a puppet emperor in the West; Theodosius defeated him at the Battle of the Frigidus in 394 AD.</note>: that he would defeat him, but that he [Theodosius] would reach the end of his life there and leave the kingdom in that place to his son. The fame of this man as a person of great virtue spread far and wide. Therefore, being numbered as if among the angels, Emperor Theodosius held him in prophetic honor. Since...

## OF PALLADIUS
### [The Discovery of the Holy Fool]

...all of them. He said to them, "One is missing, whom the angel showed to me."

They said to him, "We have one who is mad <note>original: "Σαλήν" (Salēn) — A term denoting someone who is "shaken," mentally ill, or acting as a "fool for Christ."</note>. She is in the kitchen." For that is what they call those who suffer in this way. 

The great man <note>referring to Piteroum, the renowned hermit</note> said, "Bring her as well. Let me see her." 

So they went to call her. But she did not obey, having perceived what was happening—for perhaps it had been revealed to her by God. Therefore, they dragged her along by force and said to her, "Saint Piteroum wants to see you." For he was very famous. 

When she was brought before him, the great man gazed upon her face and the rag <note>original: "ῥάκος" — The dishcloth she wore on her head as a mock veil to maintain her appearance of madness.</note> on her head and forehead. And falling at her feet, he said to her: 

"Bless me, Master <term>Abba</term>." <note>original: "ἀββᾶ" — Although she is a woman, Piteroum uses the masculine title for a spiritual father to show her superior rank in the eyes of God.</note>

But she also fell at his feet, saying, "You bless me, my lord." 

Seeing this, all the other sisters were astonished and said to him, "Abba, do not suffer this insult, for she is mad!" 

Saint Piteroum said to them all, "You are the ones who are mad. For she is the Mother <term>Amma</term> <note>original: "ἀμμάς" — A spiritual mother; the female equivalent of 'Abba'.</note> of both you and me. And I pray that I may be found worthy of her portion on the Day of Judgment." 

Hearing these things, they all fell at his feet, weeping and confessing <term>exomologoumenai</term> <note>original: "ἐξομολογούμεναι" — Formal, public acknowledgement of sins.</note> the various ways they had mistreated this holy woman. 

One said, "I always mocked her." Another: "I ridiculed her lowly appearance." Another: "I insulted her while she remained silent." And another again: "I often poured the dishwater over her." Another: "I struck her with blows." And another again: "I was the one who boxed her ears." Another again: "I often put mustard in her nose." <note>A cruel prank meant to cause burning or sneezing, treating her as an object of sport.</note> 

And in short, every one of them reported various insults they had committed against her. So Saint Piteroum, having received their confession and having prayed for them together with her, and having exhorted them at great length...

->About Piteroum<-

Regarding this holy woman, an angel appeared to Saint Piteroum as he was dwelling in the Porphyry Mountain <note>original: "Πορφυρίτῃ" — A remote desert region in Egypt known for its imperial stone quarries and ascetic cells.</note>. Piteroum was an <term>anchorite</term> <note>A monk who lives in complete seclusion or isolation.</note> of the highest reputation for the virtue of his <term>ascesis</term> <note>original: "ἀσκήσεως" — The practice of intense self-discipline and abstinence for spiritual growth.</note>. 

The angel said to him, "Why do you think so highly of your own successes as a pious man while dwelling in such a remote place? Do you wish to see a woman who is more pious than you? Depart for the monastery of the Tabennesiote <note>A famous communal monastic order founded by St. Pachomius in Egypt.</note> women, and there you will find one wearing a diadem <note>original: "διάδημα" — A crown. In this context, the 'crown' is the filthy dishcloth she wore, which the angel recognizes as a mark of spiritual glory.</note> upon her head. She is better than you. For although she struggles against such a great crowd and serves everyone in various ways, her heart has never departed from God, even though she is looked down upon by everyone. You, however, while sitting here, wander through cities in your mind, even though you have never actually set foot in the inhabited world." 

Rising from that place, the great Piteroum came to the monastery of the Tabennesiotes and asked the leaders for permission to cross over to the women's monastery. Because he was a renowned father who had grown old in the ascetic life, they trusted him and brought him across the river. After he had offered a prayer, the great man requested to see all the virgins face to face. When they had all come into the assembly, that specific woman did not appear. Finally, Saint Piteroum said to them, "Bring all of them to me." They replied, "We are all [present...]"

# PALLADIUS

When the woman who had slandered her came to her senses and saw that she had slandered out of malice—and having caused such grief to the sisterhood—she too was unable to bear the weight of the matter; she went and hanged herself. When the elders arrived, the rest of the virgins reported these events. The elder then ordered that no offering <note>original: "προσφορὰν" — Referring to the Eucharistic sacrifice or liturgical prayers offered for the departed.</note> should be performed for either of them. As for the rest of the sisters, because they were aware of the situation and had not sought to peaceably correct the slanderer, but instead believed the things she said, he imposed a penance upon them.

->About the woman who feigned madness.<-

In this same monastery, there was a certain virgin named Isidora. For the sake of Christ, she feigned madness <note>original: "μωρίαν" — The spiritual tradition of the 'Holy Fool' (Salos), where an ascetic acts insane to hide their piety from the world and avoid the temptation of pride.</note> and possession by a demon. She had set out to reach the peak of virtue through these means, humbling and <term>debasing</term> <note>original: "ἐξευτελίζουσα" — To make oneself common or of no value; a deliberate stripping away of social status.</note> herself. The other sisters loathed her to such an extent that they would not even eat with her. She received this treatment with joy. She wandered about the kitchen performing every service for all of them, obeying everyone like a slave in total submission. This blessed woman was, as the saying goes, the "stain" <note>original: "σπίλος" — Literally a spot or blemish. While the sisters saw her as a disgrace to their order, the author suggests she was the one truly fulfilling the Gospel.</note> of the community, fulfilling in her deeds that which is written in the Holy Gospel: *Whoever wants to be great among you, let him be the servant and the helper of all.* <note>A paraphrase of Matthew 20:26 and Mark 10:43.</note> And: *If anyone among you thinks he is wise in this age, let him become a fool, so that he may become wise.* <note>1 Corinthians 3:18.</note> All the other virgins had their hair shorn and wore cowls <note>original: "κεκύλια" — A specific type of monastic hood or head-covering used by the Pachomian monks in Egypt.</note> upon their heads. She, however, merely tied a rag around her head while she performed all her duties. Not one of the four hundred sisters ever saw her eating during her entire life. She never sat at the table...

# LAUSIAC HISTORY
### XVI.

...down; others in the late evening; others after two days; others after three; and others after five; so that each group <note>original: "στοιχεῖον" — literally "element," referring here to the different ranks or classes of monks based on their level of ascetic practice</note> recognizes its own appointed hour. Their labors were organized in the same way: one works in agriculture, another in the garden, another at the forge, another in the bakery, another in the carpenter’s shop, another in the scriptorium <note>original: "γραφεῖον" — a place for copying and preparing manuscripts</note>, another weaving large baskets, and another making what are called *malakia* <note>original: "μαλάκια"</note>—which are small baskets. They also learn all the Scriptures by heart <note>original: "ἀποστηθίζουσι" — literally "to take from the chest," meaning to memorize thoroughly</note>. 

Among these people there is also a monastery of women, numbering about four hundred, which has the same organization and the same way of life <note>original: "πολιτείαν" — the structured "citizenship" or conduct of a monk</note>, with the exception of the sheepskin cloak <note>original: "μηλωτῆς" — a rough garment made of sheepskin, typical of male desert ascetics</note>. These women live on the far side of the Nile River, and the men live opposite them. If a virgin <note>original: "παρθένος" — the standard term for a nun in this period</note> passes away, the other virgins prepare her for burial, carry her out, and place her on the bank of the river. Then the brothers, crossing over in a ferry with palm and olive branches and the singing of psalms, carry her across to their side and bury her in their own tombs. Aside from the priest <note>original: "πρεσβυτέρου"</note> and the deacon, no one ever crosses into the monastery of the women, and even they only do so on the Lord’s Day.

In this monastery of women, the following incident occurred: a secular tailor <note>original: "ῥάπτης κοσμικός" — a layman who was a tailor by trade</note>, having crossed over in ignorance, was looking for wool. One of the younger virgins, going out on her own business—for the place was deserted—encountered him unintentionally and gave him this answer: "We have our own tailors." 

But another sister, having seen this encounter, after some time had passed and a quarrel had broken out, through the instigation of the devil and out of great wickedness and a boiling anger, slandered <note>original: "ἐσυκοφάντησεν"</note> her before the sisterhood because of that meeting. A few others joined in, being carried away by great malice. The girl was deeply grieved at having suffered such slander—a thing that had not even entered her mind...

<meta>This page continues the description of the Pachomian monastic system. # PALLADIUS

This is the first and great monastery where the blessed Pachomius <note>Saint Pachomius (c. 292–348 AD) is credited with founding cenobitic (communal) monasticism, where monks lived together under a Rule rather than as hermits.</note> himself lived, which also gave birth to the other ascetic houses; it contains thirteen hundred men. Among them is the servant of God called Aphthonius, who became a true friend to me. He is now the second-in-command in that monastery. Because he is beyond reproach <note>original: ἀσκανδάλιστον — literally "unscandalizable," meaning a man of such integrity that he would not cause or be caught in a scandal.</note>, they send him to Alexandria for necessities, to sell their handiwork and to purchase what is needed. There are also other monasteries of two hundred and three hundred souls, including one in the city of Panos <note>modern-day Akhmim, Egypt</note> which I entered, where there is a community of three hundred men. In this monastery, I saw fifteen tailors, seven blacksmiths, four carpenters, twelve camel-drivers, and fifteen fullers <note><term>fullers</term> — workers who clean, shrink, and thicken cloth, often using water and clay.</note>. They practice every trade, and with their surplus, they also support the women's monasteries and the prisons.

They also raise pigs. When I criticized this practice <note>The author likely found raising pigs odd or "un-monastic" due to the animal's association with impurity or luxury.</note>, they told me: "In our tradition, we have received the instruction that they should be raised to use up the leftovers <note>original: σιπάσματα — scraps or sweepings</note>, the vegetable trimmings, and the waste thrown out by everyone, so that nothing goes to waste. The pigs are then slaughtered and the meat is sold, but the offal <note>original: ἄκρα — literally 'the extremities' (feet, ears, etc.), considered softer or less valuable parts.</note> is consumed by the sick and the elderly, because the region is poor and overpopulated." For the tribe of the Blemmyes <note>A nomadic people who lived in the Eastern Desert and frequently interacted with or raided the borders of Roman Egypt.</note> lives nearby.

Those on duty for the day rise at dawn; some busy themselves in the kitchen, while others attend to the tables. They set the tables, finishing their work by the third hour <note>approximately 9:00 AM</note>. On each table they place loaves of bread, mustard greens, pickled olives, cow's cheese, the offal of the meat, and fine vegetables. Those who come for the meal enter at the sixth hour <note>noon</note> and eat. Those who are weaker enter at the seventh hour; others at the eighth, others at the ninth, and still others at the tenth.

<meta>catchword: tenth.</meta>

### 16. THE LAUSIAC HISTORY

...of their inclinations, behaviors, and lives, you shall assign to each rank the character of a letter <note>The Pachomian system used letters of the alphabet as a shorthand code to classify the spiritual progress and character of the monks.</note>, so that only the spiritual leaders know what the signs mean. It was also written on the tablet <note>original: δέλτῳ — referring to the bronze tablet the angel allegedly gave to Pachomius containing the Rule.</note> that if a stranger from another monastery should come, following a different rule <note>original: τύπον — "type" or "model" of monastic life.</note>, he shall not eat with them, nor drink with them, nor enter the monastery with them, unless he is found on the road <note>This indicates that while the monastery was exclusive to its rule, hospitality was still mandatory for travelers in need.</note>.

However, as for the person who enters to remain with them permanently, you shall not admit him to the assembly of the sanctuary <note>original: ἀγῶνα ἀδύτων — the "contest of the inner sanctum," likely referring to the full liturgical life or the inner mysteries of the community.</note> for three years. Rather, after he has performed more laborious tasks, let him then enter the arena <note>original: στάδιον — a common athletic metaphor for the disciplined struggle of the ascetic life.</note> after the three-year period. 

While eating, they should cover their heads with their hoods <note><term>koukoullion</term> — a cowl or hood worn by monks, symbolizing humility and the "helmet of salvation."</note>, so that one brother may not see another brother chewing. It is not permitted to speak while eating, nor to let the eye wander anywhere else away from one's plate or the table. 

The angel also prescribed that they should say twelve prayers throughout the day, and twelve at the lighting of the lamps <note>Vespers or the evening prayer service.</note>, and twelve during the night-vigils, and three at the ninth hour <note>The ninth hour corresponds to approximately 3:00 PM.</note>. And when the community is about to eat, the angel prescribed that a psalm should be sung by each rank at every prayer. 

When the great Pachomius objected to the angel that the prayers were too few, the angel said to him: "I have prescribed these so that even the little ones <note>Refers to novices or those who are physically or spiritually weaker.</note> might be able to fulfill the rule <note>original: κανόνα — the "canon" or standard measure of prayer.</note> and not be distressed. For those who are perfect have no need of legislation. For while they are by themselves in their cells, they have devoted their entire lives to the contemplation <note><term>theoria</term> — the highest state of spiritual life, involving the direct "vision" or contemplation of God.</note> of God. But I have given laws to those who lack such discernment <note>original: ἐπιγνώμονα — intuitive knowledge or spiritual understanding.</note>, so that even if they are like rebellious servants, they may fulfill the requirements of their community life through fear of the Master and shine forth with confidence." 

Having commanded these things and fulfilled his service, the angel departed from the great Pachomius. These, then, are the monasteries which mostly follow this rule, numbering in total some seven thousand men.

# OF PALLADIUS

...on which these things were written: "You shall allow each one to eat and drink according to his strength. And in proportion to the strength of those eating, assign them their labors accordingly; and do not prevent them from fasting, nor from eating. However, assign the strenuous tasks to those who are stronger and eat more, and the light and easy tasks to those who are more ascetic <note>original: ἀνασκητικωτέροις — those practicing more rigorous self-denial</note> and weaker. 

And make various cells within the same monastery, and let three stay in each cell. But let the food for everyone be provided under one roof. 

They should sleep not lying down, but having built seats with backs leaning against the wall, and having placed their bedding there, let them sleep sitting up <note>The Pachomian Rule famously required monks to sleep in a seated position in specially constructed stalls to maintain a state of vigil even during rest.</note>. 

At night they shall wear linen tunics <term>lebitones</term> while girded with a belt. Each of them shall have a processed white goatskin <term>melote</term>, without which they shall neither eat nor sit down. 

When entering for the communion of the mysteries of Christ <note>Refers to the Eucharist or Holy Communion.</note> on Saturday or Sunday, they shall loosen their belts, put away the goatskin, and enter with only their hoods. 

He fashioned hoods <term>koukoullia</term> for them that were smooth <note>original: ἅμαλλα — likely meaning without a nap or fleece, signifying a simpler, less comfortable garment</note>, like those worn by children. On these hoods, he commanded that a small brand of a purple cross be placed upon the crown <note>original: τύφῳ — while this usually means 'pride,' in this context it refers to the top or 'tuft' of the hood.</note>. 

He also commanded that there be twenty-four ranks <note>original: τάγματα — 'tagmata', divisions or orders</note> of brothers, according to the number of the twenty-four letters of the Greek alphabet. And he ordered that each rank be given the name of a Greek letter, from Alpha and Beta and so on until Omega. 

This was so that when the superior <term>archimandrite</term> asks and inquires about someone among such a large multitude, he may ask his second-in-command, 'How is the Alpha rank doing?' or 'How is the Beta rank?' Or again, 'Greet the Rho rank,' following a specific code based on the names of the letters. 

To the simpler and more innocent, you shall assign the letter Iota <note>The letter Iota (Ι) is a single straight line, symbolizing a straightforward and uncomplicated character.</note>. To the more difficult and crooked, he assigned the letter Xi <note>The letter Xi (Ξ) is written with three zigzagging or 'crooked' lines, representing a more complex or difficult temperament.</note>; and so on according to the proportion of their disposition...

### Concerning the virgin Piamoun.

A virgin named Piamoun lived all the years of her life with her mother. She ate only every other day <note>original: μίαν παρὰ μίαν — literally "one [day] beside one," a common desert practice of fasting on alternate days.</note> in the evening and spent her time spinning flax. She was granted the gift of prophecy <note>original: προρρήσεως — the ability to foretell or perceive future events.</note>. It happened once in Egypt, during the rising of the Nile, that one village attacked another. For they often fight over the water-allotments <note>original: ὑδρεμερίας — the regulated distribution of Nile water to the fields; these shares were vital for survival and frequently the cause of violent rural disputes.</note>, to the point that murders and bloodshed frequently occur.

A stronger village, therefore, attacked her village. A great crowd of men came against it with spears and clubs, intending to destroy her village. But an angel of the Lord God stood by this blessed woman and revealed the coming attack to her. She sent for the elders of her village and said to them: "Go out to meet those who are coming against you from that other village, so that we who live here might not all perish together. ### Concerning Elias.

A certain man named **Elias** was a most excellent ascetic, a great *lover of virgins* <note>original: Φιλοπάρθενος (Philoparthenos)—a term used here to describe his spiritual devotion to supporting and protecting women in the monastic life.</note>, and one who took great care for the weaker sex. For there are indeed such souls to whom the end of their lives bears witness that they acted entirely according to virtue. This man, taking pity on the circumstances of the female ascetics in the city of **Athribis**, and having sufficient resources, built a large monastery. He gathered all those women who were wandering or displaced into the monastery, providing for them in all things accordingly. He ensured they had every comfort required for their needs, including gardens and the necessary tools for their work; in short, he provided everything that the life of asceticism demands.

However, because these women had been brought together from different walks of life and various personal habits, they were constantly engaged in quarrels with one another. It was therefore necessary for the holy man to serve <unclear>among them</unclear> to bring them into a state of peace. For he had gathered about three hundred women, and he was required to act as a mediator for them for two years, even though he was still at a young age—being roughly thirty or forty years old. 

In this position, he was tempted by sensual pleasure <note>original: ἡδονήν—referring here to sexual temptation or desire arising from his constant proximity to the women.</note>. Departing from the monastery, he wandered through the desert for two days while fasting, praying and saying: "Lord, either kill me so that I do not see them suffering, or take away my passion <note>original: πάθος—the internal drive of desire</note> so that I may care for them in a rational and godly manner." 

When evening came, he fell asleep in the desert. Then, as he himself later related the story, three angels came to him, took hold of him, and said: "Why have you come out from the monastery of the women?" He explained the situation to them, saying: "Because I was afraid that I might eventually harm both them and myself." The angels said to him: "If we strip you of this passion and deliver you from it, will you go back and care for them?" On these terms, he agreed. They then exacted <note>The text here breaks off as they begin to demand a formal oath from him.</note>

<header>16. LAUSIAC HISTORY</header>

...never partaking in anything that led to pleasure, but practicing a most rigorous way of life <note>original: πολιτείαν (politeian)—refers to a monk's "conduct" or "citizenship" in the kingdom of God.</note> of self-control among the women. But later, having been abandoned by the help of God because of her excessive pride—which is the source of all evils—she fell into a terrible ruin. For having opened the window of her cell, she admitted the man who served her, and thus was defiled. This happened to her because she did not maintain her ascetic practice <note>original: ἄσκησιν (askesin)—the disciplined training of a monk.</note> according to a spiritual purpose and love for God, but as a human display—that is, a product of vainglory <note>original: κενοδοξία (kenodoxia)—meaning empty glory or vanity.</note> from a corrupt intention. For when her pious thoughts were distracted by the demon of vainglory, he trained her to pass judgment on others. As she was driven into a frenzy by the demon of pride, and took more and more delight in it, the holy angel who is the guardian of chastity <note>original: σωφροσύνη (sophrosyne)—meaning moderation, self-control, or moral sanity.</note> departed from her.

It is for this reason, O most faithful of men, that I say these things have been recorded: both concerning those who have successfully ordered their lives through virtue, and those who, through negligence, after many labors in an extreme way of life, were mastered by the devil in his various nets. My purpose is that each person, observing the hidden nets of the "Hater of Good" <note>original: μισόκαλος (misokalos)—a common title for the Devil, literally the "Hater of Beauty" or "Hater of Excellence."</note> within their own way of life, might flee from his traps. 

Therefore, although there were many great men and women who at first succeeded in the virtuous life of asceticism, but were ultimately drained by the adversary of all mankind, I shall mention only a few out of the many and pass over the rest in silence. I do this because I feel it would not benefit me to dwell on these cases for too long, and I would be neglecting the excellent athletes of Christ <note>original: ἀθλητῶν (athletōn)—early Christians viewed monks as spiritual athletes competing for a heavenly prize.</note> if I did not instead relate their virtues and their divine ascetic practice.

<header>PALLADIUS</header>

"...Why did you subdue yourself so? To what end does it benefit you, **Ptolemy**, if you have no reward? What sort of reward was sufficient for these labors, and who is the one to repay you? What kind of judgment is it that the Scriptures threaten, when all things exist without a divine providence?" Broken by these satanic words, the most wretched **Ptolemy** is said to be carried along in a distracted state, having lost his natural senses; wandering in **Egypt** until this day, he has given himself over to gluttony <note>original: γαστριμαργίᾳ (gastrimargia)—literally "stomach-madness," one of the primary vices in early monastic thought.</note> and drunkenness, speaking to no one. Instead, he wanders aimlessly through the marketplace, a pitiable sight and a cause for many tears in the eyes of Christians, but a plaything and a mockery to those who do not know our way of life.

This incurable misfortune befell the miserable **Ptolemy** because of his irrational conceit <note>original: οἰήσεως (oieseos)—a term for self-delusion or vanity.</note>. He was deceived by the Ancient One <note>A reference to the Devil.</note> into believing that he possessed knowledge superior to all the holy fathers. Puffed up by this, he became his own enemy and was plunged into this abyss of destruction. He never sought out any of the holy fathers who could have piloted him with wisdom, nor was he healed by their spiritual teaching; instead, he was found to be without a pilot <note>original: ἀκυβέρνητος (akybernetos)—the imagery of a ship without a navigator was a common metaphor for a monk living without a spiritual guide or "abba."</note>. Buffeted by a dark whirlwind, he ran aground at the very bottom of death. He was like a noble and flourishing tree, thick with leaves and fruit, which in a single moment is stripped of everything and shown to be withered; for as it is written: *Those who have no guidance fall like leaves.* <note>A reference to Proverbs 11:14 in the Septuagint version.</note>

...or having recovered and returned once more to the use of his limbs, and having attained a godly mindset and the memory of the heavenly way of life, and having confessed all that had happened to him to the holy fathers, he fell asleep after a few days, before he could put his repentance into action.

->**Concerning Ptolemy**<-

There was another man named **Ptolemy** who lived a life of virtue that is difficult—or rather impossible—to describe. He dwelt beyond **Scetis** <note>A major center of early Christian monasticism in the Egyptian desert, known for its rigorous environment.</note> in the place called **The Ladder** <note>original: Κλίμακα (Klimaka).</note>. This is a place so named where no monk was ever able to dwell, because the water well is eighteen miles <note>original: σημείων (semeion)—referring to Roman miles.</note> away. Therefore, having provided himself with many hair-cloth vessels <note>original: κεράμια κιλίκια (keramia kilikia)—likely pottery jars wrapped in or associated with Cilician goat-hair cloth to preserve moisture.</note>, he carried them there. In the months of **December** and **January**, he collected the dew from the rocks, for it dews heavily in those regions during that time. By filling the vessels with dew, he sustained himself; he lived there for fifteen years, meeting with no one for a long time.

But because he became a stranger to spiritual teaching, and to the company of holy men, and to the benefit of the holy fathers, and to the continuous communion of the mysteries of **Christ** <note>A reference to the Eucharist, or Holy Communion.</note>, he became so maddened and turned aside from the straight path that he began to profess what is said by some of the impious: that "all things happen by chance" <note>original: αὐτομάτισον (automatison)—the philosophical error of believing the universe is governed by random chance rather than divine providence.</note>. This wretched man was then led by the demon of error. Indeed, this man, slow to learn the truth, undertook to say this new thing: that things have no essence, but simply that all things exist because the world is carried along by chance. Then the enemy of the life of all, having fallen with his whole weight into this man's soul, suggested to him, saying: "Since plants are thus, why in vain [do you...]"

## PALLADIUS

...not wishing, because of his pride, to approach even the mysteries <note>original: μυστηρίοις (mysteriois)—referring to the Holy Sacraments or the Eucharist.</note> themselves. But truth is a dear friend. He was exceedingly refined in his way of life <note>original: πολιτείᾳ (politeia)—referring here to his disciplined ascetic conduct.</note>, so much so that many who were familiar with him related the extreme nature of his asceticism. Some said that he often ate only once every three months, being satisfied by the communion of the mysteries alone, and perhaps whatever wild herbs might appear.

I myself experienced a proof of his endurance when I was traveling to **Scetis** with the blessed **Albinus**. Scetis was forty miles <note>original: σημείοις (semeiois)—referring to Roman milestones.</note> away from us. We ate twice and drank water three times during the journey; but he, without tasting anything at all, walked on foot reciting from memory fifteen psalms, then the "Great One" <note>A reference to Psalm 119 (Psalm 118 in the Septuagint), the longest psalm in the Bible.</note>, then the prophet **Isaiah** and parts of **Jeremiah**, then **Luke** the Evangelist, and finally the **Proverbs**. And even with all this, we were unable to catch up with him as he walked.

At last, however, this man was seized by the activity of the evil demon. As if driven by a most violent fire, he could no longer bear to remain in his cell. He went off to **Alexandria**—perhaps even this was according to a divine arrangement <note>term: <term>Divine Providence</term> (original: θείαν οἰκονομίαν/theian oikonomian)—the idea that God can use even a person's failures to bring about their ultimate salvation.</term></note>—where, as the saying goes, "he drove out one nail with another." <note>original: ἥλῳ τὸν ἧλον ἐξέκρουσεν—a Greek proverb meaning to cure one ill with another, or to replace one obsession with another.</note> For he fell voluntarily into a state of indifference <note>original: ἀδιαφορίᾳ (adiaphoria)—here meaning a total neglect of his former religious rules.</note>, finding afterward an involuntary salvation. He began to frequent theaters and horse races, and spent his time in taverns; being thus given over to gluttony and drunkenness, he fell also into the mire of lust for women. 

As he was planning to commit a sin, consorting constantly with a certain actress and discussing the very things that would lead to his ruin, it happened by some providence that a carbuncle <note>original: ἄνθραξ (anthrax)—not the modern disease, but a malignant, burning ulcer or boil.</note> developed upon his genitals. He suffered such rot for a period of six months that his members became putrid and fell off of their own accord. Afterward...

<meta>This page concludes the account of a monk (likely Ptolemy, mentioned on the previous page) who was restored from his pride, and begins the narrative of Heron of Alexandria. It establishes the author's rationale for including stories of spiritual failure as a warning to readers, paralleling the presence of the Tree of Knowledge in the Garden of Eden.</meta>

# 16. LAUSIAC HISTORY

...and even today. When the holy fathers had bound him in iron chains for fifteen <gloss>15</gloss> years, they cured him, breaking down his pride through prayers, a varied kind of indifference <note>original: ἀδιαφορήσει (adiaphoresei)—here implying a treatment of ignoring his erratic behavior to humble his ego.</note>, and a more secluded way of life; for, as it is said: "contraries are the cures for contraries." <note>A classic medical principle, *contraria contrariis curantur*, suggesting that pride is cured by humiliation, or excess by restraint.</note> It is necessary also to include the lives of those who were deceived in this little book for the safety of those who read it, just as the tree of the knowledge of good and evil was planted naturally among the holy plants of paradise. This is so that, if anyone should ever succeed in their achievements, they should not think too highly of their virtue. For often virtues themselves become the starting point of a fall, when one does not achieve them with spiritual wisdom for the purpose of piety, according to what is written: *I have seen a righteous man perishing in his righteousness. And this too is vanity.* <note>A reference to Ecclesiastes 7:15.</note>

->**About Heron.**<-

There was a certain **Heron**, a neighbor of mine, an **Alexandrian** by birth; he was a refined young man, naturally gifted in intellect and pure in his way of life. He also, after great labors and much sweat, and a life of extreme virtue, was raised to a dizzying height by the vanity of his own conceits. From there, he was cast down in a fall that was pitiable to all, and he was spiritually broken. Moved by empty pride against the holy fathers, he thought highly of himself, insulting everyone—and among them, he insulted **Evagrius** <note>**Evagrius Ponticus** (345–399 AD), a highly influential monastic teacher and theologian known for his works on prayer and the "eight evil thoughts."</note>, saying: "Those who follow your teaching are deceived. For," he claimed, "one ought not use other teachers besides **Christ**." He even misused the testimony of Scripture to serve the ruined aim of his own folly, citing as proof that the Savior himself says: *Do not call anyone "teacher" on the earth.* <note>A reference to Matthew 23:8-10.</note> He was so darkened in his reasoning by the empty glory of his pride that, having fallen from the heights to the depths, he too was later bound in irons...

# PALLADIUS

...to the brotherhood. Therefore, our lord Macarius the elder, having received [some gifts], sent a handful to each man in his cell; he also sent some to this Valens. Valens, however, seized the one who brought the gift, insulted him, and struck him, saying: "Go and tell Macarius: I am not inferior to you, that you should send me blessings." <note>original: εὐλογίας (*eulogias*)—these were "blessings" in the form of small gifts or blessed bread sent between monks as a sign of communion and respect.</note> Knowing then that he had been led astray, the holy Macarius went to him after one day to plead with him, saying: "Brother Valens, you have been mocked by a demon; stop, and pray to God." But Valens paid no heed to the advice of father Macarius. When he would not listen to his plea, Macarius then departed, greatly grieving over Valens's defeat.

The demon, having become fully convinced that Valens was entirely persuaded by the delusion, then came forward. The devil transformed himself into the Savior and appeared to him at night, accompanied by his own demons in a vision as though they were a thousand lamp-bearing angels. He showed him what appeared to be a fiery wheel, in the center of which the Savior seemed to be manifest. Then one of the demons, appearing as an angel, said to him: "Christ is pleased with your way of life <note>original: πολιτείᾳ (*politeia*)—a technical term for the disciplined, ascetic life of a monk.</note> and the boldness of your conduct, and he has come to see you. Therefore, come out of your cell, and do nothing else but stand at a distance; and when you see him standing in the center of everyone, bow down and worship <note>original: προσκύνησον (*proskyneson*)—to prostrate oneself in total submission.</note> him, then return to your cell."

So Valens went out, and seeing the ranks of lamp-bearers, he fell down and worshipped the Antichrist from about a stadium <note>A unit of distance roughly equal to 600 feet.</note> away. The deceived man was so shattered in his mind <note>original: ἐφρενοβλάβησεν (*ephrenoblabesen*)—literally "suffering damage to the wits," used here for spiritual insanity.</note> that the next day he went into the church and, while the entire brotherhood was assembled, said: "I have no need of communion <note>original: κοινωνίας (*koinonias*)—the Holy Eucharist or the shared sacramental life of the monks.</note>; I have seen Christ today."

<header>XVI. LAUSIAC HISTORY</header>

...having exhorted him, and having urged him with words of patience, he encouraged him to endure through his afflictions. I have related these things so that we might not be astonished when we see certain holy men fall into such terrible trials.

### Concerning Valens.

There was a certain man named Valens, a Palestinian by birth, but a **Corinthian** in his spirit, being puffed up in his thoughts. For the blessed Apostle Paul, when writing to the Corinthians, attributed this passion of "puffing up" to them, saying: *And you are puffed up.* <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 5:2. The author uses "Corinthian" as a shorthand for someone struggling with spiritual pride or intellectual arrogance.</note> This same Valens arrived in the desert and lived among us for many years. Having lived a life of great hardship and achieved the highest peak of the **ascetic life** <note>original: ἀσκήσεως (*askeseos*)—referring to the rigorous physical and spiritual discipline practiced by the desert monks.</note>, he was nonetheless enticed by a demon through vanity and delusion. He leaped to the very summit of pride, so that he was led astray by demons. For, being lifted up by the destructive passion of conceit and deceiving his own mind little by little, he convinced himself that angels were meeting with him and preparing everything for his daily service.

One day, at least as his companions used to recount, while he was working on his baskets in the deep darkness of evening, he dropped the needle with which he was sewing the rows onto the floor. When he could not find it, the demon caused a lamp to appear for him, and so he found the needle he had lost. Being puffed up again with empty arrogance, he fell even deeper into delusion and harbored grand thoughts. The wretched man was so puffed up that he even came to despise the **holy mysteries** <note>original: μυστηρίων (*mysterion*)—the sacraments, specifically the Eucharist or Holy Communion.</note> of Christ. It happened that some strangers arrived and brought offerings of dried fruits to the church—

# PALLADIUS

->### Concerning Stephen.<-

There was a certain man named Stephen, a Libyan by birth from the regions of Marmarica and Mareotis <note>Marmarica and Mareotis were border regions in North Africa, west of the Nile Delta, famous for their monastic settlements.</note>, who lived as a hermit for sixty years. Having reached the very summit of **ascetic discipline** and discernment, he was granted a special gift: anyone who came to him burdened by any kind of sorrow or grief would depart from him comforted and free of pain. He was an acquaintance of the blessed Anthony <note>St. Anthony the Great (c. 251–356 AD), the most famous of the Desert Fathers.</note> and lived even into our own times. I did not meet him personally because of the great length of the journey, but the companions of the holy Ammonius <note>Ammonius was one of the "Tall Brothers," famous monks in the Egyptian desert.</note>, who did meet him, related his story to me.

They told me they found him suffering from a grievous illness of the body. A "canker" <note>original: Φαγέδαινα (*Phagedaina*)—literally "the devourer," a medical term for a rapidly spreading, gangrenous ulcer.</note> had formed on his genitals. We found him, they said, being treated by a physician; even then, the holy man was working with his hands and weaving palm leaves, conversing with us while the rest of his body was being surrendered to medical treatment. He possessed such a grace of **endurance** <note>original: ὑπομονῆς (*hypomones*)—the spiritual virtue of patient, steadfast waiting under pressure.</note> that it seemed as if someone else’s body were being cut. Even as his own limbs were being amputated like mere hair, he remained unperturbed through the excellence of his divine asceticism.

They told me that they were deeply distressed and saddened, wondering how a man of such great character could have fallen into such a terrible affliction and be forced to undergo such surgeries. Blessed Stephen, perceiving their thoughts, said to them: "Do not be spiritually harmed, my children, by this matter. For nothing that God does is evil; everything is for a good end. Perhaps these members of mine are paying a penalty to the Lord, and it is better to settle the account here rather than after departing from this earthly arena." <note>original: σταδίου (*stadiou*)—referring to the "stadium" or race-course, a common metaphor for the struggle of a Christian life.</note> In this way he...

<header>CH. 16 LAUSIAC HISTORY 77</header>

"...I once saw a young woman in my youth, during the summer, gathering stalks in the field <note>original: καλαμωμένην (*kalamomenen*)—the act of gleaning or gathering leftover grain after a harvest.</note>. The image of her seemed to sit upon my knees, and it stirred me to such an extent that I imagined I had actually been intimate with her. Driven to a frenzy of rage at myself, I struck her with a blow of my hand, and at once she vanished. And believe me when I say this: for two years afterward, I could not endure the **foul stench** of my own hand.

Becoming faint-hearted and greatly discouraged because of this, and utterly despairing of my own soul, I went out into the deep desert to wander. There I found a small asp, and taking it up, I applied it to my genitals so that I might be bitten by it and die. I thrust the head of the beast against my members—since I viewed them as the cause of my temptation—having bared them for that purpose. Yet, even then, I was not bitten, for I was protected by the **providence of grace** <note>In monastic theology, "providence" refers to God's active intervention and care in the lives of the faithful.</note>.

After this, I heard a voice speaking in my mind: 'Go your way, Pachon, and keep up the struggle. For I allowed you to be pursued to such an extent so that you would not become **high-minded**, as if you were able to overcome the demon by your own power. Now that you have recognized your own weakness, never put your confidence in your own way of life <note>original: πολιτείᾳ (*politeia*)—a technical term for an ascetic's rigorous daily discipline and spiritual conduct.</note>, but always run to the help of God.'

Having been thus reassured, I returned to my cell. From then on I remained there with courage, no longer troubled by the war of **obsessive lust** <note>original: γυναικομανίαν (*gynaikomanian*)—literally 'woman-madness,' referring here to a spiritual affliction of intrusive sexual thoughts.</note>, and I lived in peace from that battle for the rest of my days. The demon, seeing my contempt for him and being put to shame, no longer approached me."

With these words concerning his wrestling matches against Satan, the holy Pachon strengthened me. Having "anointed" me <note>original: ἀλείψας (*aleipsas*)—a metaphor from the ancient gymnasium where athletes were oiled before a wrestling match; here it means spiritual preparation.</note> for greater labors and having taught me how to easily endure the war of the **demon of fornication**, he sent me away, exhorting me to act like a man in all things.

...here. But rather, this happens to you out of your zeal for virtue, being brought upon you by the adversary <note>original: τοῦ ἐναντίου (*tou enantiou*) — a common monastic term for the Devil or a spiritual opponent.</note>. For the **war of lust** <note>original: ὁ πόλεμος ὁ πορνικός (*ho polemos ho pornikos*)</note> is threefold: sometimes the flesh attacks us when it is well-fed <note>original: εὐπαθοῦσα (*eupathousa*) — literally "luxuriating" or living in comfort.</note>; at other times the passions rise up against us through our own thoughts; and sometimes the demon himself tyrannizes us through envy. For I, having observed much, have found this to be true.

Look at me, as you see an old man. This is my fortieth year in this **cell** <term>cell: a simple, often isolated room or cave used by a monk for prayer and living</term>, caring for my own salvation, and even at this age, I am tempted until this very moment. And he swore, saying that for twelve years, after I had reached my fiftieth year, the demon allowed me no rest by night or by day, attacking me.

Thinking, therefore, that God had abandoned me—which was why I was being condemned in this way—I chose to die an irrational death <note>original: ἀλόγως (*alogos*) — meaning to die like an animal or without the use of reason, implying suicide.</note> rather than to be shamefully disgraced by a passion of the body. And going out of my cell and wandering through the desert, I found the **cave of a hyena** <note>original: ὑαίνης (*hyaines*)</note>. I placed myself inside that cave, naked, during the day, so that when the beasts came out, they might devour me.

When evening came, as it is written in the Scriptures: *The sun knows its going down. You appoint darkness, and it becomes night* <note>A quotation from Psalm 104:19-20 (Septuagint 103). The monk views the natural world through the lens of the Psalms.</note>. *In it all the beasts of the forest will prowl. The young lions roar for their prey and seek their food from God.* When the beasts came out at that hour, both the male and the female smelled me and licked me from my feet to my head. And just as I expected to be eaten, they departed from me.

Having lain there through the whole night, I was not eaten; I reasoned that God had surely spared me. So I returned again to my cell. After waiting for only a few days, the demon attacked me again more violently than before, so that I was almost driven to blasphemy. For he transformed himself into an **Ethiopian girl** <note>original: Αἰθιόπισσαν κόρην (*Aithiopissan koren*). In early Christian monastic literature, demons were frequently depicted as "Ethiopians." This was a common literary trope of the period used to symbolize "darkness" or the "foreign" nature of temptation, reflecting the cultural biases of the Mediterranean world at the time.</note> whom...

...I do not know where they went <note>original: οὐκ οἶδα (*ouk oida*) — The author, Palladius, is concluding a story about Paul the Simple.</note>. And immediately the spirit of the demon went out and was transformed into a massive **dragon** <note>original: δράκοντα (*drakonta*) — often used in early Christian literature to describe a physical manifestation of a demonic entity or a great serpent.</note> about seventy cubits <note>A cubit is roughly 18 inches; this dragon is described as over 100 feet long.</note> in length. It went slithering away into the **Red Sea** <note>original: τὴν θάλασσαν τὴν ἐρυθράν (*tēn thalassan tēn erythran*)</note>. This was so that the word spoken by the Holy Spirit might be fulfilled: *In the one being revealed, to whom shall I announce that I am righteous?* And again in another place: *Upon whom shall I look?* says the Lord. *Upon the humble and the quiet, and the one who trembles at my words.* <note>A reference to Isaiah 66:2.</note> For by dawn, the lesser demons are cast out by those men who are leaders in the faith; but the leading demons, in turn, are put to flight by the **humble** <note>original: ταπεινῶν (*tapeinōn*) — humility is presented here as the most powerful spiritual weapon, capable of defeating high-ranking demons that "stronger" men cannot conquer through power alone.</note>. These are the miracles of the **Simple One** <note>Paul the Simple, a famous disciple of Anthony the Great known for his child-like faith.</note>, and there are many more and greater than these. He was called "the Simple" by the entire brotherhood.

# Of Pachon

A certain man named **Pachon** <note>A desert father known for his endurance against spiritual temptation.</note>, having reached about seventy years of age, lived in **Scetis** <term>Scetis: the Wadi El Natrun in Egypt, a famous center of early Christian monasticism</term>. It happened that I <note>Palladius, the author, now recounts his own struggle with temptation.</note>, being troubled by a demon and gripped by a desire for women, was in great distress because of both my thoughts and **nocturnal fantasies** <note>original: φαντασίας τὰς νυκτερινάς (*phantasias tas nykterinas*) — a common term for dreams or mental images that tempted monks.</note>. I was on the verge of leaving the desert because this temptation and passion were driving me so violently. I did not disclose the matter to the lesser fathers, nor even to my teacher **Evagrius** <note>Evagrius Ponticus (345–399 AD), a highly influential monk and scholar known for his psychological analysis of spiritual struggle.</note>. Instead, I went secretly into the deep desert and for fifteen days met with the fathers at Scetis who were growing old in the wilderness. Among those I encountered was Pachon. Finding him to be pure-hearted and more advanced in the **ascetic life** <note>original: ἀσκητικώτερον (*askētikōteron*)</note>, I dared to lay bare the state of my mind to him. And this holy man said to me: "Do not let this thing seem strange to you, for you do not suffer it because of laziness. Both your location and your lack of basic needs bear witness to you, as does the fact that there are no meetings with women here..."

->**PALLADIUS**<-

### 28.
In prayer, having summoned the demon-possessed man, he said: "Abba Anthony <note>original: ὁ ἀββᾶς Ἀντώνιος (*ho abbas Antōnios*) — Anthony the Great, the 'Father of Monasticism' and Paul's mentor.</note> has said, ‘Depart from the man so that he may glorify the Lord.’" But the demon, with blasphemous insults, cried out: "I will not depart, you gluttonous old man, you trifler!" Paul then took his **sheepskin cloak** <note>original: μηλωτήν (*mēlotēn*) — a rough garment made of sheep’s wool, typical of the desert hermits.</note> and struck him across the back, saying: "Abba Anthony has said, ‘Depart.’" But the demon insulted Anthony even more fiercely along with Paul, saying: "These **lentil-eaters** <note>original: πολιοφάγοι (*poliophagoi*) — a derogatory term used by the demon for the monks, referring to their meager diet of boiled lentils or pulses.</note>, these lethargic fools, these greedy ones who are never satisfied with what they have—what do you have to do with us? Why do you tyrannize us?" Finally, Paul said to him: "Depart, or I am going to tell Christ. By Jesus, if you do not depart, I am going right now to tell Christ, and he will have something to do to you!" The fierce demon even blasphemed Jesus, shouting: "I will not depart!"

Being moved to anger by the demon’s **insolence** <note>original: ῥύπῳ (*rhypō*) — literally 'filth' or 'moral foulness,' referring to the demon's blasphemous language.</note>, Holy Paul went out from his dwelling at the very hour of **high noon** <note>original: σταθερὰν μεσημβρίαν (*statheran mesēmbrian*) — the point when the sun is directly overhead and the heat is most intense.</note>. The heat of the Egyptians in those places is very nearly like that of the **Babylonian furnace** <note>A reference to the Book of Daniel, where three youths were cast into a fiery furnace; the desert heat is described here as a literal trial by fire.</note>. Standing like a pillar—he who was himself a pillar of heaven—upon a rock on the mountain, he prayed, speaking in this way:

> "You see, Jesus Christ, who was crucified under Pontius Pilate, that I will not descend from this rock, I will not eat, and I will not drink until I die, unless you hear me now and cast this demon out from the man, and make him free from that unclean spirit from this moment on."

While the simple and humble-minded Paul was still speaking to Jesus, and before the words of his pure mouth were even finished, the demon cried out from down near the cell: "I am going! I am departing! I am driven out by force and tyranny! I am withdrawing from the man and will no longer approach him. The **humility** <note>original: ταπεινὸν (*tapeinon*) — humility is the central theme of this story; the demon cannot withstand the monk's lack of ego.</note> and simplicity of Paul drives me away in flight, and where—

...[how to speak] I do not know; however, those things which I saw you doing, I also do with ease. Then Anthony said to him on the following day: "In the name of the Lord, behold, you have become a monk." Because the blessed and great Anthony <note>original: Ἁντώνιος (*Antōnios*) — Anthony the Great, the famed desert father.</note> was exceedingly assured in all things concerning him—that the servant of the Lord, Paul the Simple <note>original: Παῦλος ὁ ἄπλαστος (*Paulos ho aplastos*) — 'aplastos' suggests someone who is unformed by worldly craftiness, guileless, or "simple."</note>, was perfect in soul, being naturally very simple—after a specified number of months, with the grace of God assisting the blessed Anthony, he built a cell <note>original: κέλλαν (*kellan*) — a small, solitary dwelling for a monk.</note> for him about three or four miles <note>original: σημείων (*sēmeiōn*) — literally 'signs' or milestones, referring here to Roman miles.</note> away from his own.

And he said to him: "Behold, with the power of Christ assisting you, you have become a monk. Live by yourself from now on, so that you may also gain experience with demons." So Paul the Simple, having lived by himself for one year, was granted a spiritual gift <note>original: χαρίσματος (*charismatos*) — a divine gift or favor, often involving miraculous powers.</note> over demons and all kinds of diseases, having achieved the peak of ascetic virtue.

One day, therefore, a young man who was most terribly possessed by a demon was brought to the blessed Anthony; he possessed a most cruel **ruling demon** <note>original: ἀρχικὸν... δαίμονα (*archikon... daimona*) — a demon of high rank or a 'prince' among spirits, considered more difficult to exorcise.</note>, who even blasphemed heaven itself. The great Anthony, having prayed for the young man, said to those who brought him: "This is not my work. For I have not yet been granted the gift of authority over the rank of the ruling demons; rather, this is the gift of Paul the Simple." 

So the blessed Anthony went to the worthy Paul and brought the man to him, and said: "Abba Paul <note>original: ἀββᾶ Παῦλε (*abba Paule*) — 'Abba' is a respectful title for a monk, meaning 'Father.'</note>, cast out this demon from the man, so that he may return to his own home in good health and glorify the Lord." Paul said to him: "What about you?" Anthony replied: "I am not at leisure; I have work to do." And leaving the boy there, the great Anthony returned again to his own cell. So the innocent old man, having stood aside and prayed...

# PALLADIUS

Paul said to him: “As it seems best to you, Father <note>original: ἀββᾶ (*abba*) — A title of respect for a spiritual father or elder monk.</note>.” And this again moved Anthony even more—the fact that Paul did not run forward eagerly at the announcement of food, but instead left the authority over the matter to him. Anthony then said to the old man: “Set the table.” And he obeyed. Anthony brought the bread and placed on the table four dried barley loaves <note>original: παξαμάτας (*paxamatas*) — Twice-baked bread or rusks, common in monastic diets for their long shelf life.</note>, each weighing about six ounces. He soaked one for himself—for they were very dry—and three for Paul. Anthony struck up a psalm which he knew by heart, and having sung it twelve times, he then prayed twelve times <note>The number twelve was a common liturgical division in early Egyptian monasticism, symbolizing the hours of the day or the apostles.</note>, so that in this way he might test Paul’s endurance. But the old man joined in prayer with the Great One <note>original: τῷ μεγάλῳ (*tō megalō*) — Refers to Anthony the Great.</note> even more eagerly. For he preferred, I think, to herd scorpions than to live with an adulteress <note>This refers to Paul’s background: he fled to the desert after discovering his wife’s infidelity; the author suggests the rigors of the desert were easier than that domestic betrayal.</note>. After the twelve prayers, they sat down to eat, as evening was already deep.

Now, when the Great Anthony had eaten his one dried loaf, he touched no other. But the old man, eating more slowly, still had some of the loaf he had started. Anthony therefore waited until he should finish, and then said to him: “Eat another loaf, little father <note>original: παπία (*papia*) — A term of endearment or respect for an elderly person, similar to "grandpa" or "dear elder."</note>.” Paul said to him: “If you eat, I will also. But if you do not eat, I will not eat.” Anthony said to him: “It is enough for me, for I am a monk.” Paul answered the Great One: “It is enough for me too; for I also wish to become a monk <note>original: μοναχός (*monachos*) — literally "solitary" or "single-minded."</note>.” They rose again and performed twelve prayers, and sang twelve psalms. And after the prayers, they slept for a short while during the first watch of sleep. Then they rose again to sing psalms from midnight until daybreak. 

When the Great Anthony saw that the old man followed him eagerly in every part of the ascetic life <note>original: τὴν τῆς ἀσκήσεως πολιτείαν (*tēn tēs askēseōs politeian*) — literally "the citizenship of the training"; refers to the daily conduct and discipline of a monk.</note>, he said to him again: “See, brother, if you are able to do this every day, then stay with me.” Paul said to him: “If you ever have anything more to show me <note>The sentence continues on the following page.</note>...”

...[for him]. And as he could not endure his presence, Anthony closed the door and did not come out for three days because of him, not even for his own needs. But the old man <note>The "old man" refers to Paul, who was famously older than Anthony when he entered the desert, earning him the nickname "the Simple."</note> remained there, not departing. Therefore, on the fourth day, as necessity forced him, Anthony opened the door and came out; and seeing him again, he said to Paul, "Go away from here, old man! Why do you trouble me? You cannot stay here." Paul said to him, "It is impossible for me to die anywhere else but here."

Then Anthony, looking around and seeing that he carried nothing for food—neither bread, nor water, nor anything else—and that he had endured fasting for four days, the Great Anthony reasoned that he might perhaps die, being inexperienced in fasting, and so stain his own soul with guilt. Thus, he finally received him in.

And Anthony took up such a disciplined way of life <note>original: πολιτεία (*politeia*) — In a monastic context, this refers to the "citizenship" or conduct of a monk, his ascetic rule of life.</note> through hardship <note>original: σκληραγωγία (*sklēragōgia*) — Literally "hard-leading," referring to severe physical discipline or asceticism.</note> in those days, the likes of which he had not practiced even in the beginnings of his youth. And having soaked palm shoots <note>original: θάλλους ἐκ φοινίκων (*thallous ek phoinikōn*) — Used for weaving baskets and mats, a staple labor for desert monks meant to keep the mind focused and the body humble.</note>, he said to Paul, "Take these, and weave a basket <note>original: σπυρίδα (*spyryda*) — A large basket or hamper.</note>, just as you see me doing." The old man wove until the ninth hour <note>Roughly 3:00 PM, the traditional time for the daily monastic meal.</note>, finishing fifteen fathoms <note>original: ὀργυιὰς (*orgyias*) — A unit of length based on the span of outstretched arms, roughly six feet.</note> of weaving with great labor.

But when Great Anthony beheld the weaving, he pretended to be displeased, and reproaching him, said, "You have not woven it well; unweave it, and weave it again from the beginning." Although Paul was fasting and entering his fourth day without food, and was an elderly man, Anthony brought such distress upon him so that the old man, becoming vexed, might flee from Anthony and the life of the monks. But Paul both unwove it and wove the same shoots again, though it was much more difficult because they were crumpled <note>original: ἐρυσιδωμένους (*erysidōmenous*) — Meaning wrinkled or shriveled from the previous weaving, making them brittle and hard to handle.</note> from the first braiding. 

Therefore, when Great Anthony saw that the old man did not grumble, nor lose heart, nor show even the slightest indignation, nor even change his facial expression at all, he was moved to compassion for him. ### Concerning Paul the Simple.

The holy servant of Christ, Hierax, and also Cronius, along with many other brothers, used to relate the story of the man I am about to speak of. They told of a certain Paul, a country farmer who was extraordinarily innocent and simple <term>simple (haplous) — In this context, it refers to a person who is guileless, sincere, and without social pretense or complexity.</term> in his ways. He was joined in marriage to a very beautiful woman who was, however, wicked in her character; she had managed to hide her infidelities for a very long time. One day, returning suddenly from the field to his house, Paul found her and another man acting shamefully—Providence having guided Paul there for his own benefit. 

Upon witnessing his wife and her lover together, Paul laughed with a certain gravity <note>The Greek term "gelasas semnon" suggests a dry, dignified, or even ironic laugh rather than one of amusement.</note> and called out to them, saying: "Good, good! Truly, by Jesus, it matters nothing to me. I shall no longer take her as my wife. Go then; you may have her and her children, for I am departing to become a monk." Without saying another word to anyone, he ran for eight stages <note>original: monas — This refers to resting stations or a day's journey.</note> and came to the blessed Anthony, and knocked at his door.

The blessed Anthony came out and asked him, "What do you want?" 

Paul said to him, "I wish to become a monk."

Anthony answered and said, "You are a sixty-year-old man; you cannot become a monk here. Rather, go away to a village, work, and live a life of labor, giving thanks to God. For you cannot endure the afflictions of the desert." 

The old man answered again, saying: "Whatever you teach me, that I will do." 

Anthony said to him: "I have told you that you are an old man and you cannot. Go away; if you truly wish to be a monk, go into a cenobium <term>cenobium — A communal monastery where monks lived together under a rule, which was considered less physically demanding than the solitary life of a hermit.</term> with many brothers who can bear with your weakness. For I sit here as a monk eating only once every five days, and even then, I am hungry." 

With these words and other daunting speech, he tried to test Paul’s resolve...

### A Narrative of the Blessed Anthony.

**C**ronius also related this: that on the night we finished the business concerning the blessed Eulogius and Anthony dismissed him, the Great man told us: "I prayed that the entire place of the righteous and the sinners might be revealed to me. And I beheld a tall, black giant reaching up to the clouds, having his hands stretched out into the sky. And beneath him was a lake, the size of a sea, and I saw souls flying up like birds. And as many as flew over his hands and his head were saved by angels. But as many as were struck by his hands fell into the lake. Then a voice came to me, saying: 'These souls which you see flying over the giant's head and his hands are the souls of the righteous, who are saved by the angels and led into **Paradise**. But those struck by the hands of the black one are plunged into **Hades** <note>original: "Haden" — In this Christian context, it refers to the place of the dead or a state of punishment.</note>; these are the ones who obeyed the desires of the flesh and followed after malice.'"

Being thus persuaded, they quickly departed and came to their own cell in perfect love. And within forty days, the blessed Eulogius died, departing to the Lord. And within another three days, the man who was maimed in body but strong in soul also died, entrusting his spirit into the hands of God. 

After spending some time in the regions around the **Thebaid** <term>Thebaid — A region in Upper Egypt, named after the city of Thebes, which became a famous desert retreat for early Christian monks.</term>, Cronius went down to the monasteries of **Alexandria**. Having learned this, Cronius was amazed; and taking a Gospel book to ensure the faith of his listeners, he placed it in the midst of the brothers and swore an oath to them, recounting the prophecy...

"I found this maimed man <term>maimed man (lelobēmenos) — A term used for someone with severe physical disabilities or disfigurement, often living as a social outcast.</term> cast out in the marketplace, neglected by everyone. Having taken pity on him, I prayed to God that the grace of endurance might be granted to me for his sake. I gave my right hand <note>original: "δεξιὰς" — a formal gesture representing a solemn oath or covenant</note> to Christ that I would nurse him in his illness, so that I might be saved through him, and he might find rest through me. We have been together in the same place for fifteen years now, just as it was surely revealed to your holiness; but I do not know of any wrong he has suffered from me. Yet after so many years, he now troubles me to the extreme. I have considered casting him out, as he himself is forcing me to this point. For this reason, I came to your holiness, so that you might advise me what I ought to do, and pray for me, for he troubles me terribly."

The great Anthony said to him in a very heavy and stern voice: "Would you cast him out, Eulogius? But He who made him does not abandon him. Do you reject him? God will raise up someone better than you, and He will gather him in." 

Upon hearing these things, Eulogius fell silent and stood transfixed <note>original: "κατέπηξεν" — literally 'frozen' or 'fixed' in place, as if struck by the weight of Anthony's words.</note>. It happened the next day that the Great One arrived late in the evening, as Cronius related, wrapped in a leather cloak. Coming then into the monastery, he went in by himself. He had this custom of calling Macarius <note>Macarius of Alexandria, a disciple of Anthony.</note> and asking him: "Brother Macarius, have any brothers come here?" Macarius answered: "They have come." And the Great One asked: "Are they **Egyptians**, or **Jerusalemites**?" 

The Great One had given him a sign, saying: "When you see some simpler <note>original: "ἀπρακτοτέρους" — literally 'less active' or 'unrefined,' referring to simple monks or laborers.</note> people arrive, say they are **Egyptians**. But when you see some who are more devout and learned, say they are **Jerusalemites**." 

So, according to his custom, the Great One asked, saying to his disciple Macarius: "Are the brothers Egyptians, or Jerusalemites?" Macarius answered him, saying: "They are a mixture." Now, when Macarius would say they were **Egyptians**, the Great One would say to him: "Prepare some lentils <note>A simple, quick meal indicating a short visit.</note> for them and give them to eat," and he would offer a single prayer for them and dismiss them. But when he said they were **Jerusalemites**, he would sit through the entire night and speak with them about the things necessary for salvation. 

On that evening, then, having sat down, the Great One called them all together. Although no one had told him what name the **scholar** <term>scholar (scholastikos) — A term for a person of learning, often a lawyer or a gentleman of leisure who has turned to the spiritual life.</term> had, late in the evening he called to him, saying: "**Eulogius, Eulogius, Eulogius.**" 

Though he had spoken it three times, the scholar Eulogius did not answer, thinking that someone else was being called by that name...
