Take me away, throw me back in the marketplace where you found me; there I had rest." But Eulogius said: "I beseech you, be healed <note>In this context, meaning 'be pacified' or 'restored to peace of mind.'</note> if you are grieving in anything, great lord." But the maimed man, becoming wild with rage against him, said: "I can no longer endure your deceitful and ironic flattery. I am not pleased with this life of yours, which is so constrained and stingy. I want to eat meat!"

And the long-suffering Eulogius brought him meat. But seeing it, the ill-tempered man cried out again: "I am not satisfied being with you alone; I want crowds!" Eulogius said to him: "I will bring you a multitude of brothers right now." 

But he, again becoming bitter, said: "Woe is me, miserable man! Must I see your face, and now you bring me others like you, those **eaters-of-idleness** <note>original: "ἀργοφάγους" — A derogatory term for monks, suggesting they eat without performing manual labor.</note>?" And tossing himself about, he cried out with a disordered voice: "I do not want this! I want the marketplace! Oh, the violence! Throw me back where you found me!" 

I tell you that if he had hands, he would not have hesitated to use a noose or to kill himself with a sword. Since the demon had made him so wild, after these events, Eulogius went to the **ascetics** <term>ascetics (askētas) — Monks or practitioners who lived a life of rigorous self-discipline and prayer.</term> who were his neighbors and said to them: "What shall I do? For this maimed man has driven me to despair." They said to him: "Why?" He replied: "He treats me terribly, and I do not know what to do. If I cast him out, I have **given my right hand** <note>original: "δεξιὰς ἔδωκα" — A formal idiom meaning to make a solemn oath or a sacred pledge.</note> to God, and I am afraid. But if I do not cast him out, he gives me bad days and nights. I do not know what to do with him." 

They said to him: "While the Great One still lives"—for that is how they called **Saint Anthony**—"go up to him, putting the maimed man into a boat, and bring him to the monastery, and wait..."

### Page 64

...to him, and he prays to God, and makes a **Covenant** <term>Covenant (Diathēkē) — A solemn religious vow or formal contract made between a believer and God.</term> with God, saying: "Lord, in your name I take this **maimed man** <term>maimed man (lelobēmenos) — A person with severe physical disabilities, possibly a leper or someone with withered limbs, requiring total care.</term>, and I will provide him rest until the day of his death, so that through him I too might be saved. Therefore, O Christ, grant me the endurance to serve him." And approaching the maimed man, he says to him: "Great sir, do you wish that I take you into my house and provide you with rest?" That man says to him: "I wish you would deem me worthy, but I am unworthy." Eulogius says: "Then I am going to bring a donkey and I will take you from here." The maimed man agreed with much joy. So, having brought a donkey, Eulogius lifted him up and carried him away to his own little **hospice** <term>hospice (xenidion) — A modest guest-room or private cell intended for charitable hospitality.</term>. And he took care of him according to his every need. 

The maimed man survived for fifteen years, being nursed by Eulogius as kindly as a father; he was bathed, anointed, served in all things, and carried in the very arms of Eulogius. He was satisfied even beyond what he deserved, resting in a manner appropriate to the nature of his disease. But after fifteen years, a **demon** <note>In the tradition of the Desert Fathers, sudden and irrational changes in behavior or temperament were often attributed to the interference of a "demon" testing the monk's virtue.</note> fell upon the maimed man, wishing perhaps to rob Eulogius of his reward and his purpose, and to deprive the maimed man of his rest and his gratitude to the Lord. 

And this man revolted against Eulogius, and began to speak foul words against him, so that he eventually flooded the man with insults, saying to him: "**Faker! Scoundrel! ### Concerning Eulogius and the Maimed Man.

Cronius, the priest of Nitria, related to me that when he was a younger man at the beginning of his vocation, having fled from the monastery of my **superior** <term>superior (archimandrite) — The head of a monastery or a group of monasteries.</term> on account of **spiritual listlessness** <term>spiritual listlessness (akēdia) — Also known as the 'noonday demon,' this was a state of boredom, apathy, or restlessness that often tempted monks to abandon their spiritual practice.</term>, he wandered until he reached the mountain of the holy Anthony. This blessed man used to reside between Babylon and Heracleia in the deep desert that leads toward the Red Sea, about thirty miles from the river. When Anthony came to the monastery by the river, where his disciples Macarius and Matoios were staying—the same ones who buried the blessed Anthony when he **fell asleep** <note>original: "koimēthenta" — a common Christian euphemism for death, implying the hope of resurrection.</note>—I waited five days in order to meet the holy Anthony. It was said that he visited this monastery sometimes after ten days, sometimes after twenty, and sometimes after five days, according as God moved him for the benefit of those who happened to be at the monastery.

Therefore, various brothers were gathered together there having various needs, among whom was a certain Eulogius, a monk from Alexandria, and with him another man who was **maimed** <term>maimed (lelobēmenos) — A person with severe physical disabilities; in this case, a man without hands or feet who lived as a beggar.</term>. These two had come for the following reason: 

Eulogius had been a **scholar** <term>scholar (scholastikos) — A person who had received a professional education, often a lawyer or an administrative official.</term> of the **standard curriculum** <term>standard curriculum (enkyklia paideumata) — The "circle of studies" or liberal arts education typical of the Greek elite, including grammar, rhetoric, and philosophy.</term>. Having been struck by divine love and a desire for immortality, he renounced the turmoils of the world. After dispersing all his possessions, he kept back a small amount of money for himself, as he was unable to perform manual labor. 

Finding himself in a state of spiritual listlessness while living by himself—neither wishing to enter a communal monastery nor being fully satisfied to live alone—he found a certain maimed man cast out in the marketplace, who had neither hands nor feet. To this man, only his tongue remained vigorous for recounting his misfortunes to those who passed by. Eulogius, standing there, stared intently...

# PALLADIUS

...dwell in the desert about five hundred men practicing the ascetic life. Among them was a certain monk named Paul, called "the Excellent," who throughout his entire time maintained this **way of life** <term>way of life (politeia) — A technical term in early monasticism referring to a monk's specific rule of conduct, discipline, and spiritual practice.</term>. He never turned his hand to work or any business, nor did he receive anything from anyone except for what he was to eat on that very day. His work of asceticism was to pray without ceasing. He had three hundred **prescribed prayers** <note>original: "tetypōmenas... tas euchas" — prayers that were likely fixed or formulaic, rather than spontaneous.</note>, and he would collect **pebbles** <term>pebbles (psēphous) — Small stones used as a counting device, a precursor to the prayer rope or rosary.</term> and keep them in his lap, throwing one pebble out of his lap for each prayer. This godly man, having gone to visit the holy Macarius, known as the **City-Dweller** <note>original: "politikō" — Macarius of Alexandria, distinguished from Macarius the Egyptian (the "Great"). He was called "City-Dweller" because he had lived in the city before becoming a monk.</note>, for the sake of conversation and spiritual benefit, said to him:

> "Abba Macarius, I am greatly distressed."

The servant of Christ then compelled him to tell the reason why he was grieving. And he said to him:

> "In a certain village dwells a virgin who has already spent thirty years in ascetic practice. Many have told me about her, saying that except for Saturday and the Lord's Day <note>original: "kyriakēs" — Sunday.</note>, she never eats on any other day; instead, stretching her fast through the whole week and eating only every five days, she offers seven hundred prayers. When I learned this, I was discouraged with myself, because I, being a man created with more physical strength than her, was not able to perform more than three hundred prayers."

The holy Macarius answered him, saying:

> "I have now reached my sixtieth year, during which I have offered one hundred prescribed prayers, while also working with my hands for my food and giving the brothers the time I owe them for conversation. Yet my **conscience** <note>original: "logismos" — literally "thought" or "reasoning," often used by desert fathers to mean the internal dialogue or judgmental thoughts.</note> does not judge me as being negligent. But if you, while offering three hundred prayers, are judged by your conscience, it is clear that you either do not pray them with a pure heart, or you are capable of praying more and are not doing so."

This holy man was deemed worthy of a **gift** <term>gift (charismatos) — A spiritual grace or power granted by the Divine.</term> against demons. Indeed, just as we disregard mice in our tunics, so much more did this great Moses, the wrestler against demons, hold them in contempt.

This was the godly way of life of the invincible athlete Moses, who was an **Ethiopian** <note>Moses the Black is one of the most famous Desert Fathers; a former bandit and slave who became a leader of the monastic community in Egypt.</note> by race, but whose soul was strengthened by divine grace. He is numbered among the great achievements of the fathers. He died at seventy-five years of age in **Scetis** <note>original: "Skētē" — Wadi El Natrun in Egypt, one of the most important centers of early Christian monasticism.</note>, having become a priest and leaving behind seventy disciples.

In those regions, they must get their water from a great distance—some two miles away, others five, and others half a mile <note>original: "semeiōn" — literally "signs," referring to Roman milestones or miles.</note>.

He partook of nothing except twelve ounces <note>original: "deka dyo onkiais" — roughly 340 grams; the standard daily ration for an Egyptian monk.</note> of dry bread, while performing a great amount of manual labor and reciting fifty prayers each day. 

Yet, even though he wasted away his little body, he remained burning with passion and continued to be troubled by uncontrolled dreams. He went again to another monk among the saints, one who was very well-tested, and said to him:

"What shall I do, Abba? For my thoughts darken my mind—the dreams of my soul, which takes pleasure in them according to its old habits."

That holy man said to him, "Because you have not withdrawn your mind from these fantasies, you endure this wretchedness. Do, therefore, what I tell you: give yourself to a **vigil** <term>vigil (agrypnia) — The practice of staying awake through the night for prayer and meditation.</term> little by little, pray with a sober mind, and you will quickly be freed from these things."

The noble Moses, having heard this advice as if from a master craftsman of experience, went away to his cell and gave his word to his own conscience that he would not sleep through the entire night. He would not even bend his knee—even under the pretext of prayer—so that he might flee the tyranny of sleep. # 16. THE LAUSIAC HISTORY

### About Moses the Ethiopian.

There was a man called Moses, an Ethiopian by race and dark-skinned, who was a domestic servant to a high-ranking government official. Because of his extreme hostility and a reputation for robbery, his own master cast him out. It was even said that he had gone so far as to commit murder. I am compelled to recount his wicked deeds so that I may illustrate the great virtue he achieved afterward through **repentance** <term>repentance (metanoia) — Literally a "change of mind" or "turning around," signifying a total transformation of one’s life and character.</term>.

It was told by some that he became the leader of a massive band of robbers, and this specific exploit is recorded among his criminal deeds: Once, he harbored a grudge against a man who had obstructed him in some matter. Knowing the man was a shepherd, Moses sought out the place where he kept his flock at night, intending to kill him. He was informed that the man was on the other side of the **Nile** <note>original: "Neilou"</note>.

Because the river was in flood at that time and was nearly a mile wide at that crossing, Moses gripped his knife between his teeth, placed the tunic he was wearing on his head, and swam across the river in this manner. While he was swimming across, the shepherd was able to escape notice by burying himself in a hiding place.

Having failed in his attempt to find the man, Moses instead took four choice rams, tied them together with a rope, and swam back across the Nile with them. Upon reaching a small courtyard, he butchered the rams, ate the finest portions of the meat, and sold the skins in exchange for wine. After drinking about eighteen Italian measures <note>original: "saitēn pioon hōs Italikon dekaoktō" — A "saïtes" was a specific type of vessel; eighteen measures would be an enormous, almost superhuman amount of wine, emphasizing his legendary physical constitution.</note> of wine, he traveled fifty miles to the place where he kept his gang of thieves.

At a later time, this bandit leader, being moved to his core by some circumstance, surrendered himself to a monastery. He approached the path of **repentance** <term>repentance (metanoia)</term> with such devotion that even...

# PALLADIUS

...to the holy and blessed Mark. This **sheepskin** <note>original: "kodion" — a simple sheepskin cloak or fleece, the common garment of a monk</note> the servant of Christ [Mark] left behind for the holy and blessed **Athanasius the Great** <note>The famous Bishop of Alexandria (c. 296–373), a staunch defender of orthodoxy</note>. As the blessed servant of Christ, Melania, told me: "I received from that holy and wonderful man, Macarius, that sheepskin which is called 'the gift of the hyena.'" And why is this surprising among men who are **crucified to the world** <note>A reference to Galatians 6:14, meaning they are dead to worldly desires</note>, that a hyena, having been shown kindness for the glory of God and sensing the holiness of His servants, should bring them a gift? For He who tamed the lions for the prophet Daniel also granted understanding to this hyena.

It was said of this holy Mark that from the moment he was baptized, he never spat upon the ground. This true servant of Christ and immortal Mark was in his sixtieth year since his baptism. 

Now, the physical appearance of the invincible **athlete of Christ** <term>athlete (athlētēs) — In early Christian writing, this referred to monks who "trained" the soul through discipline and struggle, much like a physical competitor</term>, Macarius, in his old age was like this. For I must describe him to you, servant of Christ, as I knew him accurately, since my own lowliness lived alongside him. His stature was somewhat short and he was thin-bearded, having hair only upon his lip and a few at the tip of his chin. Indeed, because of the extreme hardships of his ascetic practice, the hair of his beard never fully grew. 

One day, I approached this holy man and spoke to him while I was suffering greatly from **listlessness** <term>listlessness (akēdia) — Often called the "noonday devil," this was a state of spiritual boredom, restlessness, or depression common to desert hermits</term>. I said, "Abba Macarius, what should I do? For my **thoughts** <note>original: "logismoi" — In ascetic theology, these are not just ideas but intrusive, tempting impulses that trouble the mind</note> distress me, saying: 'You are achieving nothing here; leave this place.'" 

The holy father Macarius answered me, saying: "Tell your thoughts: 'For the sake of Christ, I am guarding these walls.'" 

These things, O Christ-loving and knowledge-loving servant of God, I have made known to you from the many great signs and struggles of the renowned and virtuous man, Macarius.

### XVI. THE LAUSIAC HISTORY

...having memorized both the Old and New Testaments. He was exceedingly gentle and self-controlled, more so than almost any other. One day, taking the opportunity during his extreme old age, I went to visit him. I sat down by the door of his cell, for as a beginner <note>original: "archarios" — a novice or trainee in the monastic life</note>, I regarded him as something more than human, which indeed he was. I listened intently to hear what he might be saying or what he was doing. Though he was entirely alone inside, having reached nearly a hundred years of age and having already lost his teeth, he was struggling against himself and the devil, saying: "What more do you want now, you wretched old man? Look, you have already taken wine and you have tasted oil. What else should I do for you, you white-haired glutton, you slave to your belly?" He was thus heaping insults upon himself.

Then he spoke to the devil: "Depart from me now, devil! You have grown old with me in this state of indifference <note>original: "adiaphoria" — here implying a spiritual listlessness or lack of discipline</note>. You attributed the weakness of my body to me as an excuse; you made me partake of wine and oil, making me a lover of pleasure. Do I still owe you anything? You will find nothing in me that you can plunder. Depart from me at last, you hater of mankind." And as if muttering or chirping to himself, he would say: "Come now, you trifler, you white-haired glutton, you old consumer, how much longer shall I be with you?"

The servant of Christ, Paphnutius <note>A prominent disciple of the desert fathers</note>, who was a student of the **athlete of the women’s quarters** <term>athlete of the women's quarters (gynaikōtou) — A title for Macarius of Alexandria, who served as a spiritual guide to a community of nuns</term>, also used to tell us this story: One day, while the blessed and immortal Mark was sitting in his courtyard conversing with God, a hyena appeared, carrying her cub which was blind. She knocked her head against the small door of the courtyard and entered while he was still sitting there, and she dropped the cub at his feet.

The holy Mark took this cub, spat upon its eyes, and prayed <note>This mirrors the healing method used by Jesus in the Gospel of Mark 8:23</note>. Immediately, the cub regained its sight. After its mother had nursed it, she took it and departed. The saint then stood up and took a basket that held about two **modii** <term>modius: a Roman unit of dry measure, approximately 9 liters; here indicating a very heavy load for an old man</term>, filled it with sand, and placing it upon his shoulders, he walked through the desert in this manner.

And having performed this service for a long time, he returned to his cell with his body thoroughly exhausted.

# THE LAUSIAC HISTORY

### Chapter 16

...of deceiving God. That man said: “I was not able, my lord.” The great Macarius said to him: “If you recognize your sin and the discipline of God, because of which you have suffered these things, correct yourself for the future.” And he confessed his sin. He gave his word no longer to sin, nor to serve at the altar, but to embrace the **status of a layman** <note>original: "laikon kleron" — the man was a member of the clergy who was demoted to the rank of the laity as a form of lifelong penance</note>. 

Then the saint laid his hands upon him. In a few days, he was healed and his hair grew back; he departed for his home in good health, glorifying God and giving thanks also to the great man.

Once again, there was brought before the spiritual physician of all irrational passions, the holy Macarius, a young boy possessed <note>original: "energoumenos" — literally "acted upon" by an external force; the standard term for one afflicted by a demon</note> by a cruel spirit. Placing his right hand upon the head and his left upon the heart, the saint prayed over him to such an extent that he caused the boy to be suspended in the air. 

The boy then swelled up like a **wineskin** <note>original: "askos" — an animal skin container that expands when filled</note> throughout his entire body; he became so inflamed that he grew to be **many talents** <term>talent: an ancient unit of weight (roughly 26–33 kg); the text suggests the boy became unnaturally heavy as he swelled</term> in weight. Suddenly, crying out, he discharged water through all his senses. Having subsided, he returned to the measure he had been at the beginning. 

The saint then returned him to his father, having anointed him with holy oil and poured blessed water over him. He commanded his relatives that the boy should not touch meat or wine for forty days. And so, having healed him, he sent him away.

At one time, this great Macarius was troubled by thoughts of **vainglory** <note>original: "kenodoxia" — the "empty glory" or pride that was considered one of the most dangerous temptations for a famous monk</note>, which attempted to call him out of his cell. These thoughts suggested that, for the sake of **stewardship** <note>original: "oikonomias charin" — a pretext of performing a useful or charitable administrative task for the church</note>, he should go to Rome, ostensibly for the benefit of those who were sick there. For the grace of the Lord regarding the spirits was flowing through him to a great degree. And as if...

# PALLADIUS

...seraphim <note>original: "raphim" — the end of "Seraphim," the highest order of angels</note>. There they worshipped God, the creator of all things. [He said:] "Do not descend below the heavens. Do not fall into worldly thoughts." 

And having persevered, he says, for two days and two nights, I so provoked the demon <note>likely the demon of vainglory or pride mentioned on the previous page</note> that he became a flame and burned everything in the cell, so that even the rush mat on which I stood was consumed by fire. Thus, I thought that I was being entirely burned up. Finally, struck with fear, I abandoned this attempt on the third day, being no longer able to keep my mind undistracted. Instead, I returned to the contemplation of this world, so that this might not be counted against me as pride <note>original: "typhon" — meaning conceit or vanity; Macarius intentionally lowered his spiritual focus to avoid the sin of pride</note>.

Afterwards, I visited this spiritual monk, the great Macarius, and found lying outside his cell a certain priest of a village whose entire head had been eaten away by the affliction called **cancer** <note>original: "karkinou" — in antiquity, this term referred to various gnawing or spreading ulcers as well as what we now call cancer</note>, so that the very bone of his skull was visible from the top. 

He had come to him to be healed, but Macarius would not grant him an audience. So I pleaded with him, saying: "I beg you, have compassion on this wretched man and give him an answer." And he answered me, saying: "He is unworthy of being healed, for this discipline <note>original: "paideia" — often used to mean divine correction or schooling through suffering</note> was sent upon him. But if you wish him to be healed, persuade him from this moment on to cease from the **ministration of the mysteries** <term>mysteries: the sacraments of the Church, specifically the Eucharist or Holy Communion</term>." 

I said to him: "For what reason, I ask you?" He said to me: "He was performing the liturgy while committing fornication, and for this reason he is now being disciplined. If, therefore, he submits in fear—having previously dared to act with contempt—God will heal him." 

I then told these things to the suffering man, and he agreed, swearing before God no longer to serve as a priest. Then Macarius received him and said to him: "Do you believe that there is a God from whom nothing is hidden?" He replied: "Yes, I beg you." ### CHAPTER 16. LAUSIAC HISTORY. 49

They spoke to their leader, saying: "Where did you bring this **fleshless man** from to our own condemnation? <note>original: "ἄσαρκον ἄνθρωπον" (asarkon anthrōpon). The monks use this term to describe Macarius's extreme asceticism, as if he had no physical body left.</note> Either cast him out from here, or know for certain that we shall all depart this very day." When the great Pachomius heard these things from the brothers, he inquired into the man’s circumstances. And having learned the details regarding his **way of life**, <term>politeia: a technical term for a monk's disciplined spiritual conduct and daily regimen</term> he prayed to God that He might reveal to him who this man was. It was then revealed to him that this was Macarius the monk. 

Then the lord Pachomius took him by the hand and led him outside. Bringing him into the **house of prayer** <term>euktērion: a chapel or oratory</term> where their **altar** <term>thysiasterion: the sanctuary or the altar where the Eucharist was celebrated</term> was located, he embraced him and said: "Welcome, good elder. <note>original: "καλόγηρε" (kalogēre), a respectful address for a monk, meaning 'good old man.'</note> You are Macarius, and yet you hid it from me! For many years I have desired to see you, having heard reports of your deeds. I am grateful to you because you have 'buffeted the ears' of my children <note>original: "ἐκονδύλισας," literally 'to strike with the fist,' here meaning to provide a sharp correction or wake-up call</note> so that they might not think too highly of their own **spiritual discipline**. <term>askēsis: the practice of self-denial and religious exercise</term> I entreat you, depart now to your own place; for you have sufficiently built us up. Pray for us." Thus, having been honored by him and requested by all the brothers, he departed.

At another time, this same **dispassionate man** <note>original: "ἀπαθής" (apathēs), one who has achieved 'apatheia,' a state of being free from the disturbance of passions and worldly desires</note> related to us: "When I had successfully completed every ascetic practice I had desired, I then came to another spiritual desire. I decided at one time to approach this desire of works; I wished," he said, "to keep my mind **undistracted** <note>original: "ἀπερίσπαστον" (aperispaston), a central goal in desert spirituality—maintaining a mind totally focused on God without wandering thoughts</note> from God for five days only, and to perceive nothing else at all, but to cling to Him alone. Having made this decision within myself, I shut my cell and the outer courtyard, so as not to give an answer to any person who might come. And I stood there, beginning from **Monday**, <note>original: "δευτέρας" (deuteras), the 'second day' of the week</note> commanding my mind and saying to it: 'Take care that you do not descend from the heavens; you have the angels there, and all the powers above—the Cherubim and the Se—'"

->48<-

The great **Pachomius** says to him: "Well now, you have reached old age; how can you practice **asceticism**? <term>askēsis: the disciplined practice of self-denial and spiritual exercise</term> There are brothers here who have practiced since their youth and, having been raised alongside such labors, they can bear the toil. But you, at this age, cannot endure the trials of the **ascetic life** <note>original: "τὰ τῆς ἀσκήσεως πείρασμα" (ta tēs askēseōs peirasma)</note> in this way; you will be offended, you will leave, and you will speak ill of us." And he did not receive him on that first occasion, nor on the second, for a period of seven days. But he [Macarius] persisted, remaining without food. 

Later, the elder **Macarius** said to him: "Receive me, **Abba**, <note>Abba: a title of respect for a monastic father, derived from the Aramaic for 'father.'</note> and if I do not fast as they do, and work as they work, then command that I be cast out of the **monastery**. <term>monē: a dwelling or communal house for monks</term>" Thus the great Pachomius persuaded the brothers to receive him. Now the community <note>original: "σύστημα" (systēma), referring here to the organized body or assembly of monks</note> of that one monastery consists of one thousand four hundred men to this day. 

So the great Macarius entered it. After a little time had passed, the **Forty Days** <term>tessarakostē: the period of Lent leading up to Easter</term> arrived. And the elder Macarius saw each monk taking up various **ways of life**. <term>politeia: a technical term for a monk's specific disciplined spiritual conduct and daily regimen</term> One would eat only in the evening; another every five days; another again would stand through the whole night, while sitting at work during the day. 

Macarius, having soaked a great number of palm-fronds <note>Palm-fronds were the standard material for weaving baskets and mats, the primary labor used by desert monks to provide for themselves and the poor.</note> while standing, took his place in a single corner. And until the forty days were fulfilled and **Easter** <note>original: "τὸ πάσχα" (to pascha)</note> had arrived, he took no bread, nor touched water; he did not bend a knee, nor sit, nor lie down; he tasted nothing else except a few raw cabbage leaves, which he took on Sundays so that he might appear to eat, and not fall into **pride**. <term>oiēsis: spiritual self-conceit or the vain opinion of one's own holiness</term> And whenever he went out for his bodily needs, he returned quickly and stood at his work, not opening his mouth, not speaking a word, small or great, but standing in silence. He did nothing else except the prayer in his heart and working the palm-fronds he had in his hands. 

When all the **ascetics** <term>askētai: those who practice rigorous self-discipline</term> of that monastery saw this, they rebelled against the **superior**... <term>hēgoumenos: the leader or abbot of the monastery</term>

### XVI. LAUSIAC HISTORY

->47<-

"...you dared to come to me." This man had various **cells** <term>kella: a small, simple room or hut used by a monk</term> in the desert, in which he performed the **contests of virtue**. <note>original: "ἄθλους τῆς ἀρετῆς" (athlous tēs aretēs). Monastic life was often described using athletic or military metaphors.</note> One was in **Scetis**, in the deeper part of the great desert; one was in **Libya** at the place called "The Cells" <note>original: "κελλία" (kellia), referring to the monastic settlement of Kellia</note>; one was on the mountain of **Nitria**. Some of these were windowless, in which **Macarius** was said to sit during **Lent** <term>tessarakostē: the "forty days" of fasting before Easter</term> in darkness. Another cell was very narrow, so much so that it was impossible to stretch out one's feet. The third cell was wider, where he would meet with those who came to visit him. 

This divine man healed such a great multitude of those possessed by demons that they could not easily be numbered. While we were present with this holy man, a certain virgin of the family of **Phorias** was brought from **Thessaloniki**, in the province of **Achaia**. She was physically weak and wealthy in lifestyle, having spent many years in **paralysis**. When she was brought to him, she was laid down near his cell. Having been moved with compassion for her and having prayed over her, he anointed her with **holy oil** with his own hands; and by praying for her continuously—or rather, intensely—he sent her back to her own city healthy within twenty days. She, departing on her own feet, sent back much fruit <note>meaning "offerings" or "donations" resulting from her gratitude</note> to the holy men. 

Again, this great **Macarius**, hearing that the **Tabennesiotes** <note>the community of monks established by Saint Pachomius at Tabennisi</note> followed a great **way of life**, <term>politeia: the specific rule or disciplined lifestyle of a monastic community</term> disguised himself. Taking on the worldly dress of a laborer, he traveled for fifteen days through the desert and arrived in the **Thebaid**. Having entered the **ascetic retreat** <term>askētērion: a place for spiritual exercise or a monastery</term> of the Tabennesiotes, he sought out their **archimandrite**, <term>archimandritēs: the head of a monastery or group of monasteries</term> a man of most proven character named **Pachomius**, who possessed the gift of prophecy. At that time, the facts concerning the great Macarius were hidden from that holy man. So, when Macarius met him, he said: "I beg of you, lord, receive me into your **monastery** <term>monē: a communal religious dwelling</term> so that I may become a **monk**."

...of the Sun. <note>original: "Ἡλίου" (Hēliou). This likely refers to a location or a period of time associated with the sun or a specific site like Heliopolis.</note> Having departed from there without being led into temptation, the **holy man** returned to his own **cell** <term>kella: a monk's private living quarters</term> after twenty days. However, because the bread and water he had been carrying were exhausted, he found himself in great distress. For during those days of journeying through the desert, he had tasted almost nothing at all. As the situation revealed, he was perhaps being tested for his **endurance**. <note>original: "ὑπομονὴν" (hypomonēn). And when he was near the point of collapsing, there appeared to him someone in the form of a **maiden**, as he himself related, wearing a clean linen cloth and holding a small **vessel** <term>kaukalion: a small cup or bucket</term> of dripping water. **Macarius** said she appeared to be at a distance from him, about a **stade** <note>A Greek unit of distance, approximately 600 feet or 185 meters.</note> away. For three days he traveled, seeing her with the vessel as if she were standing still and calling out to him. Yet he was unable to catch up to her—just as one might experience in the presence of priests and the **mysteries**. <note>original: "μυστηρίων" (mystēriōn). This suggests a spiritual vision that remains just beyond physical grasp, similar to the awe-filled distance maintained during the liturgy.</note> Nevertheless, through the hope of drinking, he endured the toil of those three days with persistence.

After this, a herd of **buffaloes** <note>original: "βουβάλων" (boubalōn). These were likely wild oxen or water buffalo common to the marshy regions of Egypt.</note> appeared. One of them, which had a calf, stood directly in front of the monk. (For there are many such animals in those places). And as Macarius used to tell it, her milk flowed as freely as water. "So I went under her," he said, "and having suckled, I was satisfied."

But so that the Lord might demonstrate His grace even further, and to instruct my own insignificance, He commanded the buffalo to follow me all the way to my cell. She, obeying the command, followed me and provided me with milk, while refusing to let her own calf suckle.

On another occasion, this virtuous man was digging a **well** for the refreshment of other monks. While some reeds and brushwood were lying nearby, he was bitten by an **asp**. (This animal possesses a deadly venom). The saint, however, seized the asp with both hands by its two jaws and tore it apart, saying to it: "If God did not send you, how [did you dare]..."

...from the voice alone was it recognized that he was the master **Macarius**. <note>original: "κύριος" (kyrios). For it was said that very many and fierce demons had been assigned to that place by those very men, due to the extreme nature of their dishonorable magical craft.

This garden-tomb was built by the brothers **Jannes** and **Jambres** <note>In Jewish and Christian tradition, these are the names of the magicians who opposed Moses in Exodus 7:11.</note> who, through their mastery of the magical art, held the highest positions at that time under Pharaoh and possessed great power in **Egypt** during their lives. They built the structure from squared stones, making a monument within it and depositing a great deal of gold. They also planted all kinds of trees and dug a massive well of water, for the place is naturally moist. <note>original: "ὕπιγμον" (hypigmon). A rare term suggesting the area was a damp oasis or marshy ground capable of supporting a garden in the desert.</note> They did all these things in the hope that even after their departure from this life, they might enjoy luxury in this "paradise."

Finally, though he did not know the road leading to this place, the servant of Christ, **Macarius**, set out. He followed the stars by calculation alone, just as sailors do when crossing the open seas. In this way, the **holy man** traversed the entire desert. Taking a bundle of reeds, he placed one at every mile to serve as markers, so that by their means he might find his way back. After journeying through the desert for nine days and coming near to that garden, he slept for a short while as night fell.

Then the **wild demon**, who always works against the **champions** <term>athlētais: literally "athletes" of Christ; a term used for monks who disciplined their bodies and spirits like competitors in an arena</term> of Christ, gathered up all those reeds of Macarius while he slept. ...strangled by hunger, it no longer approaches there, having no fodder. In this way you also, if you persist in your practice of self-control, will **mortify your members that are upon the earth**, <note>A reference to Colossians 3:5, a common scriptural justification for asceticism—the practice of denying the body's desires to strengthen the spirit.</note> and you will shut the entrance against **gluttony**, which gives birth to **licentiousness**. The demon of fornication will grow listless; <note>original: "ἀκηδιάσει" (akēdiasei). This refers to *acedia*, a state of spiritual listlessness or "the noonday demon" that plagued monks.</note> finding only the filth of your food cast out, he will thus depart from you.

So **Moses**, the servant of Christ, having withdrawn from that hour and shut himself in his cell, practiced the most intense endurance in all things, but especially in abstaining from food. He partook of nothing else except twelve ounces <term>uncia: a Roman unit of weight; twelve ounces was a common daily ration for an ascetic</term> of dry bread, while performing a great amount of physical labor and completing fifty prayers a day. Though he withered his little body, he still remained burning with desire, and continued to experience fantasies in his dreams without distinction. <note>original: "ἐνυπνιαζόμενος" (enypniazomenos). This often refers to nocturnal emissions or erotic dreams, which the Desert Fathers viewed as spiritual attacks or residues of past habits.</note>

Again, he went to another of the holy monks who was very proven, and said to him: "What shall I do, **Abba**? <note>original: "ἀββᾶ" (abba). A Syriac/Aramaic term for "Father," used as a title for a spiritual master in the desert.</note> For my thoughts darken me, and the dreams of my soul take pleasure in them according to my old habits."

That holy man said to him: "Because you have not detached your mind from fantasies concerning these things, for this reason you suffer this wickedness. Do, then, what I tell you. Give yourself to a little wakefulness <term>agrypnia: the practice of staying awake for prayer or "vigil," used to subdue the body</term> and pray with sobriety, and you will soon be freed from these things."

The noble man, having heard this advice as if from a master craftsman of experience, went back to his cell and gave a word to his own conscience not to sleep through the whole night, nor to bend his knee as if on the pretext of prayer, so as to flee the tyranny of sleep. # PALLADIUS

->58<-

...[God] brought him—who had a demon as an accomplice since his youth, one who had sinned with him in all things—directly to the knowledge of Christ. Among these accounts, it is said that once four bandits fell upon him while he was sitting in his cell, not knowing that this was Moses. Having tied them all up, the blessed man hoisted them all together onto his shoulders like a sack full of chaff <note>A vivid illustration of the legendary physical strength Moses possessed from his days as a bandit leader; chaff is the light, dry husks of grain.</note> and carried them to the church of the brothers, saying: "Since it is no longer permitted for me to wrong anyone, and I found these men attacking me, what do you command concerning them?" 

So these men, having been captured in this manner by the holy Moses, confessed their sins to God. And recognizing that this was the Moses who was once the famous and notorious bandit-chief, they glorified Christ because of him. They too renounced the world <note>original: "ἀπετάξαντο" (apetaxanto). A technical term for "renouncing" the secular world to become a monk.</note> because of his transformation, and they became most excellent monks, reasoning thus: "If this man, who was so capable in strength and so accomplished in the work of banditry, has so nobly feared God, why do we delay the salvation of our own souls?" 

Upon this blessed Moses (for he must henceforth be addressed as such), the demons of debauchery set, driving him back toward his old habit of licentiousness and fornication. He was so greatly harassed by them that he was nearly driven off course. <note>original: "ἐξωκεῖλαι" (exōkeilai). A maritime metaphor for a ship being driven aground or wrecked; here it signifies a spiritual near-collapse.</note> Coming, therefore, to the great **Isidore**, who dwelt in **Scetis**, <note>Scetis (Wadi El Natrun) was one of the three great centers of early Christian monasticism in the Egyptian desert.</note> he reported to him the matters concerning this war of fornication. To him the saint replied: 

"Do not be troubled, brother, for these are but the beginnings. Because of this they have set upon you more violently, seeking out your former habit. For just as when a dog is accustomed to the butcher shop <term>makellon: a Roman-style meat market or butcher shop</term> to satisfy its belly with scraps, it does not leave because of that habit. But if someone shuts the butcher shop, and no one gives it anything, or...

# 16. LAUSIAC HISTORY

### Concerning Moses the Ethiopian.

There was a man called Moses, an Ethiopian by birth and dark-skinned, who was the servant of a certain government official. His own master cast him out because of his extreme ill-temper and a charge of banditry; for it was said that his violence even reached the point of committing murders. I am compelled to speak of his wicked deeds, so that I may demonstrate the virtue he later achieved through repentance <note>original: "μετάνοια" (metanoia). This implies a total "change of mind" or spiritual "about-face," a central theme in Desert Father literature.</note>.

People used to recount that he was even the leader of a massive band of robbers, and among his criminal exploits, this story is told: He once bore a grudge against a shepherd who had obstructed him in some matter while he was going about with his sheep-dogs one night. Intending to kill the man, Moses scouted the place where the shepherd kept his flock. He was informed that the shepherd was on the other side of the Nile. Because the river was in flood at that time <note>The annual flooding of the Nile was a period of high, dangerous waters, making the feat of crossing it on foot or swimming particularly impressive.</note> and was nearly a mile wide at that point, he took his knife between his teeth, placed the small tunic he was wearing upon his head, and crossed the river by swimming. 

While he was swimming across in this manner, the shepherd was able to escape notice by burying himself in a hiding place. Having thus failed in his attempt, Moses slaughtered the four finest rams of the flock, tied them together with a cord, and swam back across the Nile again. Upon reaching a small courtyard, he butchered the rams, ate the best of the meat, and sold the skins in exchange for wine. After drinking a "Saïte" measure <note>This likely refers to wine from Sais, a city in the Nile Delta.</note>—roughly eighteen Italian pints <note>original: "Ἰταλικῶν δεκαοκτὼ" (Italikōn dekaoktō). A massive quantity of wine, approximately 9 liters, intended to illustrate his legendary physical capacity and excess before conversion.</note>—he walked fifty miles to where he kept his gang. 

Later on, at some point in time, this robber-chief was moved to remorse by some circumstance and gave himself up to a monastery. He approached the practice of repentance so earnestly that he even...

...the holy and blessed Mark; this sheepskin <note>original: "κώδιον" (kodion). A simple sheepskin or fleece often used as a garment or bedding by desert ascetics.</note> the servant of Christ has left behind for the holy and blessed Athanasius the Great. As the blessed servant of Christ, Melania, also told me: "I received that sheepskin from that holy and wonderful man Macarius, which was called the 'gift of the hyena.'" And what is so wonderful if a hyena, having received a benefit, should—for the glory of God and the honor of His servants—have the sense to bring them gifts, especially for men who are "crucified to the world"? <note>A reference to Galatians 6:14, describing those who have completely detached themselves from worldly desires.</note> For He who tamed the lions for the prophet Daniel also granted understanding to this hyena.

It was said of this holy Mark that from the time he was baptized he never spat upon the ground. The true servant of Christ and immortal Mark was in his sixtieth year since his baptism.

Now the physical appearance of Macarius, the invincible athlete of Christ, was as follows. For I must describe this to you, servant of Christ, since I knew him accurately, as my own insignificance <note>The author, Palladius, uses a standard formula of monastic humility here, referring to himself as "my littleness" or "insignificance."</note> spent time with him. The form of his stature was somewhat stunted, and he had hair only upon his lip and a few at the tip of his chin. For because of the excess of his ascetic labors, the hairs of his beard did not even grow out. 

Coming to this holy man one day, I said to him while I was suffering greatly from listlessness <term>accidie (ακηδία): a state of spiritual apathy, boredom, or "the noonday demon" that tempted monks to abandon their cells</term>: "Abba Macarius, what should I do? For my thoughts afflict me, saying: 'You are doing nothing here; leave this place.'" The holy father Macarius answered me, saying: "Tell your thoughts: 'For Christ's sake, I am guarding the walls.'" 

These things I have signaled to you, O Christ-lover and Lover-of-Learning <note>The Greek "Philochristos" and "Philomathes" were honorary titles for the high-ranking official, Lausus, to whom the book was dedicated.</note> servant of God, out of the many and great signs and struggles of the renowned and virtuous man Macarius.

### XVI. LAUSIAC HISTORY

...[who] had memorized both the Old and New Testaments. He was exceedingly gentle and self-controlled, as much as anyone could be. One day, finding a good opportunity during his extreme old age, I went to visit him. I sat by the door of his cell, since I, as a beginner <note>original: "ἀρχάριος" (archarios). A novice monk or someone newly entered into the ascetic life.</note>, regarded him as more than a man—which he truly was. I listened to hear what he might say or what he was doing. Being all alone inside—having reached nearly a hundred years of age and having already lost his teeth—he was struggling with himself and with the Devil, saying: 

"What more do you want now, you wicked old man? Look, you have even taken wine and touched oil. What more do you want me to do for you? You gray-haired glutton, slave to your belly!"—thus insulting himself. Then he spoke to the Devil: "Depart from me now, Devil! You have grown old with me in my negligences. By attributing bodily weakness to me, you made me partake of wine and oil, making me a lover of pleasure. Do I owe you anything while I am in my prime? <note>original: "μὴ ἀκμαίως" (me akmaios). He seems to be sarcastically questioning what the Devil could possibly want from him now that he is no longer in the "prime" of his youth.</note> You find nothing in me that you can plunder; depart from me now, you hater of mankind." 

And as if chirping <note>original: "τερετίζων" (teretizon). Refers to a whistling or rhythmic muttering sound, like a bird or a cicada.</note> to himself, he spoke in dialogue: "Come now, you dotard, you gray-haired glutton. You eater-in-old-age, how long shall I be with you?"

Having knocked at the small door of the courtyard with her head, she entered while he was still sitting there and threw it at his feet. Taking this cub, Saint Mark spat upon its eyes and prayed over it. Immediately, it regained its sight. After the mother let it suckle, she took it and departed. Though they <note>The demons of vainglory, who were tempting him to leave his solitude to seek praise elsewhere.</note> greatly troubled him, they were not obeyed; wrestling even more fiercely against him, they tried to drive him out. So falling upon the threshold of his own cell, the saint stretched out his feet and let them hang outside, saying to the demons of vainglory: "Drag me away, pull me along, you demons, if you are able! For I will not depart elsewhere on my own feet. If you are able to carry me away like this to where you say, then I will go." He swore an oath to them, saying: "I will lie here until evening; unless you carry me off, I will not obey you." 

Having lain there for a long time and remained unshaken, he finally rose late in the evening. When night came, they troubled him again by means of fire <note>This likely refers to "burning" temptations of the flesh or illusory visions of fire used by demons to disturb his peace.</note>. Rising up, the saint took a basket and filled it with about two pecks <note>original: "μοδίων" (modion). A Roman dry measure (modius), roughly equivalent to 8 or 9 liters.</note> of sand. For without cause, he suggests journeys away from home to me." And having performed this service for a long time, he entered his cell, having subdued his body.

And the man confessed his sin. He gave his word that he would no longer sin, nor would he serve at the altar, but would instead embrace the rank of a layman <note>original: "λαϊκὸν κλῆρον" (laikon kleron). In the early Church, a clergyman might be demoted to the status of a layman as a form of penance or discipline.</note>. Thereupon, the saint laid his hands upon him, and in a few days he was healed and grew strong. He departed for his home in good health, glorifying God and giving thanks also to the great man.

Again, a young boy possessed by a harsh spirit was brought plainly before the sight of the holy Macarius, the spiritual physician for all irrational passions. Placing his right hand upon the boy's head and his left upon his heart, the saint prayed over him so intensely that he caused the boy to be suspended in the air. The boy then swelled up like a wineskin <note>original: "ἀσκὸς" (askos). A common metaphor in ancient texts for extreme bloating or dropsy-like symptoms, often associated in hagiography with demonic possession.</note> throughout his whole body; he became so inflamed that he grew to be of great weight in the scales. Suddenly, crying out, he discharged water through all his senses. Having been thus relieved, he returned to the measure he had been from the beginning. Macarius then delivered him to his father, having anointed him with holy oil and poured blessed water over him. He commanded those with the boy that he should not touch meat nor drink wine for forty days. And so, having healed him, he sent him away.

At one time, thoughts of vainglory <note>original: "λογισμοὶ κενοδοξίας" (logismoi kenodoxias). In the desert tradition, "thoughts" are not merely mental states but spiritual temptations or "demons" that suggest specific sins to the monk.</note> troubled the great Macarius, attempting to lure him out of his cell. They suggested that, for the sake of stewardship, he should travel to Rome under the pretext of providing aid to the sick there—for the grace of the Lord against spirits flowed through him to a supreme degree. ### PALLADIUS

...Seraphim <note>original: "Σεραφίμ" (Seraphim). The highest order of angels, often associated with the burning fire of divine love.</note> and God, the Creator of all; stay there. Do not descend below the heavens. Do not fall into worldly thoughts." <note>original: "λογισμούς ψυχικούς" (logismous psychikous). In this context, it refers to thoughts tied to the lower, animal soul or earthly concerns, rather than spiritual focus.</note> 

And having persevered, he said, for two days and two nights, I so provoked the demon that he became a flame and burned everything in my cell, so that even the mat on which I stood was consumed by fire. And thus, thinking that I was being entirely burned up, I finally withdrew from this intention on the third day, struck with fear. I was no longer able to keep my mind in a state of dispassion <note>original: "ἀπάθεια" (apatheia). In the ascetic tradition, this is not "apathy" but a state of holy stillness and freedom from the disturbance of passions.</note>. Instead, I descended into contemplation of this world, so that this experience might not be reckoned to me as pride. <note>original: "τύφον" (typhon). A term for vanity or spiritual delusion.</note> 

After this, I visited this spiritual monk, the great Macarius, and I found a certain village priest lying outside his cell. His entire head had been eaten away by the disease called "cancer," <note>original: "καρκίνου" (karkinou). In ancient medicine, this term referred to spreading, ulcerous sores that "ate" the flesh, not strictly the modern pathological definition of cancer.</note> so that even the bare bone of his skull was visible from the top. He had come to the saint to be healed. But Macarius would not receive him in conversation. 

Therefore, I entreated him, saying, "I beg you, have pity on this miserable man and give him at least an answer." 

But he answered me, saying, "He is unworthy of being healed. For this discipline <note>original: "παιδεία" (paideia). Often used in hagiography to mean divine correction or a teaching through suffering.</note> has been sent upon him. But if you wish for him to be healed, persuade him to cease from now on from the liturgy of the mysteries." <note>original: "λειτουργίας τῶν μυστηρίων" (leitourgias tōn mystēriōn). This refers to the celebration of the Eucharist or Holy Communion.</note>

And I said to him, "For what reason, I pray you?"

He said to me, "He performed the liturgy while committing fornication, and for this reason he is now being disciplined. If, therefore, he stops out of fear of those things which he dared to do with contempt, God will heal him." 

I told these things to the suffering man. He agreed, swearing before God never to serve as a priest again. Only then did the saint receive him. And Macarius said to him, "Do you believe that there is a God from whom nothing is hidden?"

The man answered, "Yes, I beg of you."

### XVI. LAUSIAC HISTORY

...he dared to come to me. This man had various cells <term>cell: a simple, often solitary dwelling for a monk or hermit</term> in the desert, in which he worked out the labors of virtue. One was in Scetis <term>Scetis: the Wadi El Natrun, a famous center of early Christian monasticism in Egypt</term> in the innermost part of the deep desert; one was in Libya in the place known as "The Cells"; one was on the mountain of Nitria. And some of these were windowless <note>original: "ἀθυρίδωτοι" (athuridōtoi). These dark cells were intended to minimize external distractions during prayer.</note>, in which it was said that Macarius would sit during the Forty Days <note>original: "τεσσαρακοστή" (tessarakostē); referring to the Great Fast of Lent.</note> in darkness.

The second cell was very narrow, so much so that it was not possible for him to stretch out his feet. The third cell was wider, and in this one he would meet with those who came to visit him. This God-loving man healed such a great number of people possessed by demons <note>The text attributes mental and physical illnesses to demonic influence, a common view in late antiquity.</note> that they cannot easily be counted. While we were present with this holy man, a certain virgin was brought to him from the district of Thessalonica in Achaia. She was of noble birth and wealthy in life, but had suffered from paralysis for many years. 

When she was brought to him, she was laid down near his cell. Having felt compassion for her and having prayed, he anointed her with holy oil with his own hands. And by praying for her continually—or rather, perpetually—within twenty days he sent her back healthy to her own city. She, departing on her own feet, later sent much fruit-bearing <note>original: "καρποφορίαν" (karpophorian). In a monastic context, this refers to generous donations or charitable gifts sent in gratitude.</note> to the holy men. 

This same great Macarius, having heard that the Tabennesiotes <term>Tabennesiotes: the monks belonging to the cenobitic (communal) monastery founded by St. Pachomius at Tabennisi</term> practiced a great way of life <term>politeia: a term for the disciplined, ascetic conduct of a monk's life</term>, disguised himself. Taking on the worldly appearance of a laborer, he traveled for fifteen days through the desert and went up to the Thebaid. Upon entering the monastery of the Tabennesiotes, he sought out their archimandrite <term>archimandrite: the superior or abbot of a monastery</term>, a man named Pachomius, who was highly esteemed and possessed the gift of prophecy. At that time, the truth regarding the great Macarius was hidden from that holy man. So Macarius, having met him, said: "I beg of you, lord, receive me into your monastery, so that I may become a monk."

<keywords>Macarius, Pachomius, Scetis, Nitria, cell, asceticism, healing miracle, paralysis, Tabennesiotes, Thebaid, archimandrite, Lent</keywords>

# PALLADIUS

...sun <note>original: "Ἡλίου" (Hēliou). This word likely concludes a reference to Heliopolis, the "City of the Sun," mentioned on the preceding page.</note>. The saint then departed from there undisturbed and, after twenty days, returned to his own cell. Since the bread and water he carried with him had run out, he found himself in great distress. For during those days while traveling through the desert, he tasted almost nothing at all. As the event later showed, he was perhaps being tested in his endurance. 

When he was near the point of collapse, there appeared to him a figure in the form of a young girl—as he himself recounted—wearing a clean linen cloth and holding a small vessel <term>vessel: the Greek "καυκάλιον" (kaukalion) refers to a small drinking cup or bowl</term> of dripping water. Macarius said she seemed to be far off, about a stadium <note>A stadium was an ancient unit of distance, roughly 185 meters or 600 feet.</note> away. For three days he traveled, seeing her with the vessel as if she were standing still and calling out to him. Yet he was unable to reach her, much as one might see in the case of the priests and the sacred mysteries <note>The author compares the elusive vision to the way the clergy and the holy elements of the Eucharist are viewed with reverence from a distance during the liturgy.</note>. Driven by the hope of drinking, he endured the exhaustion of those three days despite his weakness.

After this, a herd of buffalo <note>These were likely the water buffalo common to the marshy regions and desert fringes of Egypt.</note> appeared. One among them, which had a calf, stood before the monk—for there are many of these animals in those regions. And as Macarius used to tell it, her udder was flowing with milk. "So I went under her," he said, "and having suckled, I was satisfied. And so that the Lord might further demonstrate His grace, while instructing my own littleness <note>original: "ὀλιγότητα" (oligotēta); a term used by monks to express humility or a sense of being "small" before God.</note>, He commanded the buffalo to follow me as far as my cell." Obeying the command, she followed him, suckling him but refusing to let her own calf nurse.

At another time, while this virtuous man was digging a well for the refreshment of the monks, he was bitten by an asp <note>The Egyptian cobra, a highly venomous snake.</note> hidden among the nearby reeds and brushwood. This animal possessed a deadly venom. The saint, grabbing the asp with both hands by its two jaws, tore it asunder, saying to it: "If God did not send you, how [did you dare...]" <note>The catchword "ἐτόλ-" (etol-) indicates the next page begins with the word "dare."</note>

<summary>This page recounts two miracles experienced by Macarius of Alexandria in the desert: being sustained by a wild buffalo's milk during a period of starvation and surviving a deadly asp bite by tearing the snake apart.</summary>

...with reeds <note>original: "καλάμοις" (kalamois). Macarius used these reeds like "breadcrumbs" to mark his path back through the featureless desert sand.</note> tied together, which he himself had fixed in the ground as a marker. Perhaps God permitted even this to happen for his greater spiritual training <term>training: the Greek "gymnasia" refers to the rigorous physical and spiritual exercise of an ascetic</term>, so that he would not place his hope in reeds to guide him, but rather in the grace of God, who guided Israel by a pillar of cloud for forty years in that terrifying wilderness. The saint said that when I only approached the garden-tomb <term>garden-tomb: "kēpotaphion," a type of ancient monumental tomb set within a private garden or orchard</term>, seventy demons came out from it to meet me in every kind of shape; some were shouting, others were leaping, and others were gnashing their teeth against me with a great grinding. Still others, like crows fluttering their wings, dared to fly against my face, saying:

"What do you want, Macarius, you tempter of monks? Why have you come to us? Will we also act against some of the monks? You have your own things there in the desert with those who are like you, and from there you have already driven out our kindred. We have nothing in common with you. Why do you trespass on our places as if to receive us as hermits of the desert? Those who built this place allotted it to us. You are not able to stay here. Why do you seek to enter this estate, into which no living man has entered since the brothers who built this were buried here by us?"

And while the demons were still doing many such things and lamenting, Macarius said to them, "I will only enter, look around <note>original: "ἱστορήσω" (historēsō), implying an investigation or an act of witnessing the history of the place</note>, and then I will depart from here." The demons replied, "Promise us this on your conscience." The servant of Christ said, "I will do so." 

Then the demons vanished. He said that having entered, he beheld everything, among which he found a bronze jar hanging by an iron chain over the well—now consumed by time—and nothing inside but the fruit of pomegranates that were not strong, for they had been dried up by the...

...from his voice alone was he recognized as the master <note>original: "κύριος" (kyrios) — a title of high respect for a monastic elder or "abba."</note> Macarius. This same man once desired to go—as he himself related to us—to enter the garden-tomb <term>garden-tomb: "kēpotaphion," an elaborate funerary monument situated within a private, irrigated garden or orchard.</term> of Jannes and Jambres, the magicians of Pharaoh’s time, for the sake of history or even for an encounter with the demons there. For it was said that many and harsh demons had been assigned to that very place by those men because of the excess of their shameful magic art. 

This garden-tomb was built by the brothers Jannes and Jambres, who held the highest positions in the art of magic at that time under Pharaoh, possessing great power in Egypt during their lifetime. They built that work out of squared stones, constructing a monument within it and depositing much gold. They also planted all kinds of trees and dug a very large well of water, for the place is parched <note>original: "ὕπιγμον" (hypigmon) — likely referring to the stifling, airless heat of the deep desert.</note>. They did all these things in the hope that after their departure from this life and arrival there, they might live in luxury in this paradise. 

Finally, being ignorant of the road leading to this place, Macarius, the servant of Christ, followed the stars by estimation <note>original: "στοχασμῷ" (stochasmō) — meaning he used celestial navigation or guesswork based on the stars.</note> alone, just as sailors do when crossing the open seas. Thus the saint, having set out, traversed the whole desert; and taking a bundle of reeds, he fixed one in the ground every mile as a sign, so that he might find his way to return by it. 

Having traversed that entire desert within nine days and arriving near that garden, he slept for a little while as night overtook him. Then the wild demon, who always opposes the athletes <term>athletes: "athleutais" — a common metaphor for monks who engaged in spiritual "gymnastics" or rigorous physical and mental discipline.</term> of Christ, gathered all those reeds of Macarius while he was sleeping; and as if for a single marker of the garden-tomb, he fixed them all at his head and departed. Having emulated this man, he broke the hard biscuit <term>boukellaton: a twice-baked, long-lasting bread crust used by soldiers and monks as a staple food.</term> that he had and placed the pieces into jars, deciding to eat only as much as his hand could draw out at once. This too was a great mortification of the body. For he used to tell us, jokingly, that when he tried to pull out several pieces at once, he was not permitted to bring them out by the "tax-collector" of the narrow opening. "For my tax-collector," he said, "would not allow me to eat [as much as I wanted]." <note>original: "ὁ τελώνης" (ho telōnēs) — Macarius humorously personifies the narrow neck of the jar as a toll-collector who extracts a "tax" by limiting his portion.</note>

For three whole years he practiced this form of self-restraint, eating only four or five ounces of bread and drinking an equivalent amount of water. He also consumed only a single pint <term>xestēs: an ancient measure of volume, roughly equivalent to half a liter or a pint.</term> of oil for his food over the course of an entire year. 

Such was the another discipline of this athlete <note>In early Christian writing, monks were often called "athletes of Christ" because they "trained" their souls through physical hardship.</note>. This adamantine man decided to conquer sleep. He related this to us: "I did not go under a roof for twenty whole days and nights, so that I might overcome sleep." He was scorched by the heat during the day and chilled by the severity of the night. As he himself said, "If I had not quickly gone under a roof and made use of sleep, my brain would have dried out so much that it would have driven me into a state of delirium <note>original: "ἔκστασίν" (ekstasin) — here referring to a mental breakdown or loss of senses caused by extreme exhaustion.</note>." He said that insofar as it was in his power, he conquered sleep, but he yielded to his nature in what was necessary for life.

While this holy man was sitting early in the morning in his cell, a gnat stung him on his foot. Feeling the pain, he crushed the insect with his hand after it had gorged itself on his blood. Condemning himself afterward for having taken revenge on the creature, he sentenced himself to sit in the marsh of Sketis <note>Sketis: a desolate region in the Egyptian desert (Wadi El Natrun), famous as a settlement for the Desert Fathers.</note>, which is in the deep desert, sitting there naked for six months. In that place, the gnats are so fierce they can pierce even the hides of wild boars, for they are like large wasps. He was so completely bitten by them that his whole body broke out in hard swellings <note>original: "σπονδύλους" (spondylous) — literally "vertebrae" or "knuckles," describing the hard, protruding welts caused by the insects.</note>, to the point that some people thought he had leprosy <term>elephantia: a term for severe skin diseases (like leprosy) that made the skin thick and gray like an elephant's hide.</term>. When he finally returned to his cell after six months...

And in this manner, by the grace of God and his divinely-inspired way of life <term>politeia: a term referring to a monk’s conduct, citizenship in the kingdom of heaven, or daily spiritual discipline.</term>, he healed the boy and restored him to his mother—this Macarius, the athlete of Christ and adversary of every demonic operation. ### Concerning Macarius of Alexandria.

But as for the one who was a most close comrade in the works of faith, and who shared that venerable name—I mean the holy Macarius of Alexandria—I did meet him; he was a priest of the place called the Cells <term>Kellia: A monastic settlement in the Egyptian desert where monks lived in semi-seclusion, each in their own small hut or "cell."</term>. I myself lived in these Cells for nine years, during which time this Macarius lived for three of those years, dwelling in stillness. Some of the works and signs of his holy way of life I saw with my own eyes; others I learned from those who lived alongside him; and still others I heard from many more witnesses. 

Now, the discipline <term>askēsis: rigorous spiritual training or "exercise" involving self-denial, from which we get the word "asceticism."</term> of the blessed Macarius, which many of us observed closely, was as follows: if he ever heard that someone had performed some feat of asceticism, he would fervently strive to surpass it. For instance, having heard from certain people that the Tabennesiotes <note>The monks of Tabennisi, a community in Upper Egypt founded by St. Pachomius, known for their strict communal rule.</note> eat only uncooked food throughout the entirety of Lent <note>original: "τεσσαρακοστῆς" (tessarakostēs) — the forty-day period of fasting preceding Easter.</note>, this saint decided that for seven years he would not eat anything that had passed through fire, consuming nothing but raw vegetables. On occasion, he might also have soaked pulses, but he tasted nothing else during those seven years. Once he had mastered this, he ended that particular practice. Then he heard of another monk, one of the most excellent, who ate only a pound of bread, and...

Concerning this man, a **Report** went out that he had even raised the dead, specifically to persuade a heretic who did not confess that there is a resurrection of bodies <note>A significant theological debate in the 4th century; some thinkers influenced by Platonic philosophy argued only the soul was immortal, whereas Macarius here defends the orthodox view of the physical body being raised.</note>. To this holy man, a certain youth was once brought who was possessed by a demon. He was brought by his own mother, who was wailing over him, and he was bound to two other young men, one on either side to restrain him. The demon within him had this particular operation: after the boy had eaten three <term>modii: A Roman dry measure equivalent to about 2 gallons or 8.7 liters. Three modii of bread is a supernatural, impossible amount for a human to consume at once.</term> of bread and drunk a Cilician jar of water, he would belch it out into a vapor and thus dissolve the food. In this way, everything eaten and drunk by him was consumed as if by fire. 

For there is indeed a category of demons called "fiery." There are, you see, differences among demons just as there are among men—not that their essence changes, but their nature is altered <note>original: "οὐκ οὐσίας ἀλλασσομένης, ἀλλὰ τῆς φύσεως ἀλλοιουμένης" — Palladius suggests that while all demons share a basic spiritual substance, they vary in their "temperament" or specific type of malice.</note>. This youth, therefore, not being satisfied by the food provided by his mother, would often eat his own excrement and drink his own urine. 

As his mother wept and blamed herself heavily for her son’s secret and terrible calamity, she repeatedly entreated and begged the saint. Taking the boy, the **unconquered athlete of Christ** <term>Athlete of Christ: A standard early Christian term for a monk who "competes" in spiritual warfare and self-discipline, much like a physical athlete trains for a race.</term> Macarius, prayed to God, making supplication for him. After one or two days, the demon eased its grip on the boy's terrible suffering. 

Then the lord Macarius said to the youth’s mother, "How much do you wish for your son to eat?" 

She answered, saying, "I beg of you, command that he eat ten pounds of bread." 

But Macarius rebuked her severely, as one who had asked for far too much, saying, "O woman, why have you spoken thus?"

...fifteen years, or rather twenty, he suffered from elephantiasis <note>original: "ἐλεφαντιάσαι" (elephantiasai) — In antiquity, this term referred to a severe form of leprosy or skin disease that made the skin thick and grey, resembling an elephant's hide.</note> as punishment for stealing the goods of the poor, so that no healthy spot could be found on his body where one might place a finger. And such was the prophecy of Saint Macarius.

Regarding food and drink, it is superfluous to even recount these matters, since gluttony cannot be found even among the more lax monks living outside the desert, nor among those living in those regions—both because of the scarcity of necessities and because of the zeal for God among the inhabitants there, where everyone is always competing with one another to surpass their neighbor in their various ways of life <note>original: "πολιτείαις" (politeiais) — A technical term in monastic literature referring to the specific "citizenship" or disciplined lifestyle and ascetic conduct of a monk.</note>.

Regarding the other ascetic practice of this heavenly man—I mean Macarius—it was said that the saint was incessantly in ecstasy, and for the greater part of the time he attended to God rather than to the things under heaven; through him, various miracles were manifested. 

A certain dissolute Egyptian man fell in love with a free woman who was married, and being unable to seduce her because of her modesty toward her husband—who was also chaste <note>The text describes him as her "virginal husband" (παρθενικὸν αὐτῆς ἄνδρα), suggesting a couple who practiced "spiritual marriage" or celibacy within their union.</note>—this wicked man approached a sorcerer <term>sorcerer (goēs)</term> <note>A "goēs" was a practitioner of low-level magic or sorcery, often associated with demonic deception and illusions.</note> and said to him: "Either win this woman over so she loves me, or use your craft so that her husband casts her out." 

Having received sufficient payment, the sorcerer employed his enchantments and magic <note>original: "μαγγανείαις" (manganeiais) — This term refers to deceptive magical arts, "mummery," or trickery intended to mislead the senses.</note>. When he was unable to sway her mind to agree with the man, he instead made her appear as a mare to everyone who looked at her. Therefore, when her husband came home from outside, he saw his own wife in the form of a mare; and when he went to his own bed, he was shocked to find a mare lying on his couch. Her husband wept and lamented, finding no explanation for what had occurred.

# PALLADIUS
18

He lived ninety years in total. Of these, he spent sixty years in the desert. Having gone up to the desert at thirty years of age, while still a young man and full of vigor, he so readily applied himself to such endurance of ascetic labors throughout ten whole years that he was deemed worthy of great and profound discernment, so much so that he was called the "boy-elder," <note>original: "παιδιογέροντα" (paidiogeron) — A title given to young monks who displayed the spiritual maturity and wisdom of an elder (Gerōn).</note> because he progressed in virtues faster than his age would suggest. For when he reached forty years of age, he received authoritative power over spirits, the gift of healings, and the spirit of prophecy. He was also deemed worthy of the divine priesthood. 

Two disciples lived with him in the innermost desert, in the place called Scetis <note>original: "Σκῆτιν" (Skētin) — One of the three most important centers of early Christian monasticism in the Nitrian Desert of Egypt.</note>. One of these was his assistant, who remained near him at all times so that those who came to him might be cared for. The other disciple withdrew privately to a cell. 

As time passed, the saint, looking ahead with a clairvoyant eye, said to his assistant named John—who later became a priest in the place of Saint Macarius, for the Great Macarius had been honored with the priesthood: "Listen to me, brother John, and heed my warning, for it will be to your benefit. For the spirit of avarice," <note>original: "φιλαργυρίας" (philargyrias) — Literally the "love of money," considered one of the most dangerous vices in the monastic tradition because it distracts the soul from total reliance on God.</note> he said, "is pressing and testing you. For this is what I have seen. I know that if you listen to my exhortation, you will be perfected in the work of God in this place, and you will be glorified, and no plague shall come near your dwelling. But if you disobey me, the end of Gehazi <note>A reference to the biblical servant of the prophet Elisha (2 Kings 5), who was struck with leprosy as punishment for his greed and deception.</note> shall come upon you, for you suffer from the same disease as he." 

It happened then that he disobeyed the saint at that time after the falling asleep <note>A traditional Christian term for death, implying the hope of resurrection.</note> of the immortal Macarius, and instead he obeyed the one who bound Judas to the noose through avarice; and after another ten-
