wherefore, God looked upon me even now so that He might reveal this drama <note>original: "δρᾶμα" (drama) — here meaning a deceptive performance or a trick played by a demon.</note> to me. Having closed the door of the cell, he went further inside. But the demon, shamed by this defeat, was dissolved into a whirlwind; taking the form of leaping and fleeing wild asses <note>original: "ὀνάγρους" (onagrous) — wild donkeys, often used in desert literature to symbolize untamed, chaotic spirits.</note> making loud noises as they went, he thus became invisible. This was the spiritual feat <note>original: "ἆθλον" (athlon) — a term borrowed from athletic games to describe the "contest" of a monk against spiritual forces.</note> of the blessed Nathanael, and these were his virtues of ascetic practice, his invincible battle against the adversary, his way of life, and the end of his illustrious life.

### Concerning Macarius the Egyptian.

Regarding the lives of the holy and immortal fathers, Macarius the Egyptian and Macarius the Alexandrian—who were noble men and invincible athletes of a virtuous life—their feats were many and great, and so nearly incredible to those who lack faith that I hesitate to narrate them or commit them to writing, lest I should gain a reputation for lying. For the Holy Spirit has clearly declared that the Lord destroys all who speak falsehoods. Therefore, since I am not lying, by the grace of the Lord, O Lausus <note>The book is dedicated to Lausus, a high-ranking chamberlain in the court of Emperor Theodosius II.</note>, most faithful of men, do not yourself disbelieve the feats of the holy fathers. Instead, take pride in these illustrious and truly Blessed <note>original: "Μακαρίων" (Makariōn) — a play on words, as the name Macarius literally means "Blessed" in Greek.</note> men, who possessed names worthy of their holy feats of asceticism. The first athlete of Christ, named Macarius, was Egyptian by birth. The second in age, but first in the merits of the monks, also called Macarius by name, was a citizen of Alexandria who used to sell confections <note>original: "τραγήματα" (tragēmata) — specifically dried fruits, nuts, or sweetmeats served as dessert; Macarius of Alexandria was a merchant before becoming a monk.</note>. I shall first relate the virtues of Macarius the Egyptian, who lived

Nathanael heard the voice of the supposed boy, and opening the door of the cell, he spoke to him from within: "Who are you, and what do you want me to do for you?" For there are many hyenas in these parts." 

Then the blessed Nathanael stood speechless; he was greatly distressed and his heart was troubled with much compassion <note>original: "συμπαθείας" (sympatheias) — a technical term here for the internal struggle between natural pity and spiritual discipline.</note>. He reasoned what he should do, saying: "I must either fail the commandment <note>The commandment of hospitality and helping the needy.</note> or slip from my purpose <note>His vow never to cross the threshold of his cell.</note>." Afterward, reflecting with pious reason, he said to himself: "It is better not to rob a purpose of so many years, which would result in shame and a victory for the devil." Having prayed to the Lord, he said to the one who seemed to be a boy: "Listen, child, or whoever you are: I believe in Him whom I serve, the Master of all breath. If you truly need help, my God will send you aid, and neither hyenas nor anything else will harm you. But if you are a temptation..."

### CHAPTER XVI. LAUSIAC HISTORY

...at the beginning by the demon who mocks and deceives everyone, who also cast this man into <term>akedia<note>original: "ἀκηδίᾳ" — a state of spiritual listlessness, boredom, or "the noonday devil" common in monastic literature.</note></term>, and drove him out of his cell. For he seemed to succumb to listlessness in his first cell. Having withdrawn from there, he went and built another closer to a village. After completing the cell and dwelling in it for three or four months, the demon came to him at night, holding a whip of bull-hide <note>original: "ταυρείαν" — a whip made of ox-leather, often associated with the brutal instruments used by public executioners.</note> just like the executioners, and taking the form of a soldier dressed in rags, making loud noises with the whip.

The blessed Nathanael, being distressed by him, spoke to him, saying: "Who are you, that you do such things in my lodging?"

And the demon answered: "I am the one who also drove you out of your first cell. And now I have come to make you flee from this one as well."

Then the blessed Nathanael, realizing that he had been mocked <note>original: "ἐνεπαίχθη" — deceived or made a fool of.</note>, returned once more to his first shelter. Having completed thirty-seven years there, he never stepped over the threshold <note>original: "οὐκ ὑπερέβη τὴν ὁδὸν" — literally "did not cross the road," signifying his vow of stability.</note>, contending against the demon. That demon showed him so many things to try and force him to leave his cell that it is impossible to even describe them all.

Among these was the following: the hater of good <note>original: "μισόκαλος" (misokalos) — a standard epithet for the Devil, meaning one who hates what is beautiful or virtuous.</note>, keeping watch, took pains to trouble him into breaking his resolve. Seven holy bishops visited the saint—whether by God's providence or by a pretext of temptation—and he almost strayed from his purpose because of them. For after the bishops had finished their visit and offered a prayer, and were about to depart, the noble monk did not escort them out even a single footstep, so as not to give ground to the hater of good.

The deacons of the bishops said to him: "You are doing a proud thing, Abba, by not escorting the bishops."

# PALLADIUS
### PAGE 34

...solitude <note>original: "μονότητι" — the state of being alone or a monk.</note>. Having spent much time with this man, I learned from him how his inner reasoning checked the sin of murder. He was so far removed from the reproach of sorrow that he even gave thanks for the cause of the murder. "For," he said, "the involuntary murder became for me the basis of salvation." He spoke of this by bringing forward the testimony regarding the great servant of God, Moses, and the murder he committed in Egypt; for if not for the fear of that murder, Moses would not have been deemed worthy of the divine vision <note>original: "θεοπτίας" — the act of seeing God, specifically the Burning Bush or on Sinai.</note>, nor of such a great gift, nor of the writing of the holy words of the Spirit, had he not fled from Pharaoh because of what he had done in Egypt and reached Mount Sinai. I say these things not to clear a path for anyone to commit murder, but rather to point out that there are also **circumstantial virtues**, when someone does not approach the Good by a voluntary choice. For of the virtues, some are **voluntary** <note>original: "προαιρετικαί" — chosen by free will.</note> and others are **circumstantial** <note>original: "περιστατικαί" — arising from external necessity or crisis.</note>.

->**About Nathanael.**<-

Another of the holy ones among the ancients was a champion athlete of Christ <note>original: "ἀριστεὺς ἀθλητὴς τοῦ Χριστοῦ" — a common monastic metaphor comparing the monk's spiritual struggle to an Olympic athlete's physical training.</note>, named Nathanael. I did not meet this man in the flesh, for he had fallen asleep <note>original: "κεκοίμητο" — a common Christian euphemism for death.</note> fifteen years before my arrival at the mountain. But having encountered those who had practiced the ascetic life with this holy man and who had lived at the same time, I inquired kindly about the man’s virtue. They showed me the cell in which he lived, though no one dwelt there anymore because it was quite close to the inhabited world. That blessed man had built it in the days when hermits <note>original: "ἀναχωρηταί" — anchorites; those who withdraw from society.</note> were still rare. They told me of the extraordinary fire of his ascetic practice: that he possessed such great endurance in his cell that he was never shaken from his purpose. Every Saturday and Sunday, he would set out three or four tables of food, welcoming those who were left in want. In this manner, he spent his entire life. 

Now, when both of these men had died, different praises were offered for each of them by the brothers, as both were found to be perfect in virtue. Some favored the one who had once and for all renounced the world <note>original: "ἀποταξαμένου" — refers to the formal monastic act of renouncing property and secular life.</note>, while others favored the one who shared himself with all those in need. 

Therefore, when a dispute broke out among the brotherhood over the different lifestyles <term>politeia: a Greek term for one's way of life, conduct, or spiritual "citizenship"</term> of these blessed men—especially regarding the different praises given to them—they went to the blessed and holy Pambo. They submitted the judgment of these matters to him, asking to learn from him which was the better way of life. 

He said to them: "Both are perfect before the Lord. For the one performed an Abrahamic work <note>A reference to the hospitality of Abraham in Genesis 18, often cited as the ultimate example of welcoming the stranger.</note> by welcoming everyone, while the other demonstrated the unbending zeal of the prophet Elijah <note>Elijah was the biblical model for the solitary, uncompromising ascetic life.</note> in pleasing God." 

But as some continued to argue, "How is it possible for these to be equal?" they preferred the ascetic, saying, "This man fulfilled the Gospel command by selling everything and distributing it to the poor, persisting in prayers, and carrying the cross every day, hour, and night, and following the Savior." 

Others, however, argued in favor of the second brother: "But this one, we beg you, showed such great and profound compassion <note>original: "σπλάγχνα" (splangchna) — literally "bowels" or "internal organs," used in the Bible to denote the seat of deep-seated mercy and empathy.</note> to all those in need, even going out to sit by the main roads to gather and help those in distress. And he provided rest not only for his own soul, but for many others, nursing the sick and assisting them."

->About Paesius and Isaias<-

There were two others, Paesius and Isaias by name, who were brothers and the sons of a merchant named Spanodromus. When their father died, they divided his estate of movable goods, which consisted of five thousand gold coins, as well as the clothing and servants they inherited. They conferred with one another and, seeking each other's counsel, said: "What way of life shall we pursue, brother? If we take up the merchant trade that our father followed, we will eventually have to leave the fruits of our labors to others. Moreover, we will certainly fall into various dangers, either from bandits or from the sea. Come then, brother, let us enter the monastic life, so that we may both gain [a spiritual] profit from our father's wealth and not lose our own souls." 

Therefore, the goal of the monastic life pleased them both. However, they were found to differ from one another in their approach. Having divided the money and everything else, each held to the goal of pleasing God, but through a different way of life. For one brother scattered everything he had and gave it to monasteries <note>original: "ἀσκητηρίοις" (asketeriois) — early monastic dwellings or places of ascetic practice.</note>, churches, and prisons. He then learned a small trade so that he might provide his own bread, and he devoted himself to asceticism and prayer. But the other brother scattered nothing in this way; instead, having built...

A certain man named Apollonius, formerly a merchant, submitted himself to the monastic life and dwelt on the Mount of Nitria <note>Nitria was one of the earliest and most important Christian monastic centers in Egypt.</note>. From his own funds and through his own hard work, he would buy all sorts of medicines and pantry supplies <note>original: "κελλαρικὰ" (kellarika) — items stored in a cellar, such as specialized foods or groceries.</note> in Alexandria and provide them to the entire brotherhood for their illnesses. One could see him from dawn until the ninth hour <note>The "ninth hour" in the ancient world was roughly 3:00 PM.</note> making rounds to the hermitages, the monasteries, and all the dwellings there, entering every door to see if anyone was lying ill. A martyrium is a shrine or church built over the relics of a martyr or a holy site.</note>. In these very shrines, the saint received Rufinus <note>Rufinus of Aquileia, a famous monk and translator who traveled extensively in Egypt.</note> from the pure baptismal waters at the hands of the bishops; Rufinus was honored in a manner worthy of his way of life, obeying the holy man Ammonius in all things. His tomb is said to cure all those afflicted with shivers <note>original: "ῥιγιαζομένους" (rhigiazomenous). This refers to those suffering from the rigors or chills associated with high fevers, such as malaria.</note>.

### About Benjamin.

In this mountain of Nitria <note>Nitria was one of the earliest and most important Christian monastic settlements in the Egyptian desert.</note>, there was a certain wonderful man named Benjamin, who lived a virtuous life for eighty years. Having reached the summit of ascetic practice, he was deemed worthy of the gift of healing; so it was that in every case, whomever he laid his hands upon or gave blessed oil, the sufferer was delivered from every ailment. This man, who had been deemed worthy of such a great gift, eight months before his death, developed dropsy <note>original: "ὑδρωπίασεν" (hydrōpiasen). Now known as edema, this condition involves the massive accumulation of fluid in the body's tissues.</note>. His body swelled with the edema to such an extent that he was seen as another Job in his sufferings in our own times. 

Dioscorus the bishop—who was then a priest of the mountain of Nitria—took me and the blessed Evagrius <note>Evagrius Ponticus (345–399 AD), a highly influential monk and theologian known for his writings on the spiritual life.</note> and said to us: "Come, see a new Job, who despite such a massive swelling of the body and an incurable disease, possesses a limitless sense of gratitude." 

When we went, we beheld such a massiveness in his body that a person’s two hands could not even wrap around his little finger. Being unable...

# PALLADIUS
### PAGE 28

...to go into the desert. Since they would not permit him to do so, while everyone was watching, he snatched up a pair of shears and cut off his own left ear <note>Ammonius did this to intentionally disqualify himself from the priesthood based on physical requirements.</note> right to the base, saying to them: "From this moment on, you should be fully convinced that it is impossible for me to become what you are forcing me to be." He said this because the law <note>original: "νόμος" (nomos). Referring to the Levitical law (Leviticus 21:17–23) which forbade a man with a physical blemish or defect from serving as a priest.</note> forbids that a man with a cut ear be brought into the priesthood. Thus, they let him be and withdrew, and they went away to report these events to the bishop. 

But the bishop said to them, "Let this law be observed among the Jews; as for me, even if you bring me a man with a cut nose, provided he is worthy in his character, I will ordain him." They went back again and entreated him. When the holy man would not be persuaded, they attempted even to lead him by force. But he swore a solemn oath to them, saying: "If you force me, I will cut out my tongue as well." Upon hearing this, they finally left him and withdrew.

This miracle is related concerning this holy Ammonius: he said that never, when his body felt the stirrings of pleasure, did he ever spare his flesh; instead, he would heat an iron until it was glowing red and apply it to his limbs, so that he was always covered in sores <note>Self-cauterization was an extreme ascetic practice used to suppress sexual desire by replacing temptation with intense physical pain.</note>. Furthermore, his diet from his youth until his death consisted of raw food <note>original: "ὠμοφαγία" (ōmophagia). This refers to eating uncooked vegetables and herbs, a common sign of extreme monastic rigor.</note>; he never ate anything that had passed through fire, except for bread.

He memorized both the Old and New Testaments by heart, and he read through six million lines <note>original: "μυρίας ἑξακοσίας" (myrias hexakosias). In ancient book production, a 'myriad' was 10,000 lines. This suggests he read roughly 6,000,000 lines of theology—an immense library for the time.</note> of the writings of the chosen men Origen, Didymus, Pierius, and Stephen <note>These were famous scholars of the Alexandrian school of theology. Origen and Didymus the Blind were particularly influential but controversial.</note>. The Great Fathers of the desert also bear witness to his virtue. Prophecies are also attributed to him. He was, moreover, as encouraging to the brothers in the desert as anyone could be. 

This blessed man, having been entreated with many supplications by Rufinus <note>This is Rufinus the Praetorian Prefect, a powerful Roman official, not to be confused with the monk Rufinus of Aquileia mentioned previously.</note>, who was at that time the Praetorian Prefect, traveled during those times—the great Ammonius having come to Con- <note>The text breaks here; the catchword indicates the word completes as "Constantinople."</note>

### Concerning Pior.

Among other accounts, this deed of the holy Pambo is also related: that the blessed Pior the ascetic <note>Pior was an Egyptian hermit and disciple of Antony.</note> once visited Pambo's cell, carrying his own bread with him. When Pambo questioned him, asking, "For what reason have you done this?" Pior replied, "So that I might not be a burden to you." Pambo remained silent and let him depart. Some time later, the great Pambo visited Pior's cell. He had soaked his bread in water beforehand and brought it with him. When Pior asked him for what reason he brought the bread already soaked, Pambo replied: "I soaked it so that I, too, would not be a burden to you <note>By soaking the bread at home, Pambo avoided using Pior's limited supply of water in the desert.</note>."

### Concerning Ammonius.

This Ammonius had been a disciple of the great Pambo, along with three other brothers and two sisters. They reached the very summit of devotion to God <note>original: "φιλοθείας" (philotheias). Because this excellent man Ammonius was exceedingly learned <note>original: "φιλολόγος" (philologos), meaning a lover of literature or learning. Approaching the blessed Bishop Timothy, they begged him to ordain Ammonius as their bishop. The bishop said to them: "Bring him to me, and I will ordain him for you." 

Therefore, they went out with a large company of helpers to seize him, but he turned and fled. When he saw that he was eventually overtaken, he pleaded with them to let him go. But they would not be persuaded, and he swore a solemn oath that he would not accept the matter, nor could he...

"Thus, then," she says, "the grace of the Lord arranged it upon my arrival at the mountain <note>The "mountain" refers to the desert of Nitria in Egypt, a major center of early Christian monasticism.</note>. After a short time, the man of God fell asleep <note>A common early Christian euphemism for death.</note> without having fallen ill, and without suffering pain in any of the members of his body. Just as the stitching of the work was reaching completion, he said to me: 'Receive this basket from my hands, so that you may remember me, for I have nothing else to leave to you.' Having passed away without fever at seventy years of age, he commended his spirit to the Lord. I prepared him for burial, wrapped the body of the saint in linen cloths, and laid him to rest; and so I departed from the desert, keeping that basket with me until the day of my death." 

This Pambo, when he was about to die at the very hour of his passing, spoke these words to those standing by—to Origen the priest and steward <note>The "oikonomos" or steward was the monk in charge of the monastery's administration and finances.</note>, and to Ammonius, both men of great renown: "From the time I came to this place in the desert, built my cell <note>original: "κέλλαν" (kellan). A small, simple dwelling for a monk.</note>, and dwelt here, I have not spent a single day without the work of my hands. Nor do I remember ever eating bread from anyone as a gift. I do not regret any word I have spoken up to this very hour. And yet, I go to God as one who has not even begun to serve Him <note>original: "θεοσεβεῖν" (theosebein). This reflects the deep humility of the desert fathers, who viewed their lifelong asceticism as merely a beginning.</note>." 

The servants of Christ, Origen and Ammonius, also bore witness to him, telling us that whenever he was asked a question by anyone—whether concerning the Scriptures or some other practical matter—he never answered immediately or on the spot. Instead, he would say that he had not yet found the answer. Often as much as three months would pass and he would still not give an answer, saying he had not yet grasped it. Consequently, he gave his pronouncements with such godliness and deliberation that everyone received them with great awe, as if they came from God Himself. For this

But he, she said, while sitting and weaving palm leaves <note>original: "πλέκων θαλλοὺς" (plekon thallous). Weaving palm shoots into baskets or mats was the standard manual labor for desert monks, used to fund their basic needs and provide for the poor.</note>, blessed me with his voice alone, saying: "May God give you your reward." And he said to his steward, Origen: "Take these and distribute them to all the brothers in Libya and the islands <note>Libya and the desert oases (often called 'islands' of the desert) were far more remote and impoverished than the monasteries closer to the Nile.</note>, for these monasteries are poorer than the others." He commanded him not to give any of that money to anyone in Egypt, because that land is more prosperous.

"I, however," she said, "stood there waiting to be honored with a blessing or to be praised in speech for such a large gift. Hearing nothing at all from him, I said to him: 'Master, just so you know how much it is—there are three hundred pounds of silver.' But he, without even looking up or glancing toward the chest in the darkness, answered me: 'Child, He to whom you have brought it has no need to learn the weight from you. For He who weighed the mountains and set the valleys in a balance <note>An allusion to Isaiah 40:12, emphasizing that God, as Creator, already knows the measure of all things.</note> knows much better the quantity of the silver. If you were giving it to me, you would do well to tell me the weight; but if you have offered it to God—who did not overlook even the two small coins, but honored them more than all else <note>A reference to the story of the Widow’s Mite in Luke 21:2-4.</note>—be silent, and be at peace.'"

<header>PALLADIUS</header>

A miracle was recounted by the blessed Athanasius, the Bishop of Alexandria, who wrote of it in his *Life of Antony* <note>original: "εἰς τὸν περὶ Ἀντωνίου βίον". Athanasius’s biography of St. Antony was the most influential text of early Christian monasticism.</note>. He tells how once, when Amun was about to cross the Wolf River <note>original: "Λύκον ποταμὸν" (Lykon potamon). This was a branch of the Nile or a canal near the Nitrian desert.</note> with his disciple Theodore, he was hesitant to undress, so that he might never see his own body naked. And while he was pondering how to get across, he suddenly found himself on the other side of the river, having crossed as if in a trance, transported by angels without the use of a ferry. This was the same Amun who lived in such a manner, and died in such a manner, that the blessed Antony saw his soul being carried up into heaven by angels. I myself once crossed this Wolf River by ferry with great fear; for it is a branch of the great Nile.

### Concerning Hor.

In this mountain of Nitria <note>Nitria was one of the earliest and most famous Christian monastic settlements in Egypt.</note>, there lived a certain man, a wonderful ascetic named Hor. To his great virtue the entire brotherhood bore witness, and especially the man of God, Melania <note>original: "ὁ ἄνθρωπος τοῦ Θεοῦ Μελαίνη". Though Melania was a woman, the author uses the masculine title "man of God," a common hagiographic honor for a woman who has attained the highest level of spiritual discipline.</note>, who entered this mountain before I did. I myself did not encounter him while he was alive. In the accounts of his noble deeds, they said that he never lied, nor did he ever swear an oath, nor did he curse anyone, nor did he ever speak except when it was necessary.

### Concerning Pambo.

A resident of this same mountain was the blessed Pambo, the teacher of Bishop Dioscorus, and of the brothers Ammonius, Eusebius, and Euthymius <note>These four brothers were known in church history as the "Tall Brothers," prominent and often controversial figures in the Origenist disputes of the late 4th century.</note>, as well as of Origen, the nephew of the noble and admirable Dracontius. Finally, that blessed woman said to him: "I have something to say to you, my lord; and if you will listen to me, I shall be fully assured that you truly love me in a godly way." He said to her, "Say whatever you wish."

She said to him: "It is a right and just thing that you, being a god-fearing man who practices righteousness, and I likewise emulating the same path as you, should live as monastics separately, so that many others might be benefited. For it is improper that, because of me, such a great and such a kind of virtue of philosophy <note>original: "φιλοσοφίας" (philosophias). Early Christians often called the monastic life 'the true philosophy' because it sought the ultimate truth through disciplined living.</note> should be hidden, while you dwell with me in purity <note>original: "ἁγνείᾳ" (hagneia), referring here to their celibate marriage.</note> for the sake of the Lord."

He, giving thanks to her and giving glory to God, said to her: "This has seemed good to you, my lady and sister. And if this pleases you, you may keep this house, and I shall depart and make another house for myself." So he went out from her and entered further into the mountain of Nitria <note>The Nitrian Desert became one of the most famous sites of early Christian monasticism.</note>; for at that time there were not yet continuous monasteries there. 'Askesis' originally referred to the training of an athlete; here it refers to the spiritual training of a monk.</note> in the solitary life, he died. ### About Amun.

They said that this Amun lived in the following manner. They say that when he was a young man, an orphan of about twenty-two years, he was joined to a wife by force by his own uncle. Being unable to resist his uncle’s compulsion, he submitted to being crowned <note>In the Eastern Christian tradition, 'crowning' is the central act of the marriage ceremony, symbolizing the couple becoming the king and queen of a new domestic church.</note> and to sitting in the bridal chamber <note>original: "παστῷ" (pastō). This refers to the decorated bridal bed or the chamber itself.</note>, and to having performed everything according to the marriage custom.

Sitting down, he called to his blessed and true spouse and said to her: "Come now, my lady and sister, and I will now explain the matter to you. The marriage which we have entered into has nothing more to offer <note>meaning the physical union is superfluous to their spiritual goal.</note>. We would do well, therefore, if from now on each of us sleeps separately, so that we may henceforth please Christ by having preserved our virginity untouched."

...faults. So that all who stumble and are convicted, and are thus subject to blows, the Phoenix <note>original: "Φοίνικα" (Phoinika). In the Nitrian community, this referred to a palm tree or a wooden post where monks who had committed offenses were disciplined with lashes.</note> receives them; and receiving a set number of lashes on the back, they are thus released. On this mountain, physicians also live, and pastry-makers. They also make use of wine, and wine is sold. All these men work linen with their own hands, so that all of them are self-sufficient. 

And when evening comes, one can stand and hear from each monastery hymns and psalms sung to Christ, and prayers sent up to the heavens, so that one would think they were caught up on high and shared in the paradise of delights <note>A reference to the garden of Eden or the heavenly realm.</note>. They gather at the church only on Saturday and Sunday. There are eight priests presiding over this church; in which, as long as the first priest lives, none of the others offers the sacrifice <note>The Eucharist.</note>, nor judges, nor speaks, but they only sit together with him in silence.

# PALLADIUS

### On those in Nitria.

Having then met many of the saints, and having spent three years in the monasteries around Alexandria <note>A major center of learning and Christianity in Roman Egypt.</note>, I flourished together with the most noble and diligent great men—about two thousand of them—who were adorned with every virtue. Departing from there, I came to the mountain of Nitria <note>Nitria was one of the earliest and most important Christian monastic centers in Egypt, located in the Nitrian Desert.</note>. Between this mountain and Alexandria lies a lake called Maria <note>original: "Μαρία" (Maria); known today as Lake Mareotis.</note>. It extends for seventy miles. Having sailed across it in a day and a half, I came to the mountain on the southern side. 

Adjacent to this mountain is the Great Desert, which stretches as far as Ethiopia, the Mazikes <note>A group of Berber tribes from North Africa often mentioned in late Roman histories.</note>, and Mauritania. On this mountain live about five thousand men, who follow various ways of life, each as he is able and as he wishes; so that it is possible to live alone, or with one other, or with many. 

On this mountain there are seven bakeries, which serve both those living there and the anchorites <note>Hermits or monks who have retired into total solitary life.</note> in the Great Desert, who number six hundred men. Having resided on this mountain for a whole year, and having benefited much from the blessed and holy fathers—Arsisios the Great, Petubastos, Hagion, Chronios, and Serapion—and having been spurred on by many stories of the more ancient spiritual fathers I heard from them, I went into the innermost desert. 

On this mountain of Nitria there is one very large church. In this church stand three Palm Trees <note>original: "Φοίνικες" (Phoinikes). In this context, they were likely used as pillars or posts for public discipline.</note>, each having a whip hanging upon it. One is for the correction of the monks who stumble <note>Meaning those who commit a sin or break monastic rules.</note>; the second is for the punishment of robbers, if they should ever break in; and the third is for the correction of those who fail and fall into whatever sort of...

# THE LAUSIAC HISTORY

...the needs of the poorhouse <note>original: "πτωχείου" (ptocheion); a charitable institution for the poor, common in the early Byzantine East.</note>. After some time had passed—since the man had a very high reputation in Alexandria for being a lover of God and a most merciful elder <note>The term "elder" (presbytes) here refers to a senior monk or priest, likely Isidore, who managed the church's charities.</note> (for he flourished until the age of one hundred, and we ourselves were his contemporaries)—she was hesitant to remind him of her gift. 

At last, finding him in the church, she said to him: "I beg of you, what have you to say about those stones for which we gave you the five hundred coins?" <note>original: "νομίσματα" (nomismata); the standard gold currency of the time, often called solidi.</note>

He answered her, saying: "From the day you gave me the gold, I paid it out for the price of the stones. And if you wish to see them, come to my hospice <note>original: "ὀσπιτίῳ" (ospitio); a guesthouse or hospital for the sick and poor.</note>; for there the stones are kept. See if they please you, or if you would rather take back your gold."

She went most gladly. Now the poorhouse had women on the upper floors and men on the lower floors. When she arrived, the holy man led her into the gateway and said to her: "What do you wish to see first, the sapphires <note>original: "ὑακίνθους" (hyakinthous); these were blue precious stones, often translated as jacinths or sapphires in late antiquity.</note> or the emeralds?"

She replied, "Whatever you think best." 

When he had led her up to the upper floors, he showed her crippled women with disfigured faces <note>original: "ἠκρωτηριασμένας... λελωβημένας" (maimed... mutilated); likely victims of disease such as leprosy or severe injury who were cared for in the almshouse.</note> and said to her: "Behold the sapphires." 

Then he led her down to the lower floors and showed her the men, saying: "Behold the emeralds. I did not think more precious stones than these could be found. If they please you, then it is well; otherwise, take back your gold." 

When these things had happened, the virgin went out feeling deeply humbled and returned to her house, where she fell ill from great sorrow because she had not performed this deed for the sake of God, but under pressure. Later, however, she thanked the elder on behalf of the girl she had cared for, who had become wealthy through a childless marriage. Thereafter, she spent the rest of her life devoted to God.

The author uses this to contrast true purity with the superficial piety of the woman described next.</note>. ### Concerning Macarius the Elder.

This virgin, who possessed her title in name only but was a stranger to the ways of ascetic practice <note>original: "ἀσκήσεως" (askeseos); the disciplined lifestyle of a monk or nun.</note>—as the saying goes—the most holy Macarius the Elder <note>Macarius of Egypt (c. 300–391 AD), one of the most famous Desert Fathers.</note> wished to "bleed" <note>An ancient medical metaphor for curing an illness by removing excess; here, he wishes to cure her of the "excess" of greed.</note> her to relieve her of her greed. Having already spoken of the poverty of those who were crippled, he devised a scheme like this:

For in his youth, he had been a stone-cutter, what they call a <term>jeweler</term> <note>original: "καβιδάριον" (kabidarion); a specialist in cutting and polishing precious stones.</note>. He went to her and said: "Some necessary stones—<term>emeralds</term> <note>original: "σμάραγδοι" (smaragdoi)</note> and <term>jacinths</term> <note>original: "ὑάκινθοι" (hyakinthoi); in antiquity, this term often referred to blue stones like sapphires.</note>—have come into my possession. Whether they were stolen or obtained through trade, I cannot say. They are beyond valuation and cannot be assigned a standard price. However, the man who has them is selling them for five hundred <term>gold coins</term> <note>original: "νομισμάτων" (nomismaton); the gold solidus, the standard currency of the later Roman Empire.</note>. If it seems right to you to take them, give me the five hundred coins. You can then use the stones to create a variety of ornaments for your niece."

The one called a virgin, hanging on his every word for the sake of the girl, was enticed by her love of finery. She fell at his feet and said: "I beg of you, let no one else take them." The holy man then urged her, saying: "Come by my house and see them." But she would not hear of it; instead, she threw the five hundred gold coins before him, saying: "I beg of you, take them as you wish; for I do not want even to see the man who is selling them."

Having taken the five hundred coins from her, the holy Macarius distributed them for the

# XVI. THE LAUSIAC HISTORY
### Page 17

...having recounted these things <note>This sentence fragment concludes a previous thought, likely about the benefits of recording the lives of the saints.</note> for the praise of those who have attained virtue, and for the security and preservation of those who read this. There was a certain virgin in Alexandria, but she was a virgin in name only. In her outward appearance she was seemingly humble, but in her secret intent she was miserly, arrogant, and excessively a **lover of wealth** in matters of money. Indeed, she was a **lover of gold** <note>original: "φιλόχρυσος" (philochrysos)</note> rather than a **lover of Christ** <note>original: "φιλόχριστος" (philochristos); Palladius uses this clever wordplay to highlight her misplaced devotion.</note>. She never gave away a single thing of her own—not to a stranger, not to the poor, not to someone in distress, not to a monk, not to a fellow virgin, nor even a single <term>obol</term> <note>A small ancient Greek coin of very low value.</note> to the church. Despite many admonitions from the holy fathers, she did not rid herself of the heavy burden of her wealth.

She also had family, among whom she had adopted her own sister’s daughter as her own child; night and day she promised this girl that she would inherit her wealth, claiming this was a "heavenly desire." For this too is a form of the **Devil’s deception**: preparing the birth of greed under the mask of family affection. It is clear from this that the Devil does not truly care for family, for he himself is the one who taught men to be murderers of brothers, mothers, and fathers; this is acknowledged in the Holy Scriptures. 

Even if the Devil seems to allow some to have care for their kin, he does not do this out of goodwill toward them, but in order to train the soul to be unjust. He knows very well the final verdict: that the **unjust shall not inherit the kingdom of God**. To be sure, someone moved by spiritual understanding and divine desire is able to both care for their own soul and help their relatives if they are truly in need, providing various comforts. But when someone tramples their entire soul for the sake of "caring" for relatives, they fall under the law’s judgment, considering their own soul as a thing of vanity. 

The holy psalmist David also sings concerning those who care for their soul in the fear of God, saying:

> Who shall ascend the mountain of the Lord? Or who shall stand...

She spoke to me through that opening, saying: "Because a certain man’s thoughts were harmed by desire for me. So that I would not appear to cause him grief or invite slander, I chose to bring myself alive into this tomb rather than cause a soul made in the image of God to stumble." 

When I asked her, "How do you endure, servant of Christ, never meeting with anyone at all, but only wrestling with **spiritual apathy** <note>original: "ἀκηδίᾳ" (akēdia); often called the 'noonday demon,' this was a state of listlessness or depression that particularly afflicted desert monastics.</note> and your own thoughts?" 

She answered: "From morning until the **ninth hour** <note>Approximately 3:00 PM.</note> I pray, spinning flax every hour. During the remaining hours, I turn my mind to the faith of the holy fathers and patriarchs, and the struggles of the blessed apostles, prophets, and martyrs. When evening falls, I glorify my Master and eat my morsel of bread, persisting in my prayer through all the hours of the night. Expecting the end, I will one day depart from here with good hopes, and I shall look upon the face of Christ."

...a torture more cruel than any other punishment was devised by the one then acting as judge. He ordered a great cauldron to be filled with pitch <note>original: "πίσσης" (pissēs); boiling pitch was a common and agonizing method of execution for martyrs, intended to prolong suffering.</note> and set over a blazing, roaring fire. While the pitch was boiling and burning intensely, the inhuman governor addressed that blessed woman, saying: "Either go and submit yourself to the desires of your master, or know that I have ordered you to be cast into this cauldron." But she answered, saying: "May there never be such an unjust judge who commands me to submit to licentiousness <note>original: "ἀσωτίᾳ" (asōtia); referring to the sexual advances of her master which she had resisted.</note>." Driven to madness with rage, he ordered her to be stripped and cast into the cauldron. But she let out a cry, saying: "By the head of your king, whom you fear—if you have judged it right to punish me thus, do not order me to be stripped. Instead, command that I be lowered little by little into the boiling pitch, so that you may see how much endurance the Lord, whom you do not know, has granted me." And being lowered bit by bit, for a duration of about three hours, she finally breathed her last when the pitch reached her neck. Indeed, a vast company <note>original: "σύστημα χοροῦ" (systēma chorou); literally a 'system' or 'choir,' implying a harmonious community of believers.</note> of holy men and women then multiplied in the Church of Alexandria, having been found worthy of the land of the meek <note>A reference to the Beatitudes in the Gospel of Matthew: "Blessed are the meek, for they shall inherit the earth."</note>.

But if she remains in her original austerity," he requested that she be put to death as a punishment. # PALLADIUS — LAUSIAC HISTORY

### CHAPTER XVI

In the desert by the sea he gathered stones. And always building with these and making cells, he turned them over to those who were unable to build for themselves, finishing one cell every year. Once, when I said to this holy man: "What are you doing, Father, in such old age, killing your poor body <note>original: "σωμάτιον" (somation); literally 'little body,' a diminutive often used by monks to express humility or the insignificance of the flesh.</note> in this heat and these hardships?" He answered me, saying: "If it kills me, I too shall kill it." 

For he ate six ounces of bread each day and a bundle of green vegetables. He drank only as much water as was necessary. With God as my witness, I never knew this man to stretch out his feet, or to sleep intentionally upon either a rush mat or a bed. But throughout the whole night, while sitting, he wove rope from palm leaves to provide for his sustenance. 

Suspecting that he had practiced such extreme asceticism only in my presence, I inquired accurately from several of his disciples whether he maintained this discipline throughout his entire life. Those who remained with him in private, and who were themselves successfully practicing virtue, told me that from a young age he had maintained this way of life <note>original: "πολιτείαν" (politeian); a technical term in early Christian literature referring to a monk’s ascetic conduct or spiritual 'citizenship.'</note>, never sleeping on purpose—unless perhaps while working or eating his eyes closed for a moment because he was overcome by sleep, so that often the very morsel would fall from his mouth at mealtime because of his extreme drowsiness. 

When I once urged this holy man to lie down for a short while upon the mat, he said to me sadly: "If you can persuade the angels to sleep sometime, you will also persuade the diligent man <note>original: "τὸν σπουδαῖον" (ton spoudaion); used here to denote a serious or zealous ascetic.</note> of this." 

One day he sent me to his well around the ninth hour <note>Approximately 3:00 PM, the traditional hour for the first meal of the day among many desert ascetics.</note> to fill the bucket for our meal, as the time to eat was approaching. But it happened that when I arrived, I saw an asp <note>A venomous snake, likely the Egyptian cobra.</note> at the bottom of the well, and out of fear I did not draw the water, but went back running to report—

# PALLADIUS

He was well known to the entire Senate of Rome and to the wives of the leading men, dating back to when he had previously traveled there with the blessed Athanasius <note>original: "Ἀθανασίῳ"; likely St. Athanasius the Great (c. 296–373 AD), the Bishop of Alexandria who spent time in exile in Rome.</note>, and again with the holy Bishop Demetrius. Although he moved in the midst of great abundance and a surplus of resources, he wrote no will when he died; he left no money, nor any property to his own sisters, who were virgins. Instead, he entrusted them to Christ, saying: "The God who created you will himself provide for your needs and for your life, just as he did for me." There was, along with his sisters, a community <note>original: "σύστημα" (systēma); here referring to an organized sisterhood or convent.</note> of seventy virgins.

->About Dorotheus<-

**W**hen I was a young man and came to visit him, I begged to be trained in the basics of the monastic life. At that time I was still in the heat of my youth <note>original: "φλεγούσης μοι ἔτι τῆς ἡλικίας"; literally "my age still burning," referring to youthful passions.</note> and needed not words, but physical labor and bodily discipline. Like a good horse-tamer <note>original: "πωλοδάμνης" (pōlodamnēs); a vivid metaphor for a spiritual director who "breaks" the wild impulses of a student.</note>, he led me outside the city to the so-called desert regions, five miles away, and handed me over to a certain Dorotheus, a Theban ascetic who had spent sixty years living in a cave. He instructed me that after completing that number of years, I should return to him again for the rest of my spiritual instruction. However, I was unable to complete the full three years with him because I fell into a severe illness; thus I left him before the end of the appointed time. # THE LAUSIAC HISTORY 11

...in word, and character, and knowledge, to Isidore, who was a presbyter and the hospital-master <note>original: "ξενοδόχῳ" (xenodochō); an official in charge of a guesthouse or hospital for the poor and pilgrims.</note> of the Church of Alexandria. It was said that during the first years of his youth he had lived in the desert, having contended in the struggles of asceticism; I myself saw his cell on the mountain of Nitria <note>Nitria was one of the most important early Christian monastic centers in Egypt, located in the Nitrian Desert.</note>. I encountered him when he was an old man of seventy years. After living for fifteen more years, he died in peace. 

This holy man, until his very death, never wore a linen garment except for a headband <note>original: "φακιαλίου" (phakialiou); likely a small cloth or scarf worn around the head, derived from the Latin *faciale*.</note>; he never used a bath, never tasted meat, and never ate at a table until he was full. Yet his body was so strengthened by the grace of God that all those who were ignorant of his diet were convinced that he lived in great luxury. If I wished to recount the virtues of his soul in detail, time would fail me in the telling. 

He was so gentle, so loving toward humanity, and so peaceable, that even his unbelieving enemies—who were hostile because of his upright faith in Christ—respected even his shadow because of the man's extraordinary kindness. He possessed such spiritual grace, such knowledge of the Holy Scriptures, and such a grasp of divine doctrines, that even during the banquets at the usual hour when the brothers took their meal, the mind of the saint would be caught up in ecstasy <note>original: "ἐξίστασθαι" (existasthai); the state of being 'outside oneself' in spiritual rapture.</note>. He would fall silent, and when he was encouraged to describe what happened in these moments of rapture, he would say: "I have traveled away in my mind, carried off by a certain contemplation <note>original: "θεωρίας" (theōrias); the word refers to the soul's direct vision or mystical insight into divine truths.</note>." 

I myself also knew him to weep many times at the table, and when I asked the reason for his tears, I heard him say: "I am ashamed that I, being a rational being <note>original: "λογικὸς" (logikos); a human endowed with reason and the image of the Divine Logos.</note>, should partake of irrational food <note>original: "ἀλόγου... τροφῆς"; physical food, the same as that eaten by animals without reason.</note>, when I ought to be in the delights of Paradise and be filled with ambrosial food because of the authority given to us by Christ."

# PALLADIUS 10

...harm from falling into pride. For this reason, seek out the "Luminous Window" <note>original: "θυρίδα Φωτεινὴν" (thyrida Phōteinēn). Palladius uses this metaphor to describe the enlightening perspective gained from observing holy people.</note> of holy encounters with both men and women. Through these encounters, just as if you were reading a finely-written book, you may be able to see your own heart clearly. By observing their inner disposition, you will be able to test your own laziness or your diligence. For there are many things that bear witness to goodness: the healthy color of the face that flourishes from a life of discipline <note>original: "πολιτείᾳ" (politeia); referring here to the "citizenship" or way of life of a monk.</note>, the arrangement of one’s clothing, an unpretentious character, humility in speech, piety in manners, simplicity in words, the grace of one’s judgment, and the intelligence of one’s thoughts.

May the Lord strengthen your own decorum, as well as all those who follow the goal of godliness, even if you should find yourselves in a state of listlessness <note>original: "ἀκηδίᾳ" (akēdia); a state of spiritual apathy or "the noonday devil" common in monastic literature.</note> or any other difficult circumstance. For "the dress of a man, the stride of his foot, and the laughter of his teeth, reveal the things concerning him," as the proverb says <note>This is a quotation from the Wisdom of Sirach 19:30.</note>.

### Concerning Isidore the Hospitaller

As I begin my account of the lives of the holy fathers, I will not omit those who lived in cities, villages, or caves unknown to you, as well as those who lived in monasteries <note>original: "κοινοβίοις" (koinobiois); communal monasteries where monks lived together under a rule.</note>. For it is not the location that we look for where men have succeeded in virtue, but rather the manner of their correct purpose, through which they practiced the "angelic way of life" <note>A common early Christian term for the monastic life, suggesting that monks lived like angels, focused entirely on God.</note> with the help of Christ.

I first set foot in the city of the Alexandrians during the second consulship of Theodosius the Great King <note>Theodosius I; his second consulship was in the year 388 CE.</note>—who is now among the angels because of his upright faith in Christ. # LAUSIAC HISTORY
### Page 9

...acting in any other way out of irrational desire, or with a corrupted conscience while still claiming to rely on faith, is mistaken. For the Savior gave a clear rule, saying: **"By their fruits you shall know them."** <note>Matthew 7:16. Palladius emphasizes that outward religious profession must be validated by the "fruit" of one's character.</note> It is widely agreed that the fruit of those who conduct their lives according to the Divine Word and spiritual understanding—as described also by the divine Apostle <note>original: "θείῳ ἀποστόλῳ" (theiō apostolō); referring to St. Paul.</note>—is love, joy, peace, longsuffering, kindness, goodness, faithfulness, gentleness, self-control, and purity. For the blessed Paul himself testified to this, saying: **"But the fruit of the Spirit is these things."** <note>Galatians 5:22-23.</note>

And furthermore: anyone who strives to possess such fruits in an irrational, untimely, or aimless manner—refusing to eat meat, or drink wine, or dwelling with a wicked conscience—fails the test. Again, the same blessed Apostle Paul declared: **"Every man that strives for mastery is temperate in all things."** <note>1 Corinthians 9:25. The term for "strives" refers to an athlete in training, a common metaphor for the monastic life.</note>

This means that while the flesh is healthy, one should abstain from those things that cause [spiritual] heaviness. But when the body is weak, or in pain, or suffering from sorrow or difficult circumstances, one may participate in and use foods or drinks as protective medicines <note>original: "φαρμάκοις ἀλεξιτηρίοις" (pharmakois alexitēriois); literally "warding-off medicines." This reflects the moderate "Middle Way" of desert asceticism.</note> for the sake of healing. However, even then, one must strictly hold back from those things that are truly harmful to the soul: anger, envy, vanity, listlessness <term>akedia: a state of spiritual apathy or "weariness of heart" often discussed by the desert fathers</term>, slander, and irrational suspicions, while always giving thanks to the Lord.

Having discussed these matters sufficiently, I bring another exhortation to your **Love of Learning** <note>original: "Φιλομαθείᾳ" (Philomatheia). This is a personified title Palladius uses to address Lausus, much like "Your Excellence."</note>. Flee, with all your might, from the company of men who offer no spiritual benefit. These are men who adorn their outer skin inappropriately; even if they seem to be orthodox—let alone if they are heretics—they cause harm through their hypocrisy. They may seem to command respect because of the gray hairs on their head or the wrinkles on their face from the length of time they have lived. 

Yet, even if you are not directly corrupted by them because of your own pious character, at the very least, some seemingly insignificant thing will wound you. Your mind may become lax toward them, and you might be provoked to mock them; which is a harm to you...

->B<-

<summary>Palladius explains that true spirituality is judged by the "Fruits of the Spirit" rather than extreme or aimless asceticism. He warns Lausus to avoid hypocrites who look holy but lack inner virtue, as even mocking such people can damage one's own soul.</summary>

# PALLADIUS
### Page 8

...the ancients <note>The text likely refers to ancient pagans or misguided thinkers mentioned on the preceding page.</note>, and the rest of the company of **self-styled philosophers** <term>ἐθελοφιλοσόφων (ethelophilosophōn): those who pursue a "philosophy" based on their own will or whims rather than divine truth</term>. They were driven to such a degree of **vainglory** and **lack of self-control** that they even ignored God, the Creator of all things, and worshipped lifeless idols. 

This issue also touched upon the blessed Apostle Peter and his use of wine, such that even the Lord and Teacher of these men—the Savior of all—was criticized. This occurred when the Jews brought charges regarding the partaking of food, asking, **"Why do your disciples not fast as John’s do?"** <note>Matthew 9:14. A reference to the followers of John the Baptist who practiced strict asceticism.</note> And again, those who were unfaithful approached the disciples and said reproachfully, **"Your teacher eats and drinks with tax collectors and sinners."** <note>Matthew 9:11.</note> Now, they certainly would not have found fault with him for bread and water; they clearly meant he was partaking of **cooked meat** <note>original: "ὄψῳ" (opsō); often referring to a relish, fish, or meat eaten with bread to make it more palatable.</note> and **wine**.

To those who quite irrationally blame the drinking of wine while marveling at the drinking of water, the Savior answers: **"John came in the way of righteousness, neither eating nor drinking"**—meaning meat and wine—**"for he was unable to live as others did. And they say, ‘He has a demon.’ The Son of Man came eating and drinking, and they say, ‘Look, a glutton and a wine-drinker, a friend of tax collectors and sinners,’"** <note>Matthew 11:18-19.</note> simply because he ate and drank.

What then shall we do? Let us follow neither those who blame nor those who praise [outwardly]. Rather, let us fast with John by means of **reason** <note>original: "λόγῳ" (logō); implying a rational and spiritual purpose for fasting rather than just habit.</note>, even if they say to us, "You have a demon." Or let us drink wine with Jesus in **wisdom**, if the body has need of it, even if they say to us, "Look, people who are gluttons and wine-drinkers." 

For neither eating nor abstaining is what constitutes the truth, but rather **faith through love** extending into our deeds. For whenever faith follows every action, the one who eats and drinks because of faith is beyond condemnation. For **"everything that is not of faith is sin."** <note>Romans 14:23. A key Pauline principle stating that the moral quality of an act depends on the conscience and faith of the person doing it.</note> But since every lover of pleasure, or anyone who falls into error, will say, "I partake by faith," or...

<summary>Palladius defends moderate Christian practice against extreme asceticism, arguing that both Jesus and John the Baptist were unfairly criticized for their dietary habits. He concludes that true holiness is determined by faith and love rather than what one eats or drinks.</summary>

# THE LAUSIAC HISTORY
### Page 7

...boasting in **fasting**, and straining themselves in the study of the **divine oracles** <note>original: "θείων λογίων" (theion logion); usually referring to the Holy Scriptures</note>, and placing their confidence in the learning of various pursuits, or perhaps displaying a state of **dispassion** <term>ἀπαθείας (apatheias): a state of being free from emotional disturbance or "passions," a central goal in early Christian monasticism</term> through an undiscerning mind. Under the pretext of a certain piety, they have fallen ill with a **love of being first** <note>original: "φιλοπρωτίαν" (philoprotian); an ambitious desire for leadership or status over others</note>, from which meddlesomeness is born, laughing at the very mother of **minding one's own business** <term>ἰδιοπραγμοσύνης (idiopragmosynēs): the virtue of attending to one’s own spiritual duties and internal state rather than interfering in the affairs of others</term>.

Stand firm and manly, therefore, in all understanding; I exhort you, do not fatten your wealth—which indeed you have already done sufficiently by diminishing it through distribution to those in need. Therefore, let the service arising from this, which becomes a work of virtue, not be done through any irrational impulse or prejudice, nor under the pretext of seeking human approval. Do not bind your choice with an oath, as many have done who, out of rivalry and **vainglory**, declared they would not drink or eat. By turning their free will into a forced necessity through an oath, they fall back into a piteous state through either a love of life and **listlessness** <term>ἀκηδίᾳ (akēdia): "accidie," a state of spiritual exhaustion or "the noonday demon" that makes a monk feel weary of his religious life</term>, or through bodily weakness and the desire for some pleasure, thereby suffering the labor pains of **perjury**.

Therefore, if you partake with **reason** <note>original: "λόγῳ" (logo); also referring to the Divine Reason or the Word/Logos</note>, and if you abstain with reason, you will never sin. For the **Logos** is God, who drives out harmful movements within us and welcomes what is beneficial. For it is said that "the law is not laid down for the righteous." <note>1 Timothy 1:9</note> For **wine-drinking with reason** is better than **water-drinking with pride** <note>original: "τύφου" (typhou); literally a "smoke" or "fog," referring to the blinding nature of conceit or vanity</note>. Observe for me those holy men who drink wine with the reason of godliness, and those unreasoning men who drink water with arrogance, becoming profane and corrupted. Do not blame or praise the material substance of food anymore, but rather bless or pity the **disposition** <note>original: "γνώμην" (gnomen); the inner intent, mindset, or moral resolve</note> of those who use that substance well or poorly.

**Joseph** <note>The biblical patriarch in Egypt</note> once drank wine among the Egyptians, but his mind was not harmed, for he kept his disposition secure. Yet **Pythagoras**, **Diogenes**, and **Plato** <note>Famous Greek philosophers known for their strict diets or ascetic lifestyles</note> drank water—as did the **Mani-**

<meta>catchword: chaeans,</meta>

<summary>Palladius argues that the spiritual value of fasting or drinking depends entirely on one's "Reason" (Logos) and internal disposition rather than the physical act itself. He warns that rigid ascetic oaths often lead to pride or perjury, noting that even holy men like Joseph drank wine while pagan philosophers and heretics often chose water out of vanity.</summary>

# PALLADIUS
### Page 6

Therefore, through this discourse, I have encountered many of the saints, not having applied my reason to the task as a secondary matter. On the contrary, I have completed a journey of thirty days, and even twice that distance, treading all the **land of the Romans** <note>original: "γῆν Ῥωμαίων" (gēn Rhōmaiōn); referring to the Roman Empire, specifically the eastern provinces where monasticism flourished.</note> on foot, as if before God, and especially enduring the hardship of the journey for the sake of meeting a **God-loving man** <note>original: "ἀνδρὸς Φιλοθέου" (andros Philotheou); while 'Philotheos' is a name, here it likely describes the character of the ascetics Palladius sought out.</note>, so that I might gain something which I did not already possess.

For if the one who is much better than I—and perhaps better than the whole world—who surpassed many in his way of life through spiritual knowledge, understanding, and faith in Christ, the blessed apostle **Paul**, undertook the journey from Tarsus to Judea for the sake of meeting **Peter, John, and James**. Wherefore he also describes this as a kind of boast, setting forth his own labors to provoke those who live a life of hesitation and laziness regarding virtue, when he says, *"I went up to Jerusalem to visit Peter"* <note>A reference to Galatians 1:18.</note>. He was not satisfied only with the reputation of Peter's virtue, but he longed for the meeting with his person face to face.

How much more then ought I, a **debtor of ten thousand talents** <note>A biblical allusion to the Parable of the Unforgiving Servant in Matthew 18:24, used here to express the author's sense of spiritual debt and humility.</note>, to have done this—not to benefit those saints, but to help myself, a sinner. For those who recorded the lives of the fathers—**Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, Moses, Elijah**, and those who followed—did not provide these narratives so that they might glorify those men, but rather so that they might benefit those who read them.

Let it be known then, most faithful and venerable servant of Christ, **Lausus**, as you often admonish yourself, that you must also endure my chatter for the guarding of your pious mind. For the mind is naturally prone to be tossed like waves by various vices, both visible and invisible; yet our constant attention and **minding one’s own business** <term>ἰδιοπραγμοσύνη (idiopragmosynē): the practice of focusing on one's own spiritual progress and duties rather than being distracted by the affairs of others.</term> is able to keep it still. For many of the more devout brothers, who pride themselves on the labors of their asceticism and their almsgiving, and on their celibacy and complete vir-

Observe in this book how, by possessing a venerable and **soul-benefiting** <note>original: "ψυχωφελὲς" (psychōphelēs); a common term in Eastern Christian literature for material that aids the growth and health of the soul.</note> reminder, an unceasing **medicine of truth** <note>original: "φάρμακον ἀληθείας" (pharmakon alētheias).</note>, you may scatter every drowsiness of forgetfulness that arises in the soul from irrational desire. Through this, you may also cast off all double-mindedness in faith, stinginess in providing for needs, all hesitation and small-mindedness of character, as well as quickness to anger, irrational disturbance, and untimely fear. By fleeing the vain distractions of this corrupt world, you will advance with unceasing desire in your hope toward God, becoming a guide in the purpose of piety for yourself, for those with you, those under your authority, and for the most **pious kings** <note>referring to the Byzantine imperial family whom Lausus served as chamberlain.</note>.

Through these virtuous achievements, all who love Christ hasten to be united with God, while also awaiting the **dissolution** <note>original: "ἀνάλυσιν" (analysin); meaning the separation of the soul from the body at death.</note> of the soul from the body. This is clearly a daily preparation, according to what is written: *“I am hard pressed by a good desire to depart and be with Christ, for that is far better”* <note>Philippians 1:23</note>. And also: *“Prepare your works for your departure, and get things ready in the field”* <note>Proverbs 24:27</note>. For he who always remembers that death will surely come by necessity, and will not delay, will not stumble into great sins, according to what is written: *“In all your words, remember your end, and you will never sin”* <note>Sirach 7:36</note>.

And in addition to all else, I ask that you do not hold against us this brief account of explanations, nor **scorn** <note>original: "διαπτύων" (diaptyōn); literally "spitting upon."</note> the plainness and lack of elegance in my words. For it is not the task of divine teaching to speak with sophisticated rhetoric, but rather to persuade the mind with thoughts of truth, as it is said: *“Open your mouth with the word of God, and judge all things healthily”* <note>A variation of Proverbs 31:8-9</note>. And again: *“Do not miss the discourse of the elders, for they too learned from their fathers”* <note>Sirach 8:9</note>.

I, therefore, O most learning-loving man of God, having in part...

# PALLADIUS
### Account of the Life of the Holy Fathers.

Many people at various times have left behind many diverse writings. Some of these were written through inspiration from above and God-given grace, intended for the building up and security of those who faithfully follow the teachings of the Savior. Others were written from a desire for human praise or with corrupt intentions, serving only as a supposed comfort for those suffering from irrationality. Still others were produced out of an untimely madness and the work of the **devil** <note>original: "μισοκάλου δαίμονος" (misokalou daimonos); literally "the demon who hates the good," a common patristic epithet for Satan.</note>, written with pride and a destructive mania to deceive people and stain the pure and **universal Church** <note>original: "καθολικῆς ἐκκλησίας" (katholikēs ekklēsias); "catholic" here refers to the "whole" or "universal" body of believers.</note>, leaving an impression on the minds of the foolish to hinder a venerable and pure way of life.

Therefore, it seemed good also to me, a humble man, out of my hope in Christ and out of respect for the command of your **Greatness** <note>referring to the high-ranking official Lausus, the work's patron.</note>, O most learned of men, to first tell my own story: the facts of my age and the progress of my mind toward the virtue of God, which began in my youth. I have now spent thirty-three years in the community of the brothers and in the monastic life. I have spent twenty years in the **episcopate** <note>the office of a bishop.</note>, making fifty-three years in total of my whole life.

Since you desire to know the achievements of the holy fathers for the sake of spiritual benefit, I thought it necessary to set down in writing for you an account of both men and women—those I have seen myself and those I have heard of from most faithful souls. I was raised among them in the deserts of Egypt, Libya, the **Thebaid** <note>the region of Upper Egypt surrounding Thebes, a major center for early Christian monasticism.</note>, and **Syene** <note>modern-day Aswan.</note>—among whom are those called the **Tabennesiotes** <note>monks who followed the communal rule of Saint Pachomius at Tabennisi.</note>—and later in Mesopotamia, Palestine, Syria, and the regions of the West, including Rome, Campania, and the surrounding areas. I shall begin to set forth for you from the very start...

<meta>catchword: to know-</meta>

<summary>Palladius introduces his work as a reliable alternative to corrupt or misleading histories, citing his 33 years of monastic experience and 20 years as a bishop. He outlines his plan to record the lives of holy men and women he encountered across the Roman Empire, from Egypt to Rome.</summary>

<keywords>Palladius, Lausus, preface, monasticism, biography, Egypt, Thebaid, Tabennesiotes, episcopate, spiritual benefit</keywords>

### THE LAUSIAC HISTORY
### Chapter 16

->I was deemed worthy to see divine faces face-to-face.<-

As for those who were perfected in the **arena** <note>original: "σκάμματι" (skammati); literally a trench or wrestling pit, used here as a metaphor for the difficult environment of spiritual struggle.</note> of piety by the God-bearing athletes of Christ, I have learned of their heavenly life. I have wandered through many cities and very many villages, both caves and all the dwellings of the monks in the desert, traveling far for the purpose of godliness. With all accuracy, I have recorded some things that I myself investigated, while others I heard from the holy fathers. These feats of great men and of women who were **more courageous than their nature** <note>original: "ἀνδρειοτέρων τῆς φύσεως" (andreioterōn tēs physeōs); this reflects a common late antique view that women who achieved high spiritual states were overcoming the perceived 'weakness' of their gender.</note> because of their hope in Christ, I have noted in this book and sent to you—you who are a lover of the hearing of divine words, an ornament of the best and God-loving men, and the glory of our most faithful and God-loving kingdom, O genuine and Christ-loving servant of God, **Lausus** <note>the high-ranking chamberlain at the court of Emperor Theodosius II to whom this history is dedicated.</note>. 

According to your command, I have inscribed a brief account of each of Christ’s athletes, both male and female, explaining their famous names and choosing only a very few of the many and very great feats of each. I have also added, for most of them, their lineage, their city, and the location of their monastery. 

I have also made mention of men and women who reached the very summit of virtue, but who—because of the mother of pride, which is called **vainglory** <note>original: "κενοδοξίαν" (kenodoxian); literally 'empty glory,' one of the primary vices in early monastic theology, referring to the desire for human approval.</note>—fell into the deepest pit and the very floor of Hades. Their achievements of **asceticism** <note>original: "ἀσκήσεως" (askēseōs); the rigorous self-discipline and training of the monk.</note>, which had been acquired over long periods and with much labor, making them lovely and desirable, were poured away in a single moment of time by the smoke of conceit. Yet, by the grace of our Savior, the providence of the holy fathers, and the sympathy of spiritual compassion, they were snatched from the **nets of the devil** <note>original: "δικτύων" (diktyōn); a common patristic metaphor for the traps and temptations of Satan.</note>. Through the prayers of the saints, they recovered their virtuous life once again.

<header>PALLADIUS</header>

...not without the protection of the Divine Spirit, who commanded us—or rather, to tell the truth, who awakened our sluggish mind to the contemplation of higher things. This was done to prompt us toward the imitation of, and competition with, the virtues found in the spiritual discipline <term>askesis: the practice of self-discipline and abstinence for spiritual growth</term> of our holy and immortal spiritual fathers, who lived in the worship of God through great physical hardship <term>skleragogia: literally 'harsh treatment' of the body to prioritize the soul</term>. Thus, we were to record the lives of these unconquered athletes and send them to you, proclaiming the active virtues of each of these great figures.

The one who possesses this divine and spiritual longing is that best of men, **Lausus**, who by God’s decree has been appointed as a guardian of this same pious Kingdom.

Therefore, I—who am unlearned in speech and have barely tasted spiritual knowledge, and who am unworthy even to be included in the catalog of the holy fathers' spiritual lives—feared the immeasurable weight of this command, which is far beyond my powers. It is a difficult task to undertake, requiring both worldly wisdom and spiritual insight. 

Nevertheless, feeling reverence first for the zeal of your virtue which stirred me to this effort, and considering also the benefit to those who will read it, and fearing the danger of irrational disobedience, I have met your noble command through God’s providence. Using great care and supported by the intercession of the holy fathers, I have entered the arena <note>original: "σκάμματος" (skammatos); literally a wrestling pit or trench, a favorite metaphor of Palladius for the site of spiritual or intellectual struggle.</note> of these struggles. I have recorded in a brief summary only the most essential and significant deeds of these noble athletes and great men. These accounts include not only famous men who achieved the highest way of life <term>politeia: the conduct or "citizenship" of a monk in the heavenly kingdom</term>, but also blessed and ascetic women who practiced the pinnacle of the spiritual life. Of their holy deeds...

### PREFACE.

<note>A large decorative initial 'E' within a square border, decorated with floral motifs and foliage.</note>

IN this book is recorded the virtuous discipline <term>askesis: the practice of self-discipline and abstinence for spiritual growth</term> and the marvelous way of life of the blessed and holy fathers, the monks and anchorites <term>anchorites: individuals who have withdrawn from society for religious reasons to live in solitary silence</term> of the desert. This record is intended for the inspiration and imitation of those who wish to successfully achieve heavenly citizenship, and who desire to travel the smooth path leading to the entrance of the heavens. Here also are the memories of aged women and famous, divinely-inspired mothers who, with a courageous and perfect mindset, accomplished the labors of virtuous discipline. They serve as a model and an object of divine love for those women who wish to be crowned with the wreath of self-control and purity. This was undertaken because of a certain most excellent man, who is widely learned in his judgment, peaceful in his character, pious in his heart, a lover of God in his mind, and a sharer of his resources with those in need. Because of the goodness of his ways, he is honored above many elite men at the very summit of high office, and overall by the pow-

# TO THE READER.

<note>A horizontal woodcut decorative band featuring repeating symmetrical patterns of floral and foliate motifs.</note>

*Behold for you our Palladius,
kind Reader, promised five years
ago. But in the delay, first there
were my own occupations, then
also the* Printer’s *postponement.
But it is a good thing that you
may have it even now; and so,
count me among those who fulfill
their obligations.* <note>original: "inter bona nomina cense." Meursius uses a financial metaphor here; "bona nomina" refers to "good debts" or reliable debtors who pay what they owe.</note> *The* Author *is of no insignificant antiquity, and one who lived twelve hundred years ago.* *He* wrote *On the Lives of the Saints* <note>The work is titled "De vitis sanctorum" here, better known as the "Historia Lausiaca" or "Lausiac History."</note>, *which he either saw himself as an eyewitness, or received from trustworthy sources; and he dedicated this work to* Lausus, *the chamberlain of the sacred bedchamber* <term>Grand Chamberlain (Praepositus Sacri Cubiculi): One of the highest-ranking officials in the late Roman and Byzantine imperial courts.</term> *of the Emperor Theodosius the Younger. I transcribed it at* Heidelberg, *when I was staying there eight years ago, from a very ancient parchment manuscript* <term>codex: a handwritten book of the ancient or medieval period</term> *of the* Palatine Library <note>The Bibliotheca Palatina in Heidelberg was one of the most important libraries of the German Renaissance.</note>. *I am the first to bring it forth in* Greek; *whereas a* Latin *version [was published] sixty years ago by a learned—*

<summary>In this opening of the preface, editor Johannes Meursius introduces his long-awaited Greek edition of Palladius's history of the desert fathers, noting its 5th-century origins and his use of a rare Heidelberg manuscript.</summary>
