<meta>This page serves as the concluding dedication of the preface. While the previous page introduced the history and transcription of the manuscript, this section personalizes the work, addressing a specific (though unnamed in this snippet) friend or patron before providing the date and location of the edition's completion.</meta>

### PREFACE.

...you have shown. Well done, then, in that virtue of yours; and, as you have been until now, continue to be hereafter. For my part, regarding myself, I shall never change this affection. For the present, however, I dedicate this Palladius <note>original: "Palladium"; Meursius refers to the book itself by the name of its author, Palladius of Galatia.</note> to you, and I desire it to be a testimony among posterity that I love no one more, nor does anyone exist from whom I more clearly trust that I am loved in return. With this I conclude, and I bid you a most heartfelt farewell. At Leiden <note>original: "Lugduni Batavorum," the Latin name for the city of Leiden in the Netherlands, a major center for printing and scholarship.</note>, on the Nones of February <note>February 5th</note>, 1616.

<meta>catchword: TO THE REA-</meta>

<summary>In this concluding segment of the preface, Johannes Meursius offers a warm, personal dedication of the work to a close friend, affirming their mutual affection and recording the date of completion as February 5, 1616, in Leiden.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Meursius, dedication, Palladius, Leiden, 17th-century scholarship, friendship</keywords>

--- Page 240 ---
<meta>This page continues the personal dedication from Johannes Meursius to a close friend. The previous page ended with the verb "showed" (ostendebat), and this page describes their emotional parting and subsequent reunion in Britain after six years apart.</meta>

### PREFACE.

...showed. Think of what the state of mind was for each of us, when that final **Farewell** <note>original: "Vale"</note> had to be said. Then, when six years had passed and we were unexpectedly restored to one another in **Britain** <note>original: "Britannia"</note>, what a meeting was that of ours! What a state of mind! Indeed, we experienced that extreme joy can have the same power over the human soul as sadness. Afterwards, when a kind **God** <note>original: "Deus"</note> restored you to your fatherland, you brought back the same affection which you had carried away; and which I, with the best of faith, was preserving for you, so that no one could find anything lacking in our **Constancy** <note>original: "constantia"</note>. Soon, having been even further elevated by honors—the kind which are usually reckoned among the highest in our **Republic** <note>original: "Rep." or "Respublica," likely referring to the Dutch Republic</note>, and with which your fatherland wished to bless you on account of your **Virtue** <note>original: "virtutis"</note> and **Learning** <note>original: "doctrinæ"</note>—you retained that same spirit; nor [did you allow] **Fortune** <note>original: "Fortunæ"</note> to have any authority over you

3

<summary>In this excerpt from the preface, Meursius reflects on a poignant reunion with a friend in Britain after six years of separation and praises the friend's unchanging character despite his rise to high political office.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Meursius, Britain, friendship, Dutch Republic, virtue, learning, 17th-century scholarship</keywords>

--- Page 241 ---
<meta>This page continues the personal dedication. The previous page ended with "nor [did you allow] Fortune to have any authority over you," leading into the description of the friend's greatness of spirit.</meta>

### PREFACE.

<meta>catchword: bar.</meta>

...which I can truly call so great that I do not know if any greater exists among any other men. It is a pleasure to call to mind the time spent twenty years ago in this **Academy** <note>original: "Academia"; likely referring to the University of Leiden, where Meursius studied and later taught</note> of ours. I call upon your own loyalty as witness: could brothers love each other more sincerely? You will agree with me that they could not. And so, when you were preparing to depart for foreign lands, you most kindly invited me to be your traveling companion, offering that I should share in your status and your means. Since the requirements of my studies did not permit this and I declined, you entrusted a very well-stocked **Library** <note>original: "bibliotheca"</note> to my care, with the understanding that I should keep it as my own if, by some chance, the **common lot of humanity** <note>original: "humanitus"; a common Latin euphemism for death</note> should befall you while abroad. Thus, it remained in my possession for nine full years. Recall with me that day when I escorted you to **Rotterdam** <note>original: "Roterodamum"</note> as you were departing for **France** <note>original: "Gallia"</note>—

<summary>Meursius reminisces about their shared time at the University of Leiden twenty years prior and details how his friend entrusted him with a private library before traveling to France.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Meursius, University of Leiden, Rotterdam, France, library, friendship, 17th-century education</keywords>

->centered text<-
*To the Most Distinguished Man,*
<-

# EISO TIARDA, JURISCONSULT <note>original: "I. C." (*Iuris Consulto*); a title for a person learned in the law, similar to a legal counsel or judge.</note>

->centered text<-
*Assessor of the Council of the United Netherlands,*
<-

# JOHANNES MEURSIUS SENDS GREETINGS <note>original: "S. D." (*Salutem Dicit*); a standard formal greeting in Latin correspondence.</note>

HERE is our **Palladius** <note>Palladius of Galatia (c. 363–420 AD), the author of the *Lausiac History*, a chronicle of early Christian monks. Meursius is presenting his newly edited version of this text.</note> for you, Most Distinguished Man, promised to you two years ago. And you would have had it by that time, if it had been possible to keep faith with the **printers** <note>original: "Typographos"</note>—men who, as you well know, dictate the law to learned men. Therefore, I judge that no further excuse should be made. Certainly, you would not have it even now, had I not broken the delay myself. And truly, I did not wish to be put off any longer, for the sake of our friendship,

<summary>Johannes Meursius begins his formal dedication of Palladius's work to Eiso Tiarda, apologizing for a two-year delay caused by the slow pace of the printing house.</summary>
<keywords>Eiso Tiarda, Johannes Meursius, Palladius, book history, printing industry, United Netherlands, 17th-century dedication</keywords>
